==================================================================================================
nest genome :: per-project innovation extracts
183 reference projects across 20 categories
==================================================================================================

one extract per project. each names the core innovation, the concrete techniques, and exactly how
nest can steal or adapt it. priority tag is the value to nest's mission (must > high > medium >
low > reference).

##################################################################################################
# category: compression-general  (12 repos)
# focus: general-purpose byte compression and entropy coding
##################################################################################################

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] facebook/zstd
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A general-purpose LZ77-family compressor that pairs a fast match-finder with two entropy back-ends
             (Huff0 Huffman for literals, FSE/tANS table-based ANS for sequence symbols), plus
             trained dictionaries, long-distance matching, and a repeat-offset cache, all wrapped
             in a self-describing frame/block format.
innovation:  Trained dictionaries: zstd's ZDICT trainer (COVER/fastCover greedy segment selection) builds a
             small shared "prior" -- a synthetic past window plus pre-baked entropy tables
             (Huffman tree + FSE tables for literals/match-lengths/offsets + 3 seed
             repeat-offsets) -- prepended to the decode window so each tiny,
             independently-decodable payload compresses against shared cross-record statistics
             instead of starting cold. This is exactly nest's regime: many small
             structurally-similar records (chunk_ids, spans, provenance JSON, BM25 postings)
             compressed in isolation, where per-payload entropy tables and LZ history are pure
             overhead today.
techniques:
  - Two-stage entropy: Huff0 (canonical Huffman, max 11-bit codes, weight-encoded tree) for
    literals; FSE = table-driven tANS for literal-length/match-length/offset symbols, with an
    Accuracy_Log power-of-2 normalized distribution and per-symbol (Symbol, Num_Bits, Baseline)
    decode rows
  - 4 independent Huffman bitstreams decoded in parallel for ILP vs 1 stream; bitstreams read
    backward from the last byte
  - Repeat-offset cache: 3 most-recent offsets (rep1/rep2/rep3) with single-stepped wrapping
    rotation, so periodic data spends ~1 offset code instead of a full offset
  - Per-block FSE table modes: Predefined / RLE / FSE_Compressed / Repeat (reuse previous block's
    table) to amortize table cost
  - ZDICT dictionary trainer (COVER / fastCover): greedy frequent-segment selection building a
    shared prior + pre-baked entropy tables + seed repeat offsets
  - Long-distance matching (LDM): a coarse hash-chain over a large window (up to 2GB) catching
    far-apart duplicates before the normal match-finder
  - Self-describing frame: 4-byte magic + 2-14B header, 3-byte block headers, optional xxHash-64
    checksum, plus 16 skippable-frame magics (0x184D2A5x) for additive metadata old decoders skip
  - Configurable speed/ratio via levels incl. negative --fast levels; decode speed roughly
    constant across levels
nest steals: Three concrete moves, all in nest-format/src/encoding/zstd_codec.rs and the writer. (1)
             DICTIONARY: add zstd_encode_with_dict/zstd_decode_with_dict using zstd's dictionary
             API. Train ONE deterministic dictionary at build time over the small repetitive
             sections (chunk_ids, chunks_original_spans, provenance, search_contract, BM25
             postings/terms in 0x08) and store it as a new additive section (reserved id 0x09) or
             in the manifest. Determinism holds because ZDICT is a pure function of sorted samples
             (feed them in canonical/alphabetical order). (2) KNOBS: replace hardcoded
             encode_all(bytes, 19) with a ZstdParams struct (level + enable_long_distance_matching
             + window_log) set per preset (tiny -> level 22 + LDM for the whole-database/repo
             payloads in the north star). (3) SKIPPABLE-FRAME PATTERN as a format lesson for
             nest-format/src/layout: zstd's 0x184D2A5x magics are the canonical way an additive
             format lets old readers skip unknown extensions -- mirror this for nest's reserved
             section ids 0x09+. Constraints respected: zstd never touches embeddings (hot SIMD
             path), section checksums still hash physical bytes, content_hash still hashes decoded
             bytes so a dict-compressed section yields the same content_hash as raw.
compression: Central to "compress more than everyone." nest already vendors zstd but uses its weakest config:
             level-19, no dictionary, no LDM, each small section compressed cold. Trained
             dictionaries are the single highest-leverage zstd feature for nest's
             many-small-similar-records workload and can sharply cut the non-embedding footprint
             at tiny corpus sizes; LDM extends the same engine to whole-repo/whole-database
             payloads. FSE/tANS and the repeat-offset trick are reference designs for any future
             nest-native entropy coder over postings or quantized residuals.
perf:        Reference C: ~510 MB/s compress, ~1550 MB/s decompress at level -1; decode speed is roughly
             level-independent (entropy-bound, not match-bound). The 4-stream Huffman split exists
             purely to exploit superscalar ILP. FSE/tANS decode is a tight table-lookup loop (1
             lookup + bit-refill per symbol), branch-light and cache-friendly with a power-of-2
             table. Dictionary decode adds a one-time window prefill, no steady-state cost. None
             runs on nest's hot path (embeddings never zstd-compressed), so all zstd cost is
             amortized one-time at build/cold-section decode.
license:     Dual-licensed BSD-3-Clause OR GPL-2.0 (permissive BSD path -> safe to vendor/link; the Rust zstd
             crate nest already depends on wraps this).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] google/brotli
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A general-purpose lossless byte compressor combining a modern LZ77 with a 16 MiB sliding window, a
             121-transform static dictionary, per-category Huffman prefix codes selected by
             2nd-order context modeling, and block-type switching, standardized in RFC 7932.
innovation:  The static dictionary + word-transform layer: ~13,504 base words (4-24 bytes) expanded by 121
             reversible word transforms (prefix/suffix add, FermentFirst/FermentAll case-shifts,
             OmitFirstN/OmitLastN cuts) into ~1.6M effective entries, addressed as ordinary LZ77
             back-references whose distance overflows the sliding-window range. A tiny shipped
             dictionary acts as a giant priming corpus so the very first occurrence of a domain
             string compresses to one short reference, killing cold-start redundancy that pure
             window-based LZ cannot touch.
techniques:
  - 121 word transforms over 13,504 base words: Identity, FermentFirst (uppercase first),
    FermentAll (uppercase all), OmitFirst1-9, OmitLast1-9, plus optional literal prefix/suffix
    bytes (RFC 7932 Appendix B); reconstruction is word_id=distance-(maxdist+1),
    index=word_id%NWORDS[len], transform_id=word_id>>NDBITS[len]
  - Static dictionary addressed through the SAME LZ77 <length,distance> mechanism: distance <
    maxdist+1 means window back-reference, distance >= maxdist+1 means dictionary lookup; one
    unified copy path, no separate opcode
  - 2nd-order context modeling: per-literal Huffman tree chosen via CMODE[blocktype] + context-id
    from last two output bytes, indexing a 64-entry-per-blocktype context map CMAPL; distances use
    a 4-entry context map keyed on copy length
  - Block-type switching: independent block-length counters for Literals/InsertCopy/Distances;
    when a counter hits zero a new block type and run-length are read, swapping the active Huffman
    trees mid-stream (adaptive without re-sending tables)
  - Combined insert-and-copy command codes that fuse literal-run length with copy length into one
    symbol, cutting command overhead
  - Variable sliding window from 1 KiB-16 to 16 MiB-16, negotiated per stream
  - RFC 9841 Shared Brotli: custom/context-dependent dictionaries and custom transform lists that
    override Appendix B, enabling domain-trained shared dictionaries
nest steals: Two distinct steals, both in nest-format encoding/sections. (1) Domain shared-dictionary for text
             sections: nest already vendors zstd for non-embedding sections; zstd natively
             supports trained dictionaries (zstd --train / ZDICT). Train one deterministic
             dictionary over chunks_canonical at build time in nest-python, store it as a new
             additive section (reserved id 0x09+, encoding id stays a zstd variant in the 4-255
             reserved range), and have nest-runtime mmap+prime the zstd decoder with it. For
             nest's corpora (PT-BR fake-news, repeated boilerplate, JSON/YAML keys) this is
             exactly brotli's cold-start win and should materially shrink the 'tiny' preset's text
             bytes below the current 0.283 ratio while keeping content_hash stable (dict affects
             only physical encoding, not decoded canonical bytes). (2) The transform idea itself
             adapts to a nest-native lightweight text codec: a small frozen word-list + reversible
             transforms (case/prefix/suffix/cut) is a deterministic, dependency-free way to
             squeeze repetitive structured text (JSON/YAML field names, URL prefixes, dataset
             schemas) without pulling brotli's C core into the small rust core. Either way it
             lives entirely on the non-embedding text path, never touching the float embedding hot
             path that must stay raw-scored from mmap.
compression: Directly on the north-star 'compress more than everyone' axis. The shared-dictionary trick is the
             single highest-leverage gain for nest's text/JSON/YAML sections: it attacks
             inter-chunk and cold-start redundancy that per-chunk zstd misses, and is the standard
             way trained dictionaries beat generic compressors on many small similar records.
             Window/context/Huffman details are reference-grade but lower priority since nest
             delegates byte entropy coding to vendored zstd.
perf:        Brotli decompresses at deflate-class speed (browser-grade) and compresses 17-25% denser than gzip
             on web text; levels 10-11 do near-exhaustive dictionary+transform search and are
             slow, levels 1-9 are fast. The transform table is data-only (no SIMD); dictionary
             lookup is O(1) array indexing. For nest the relevant cost is build-time only
             (dictionary training in python) plus a one-time decoder prime in rust; runtime decode
             stays cheap and the embedding hot path is untouched, so zero impact on SIMD
             dot-product latency.
license:     MIT (brotli reference implementation); RFC 7932/9841 formats are open standards

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] ebiggers/libdeflate
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A heavily optimized MIT-licensed C library for DEFLATE/zlib/gzip that beats stock zlib ~2x on both
             compress and decompress by combining a minimum-cost-path ("near-optimal") parser at
             high levels with a two-level Huffman decode table and a 64-bit branchless bit-buffer
             refill on the decode side.
innovation:  Entropy-cost-driven optimal parsing: model the LZ choice as a shortest-path DAG over byte
             positions where each literal/match edge is weighted by its actual Huffman codeword
             length in fractional bits, then iterate (parse -> rebuild Huffman -> recompute costs
             -> reparse) until the bit cost converges. The encoder optimizes for the real
             post-entropy size, not a greedy length heuristic. The single transferable idea: pick
             encodings by measured bits-out, iterated to a fixpoint, rather than by a static rule.
techniques:
  - Near-optimal parser: min-cost-path over a DAG of literal/match edges, cost = Huffman codeword
    length scaled by BIT_COST=16 for fractional-bit accounting
  - Iterative cost refinement: parse->count freqs->rebuild Huffman->recompute symbol
    costs->reparse, stop when improvement < threshold; unused symbols default to
    LITERAL/LENGTH_NOSTAT_BITS=13, OFFSET_NOSTAT_BITS=10
  - Two-level Huffman decode table: main table indexed by N bits (LITLEN_TABLEBITS=11, OFFSET=8,
    PRECODE=7), long codewords escape to on-demand subtables via a HUFFDEC_SUBTABLE_POINTER entry
  - Packed decode-table entry: one u32 holds literal value / length-base / offset-base plus
    'remaining codeword length + extra bits' in low bits, so decode is one table read + shift,
    branch-free
  - 64-bit branchless bit-buffer refill: bitbuf |= get_unaligned_leword(in) << (bitsleft&7),
    advance pointer/bitcount by arithmetic with no per-symbol branch
  - Block-type selection by measured cost: emit each block as cheapest of dynamic-Huffman /
    static-Huffman / stored, comparing final bit costs incl precode overhead
  - Entropy-divergence block splitting: accumulate 10-category symbol observations every 512
    positions, cut a block when running stats diverge (SOFT_MAX_BLOCK_LENGTH=300000)
  - bt_matchfinder (binary trees, one candidate per length) for the optimal parser vs
    hc_matchfinder (hash chains) for greedy/lazy levels
nest steals: Two concrete steals into crates/nest-format/src/encoding and writer/encoding_choice.rs. (1) Make
             encoding_choice.rs cost-driven instead of preset-driven: for each non-embedding
             section (chunks_canonical text, provenance/search_contract JSON), trial-encode under
             candidate codecs and pick the smallest measured output (a one-pass analog of
             libdeflate block-type-by-cost selection). Fits the additive format: no new section
             ids, the chosen encoding id is just recorded per-section in the manifest. (2) Add a
             new entropy codec under a reserved encoding id 4+ (additive within v1) for the text
             section: a deterministic canonical-Huffman/DEFLATE-style coder whose static decode
             table uses the two-level layout and packed-u32 entries above, giving a tiny
             dependency-free Rust decoder for the runtime mmap path. The iterative measured-bits
             idea also seeds a future token-codebook builder for chunks_canonical (choose codebook
             by measured bits to a fixpoint) where extreme text gains live. Keep embeddings off
             this path: they stay raw/f16/int8 scored from mmap as today.
compression: Directly on the north star (compress more than everyone). The iterate-on-measured-bits parser is
             the principled route to squeezing text/json sections harder than single-shot zstd.
             The two-level decode table + packed entries + branchless refill is a blueprint for a
             small, fast, deterministic in-house entropy decoder running off mmap with no C
             dependency, preserving offline/single-file/no-hot-path-alloc guarantees.
perf:        ~2x zlib on x86_64/ARM at default level 6 both directions. Decode speed from a 64-bit bitbuffer
             refilled rarely via branchless unaligned word loads, single-read packed table
             entries, and a BMI2-compiled decode variant selected at runtime. Compression cost
             scales hard at top levels: experimental level 13 saves only ~0.134% over level 12 on
             Silesia but runs ~56x slower -> 'compress once, serve many', perfect for nest's
             deterministic offline build step, not real-time edits. Runtime CPU feature detection,
             no compile flags. No streaming: tuned for chunks < ~1MB, matching nest's
             per-section/per-chunk granularity.
license:     MIT (maintainer states no relevant patents)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] klauspost/compress
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A pure-Go suite of optimized compression codecs (zstd, S2/Snappy, flate/deflate) built on
             separable entropy primitives - huff0 (Huffman) and fse (Finite State Entropy / tANS)
             - with dictionary training, four match-finder tiers, and assembly-accelerated decode.
innovation:  Entropy coding and dictionary training are factored into reusable primitives independent of the LZ
             layer: fse (table-based asymmetric numeral systems) and huff0 (Huffman) are
             standalone packages, and a trained dictionary ships a precomputed shared entropy
             table plus a common-substring content prefix so a corpus of many tiny similar
             payloads stops re-transmitting its own statistics and re-discovering shared strings
             on every blob - exactly the regime of nest's thousands of short canonical chunks.
techniques:
  - FSE / tANS finite-state entropy coding (table-driven ANS) as a standalone codec for
    near-arithmetic-coding density at table-lookup speed
  - huff0 Huffman entropy coder with 1X and 4X interleaved-stream variants for parallel/SIMD
    decode
  - zstd dictionary training (ZDICT): mine common substrings across many small samples +
    precompute a shared FSE/Huffman entropy table, embed both as a reusable dictionary prefix
  - four-tier match finder (fast/default/better/best ~ levels 1/3/7/11) trading encode time for
    ratio via hash-chain variants
  - S2 seekable streams: a sparse index of frame offsets enabling random access /
    decompress-from-offset without inflating the whole stream
  - RLE detection and predefined/static FSE tables to skip header cost on tiny or low-entropy
    blocks
  - assembly matchLen and a 4-stage decoder pipeline (input / literal / sequence / output) for
    decode throughput
nest steals: Add a trained-dictionary codec to crates/nest-format/src/encoding/ alongside zstd_codec.rs. At
             build time (nest-python build_fn -> writer/encoding_choice.rs) train ONE zstd
             dictionary from the file's own canonical chunks, store it as a new additive section
             (reserved id 0x09+, NOT a v2 bump) plus a new SECTION_ENCODING id in the reserved
             4-255 range, then compress chunks_canonical (and spans/provenance) against it. For
             nest's many short PT-BR chunks this removes the per-section entropy-table and
             shared-substring overhead that zstd level-19-without-dict pays today, advancing the
             'compress more than everyone' north star while staying single-file (dict lives inside
             the container), deterministic (training is seedable + sorted input), and additive.
             Second steal: S2's seekable sparse-offset index is the blueprint for a future
             chunk-granular random-access codec so a single chunk decodes without inflating the
             whole section. Decode stays on the existing zstd crate (it reads dict-prefixed frames
             natively) so no new decode dependency; embeddings remain untouched on the mmap SIMD
             hot path per nest's hard rule.
compression: Directly central. Dictionary training is the single biggest unrealized ratio win for nest's actual
             payload shape (thousands of short, mutually-similar text chunks) where whole-section
             zstd-19 wastes bytes re-encoding shared statistics per blob; fse/huff0 as separable
             primitives are the longer-term path to a custom nest entropy stage.
perf:        zstd decode has x86-64 + ARM64 assembly (~2x); decoder runs a 4-stage concurrent pipeline, encoder
             uses light 2-goroutine concurrency. huff0 4X variant interleaves 4 bitstreams for
             parallel decode; static decode buffer ~30% faster. matchLen and FSE decode are
             hand-written asm on amd64. For nest these are build-side one-time costs; decode and
             dict-prefix reads are cheap and stay off the SIMD scoring hot path since embeddings
             are never entropy-coded.
license:     BSD-3-Clause (with portions Apache-2.0 / Go BSD-style for std-derived code)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] Blosc/c-blosc2
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A meta-compressor that does not invent an entropy coder but front-loads a SIMD-vectorized,
             pipelinable byte-transform stage (shuffle / bitshuffle / delta / bytedelta /
             trunc_prec) ahead of stock LZ codecs (BloscLZ, LZ4, Zstd), restructuring numerical
             byte streams so the downstream coder sees long runs and small residuals, all wrapped
             in a documented blocked chunk/superchunk/frame container.
innovation:  Decouple "byte rearrangement / decorrelation" from "entropy coding" and make the transform stage a
             tiny, composable, SIMD-accelerated filter pipeline applied per fixed-size block. The
             shuffle filter groups the Nth byte of every typesize-N element together (a strided
             transpose), so the high-order bytes of a numerical array (mostly constant) form long
             zero/constant runs and the low-order bytes cluster, turning data Zstd can barely
             touch into data it crushes. bytedelta then deltas within each per-byte stream for
             gradually-varying series. The codec is unchanged; the win comes entirely from
             presenting it better-conditioned bytes. This routinely lifts float32 array ratios
             from ~3.9x to ~5.9x at near-memcpy speed.
techniques:
  - Byte shuffle: strided transpose grouping byte-position k of all elements of typesize T into
    contiguous planes (SoA-of-bytes), exposing constant high-order bytes as runs
  - Bitshuffle: same idea at bit granularity, transposing bit-planes within a block for more
    redundancy on low-entropy floats
  - bytedelta filter: SIMD delta within each shuffled byte-plane; forward uses
    simd_sub+simd_concat over 16-byte lanes, inverse uses a Kogge-Stone parallel prefix-sum scan
    to un-delta
  - trunc_prec: zero the low mantissa bits of f32/f64 before shuffle to manufacture trailing-zero
    runs (lossy, controllable precision)
  - Composable 6-slot block-level filter pipeline (shuffle->bytedelta, trunc_prec->bitshuffle)
    keeping temp buffers L1/L2-resident
  - Blocked chunk format with int32 bstarts offset table + csize sentinels (csize==0 = all-zeros
    block stored as nothing, csize<0 = RLE/special token)
  - Shared Zstd dictionary trained once and stored in the superchunk header, reused across all
    chunks to lift ratio on small blocks
  - Container hierarchy chunk->superchunk->frame (contiguous cframe header+chunks+index+trailer,
    or sparse sframe one-file-per-chunk) with MessagePack metadata + metalayers
nest steals: Add a pre-codec filter stage in nest-format/src/encoding/ (new shuffle.rs + bytedelta.rs, each
             <300 loc, pure deterministic functions, no panic). Wire it as a new SECTION_ENCODING
             id (the reserved 4-255 space is additive-safe within v1) that is shuffle+zstd: before
             zstd_encode, transpose section bytes by a typesize stored in the manifest, optionally
             bytedelta the planes, then zstd; reverse it in decode_payload. Highest-value targets
             are the COLD numerical sections that already accept zstd: chunks_original_spans
             (arrays of u32/u64 offsets whose high bytes are near-constant, so shuffle yields huge
             runs) and the int8 scales array in encoding/int8.rs (n contiguous f32 LE scales, the
             classic shuffle case). CRITICAL constraint: do NOT apply it to the embeddings body
             bytes scored from mmap on the hot path (shuffle destroys the row-contiguous layout
             the SIMD dot-product reads), so gate it strictly to non-hot-path sections via
             writer/encoding_choice.rs. trunc_prec is a separate future lever for a lossy archival
             embeddings variant. The SIMD prefix-sum un-delta maps onto nest-runtime's existing
             AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatcher if decode ever becomes hot.
compression: Directly central. This is the cheapest known multiplier on top of an existing entropy coder for
             numerical/binary sections: nest already ships zstd but applies it to raw bytes;
             inserting a deterministic byte-shuffle (and optional bytedelta) transform in front of
             zstd is a pure ratio win on the offsets/spans and scale arrays with zero new
             dependency and no format break (just a new additive encoding id). Blosc's own ERA5
             numbers (3.86x -> 5.86x) quantify the headroom. It is the textbook technique behind
             'compress data more than everyone' for structured numeric payloads.
perf:        Shuffle/bitshuffle and bytedelta are SIMD-vectorized (SSE2/AVX2 on x86_64, NEON on aarch64, scalar
             fallback) over 16-byte lanes, so transform overhead is near memcpy and
             bandwidth-bound. Block-level application keeps working sets in L1/L2. Tradeoffs
             measured by Blosc: with shuffle+bytedelta, Zstd compression time can roughly double
             at high levels and decompression can slow ~20% for most codecs (Zstd less affected) -
             acceptable since nest's targets are build-time (python writer) and cold sections, not
             the mmap scoring path. The all-zeros/RLE csize sentinels make sparse numeric data
             near-free.
license:     BSD-3-Clause (permissive; safe to cleanly reimplement shuffle/bytedelta in Rust, and the format
             spec is open and documented in under 4000 words)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] Cyan4973/FiniteStateEntropy
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Reference C implementation of Finite State Entropy (tANS, table-based Asymmetric Numeral Systems)
             plus Huff0, the entropy-coder backends that power zstd, providing near-arithmetic
             compression accuracy at hundreds of MB/s via a single state-transition table lookup
             per symbol.
innovation:  tANS replaces per-symbol arithmetic with a precomputed state-transition table making decode a
             single LUT lookup plus a bit read; the accuracy depends entirely on quasi-regular
             symbol spreading across the state table.
techniques:
  - tANS decode table {symbol, nbBits, newStateBaseline} with 4-line decode hot loop
  - Symbol spread stride step=(tableSize>>1)+(tableSize>>3)+3 mod tableSize
  - FSE_normalizeCount to sum=2^tableLog with rtbTable rounding thresholds and minTableLog
    rejection
  - FSE_optimalTableLog auto-selecting smallest cache-friendly tableLog (~14)
  - nbBits/baseline assignment via next-power-of-2 shares, low states cost +1 bit
  - Normalized-count table bit-packed into the compressed header; RLE special case
  - Huff0 canonical Huffman with 4 interleaved bitstreams for OoO ALU throughput
  - FSEU16 16-bit-symbol variant for large alphabets
nest steals: Vendor a pure-Rust FSE+Huff0 entropy codec into crates/nest-format/src/encoding/, registered as
             new additive encoding ids in the reserved 4-255 range, applied only to non-embedding
             sections (text, provenance, search_contract, and especially bm25_index 0x08 postings
             and hnsw_index 0x07 neighbor lists) where skewed integer/token residuals after
             delta-coding beat zstd's generic stage at lower complexity. Table build is a pure
             function of the histogram so output is byte-identical/deterministic, preserving
             content_hash and reproducible builds; FSEU16 suits 16-bit tokenized term streams.
             Keep under 300 lines by sharing the spread/table-build core between encode and
             decode, typed thiserror errors, no panics.
compression: Core to compress-more-than-everyone: tANS hits within a fraction of a percent of Shannon entropy
             on skewed symbol streams (BM25 postings, HNSW adjacency, term dicts) where Huffman
             loses up to ~1 bit/symbol, at hundreds of MB/s, giving nest a sovereign in-house
             entropy floor below generic zstd.
perf:        FSE ~325/440 MB/s, Huff0 ~600/1350 MB/s on i7-5600U with 32KB blocks; tableLog<=14 to fit L1;
             Huff0 uses 4 interleaved streams for ALU parallelism. Pure scalar, branch-light LUT
             decode portable to x86_64/aarch64.
license:     BSD-2-Clause

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] google/snappy
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A speed-first LZ77 byte compressor that emits a stream of tag-byte-prefixed elements (literals +
             back-reference "copies") with no entropy coding, hitting ~250 MB/s compress / ~500
             MB/s decompress at ~1.5-4x ratio, plus a chunked framing format with per-chunk masked
             CRC-32C for streaming.
innovation:  The transferable idea is the tag-byte element stream: every element begins with one byte whose low
             2 bits select element type (00 literal, 01/10/11 copy with 1/2/4-byte offset) and
             whose high 6 bits pack the small-case length/offset inline, so the common case costs
             exactly one control byte with zero entropy-coding state. This makes the format
             branch-cheap, alignment-tolerant (LE unaligned loads), and table-free to decode - the
             opposite of zstd/FSE which need decode state. Paired with it: a base-128 varint
             preamble (7 data bits + continuation bit) for total length, and an independent
             chunked framing layer (4-byte header = 1 type byte + 3-byte LE length) where each
             chunk carries a masked CRC-32C of its UNCOMPRESSED bytes so streams are splittable,
             concatenable, and verifiable per-chunk.
techniques:
  - tag-byte element encoding: low 2 bits = type {literal, copy1B, copy2B, copy4B}, high 6 bits =
    inline length/offset for the common small case
  - three copy classes by offset width: 1-byte offset (0-2047, len 4-11, 3 high offset bits in
    tag), 2-byte offset (0-65535, len 1-64), 4-byte offset (full 32-bit), trading control-byte
    size against reach
  - literal length escape: high-6-bits 0-59 = (len-1) inline; 60-63 = read 1-4 following LE bytes
    as the length, keeping long literals cheap
  - LE base-128 varint preamble for total uncompressed length (7 payload bits + MSB continuation)
  - single-probe hash table of 4-byte fragments for match finding (no lazy match, no entropy
    coder) - source of the speed/ratio tradeoff
  - framing: back-to-back chunks, 4-byte header (1 type byte + 3-byte LE length), ids partitioned
    into reserved-skippable (0x80-0xfd) vs reserved-unskippable (0x02-0x7f) for forward-compatible
    extension
  - masked CRC-32C per chunk over UNCOMPRESSED data, mask = ((x>>15)|(x<<17)) + 0xa282ead8 to
    avoid CRC-of-CRC weakness
  - format assumes cheap unaligned LE 32/64-bit loads, enabling overlapping copies and
    word-at-a-time literal moves
nest steals: Two adaptations, both in nest-format. (1) Adopt Snappy's reserved-id partitioning for nest's
             section/encoding id space: Snappy splits skippable vs unskippable extension ranges so
             old decoders behave predictably on unknown chunks. nest v1 is additive-only with
             section ids 0x09+ and encoding ids 4-255 RESERVED; formalize a "skippable optional vs
             unskippable required" bit on section entries in crates/nest-format/src/layout and
             reader so a future v1 file with an unknown OPTIONAL section (e.g. a new graph index)
             still validates and serves on an older runtime, while unknown REQUIRED sections error
             cleanly via NestError - extending the additive guarantee without a v2 bump. (2) Use
             Snappy's tag-byte stream as the blueprint for a pure-Rust, panic-free, deterministic
             LZ77 fallback codec in crates/nest-format/src/encoding (new module behind a reserved
             SECTION_ENCODING id, never applied to embeddings) for when nest must drop the zstd C
             dependency to stay fully sovereign/offline in one binary. Import only the stream
             layout (varint preamble + 1/2/4-byte copy classes), not Snappy's CRC, since nest's
             four SHA-256 surfaces already cover integrity. The same varint+tag scheme is directly
             reusable to shrink small metadata sections (chunk_ids, chunks_original_spans) where
             deltas are tiny and zstd's container overhead dominates.
compression: Indirect, not on the hot ratio path. nest already uses zstd level 19, which compresses TEXT
             meaningfully better than Snappy (Snappy explicitly trades 20-100% larger output for
             ~10x speed and applies no entropy coding), so Snappy's codec must NOT replace zstd
             for the headline "compress more than everyone" goal. Its relevance is structural: (a)
             a sovereign zero-C-dependency fallback codec when nest vendors everything into one
             binary; (b) the tag-byte/varint scheme as low-overhead encoding for small metadata
             sections where zstd container overhead dominates; (c) the skippable/unskippable
             extension discipline that protects the additive-format guarantee as the format grows
             toward repos/audio/video.
perf:        ~250 MB/s compress, ~500 MB/s decompress single-core 64-bit x86; ratios ~1.5-1.7x plain text, 2-4x
             HTML, ~1.0x already-compressed (jpeg/png). Speed comes from word-at-a-time unaligned
             LE loads, overlapping copy loops, and single-probe hash-table match finding (no lazy
             matching, no entropy coder). Format assumes cheap unaligned 32/64-bit LE loads -
             already true on nest's x86_64+aarch64 targets and aligned with nest's repr(C)
             little-endian-unsigned convention. Decompression is allocation-light and streamable,
             matching nest's mmap model.
license:     BSD-3-Clause (permissive; safe to vendor or reimplement the documented, unpatented format)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] lz4/lz4
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A byte-aligned LZ77 compressor with no entropy coder, whose block format is a stream of [token |
             literal-length-extension | literals | 2-byte LE offset | match-length-extension]
             sequences decodable at multiple GB/s per core via branch-light wildcopy.
innovation:  A deliberately entropy-coder-free, byte-aligned block format whose decoder is so simple (no
             bit-unpacking, no Huffman/FSE tables, just memcpy-style literal and match copies)
             that decompression runs at RAM-bandwidth speed -- trading a few points of ratio for
             the ability to decode in a latency-critical hot path.
techniques:
  - token byte split into two nibbles: high 4 bits = literal length, low 4 bits = match length
    (both 0-15)
  - LSIC (Linear Small-Integer Code) varint extension: when a nibble is 15, read add-255
    continuation bytes until a non-255 byte, summing them onto the length
  - minmatch=4: match-length nibble of 0 already means a 4-byte copy, so the encoded value is
    matchlen-4, saving the unrepresentable short matches
  - 2-byte little-endian back-reference offset, max 65535 (64KB sliding window); offset 0 is
    reserved as a corruption sentinel
  - hash table over 4-byte sequences mapping prefix->latest position for O(1) greedy match
    finding; LZ4_HC adds chain tables / lazy+optimal parsing for higher ratio at lower speed
  - wildcopy: copy literals and matches in fixed 8-byte machine-word chunks past the exact length
    (requires output over-allocation slack) to eliminate per-byte branching
  - frozen end-of-block invariants (last 5 bytes always literals, last match >=12 bytes before
    end) that let speed-oriented decoders skip bounds checks
  - frame layer (magic 0x184D2204, FLG/BD descriptor) with selectable 64KB/256KB/1MB/4MB block
    sizes, optional block-linking within the 64KB window, and xxHash-32 block/content checksums
nest steals: Add an lz4 block codec to nest-format/src/encoding/ as a new wire-encoding id (reserve one of the
             free 4-255 slots, additive within v1, registered in layout alongside
             SECTION_ENCODING_ZSTD and wired into decode_payload in encoding/mod.rs). Its purpose
             is NOT to beat zstd on cold sections (zstd already wins on ratio) but to be the first
             compressor nest can legally apply to hot-path-decoded surfaces that zstd is currently
             banned from. Two concrete targets: (1) chunks_canonical/text when nest-runtime
             materialize() reads it per query -- GB/s lz4 decode makes 'always-compressed text'
             viable without per-citation latency; (2) a future lz4-framed transparent layer over
             int8 embeddings where the runtime decodes a 64KB block into a small scratch buffer
             just-in-time before the SIMD dot product, since the branch-light wildcopy is cheap
             enough to amortize against the AVX2/NEON kernel cost -- the only realistic path to
             compressing embeddings (today raw/f16/int8 only) without breaking 'scored directly
             from mmap.' Also steal the LSIC add-255 varint and the frozen end-of-block invariants
             pattern for nest's own section-length and manifest varints (byte-granular, zero
             bit-shift, deterministic). Respect constraints: each codec file <=300 lines, typed
             thiserror errors, no panics; the format's repr-stable, little-endian, byte-aligned
             nature matches nest's repr(C) + LE rule exactly.
compression: Indirect but strategic. lz4 LOSES to zstd on raw ratio, so it does not advance the 'compress more
             than everyone' headline on cold sections. Its relevance is unlocking compression on
             surfaces nest currently leaves uncompressed because the decode budget is too tight:
             embeddings (hot SIMD path) and per-citation text materialization. A GB/s-class,
             mmap-friendly, allocation-free codec is the prerequisite for ever compressing those,
             widening the set of compressible bytes even if each byte compresses less than under
             zstd.
perf:        Default ~780 MB/s compress at ~2.1x ratio; LZ4_HC ~41 MB/s compress at ~2.72x ratio; decompression
             multiple GB/s per core (often 4-5 GB/s), frequently RAM-bandwidth-bound. Decode has
             no entropy stage, is allocation-free and branch-light; wildcopy needs output-buffer
             over-allocation slack (copies in 8-byte chunks). xxHash-32 in the frame layer is a
             fast non-cryptographic checksum (not a substitute for nest's four SHA-256 surfaces,
             but a useful cheap per-block guard if nest ever streams sub-section blocks).
license:     BSD 2-Clause for the library (lib/), GPLv2 for the programs/ CLI. The C library nest would bind or
             reimplement is BSD-2, permissive and Rust-reimplementation-friendly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] madler/zlib
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        The canonical C reference implementation of DEFLATE (RFC 1951) and its zlib/gzip wrappers: LZ77
             sliding-window matching via hash chains plus per-block dynamic Huffman entropy
             coding, with a table-driven multi-level inflate decoder.
innovation:  The two-stage table-driven Huffman decoder optimizing the common (short-code) case.
techniques:
  - LZ77 sliding window (32KB) with backward (length,distance) references
  - hash-chain match finder: head[HASH_SIZE] + prev[w_size] linked chains, capped by
    max_chain_length
  - lazy matching (Wams): defer a literal to check if a longer match starts at the next position
    (levels >=4)
  - tunable heuristics good_match/nice_match/max_lazy_match/max_insert_length to trade ratio vs
    speed per level
  - canonical Huffman: per-block dynamic trees (dyn_ltree, dyn_dtree) built from symbol
    frequencies via a heap, code lengths transmitted compactly via bl_tree
  - block-type selection: stored vs fixed-Huffman vs dynamic-Huffman, picking the cheapest per
    block
  - multi-level inflate decode tables: root table covers root_bits, sub-tables handle overflow
    codes (1-15 bits)
  - deflateSetDictionary: prime the window with a known dictionary so the first bytes can
    back-reference it (great for many small similar payloads)
nest steals: Two concrete steals. (1) The preset-dictionary idea (deflateSetDictionary) maps directly onto
             nest-format's encoding layer (crates/nest-format/src/encoding + sections): nest
             already zstd-compresses non-embedding sections, and chunks_canonical/provenance
             across a corpus share huge vocabulary overlap. Train one shared zstd dictionary (zstd
             has native zstd_compress_usingDict / trained dictionaries) over the corpus, store it
             once as a small additive sub-blob keyed under the existing zstd encoding id, and
             compress every text/json chunk against it. This is the single biggest win for nest's
             "compress more than everyone" north star on many-small-chunk corpora, and stays
             additive (no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump, deterministic if the dictionary is built
             deterministically). (2) The two-stage canonical-Huffman decode table is the reference
             design for any future bespoke entropy codec nest adds for a section type (e.g. a
             custom int8/residual codec): implement it in nest-runtime as a small repr-aligned
             decode table read straight from the mmap, keeping the no-panic, little-endian,
             <=300-loc-per-file constraints. The LZ77 hash-chain + lazy-match design is
             reference-level only (zstd already supersedes it for nest's actual codec).
compression: High for text/json sections: shared preset dictionaries are the proven lever for compressing many
             small, vocabulary-overlapping payloads (exactly nest's chunks_canonical +
             provenance), directly serving the "compress data more than everyone" goal. Irrelevant
             to embeddings, which nest deliberately never entropy-codes because they are
             SIMD-scored off mmap on the hot path. The table-driven decode is relevant only if
             nest builds its own entropy codec rather than leaning on zstd.
perf:        Decode hot loop is a bit-buffer + single table lookup for common codes, one extra indirection for
             rare long codes; root_bits sized "one bit above average code length" to minimize
             duplicate-entry fill cost. Encode speed is dominated by hash-chain walk length
             (max_chain_length) and lazy-match second searches; levels 1-3 skip lazy matching and
             limit hash insertions for speed. zlib-ng is the modern SIMD/perf fork (SIMD hash,
             faster CRC, slide) if kernel-level speed matters. CRC32 has a well-known
             slice-by-8/PCLMULQDQ vectorization but base zlib is largely scalar table-driven.
license:     zlib license (permissive, BSD-like, commercial-friendly, no attribution-in-binary requirement) --
             vendor-safe for nest.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] zlib-ng/zlib-ng
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A modernized, SIMD-accelerated fork of zlib implementing DEFLATE (RFC 1951), zlib (RFC 1950) and
             gzip (RFC 1952) with runtime CPU dispatch over per-hot-path optimized kernels (CRC32,
             adler32, longest_match, slide_hash, compare256, inflate_fast).
innovation:  A central runtime "functable": a struct of function pointers (one per hot operation) resolved
             exactly once at startup from CPU feature detection, so every hot-loop call is a
             single indirect jump to the best kernel
             (scalar/SSE2/AVX2/AVX512/VPCLMULQDQ/NEON/PMULL+EOR3/RVV) with zero per-call CPUID
             branching. This cleanly decouples algorithm from microarchitecture across ~10 ISAs
             behind one stable dispatch surface.
techniques:
  - functable: one-time-resolved struct of fn pointers per hot op, indirect call per hot loop, no
    per-call feature check
  - CRC32 via carryless multiply folding (PCLMULQDQ/VPCLMULQDQ on x86, ARMv8.2 PMULL+EOR3) instead
    of byte-at-a-time table
  - CRC32-C hardware intrinsic reused AS the hash function for the hash-chain match finder
  - Intel-derived deflate 'quick' and 'medium' match-finding strategies as fast tiers distinct
    from greedy/lazy slow tiers
  - longest_match / compare256 SIMD match extension (Cloudflare + fast_zlib lineage) comparing 256
    bytes per SIMD step
  - slide_hash SIMD to bulk-decrement hash-chain heads on window slide
  - inflate_fast chunked copy (chunkmemset): overlapping vector loads/stores for LZ
    back-references instead of byte loops
  - ZLIB_COMPAT vs zlib-ng-native dual ABI: one engine, two header surfaces
nest steals: Two concrete steals plus a reference boundary. (1) HASHING fast-path: nest's four SHA-256 surfaces
             in nest-format/src/hashing are the integrity contract and must stay cryptographic,
             but non-crypto work (dedup keys, section identity probes, future bm25 term hashing in
             nest-runtime) should use CRC32-C via carryless-multiply / ARMv8 CRC behind nest's
             existing OnceLock dispatcher in nest-runtime/src/simd/mod.rs, reusing the exact
             'resolve once, indirect-call forever' pattern nest already uses for dot products. (2)
             DISPATCH PATTERN: generalize nest's SimdBackend OnceLock into a small functable-style
             struct of fn pointers so new kernels (f16 convert, i8 widen, a future bitpacked
             decoder) register one entry each instead of growing match arms inside every dot_* fn,
             keeping each simd/*.rs file under the 300-line cap as kernels multiply. (3) REFERENCE
             only for codecs: nest must NOT add a DEFLATE codec -- zstd-19 already beats DEFLATE
             on nest's text sections; the value is the engineering scaffolding, not the entropy
             coder.
compression: Low-to-moderate for ratio: DEFLATE's Huffman+LZ77 is strictly weaker than the zstd
             FSE/Huffman+large-window nest already uses, so adopting zlib-ng's codec yields NO
             ratio win. Transferable compression-adjacent value: the SIMD inflate chunking pattern
             (overlapping vector copies for back-references) is the right template if nest ever
             ships a lightweight bitpacked/varint section decoder, and CRC-as-hash speeds
             dedup/identity work that precedes compression. Serves the 'compress more than
             everyone' north star only indirectly.
perf:        Kernels resolved once at startup; hot loops pay one indirect call. VPCLMULQDQ+AVX512 CRC32 folds
             ~64 bytes/iter; ARMv8.2 PMULL+EOR3 is the aarch64 analog (directly relevant to nest's
             NEON target). inflate_fast replaces byte-wise LZ expansion with overlapping
             16/32/64-byte vector moves; compare256 extends matches a full vector register per
             step. Tight inner loops, minimal extra memory.
license:     zlib license (permissive, BSD-like, no binary-attribution requirement). Vendor-friendly for nest's
             single-file ethos, but nest should reimplement the patterns in Rust rather than
             vendor C.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] mcmilk/zstdmt
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C library (BSD/GPL, ~96% C, posix-threads) that adds multithreaded streaming
             compression/decompression on top of seven codecs (Zstandard, LZ4, LZ5, Lizard,
             Brotli, Snappy-c, LZFSE) by chunking input and wrapping each independently-compressed
             chunk in a self-describing skippable-frame header.
innovation:  A codec-agnostic, backward-compatible "skippable frame" chunk container: each parallel-compressed
             chunk is prefixed with a 12-byte header built on zstd's reserved skippable-frame
             magic 0x184D2A50 (a magic-range whose high nibble flags "skip me" to any compliant
             decompressor), so a multithreaded/chunked stream is still decodable by a stock
             single-threaded decompressor that simply skips the metadata frames, while a parallel
             decompressor uses the embedded compressed-size and uncompressed-size hint as
             random-access seek points and single-pass allocation sizes.
techniques:
  - Skippable-frame chunk header: 4-byte magic 0x184D2A50 + 4-byte framesize(4) + 4-byte
    compressed-size = 12 bytes per chunk for zstd/lz4/lz5/lizard
  - Extended 16-byte header for codecs lacking native framing (brotli 'BR', snappy 'SP', lzfse
    'LF'): adds 2-byte codec magic + 2-byte uncompressed-size hint as a 64KiB multiplier for
    one-shot output allocation
  - Decentralized self-scheduling worker pool: no dispatcher thread; each worker loops {acquire
    read-mutex -> read one input chunk -> release -> compress independently -> acquire write-mutex
    -> emit framed chunk} until EOF
  - Two-mutex (read/write) coordination instead of a producer-consumer queue, preserving output
    order by chunk while keeping per-thread compression fully parallel
  - User-tunable input chunk size (-b N MiB, auto default) trading parallelism granularity vs
    ratio, and thread count (-T N, defaults to core count)
  - Three interchangeable backends (single-stream st, pthread create/join mt, thread-pool tp) over
    one shared frame format
  - Uniform multi-codec abstraction: one streaming API and one container format reused across 7
    entropy/LZ backends
nest steals: nest-format/src/encoding can adopt the skippable-frame chunking convention for its zstd-compressed
             non-embedding sections (chunks_canonical text, provenance, bm25_index, future
             json/pdf/parquet payloads): instead of one monolithic zstd frame per section, store a
             section as a sequence of independently-compressed chunks each prefixed with a 12-byte
             self-describing header. This is purely additive (a new internal layout inside an
             existing encoding id, no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump, no new section id), and unlocks
             (1) parallel decompression of large text sections at materialize time in nest-runtime
             via the simd/rayon-style dispatcher, (2) random-access decode of a single chunk for
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id citation reopen without inflating the whole section, and
             (3) the 64KiB uncompressed-size hint enables exact output buffer pre-allocation so
             materialize never reallocates. Determinism is preserved by fixing chunk size and
             codec params; per-section physical-bytes SHA-256 stays stable because the framed byte
             layout is deterministic. Embeddings stay raw/f16/int8 as today (never touched, still
             scored from mmap). The two-mutex worker pattern itself is the wrong concurrency model
             for Rust (use rayon/crossbeam), but the on-disk frame format is the steal.
compression: Indirect to raw ratio (multithreading and chunking slightly HURT ratio versus one big frame
             because each chunk resets the codec window/dictionary), but high relevance to nest's
             broader 'compress AND serve' north star: it is the canonical pattern for making
             compressed sections seekable and parallel-decodable without giving up
             single-file/offline/deterministic guarantees. To recover the ratio loss when nest
             grows to large corpora, pair it with a shared trained zstd dictionary (zstd ZDICT)
             referenced by all chunks, so small-chunk random access stops costing ratio. The
             uncompressed-size-hint trick is directly reusable for any nest codec lacking
             self-framing.
perf:        posix-threads only (Windows via mingw CreateThread/WaitForMultipleObjects in later builds);
             self-scheduling worker pool was benchmarked faster than upstream zstd's job-based
             --nbWorkers and competitive with pzstd. Throughput scales near-linearly with cores up
             to memory bandwidth; chunk size -b trades parallelism vs ratio. Decompression
             historically single-threaded in zstd; the skippable-frame headers are exactly what
             make per-chunk parallel decode and seek possible. Per-chunk overhead is only 12-16
             bytes, negligible for >=128KiB chunks.
license:     BSD-3-Clause / GPL-2.0 dual (follows upstream facebook/zstd licensing convention; library code
             under permissive BSD, suitable for vendoring the format idea, though nest would
             reimplement in Rust rather than link the C)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[REFERENCE] ZongqianLi/500xCompressor
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An autoencoder-style neural prompt compressor that uses a frozen LLaMA-3-8B (with ~0.3% trainable
             LoRA params) as the encoder to squeeze up to ~500 natural-language tokens into the
             per-layer KV-cache values of a single learned special token, which the same frozen
             LLM then decodes for text regeneration or QA.
innovation:  Carry compressed information as the full per-layer key/value (KV) tensors of a handful of learned
             "gist" tokens rather than as a single final-layer embedding vector. KV-at-every-layer
             has far higher information capacity than one embedding, which is what unlocks the
             6x-480x ratios; the decoder is the identical frozen LLM, so the compressor adds no
             decode-side parameters and "stores" nothing in weights (no data leakage).
techniques:
  - Autoencoder framing: encoder = frozen LLM + LoRA, decoder = same frozen LLM, only LoRA (~0.3%)
    trained
  - Soft/gist tokens: append N learnable special tokens; their attention-aggregated state becomes
    the compressed payload
  - KV-value carrier instead of last-layer embedding (the ICAE improvement) -- store K and V per
    transformer layer for each gist token
  - Two-phase training: (1) pretrain on text regeneration with teacher forcing + cross-entropy,
    (2) fine-tune on extractive QA
  - Non-selective compression: compresses ALL prompt tokens into the latent, vs hard methods
    (LLMLingua) that drop tokens
  - Lossy reconstruction measured by F1/EM, retaining ~62-84% of uncompressed-prompt task ability
nest steals: Mostly a REFERENCE, not a steal. This is lossy neural compression needing an 8B LLM forward pass
             at both compress AND decompress time -- it cannot live on nest's mmap+SIMD hot path,
             breaks determinism (float GPU kernels), breaks offline/single-file/small-rust-core
             constraints, and cannot losslessly reconstruct bytes, so it is unusable for
             chunks_canonical, provenance, or any required section. The ONLY transferable seed:
             the KV-as-carrier insight reinforces nest's existing embedding-quantization direction
             in nest-format encoding (the int8/float16 codecs) -- it argues a richer per-layer
             latent preserves more than a flattened vector, useful as academic background for any
             future learned/product-quantization codec but nothing nest can ship now. nest should
             cite 500xCompressor as the canonical reason neural/semantic text compression costs an
             LLM at read time and therefore stays OUT of the frozen v1 format and off the offline
             runtime. No code, section, or codec adopted.
compression: Low for nest's mission. Headline "500x" is lossy latent compression of prompts for LLM
             consumption, not lossless byte compression of stored data, and the decompressor is an
             8B GPU model -- the opposite of nest's offline/single-file/deterministic/mmap-SIMD
             design. It compresses for an LLM to read, not for a byte-exact citation to reopen, so
             it cannot back content_hash stability or nest://content_hash/chunk_id span reopen.
             Useful only as the reference point for why neural/semantic text compression is
             incompatible with nest's frozen container and offline runtime.
perf:        Reported at 500x: ~27-90% reduction in attention FLOPs and ~55-83% KV-memory savings when
             generating 100-400 tokens, retaining ~70-74% F1 / ~77-84% EM vs uncompressed prompts.
             Base model LLaMA-3-8B-Instruct; LoRA adds ~0.3% params. All numbers assume GPU LLM
             inference at both ends -- irrelevant to nest's CPU mmap/SIMD throughput model and not
             a fixed-cost codec.
license:     Code repo public on GitHub (paper ACL 2025, arXiv 2408.03094); datasets/weights on HuggingFace
             marked not open to the public. No explicit OSI license confirmed in README -- treat
             as all-rights-reserved/research-use until a LICENSE file is verified before any code
             reuse.
