# category: compression-integer  (6 repos)
# focus: integer and posting-list compression (bitpacking, varint, elias-fano)
##################################################################################################

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] powturbo/TurboPFor-Integer-Compression
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C library of the world's fastest integer-array codecs, anchored by TurboPFor (a SIMD/AVX2
             patched-frame-of-reference scheme) that bitpacks fixed 128/256-int blocks with
             per-block exception lists and integrated FOR/delta/zigzag, plus directly-addressable
             random access into compressed data without full block decode.
innovation:  Patched bitpacking on small fixed blocks: pick a base bit-width b sized so MOST values fit, store
             the few outliers as cheap exceptions (either an exception bitmap + secondary
             bit-width, or variable-byte), and lay the packed bits out vertically across SIMD
             lanes so a whole block decodes branch-free. A 2-bit per-block header selects
             {constant, plain bitpack, bitpack+bitmap-exceptions, bitpack+vbyte-exceptions}, and
             dedicated DA functions allow O(1) random access to a single value without
             decompressing the block.
techniques:
  - TurboPFor patched-FOR: per-block base bit-width b + exception list for outliers, 2-bit
    block-type header (constant/bitpack/bitmap-exc/vbyte-exc)
  - Exception encoding two ways: popcount-addressed exception BITMAP + secondary bit-width for
    exception values, OR variable-byte for non-uniform exceptions
  - SIMD vertical/interleaved bitpacking (TurboPackV128 SSE, TurboPackV256 AVX2): 8x uint32 LE
    values packed across lanes, branch-free unpack >20 Gint/s (80 Gb/s)
  - Integrated FOR (subtract min), delta (d/d1 for sorted-with-dup / strictly-increasing) and
    zigzag (z) transforms folded into the codec, plus delta-of-delta + RLE for timestamps
  - Direct/random access (TurboPForDA / TurboForDA): decode a single packed entry by bit-offset
    arithmetic with zero block decompression
  - Byte/nibble TRANSPOSE+XOR+zigzag (TPbyte, 2D/3D/4D) SIMD pre-transform that columnarizes
    mantissa bytes before packing (~9.4 GB/s)
  - Gorilla-style TurboFloat / TurboFloat-LzXor float codecs: delta-of-delta + RLE on f32/f64,
    FCM/DFCM context-model prediction
  - Space-efficient varbyte byte-range classifier: 0-176 in 1 byte, 177-240 -> 14 bit, 241-248 ->
    19 bit, 249-255 -> 32 bit (denser than LEB128)
nest steals: Build a new additive encoding id (reserved range 4-255) in nest-format
             crates/nest-format/src/encoding/* that adds a TurboPFor-style patched-bitpacking
             codec for INTEGER sections, not embeddings. First target: chunk_ids and
             chunks_original_spans (offset/length arrays) and future bm25 posting lists in
             nest-runtime/src/bm25 -- monotone-ish u32/u64 arrays where delta(d1)+FOR+bitpack with
             exceptions crushes size below zstd. Highest-value target is hnsw_index(0x07)
             neighbor-id adjacency lists: per-node sorted neighbor ids delta-encode and
             patch-bitpack beautifully, shrinking the index section that dominates the tiny
             preset. The DA random-read property lets nest-runtime read one neighbor id or one
             span straight off mmap without decoding the whole block, preserving the zero-copy hot
             path. Reimplement clean-room in pure safe Rust (GPL-2.0 forbids vendoring) with
             portable u32 lane packing; gate SIMD unpack behind the existing simd dispatcher
             (AVX2/NEON/scalar). Determinism is trivial: fixed block size 256, canonical b =
             max-bits-after-FOR, exceptions in ascending index order -> byte-identical. Keep
             little-endian repr per nest invariants. Each piece (block header, bitpack/unpack,
             exception list, delta transform) fits under the 300-line cap as single-responsibility
             modules.
compression: Directly on the north star: patched-bitpacking + delta-FOR is state-of-the-art for the exact data
             nest stores as integers (ids, spans, hnsw adjacency, bm25 postings), routinely 15-25%
             size at >10 Gint/s decode -- smaller than zstd on these arrays AND faster, while
             direct-access compresses without sacrificing nest's mmap-and-score-off-disk hot path.
             Single biggest lever to shrink the index/metadata sections (everything except f32
             embeddings) below the current tiny preset.
perf:        Benchmarks (ZIPF/gov2): TurboPFor256 ~2.4 GB/s enc, ~11 GB/s dec at 15.7% ratio; SIMD bitpack
             alone >20 Gint/s (80 Gb/s); TurboByte+TurboPack hybrid ~17 GB/s enc / 12 GB/s dec at
             25%; EliasFanoV ~3.2/3.6 GB/s at 15.8%. Fixed 128 (SSE) / 256 (AVX2) int blocks are
             deliberate: whole-block decode fits L1/L2, vertical lane layout is branch-free.
             Per-block overhead is tiny (2-bit header + width byte + exception list). Vertical
             SIMD layout needs care to port to NEON (128-bit -> 4x u32) vs AVX2 (256-bit -> 8x
             u32); scalar fallback is a straightforward bit-cursor unpack.
license:     GPL-2.0 (dual-licensed; commercial license from author). GPL is incompatible with nest vendoring
             -- the algorithm must be CLEAN-ROOM reimplemented in Rust from the format
             description, never copied.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] lemire/FastPFor
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C++11 library of vectorized integer-array codecs (SIMD-BP128, FastPFor/SIMD-FastPFor,
             StreamVByte, VByte, Simple9/16, varint-G8IU) that decode 4+ billion 32-bit ints/sec
             (~15 GB/s) by combining frame-based binary bit-packing with patched
             frame-of-reference exception handling over fixed blocks of 128 integers.
innovation:  Patched binary packing: split an integer array into fixed 128-int blocks, pick a single bit-width
             b per block so most values fit in b bits, vertically bit-pack all 128 lanes at width
             b (compression ratio 32/b, decoded branchlessly with SIMD shift+mask), and store the
             rare values exceeding b bits ("exceptions") out-of-band in a separate compressed
             exception stream keyed by position + high-bits. Combined with delta coding for sorted
             lists, this turns monotone ID arrays into a few bits per element while keeping decode
             fully vectorizable and branch-free.
techniques:
  - SIMD-BP128: vertical bit-packing of 128 ints at a uniform bit-width b, decoded with SSE2
    shift/mask (32/b ratio, no branches in hot loop)
  - FastPFor / SIMD-FastPFor: patched frame-of-reference -- per-block bit-width minimizing total
    bits, exceptions (values >= 2^b) stored separately as position bytes + packed high-bit
    residuals
  - Delta / differential coding with a stored offset so sorted ID lists become small gaps before
    packing; SIMD 4-lane delta for vectorized prefix-sum on decode
  - StreamVByte: byte-oriented varint with 2-bit length control codes in a separate stream so
    payload bytes are gathered via SSSE3 pshufb
  - Simple9 / Simple16: pack as many ints as fit into a 32-bit word with a 4-bit selector
    describing the layout
  - Self-delimiting block framing: 'length u32 then that many 32-bit ints, repeat'
  - Hand-tuned unrolled per-bit-width (1..32) bitpack kernels, also shipped standalone as the
    simdcomp C library
nest steals: Two concrete targets, both storing raw u32 arrays today with zero compression. (1)
             nest-runtime/src/ann/codec.rs HnswIndex::to_bytes writes every neighbor id as a
             4-byte LE u32 ('for &id in nbrs { out.extend_from_slice(&id.to_le_bytes()) }');
             per-layer neighbor lists are small bounded id sets -- sort each list then
             delta+bitpack (SIMD-BP128 style) so each id costs ~b bits not 32, shrinking section
             0x07. (2) nest-runtime/src/bm25/codec.rs Bm25Index::to_bytes writes each posting as
             doc:u32 + tf:u32; doc ids per term are strictly increasing, the canonical
             FastPFor/delta case -- delta-code doc gaps then FastPFor-pack, bitpack tf separately,
             shrinking section 0x08. Implement as a pure-Rust scalar port of SIMD-BP128+FastPFor
             (new nest-runtime module, single-responsibility, <=300 loc; no SIMD needed for
             correctness, decode stays deterministic). Both are additive: index payloads are
             versioned (HNSW_PAYLOAD_VERSION / BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION), so bump the per-section
             payload version, NOT NEST_FORMAT_VERSION, keeping v1 frozen. Also seeds a future
             general 'bitpacked/varint' section encoding id (4-255 reserved) for other
             integer-heavy sections like chunks_original_spans offsets.
compression: Directly on-mission for 'compress more than everyone': the tiny/hybrid presets ship hnsw (0x07)
             and bm25 (0x08), both storing integer id arrays at a fixed 32 bits each -- the
             largest uncompressed integer surface in a .nest file. zstd is deliberately NOT on the
             embeddings hot path, but these index sections are cold-decoded once at load, so
             FastPFor-style delta+bitpack can cut them several-fold (sorted doc-id gaps routinely
             pack to 4-8 bits) with no recall loss, pushing the tiny-preset size ratio below
             today's 0.283 while preserving deterministic/offline/single-file guarantees.
perf:        Reference C++ decodes >4e9 ints/s (~15 GB/s) on x64 via SSE2/SSSE3; SIMD-BP128 ~2x faster than
             varint-G8IU/PFOR while saving up to 2 bits/int; SIMD-FastPFor within ~10% of
             Simple-8b compression at ~2x decode speed. For nest, a scalar Rust port loses SIMD
             throughput but index sections are tiny and cold-loaded once at mmap time, so even
             scalar decode is negligible; memory cost is one 128-int block buffer. NEON/AVX2
             kernels could later mirror nest-runtime's existing simd dispatcher pattern.
license:     Apache-2.0 (patent-free except varint-G8IU, which is patented and must be avoided)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] cruppstahl/libfor
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A tiny Apache-2.0 ANSI-C scalar codec for Frame-of-Reference (FOR) integer compression that
             bit-packs 32-bit integer residuals against a per-block minimum and supports random
             access, search, and append operating directly on the compressed bytes.
innovation:  Frame-of-Reference: store one block minimum (base) plus a single bit-width b =
             ceil(log2(max-min+1)), then bit-pack every value as a fixed-width b-bit residual
             (value - base). This makes the compressed array a flat, addressable bit-matrix so you
             can random-access element i in O(1) (bit offset = i*b), binary-search a sorted block,
             and append in place WITHOUT decoding the whole stream -- the codec computes over
             compressed bytes, not just stores them.
techniques:
  - 5-byte block metadata header: 4-byte LE uint32 base/min + 1-byte bit-width b (#define METADATA
    5)
  - Residual = value - block_min, bit-packed at b = ceil(log2(max-min+1)) bits (frame of reference
    removes the high-order common prefix)
  - Cascaded byte-aligned bitpacking via dispatch tables for_pack32/16/8/x[bits] with fully
    unrolled generated kernels in for-gen.c (32-, 16-, 8-, remainder groups)
  - for_select_bits(): O(1) random access by index*bits offset, mask across word boundary, add
    base -- no full decode
  - for_lower_bound_search_bits(): binary search executed directly on the compressed sorted block
  - for_append_*(): in-place append, only re-encodes (widen b) when new value exceeds current bit
    width
  - sorted vs unsorted variants: sorted extracts min/max from endpoints instead of full scan
  - for_compressed_size_bits/sorted/unsorted: precompute exact output size before allocating
nest steals: Adopt FOR as a new additive encoding id (reserve one in 4-255) in nest-format/src/encoding.rs,
             targeting the integer-heavy required sections: chunk_ids and especially
             chunks_original_spans (byte-offset/length pairs that are monotonic and small-range ->
             ideal for delta+FOR). The select()/lower_bound primitives map directly onto
             nest-runtime materialize and citation resolution (nest://content_hash/chunk_id): a
             FOR-packed spans section lets the reader fetch span[i] with one O(1) bit-extract
             straight off mmap, no decode of the whole section, preserving the zero-copy hot path.
             For bm25_index(0x08) posting lists (doc ids in deterministic alphabetical-term
             order), delta-code doc ids then FOR-pack each term's postings -- shrinks the index
             while keeping deterministic byte layout. Because FOR residual width is data-derived
             and the base is stored, builds stay byte-identical (deterministic). Implement as a
             small repr(C)-friendly Rust module (<300 loc, pure scalar, no panic / thiserror on
             malformed bits) since the C is trivially portable; gives a cheaper alternative to
             zstd for integer sections where random access matters and zstd block decode would
             break zero-copy.
compression: High for the specific axis of integer-section compression with preserved random access. FOR is the
             canonical first stage for compressing chunk_ids, chunks_original_spans, and bm25
             posting lists. Unlike zstd (which nest already uses but must decode a whole block,
             breaking mmap zero-copy), FOR keeps elements individually addressable, so it
             compresses AND serves directly from disk -- exactly nest's north star. It is a
             building block, not a finished codec: pairing delta-coding + FOR (and later
             PFOR-style patching for outliers, which libfor omits) is the path to beating
             general-purpose compressors on the structured-integer sections.
perf:        Pure scalar ANSI C, intentionally no SIMD (author trades peak throughput for
             portability/simplicity; runs on Linux/Windows/any sane system). Speed comes from
             fully unrolled, byte-aligned, table-dispatched pack/unpack kernels generated in
             for-gen.c rather than vectorization. Decode and especially select() are branch-light;
             select is O(1) per element with at most a 2-word read+mask. Memory overhead is just 5
             bytes per block. Used in production in upscaledb to compress 32-bit keys. Caps at
             bits<=32 (no 64-bit path in the header). For nest, a NEON/AVX2 unpack kernel could be
             added later under nest-runtime's existing SIMD dispatcher to recover the throughput
             libfor leaves on the table.
license:     Apache License 2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] cruppstahl/libvbyte
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C/C++ Apache-2.0 library that compresses 32/64-bit integer arrays with variable-byte (VByte)
             coding, delta-encoding sorted sequences and using Daniel Lemire's MaskedVByte SSE/AVX
             kernel to decode billions of integers per second, while also exposing
             select/lower-bound-search/append primitives that operate over the compressed stream.
innovation:  Random-access and search directly on the compressed VByte stream without full decompression:
             vbyte_select returns the i-th value, vbyte_search_lower_bound does a binary search
             over delta-coded sorted data, and vbyte_append extends a stream in place - turning a
             compressed integer array into a queryable structure rather than an opaque blob you
             must inflate first.
techniques:
  - MaskedVByte SIMD decode (Lemire/Plaisance): uses per-byte high-bit continuation masks +
    SSE/AVX pshufb shuffle tables to unpack variable-length codes in vectorized batches
  - Delta + VByte for sorted/monotonic sequences (vbyte_compress_sorted32/64 take a `previous`
    seed so deltas, not absolute values, are stored)
  - Separate sorted vs unsorted codecs so unsorted data skips the delta transform
  - vbyte_select_sorted/unsorted: fetch index i by walking length-prefixed varints without
    materializing the array
  - vbyte_search_lower_bound_sorted: lower_bound over the delta stream returning position plus
    reconstructed actual value
  - vbyte_append_sorted/unsorted: append one integer to the tail of a compressed buffer in place,
    returns bytes written
  - vbyte_compressed_size_* sizing functions to pre-compute output buffer length before encoding
  - 32-bit and 64-bit width variants throughout, little-endian-only assumption
nest steals: Target nest-format, path crates/nest-format/src/sections/spans.rs plus a new encoding/varint codec
             under crates/nest-format/src/encoding/. Today chunks_original_spans stores
             byte_start/byte_end as fixed 16 raw LE bytes per chunk (spans.rs lines 21-24); within
             one source_uri these offsets are monotonically increasing, the exact delta+VByte
             sorted case. Re-encode each source's offsets as a delta-coded sorted varint stream to
             cut ~16 bytes/chunk toward ~2-4 bytes for close-packed chunks, shrinking a required
             non-embedding section that delta-varint beats structurally even after zstd. Make it
             purely additive: register a new reserved encoding id (4-255 free in v1) so old
             readers ignore it and NEST_FORMAT_VERSION never bumps; content_hash stays stable
             because it hashes DECODED canonical values, not the encoding. Implement in pure safe
             Rust (no C FFI) with a scalar varint loop first respecting the never-panic
             typed-error and small-core rules; MaskedVByte SSE/AVX is a later perf upgrade behind
             the existing nest-runtime SIMD dispatcher. The select/lower_bound primitives also map
             onto bm25 posting lists in crates/nest-runtime/src/bm25 if doc-id lists move to
             compressed deltas, enabling search over compressed postings without full inflate.
compression: Directly on-mission: delta+varint is the canonical way to compress sorted integer columns (byte
             spans, posting-list doc ids, chunk-id maps) below fixed-width or even
             zstd-on-raw-u64, deterministically and byte-reproducibly. It adds a structural
             integer codec to nest's toolbox (raw/zstd/f16/int8 today, none of which exploit
             monotonicity), advancing the compress-more-than-everyone north star for every
             integer-bearing section, and random-access select means compression does not cost
             queryability.
perf:        MaskedVByte decodes billions of ints/sec on AVX/AVX2 by branchlessly resolving continuation-bit
             masks with pshufb shuffle tables; scalar VByte is branchy (one branch per
             continuation byte) but tiny and cache-friendly. Monotonic 64-bit offsets whose deltas
             fit in ~12-14 bits drop from 8 bytes to 1-2 bytes each. Library is
             little-endian-only, matching nest's all-LE invariant. select/search walk the stream
             so they avoid allocating the decoded array entirely.
license:     Apache-2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] fast-pack/simdcomp
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A BSD-licensed C library that compresses blocks of 128 (or 256) 32-bit integers by SIMD "vertical"
             binary bit-packing - computing the minimal common bit-width b and packing values into
             b bits each - with an integrated delta+prefix-sum path for sorted lists, decoding at
             ~15 GB/s (0.3 cycles/int) on SSE4.1/AVX2.
innovation:  Vertical (transposed/lane-interleaved) bit-packing: instead of concatenating b-bit integers
             end-to-end (the scalar BitPacker1x layout), values are laid out so each SIMD lane
             owns a strided subset (4 lanes for SSE = BitPacker4x/128 ints, 8 lanes for AVX2 =
             BitPacker8x/256 ints). All 128/256 integers in a block share one bit-width
             b=maxbits() and are packed/unpacked with a single branch-free shift-mask-OR loop
             across vector lanes. For sorted lists this is fused with a SIMD delta encoder
             (alignr/shift to subtract the previous lane, seeded by an initvalue) and a SIMD
             prefix-sum decoder (shift-add across lanes + a shuffle that carries the top lane into
             the next block), so compress/decompress is integrated, not a two-pass
             delta-then-pack.
techniques:
  - Vertical/lane-transposed bit-packing in blocks of 128 (SSE) or 256 (AVX2) ints, all values
    sharing one bit-width b=maxbits()
  - maxbits/maxbitsd1: OR-reduce a block (or its deltas) to find the minimal common bit-width in
    one SIMD pass
  - Integrated delta-1 coding: simdpackd1/simdunpackd1 carry a uint32 initvalue seed so block N
    continues block N-1's running offset
  - SIMD prefix-sum decode: shift-and-add across 128-bit lanes + _mm_shuffle to propagate the
    highest lane as carry
  - SIMD delta encode via _mm_alignr_epi8 (SSSE3) with a plain shift-or SSE2 fallback
  - withoutmask fast path: when deltas are provably < 2^b, skip the AND-mask step
  - Frame-of-reference (simdfor.h): pack value-minus-min so packed words stay directly
    range-searchable in place
  - Format-agnostic kernels: library ships only pack/unpack + simdpack_compressedbytes() sizing;
    container and b-selector byte are the caller's job
nest steals: Add an integer codec module in nest-format (crates/nest-format/src/encoding/bitpack.rs, <=300 loc)
             porting BitPacker4x-style vertical packing in safe Rust with NEON/AVX2/scalar
             dispatch mirroring nest-runtime/src/simd. Apply it to the two sorted-integer hot
             spots that today emit raw little-endian u32 with ZERO compression: (1) BM25 postings
             in nest-runtime/src/bm25/codec.rs - per-term doc-id lists are monotonically
             increasing, so delta+bitpack (simdpackd1 path) collapses them while staying
             losslessly decodable; tf bitpacks on its tiny bit-width; (2) HNSW neighbor-id lists
             in nest-runtime/src/ann/codec.rs - small bounded u32 ids bitpack to ~ceil(log2(n))
             bits instead of 32. Also delta-encode chunks_original_spans byte_start/byte_end
             (nest-format/src/sections/spans.rs), which are near-monotonic. Stays inside the
             frozen format: these are payload-internal layouts of optional sections 0x07/0x08
             (bump only their per-section PAYLOAD_VERSION, never NEST_FORMAT_VERSION) plus a
             span-section internal encoding; embeddings untouched (still mmap-scored).
             Deterministic because packing is a pure function of the ints, and content_hash is
             unchanged since decode reproduces identical ints.
compression: Directly advances 'compress more than everyone' on the two sections nest leaves uncompressed. BM25
             posting lists and HNSW adjacency are pure sorted/small-integer data - the exact
             workload where delta+vertical-bitpack beats zstd in BOTH ratio and decode speed (15
             GB/s, branch-free), shrinking the tiny/hybrid index sections without zstd decompress
             cost on the search path. Leaves the embedding mmap dot-product hot path untouched:
             pure upside for index-heavy presets.
perf:        ~4-8 billion ints/s (~15 GB/s, 0.3 cycles/int Skylake) fully branch-free. Rust port
             quickwit-oss/bitpacking: BitPacker4x compress ~5.3B/s, BitPacker8x decompress
             ~6.5B/s, runtime SSE3/AVX2/scalar detection, no_std. Each 128-int block packs to
             exactly b*16 bytes plus a 1-byte b selector; no heap churn, mmap-friendly. NEON has
             no native 256-bit, so aarch64 targets the 128-int (4x-equivalent) layout to match
             nest's NEON dispatcher.
license:     BSD (simdcomp C lib); the Rust port quickwit-oss/bitpacking is MIT. Both permissive, compatible
             with vendoring or clean-room reimplementation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] ot/partitioned_elias_fano
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Reference C++ implementation (Ottaviano & Venturini, SIGIR 2014) of Partitioned Elias-Fano: it
             slices a monotone integer sequence into variable-length chunks chosen by a
             linear-time approximate dynamic program, encoding each chunk with the cheapest of
             plain Elias-Fano, an all-ones run, or a raw bitvector, giving near-optimal
             compression with constant-time random access and nextGEQ.
innovation:  Elias-Fano is quasi-optimal only when gaps are uniform; real posting lists and ID sequences are
             bursty/clustered. The breakthrough is a two-level partition that adapts the universe
             locally: cut the sorted sequence into chunks, store per-chunk endpoints (upper bounds
             and value bases) at level 1, and at level 2 encode each chunk independently against
             its OWN small local universe, picking per chunk the cheapest representation
             (Elias-Fano, all-ones, or plain bitvector). Because EF size is computed exactly per
             prefix (not estimated), the optimal-cost partition is found by a DP recurrence that,
             via the Ferragina et al. (1+epsilon) pruning, runs in O(m log m) time / O(m) space
             while staying within (1+epsilon) of the true optimum. Result: strictly better-than-EF
             compression on clustered data with no loss of O(1) random access or skip (nextGEQ)
             speed.
techniques:
  - Elias-Fano coding: split each value into floor(log(u/n)) low bits packed in a fixed-width
    array, and high bits as a unary-gap bitvector; random access and nextGEQ via constant-time
    broadword rank/select on the high-bits bitvector
  - Two-level partitioned layout: level-1 sequence stores partition upper-bounds + base offsets
    (themselves EF-coded); level-2 stores each chunk re-based to a small local universe
  - Per-chunk codec selection: each chunk encoded as the cheapest of compact/strict Elias-Fano,
    all_ones_sequence (consecutive-int run, ~0 bits), or a plain bitvector when the chunk universe
    is dense
  - optimal_partition.hpp: DP minimizing total encoded bits, made linear via Ferragina (1+epsilon)
    approximation that prunes candidate cuts; EF cost evaluated EXACTLY in O(1) per window, not
    estimated
  - uniform_partitioned_sequence: cheaper fixed-size-partition variant (EF uniform) avoiding the
    DP, trading a little size for build speed
  - Constant-time select via precomputed in-word select tables + 64-bit popcount over the
    high-bits bitvector (the succinct submodule), no SIMD required
  - indexed_sequence dispatch: one sequence abstraction tagging which sub-codec each chunk used,
    so decode is branch-on-tag
  - FastPFor / block_codecs integration (PForDelta, varint, simdbp) as fixed-block comparison
    baselines
nest steals: Two distinct steals, both landing in nest-format encoding plus nest-runtime readers. (1) A new
             ADDITIVE encoding id (in the reserved 4-255 range, no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump)
             implementing partitioned Elias-Fano for monotone integer sections:
             chunks_original_spans (sorted byte offsets) and especially the HNSW
             adjacency/neighbor-id lists and BM25 posting lists inside
             hnsw_index(0x07)/bm25_index(0x08). These are exactly the sorted/clustered ID
             sequences EF was built for and are today raw or zstd; partitioned EF beats zstd on
             bits-per-id while ADDING O(1) random access and nextGEQ that zstd cannot give (zstd
             needs full-block decode). (2) The level-2 cheapest-of-three per-chunk selector plus
             the exact-cost linear DP partitioner are a reusable codec-selection pattern:
             nest-format/encoding can adopt a per-section/per-chunk pick-the-smallest tag scheme
             (raw vs delta vs EF vs all-ones) chosen at build time, keeping decode a deterministic
             branch-on-tag (preserves reproducible byte-identical builds since the DP is
             deterministic for fixed epsilon). The select/rank broadword primitives belong in
             nest-runtime as a small succinct module (repr(C), little-endian, no panic) so
             EF-coded sections are searchable straight off mmap with no decompression on the hot
             path, matching nest's existing score-from-mmap discipline. Keep each new file <=300
             lines: a bitvector+select primitive file, an ef_chunk codec file, a partition-cost
             file.
compression: Directly on the north star. Posting lists and neighbor-id lists are a large,
             currently-underexploited part of the hnsw_index and bm25_index sections; partitioned
             EF is the state-of-the-art compression/query-time tradeoff for exactly this data and
             gives random access WITHOUT decompression, which zstd structurally cannot. It lets
             nest shrink index sections below zstd while making them directly searchable from
             mmap, compressing more than everyone while preserving the
             offline/single-file/deterministic guarantees.
perf:        Plain EF uses ~2 + ceil(log(u/n)) bits per element and supports O(1) select / O(log) nextGEQ;
             partitioned EF typically cuts 10-30% off plain EF on clustered TREC/ClueWeb posting
             lists and reports the best compression-ratio/query-time frontier vs PForDelta,
             varint, simdbp, and interpolative coding. Decode/skip is constant-time via broadword
             popcount + in-word select tables on 64-bit words (no AVX/NEON needed, so it ports
             cleanly to nest's scalar fallback and stays branch-predictable). Build cost is
             dominated by the O(m log m) partition DP; the uniform-partition variant builds faster
             with slightly worse ratio. Memory-map friendly: all structures are flat bit-packed
             arrays addressable by offset, matching nest's mmap-and-score model.
license:     Believed Apache-2.0 (same license as the successor project ds2i/PISA into which this code was
             folded); confirm by reading the repo LICENSE before vendoring. README explicitly
             states the code now lives in ds2i, so prefer porting concepts/algorithms over copying
             C++ verbatim.

# focus: hashing, checksums, content addressing, authenticated encryption

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] BLAKE3-team/BLAKE3
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Merkle-tree hash built on a reduced-round (7-round) ChaCha-derived BLAKE2 compression function
             that splits input into fixed 1024-byte chunks, hashes them in parallel across SIMD
             lanes and threads, and combines chaining values pairwise into parent nodes, enabling
             parallelism, extendable XOF output, and verified streaming (Bao) where any byte range
             is verifiable against the single 256-bit root.
innovation:  A hash is a binary Merkle tree over fixed-size chunks (1024 B): the root is a single 32-byte
             commitment, but every interior chaining value lets you verify an arbitrary sub-range
             WITHOUT re-hashing the whole input. This decouples "one stable content hash for the
             whole artifact" from "cheap incremental/partial verification of just the bytes you
             touched" - exactly the tension nest has between its whole-file content_hash and
             per-section/per-chunk integrity.
techniques:
  - Binary Merkle tree over fixed 1024-byte chunks; parent node = hash(left_CV || right_CV); odd
    nodes promoted to next level
  - Domain-separation flags (CHUNK_START/END, PARENT, ROOT, KEYED_HASH,
    DERIVE_KEY_CONTEXT/MATERIAL): only the final root is flagged ROOT, preventing length-extension
    and tree-substitution attacks
  - 7-round ChaCha-quarter-round permutation over a 16-word state with 8-word 256-bit chaining
    value and per-chunk 64-bit counter
  - SIMD batch hashing of independent chunks across SSE2/SSE4.1/AVX2/AVX-512/NEON lanes with
    runtime CPU feature detection (AVX-512 = 16 chunks at once)
  - XOF extendable output via OutputReader (Read+Seek): root compression re-run with incrementing
    output-block counter yields unlimited seekable output
  - Bao verified-streaming format: 8-byte LE length prefix + chunks/parent nodes interleaved in
    pre-order so depth-first read needs no seeking
  - Bao slice format: transmit only parent CVs + chunks on the path to a target byte range,
    verifying that range against the root alone (~3.1% metadata at 1KiB chunks)
  - Configurable chunk groups (bao-tree): hash interior in 1024-B chunks for an unchanged root but
    emit parent nodes only every N KiB, cutting metadata overhead from ~6% to ~0.1%
nest steals: Two concrete, additive moves. (1) nest-format/hashing.rs: nest computes file_hash and content_hash
             as flat SHA-256 over whole bodies. Adopt the BLAKE3 tree structure (or BLAKE3 via the
             `blake3` crate, CC0/Apache, vendorable, no_std, deterministic) so content_hash
             becomes a Merkle ROOT while interior chaining values let `nest validate` verify a
             single section or chunk's byte span WITHOUT re-hashing the whole file - turning
             O(file) validation into O(touched-bytes), which matters once nest serves whole
             databases/repos/video. (2) The Bao slice format maps onto nest citations:
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id already reopens an exact byte span; add a RESERVED
             section (id 0x09+, additive within v1) holding interleaved parent-node CVs (a
             merkle_index/bao_tree section) so a partial or remote consumer can cryptographically
             verify just the cited chunk_id against the root with ~0.1% overhead via chunk-grouped
             parents. Keep SHA-256 as the FROZEN default and introduce BLAKE3 as a new
             hashing-algorithm id so the format stays additive and v1 never bumps. The 7-round
             SIMD core is also a reference model for nest-runtime's AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatcher:
             the same lane-batching discipline (N independent items per SIMD width, runtime
             detection) is how nest batches dot products - so BLAKE3's guts are a reference
             kernel, not a dependency on the hot scoring path.
compression: Indirect but real. BLAKE3 is not a compressor and does not shrink bytes. Its relevance to
             "compress more than everyone" is structural: the Merkle-tree-over-fixed-chunks layout
             is the standard substrate for content-defined chunking + dedup (the same boundary
             discipline underlies CDC dedup in restic/casync/IPFS). A nest that chunks artifacts
             on stable boundaries and addresses every chunk by its CV can dedup identical spans
             across files/datasets/versions before any entropy coder runs - cross-artifact dedup
             is often a larger win than the codec itself on repos/databases. The 1KiB-chunk vs
             chunk-group tradeoff (3.1% vs 0.1% metadata) is the exact knob nest must tune so
             verification metadata does not erode compression-ratio gains.
perf:        b3sum is ~an order of magnitude faster than sha256sum on desktop hardware due to (a) SIMD batch
             hashing of independent chunks (AVX-512=16 lanes, AVX2=8, NEON=4) and (b) rayon
             multithreading across independent subtrees. Throughput scales near-linearly with
             cores and SIMD width because chunks are independent until a cheap combine step.
             Memory: streaming with an O(log n) stack of pending chaining values, no need to
             buffer the whole input. Chunk independence is what makes both parallelism AND partial
             verification free - the property nest wants for large artifacts.
license:     Dual-licensed CC0-1.0 / Apache-2.0; the `blake3` Rust crate is permissive, vendor-friendly,
             no_std-capable with a portable scalar reference path - safe to vendor under nest's
             offline single-file constraints.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] RustCrypto/hashes
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A pure-Rust collection of cryptographic hash crates (sha2, sha3, blake2, blake3 via
             traits-preview, ripemd, etc.) unified under the `digest` crate's
             `Update`/`FixedOutput`/`ExtendableOutput` traits, with the heavy backends doing
             runtime-dispatched hardware acceleration (x86 SHA-NI, AArch64 sha2 extension, AVX2,
             NEON, WASM simd128) over a shared `block-buffer` block-accumulation abstraction.
innovation:  A single small trait surface (`Digest` = `Update` + `FixedOutput` + `Reset`, plus
             `ExtendableOutput`/`XofReader` for variable-length output) lets you write integrity
             code generically over any hash, while the concrete crate transparently selects the
             fastest CPU backend at runtime via the `cpufeatures` zero-sized-token model - so
             hashing becomes hardware-accelerated for free without the format ever naming a
             backend. The block-buffer abstraction cleanly separates "accumulate input into fixed
             compression blocks" from "compress a block," which is exactly the pattern nest's
             domain-separated, length-prefixed chunk_id preimage already imitates by hand.
techniques:
  - digest trait split: Update (streaming input) + FixedOutput (finalize) + Reset +
    ExtendableOutput/XofReader for XOF, enabling generic-over-hash code
  - cpufeatures runtime CPU detection via a zero-sized token type (cpufeatures::new!) that gates
    SHA-NI / sha2-ext / AVX2 / NEON / simd128 backends with soft fallback, no_std-friendly
  - sha2 0.11 multi-backend cfg system: sha2_backend / sha2_256_backend override flags, plus soft
    'compact' vs unrolled for binary-size vs speed tradeoff
  - block-buffer: accumulate arbitrary-length input into fixed compression-function blocks (64B
    for sha256), decoupled from the compression kernel
  - BLAKE3 Merkle-tree hashing: 1024B chunks, 64B blocks, 32B chaining values; tree shape enables
    SIMD-parallel and rayon multithreaded hashing of large inputs
  - BLAKE3 verified streaming: independently verifiable subtree chaining values let you
    authenticate a byte range without rehashing the whole input
  - BLAKE3 XOF: finalize_xof() -> OutputReader (Read+Seek) for arbitrary-length random-access
    keyed output; one primitive for hash, MAC (keyed_hash), and KDF (derive_key)
  - domain-separated + length-prefixed preimage construction to make hashes collision-safe across
    concatenated fields (nest already uses this in chunk_id)
nest steals: nest-format already depends on sha2 = "0.11" and calls Sha256::digest/Update across exactly the
             right five places: header.rs (header_checksum), section_entry.rs (per-section
             checksum), footer.rs (file_hash), reader/decode.rs (content_hash), and chunk.rs
             (chunk_id). Three concrete steals: (1) Confirm/enable sha2's default runtime-dispatch
             backends so file_hash over the whole pre-footer body and per-section checksums get
             x86 SHA-NI / AArch64 sha2-ext acceleration for free on the validate path - pure
             additive, no format change, just verify no `sha2_backend="soft"` override and that
             0.11 auto-detection is on; speeds up `validate` and build determinism checks. (2) Add
             a tiny internal Hasher abstraction in nest-format (new ~80-line src/hashing.rs,
             single-responsibility, under the 300-line cap) wrapping `impl digest::Update +
             FixedOutput` so all four integrity surfaces share one domain-separated builder
             instead of five hand-rolled call sites - reduces drift, makes the preimage spec
             auditable in one file. (3) For the content-addressing roadmap (graph search,
             dataset/repo/binary-blob compression, audio/video), evaluate BLAKE3 (blake3 crate,
             traits-preview) as an OPTIONAL additive hash-id scheme for large blobs: its
             Merkle-tree gives SIMD+rayon-parallel hashing of multi-GB inputs and
             verified-streaming of byte ranges, mapping onto nest://content_hash/chunk_id range
             reopen - but it must be a new version-tagged digest algorithm (the sha256: prefix in
             chunk_id stays the v1 default) to preserve determinism and the FROZEN v1 format.
compression: Indirect but real. Hashing is not an entropy coder, so it does not shrink bytes; its relevance to
             'compress more than everyone' is (a) making the four integrity surfaces cheap enough
             that nest can hash aggressively-compressed/deduplicated content on every build and
             validate without becoming a bottleneck, and (b) BLAKE3's verified-streaming +
             content-addressing enabling block-level dedup across datasets/repos/whole-databases
             (identical subtrees hash identically), a prerequisite for cross-document
             deduplication that actually drives corpus-level compression. Hardware-accelerated
             SHA-256 keeps deterministic reproducible builds fast as corpora grow.
perf:        sha2 0.11 default = runtime dispatch: SHA-256 uses x86-sha (SHA-NI) or aarch64-sha2 extension (~2x
             soft on SHA-NI CPUs, far more vs naive); SHA-512 uses AVX2 / aarch64-sha3. soft
             backend unrolls rounds by default (faster, bigger binary); 'compact' cfg trades speed
             for size - relevant to nest-python abi3 cdylib and CLI footprint. block-buffer works
             in 64B blocks for sha256. BLAKE3 is 5-10x faster than SHA-256 on non-SHA-NI CPUs (~2x
             even with SHA-NI), SIMD across SSE2/SSE4.1/AVX2/AVX-512/NEON/WASM with rayon
             multithreading; its update() only reaches full SIMD width with >=16 KiB buffers (8
             KiB hits AVX2 but not AVX-512) - feed it large mmap slices, not small chunks.
             cpufeatures detection is one-time and effectively zero-cost after init.
license:     Dual-licensed Apache-2.0 OR MIT (sha2 and all RustCrypto/hashes crates; blake3 crate is CC0-1.0 OR
             Apache-2.0). All permissive and already compatible with nest's existing sha2
             dependency.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] skerkour/chacha20-blake3
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A no_std Rust committing AEAD that derives ChaCha20 + BLAKE3-keyed-MAC subkeys from a 24-byte
             nonce via one BLAKE3 XOF call, encrypts with a from-scratch SIMD-dispatched ChaCha20,
             and authenticates Encrypt-then-MAC with a length-framed BLAKE3 keyed hash.
innovation:  Using BLAKE3 as a single multi-purpose primitive: one
             new_keyed(master_key).update(nonce).finalize_xof() call streams out 72 bytes sliced
             into an encryption key (0..32), an independent MAC key (32..64), and an 8-byte cipher
             nonce (64..72) - so one tree-hash invocation does KDF, subkey separation, and
             long-nonce absorption, and the same keyed BLAKE3 provides the MAC. This collapses KDF
             + MAC + commitment into one fast, parallelizable, SIMD-scaling, deterministic hash
             family.
techniques:
  - BLAKE3 XOF as KDF: new_keyed(key)||nonce -> finalize_xof().fill(72 bytes) sliced into
    enc_key|mac_key|cipher_nonce, absorbing the over-long 24B nonce for free
  - Encrypt-then-MAC committing AEAD: tag = BLAKE3.keyed(mac_key, aad || le64(aad.len) ||
    ciphertext || le64(ct.len)) with explicit little-endian length framing to prevent
    canonicalization/extension ambiguity
  - Full key/context commitment via keyed-BLAKE3 over the whole context, defeating
    partitioning-oracle attacks (unlike Poly1305/GCM); constant_time_eq_32 tag check, decrypt only
    on match
  - Compile-time + runtime SIMD dispatch ladder: aarch64 assumes NEON, wasm32 simd128 via
    target_feature, x86_64 is_x86_feature_detected!(avx512f then avx2) under std, >=128-byte
    threshold, scalar quarter_round fallback
  - Stateful streaming stream-cipher: caches last_keystream_block + index so unaligned successive
    xor_keystream calls equal one contiguous call (zero re-derivation)
  - no_std-first crate factoring with granular std/alloc/zeroize features and
    Zeroize/ZeroizeOnDrop on key material
nest steals: Two distinct steals. (1) Hashing/KDF primitive swap in nest-format/src (the SHA-256 surfaces in
             layout/header.rs, layout/section_entry.rs, layout/footer.rs, manifest/,
             writer/build.rs, sections/chunk_ids.rs): adopt BLAKE3 as the integrity hash for the
             four surfaces (header_checksum, per-section physical checksum, file_hash,
             content_hash). BLAKE3 is 5-10x faster than SHA-256, internally SIMD-parallel (AVX2 on
             x86_64, NEON on aarch64 - matching nest's existing dispatch targets), deterministic,
             and its keyed/derive_key modes give domain separation so the four surfaces use
             distinct contexts and never collide. Speeds the deterministic-build hot path and
             big-file content addressing. NOTE: changing the meaning of existing hash fields is a
             true v2 (bump NEST_FORMAT_VERSION), not a v1 add. (2) The exact ChaCha20-BLAKE3 AEAD
             is the blueprint for an optional encrypted-container codec as a RESERVED encoding id
             (4-255, additive within v1) in nest-format/src/encoding: wrap non-embedding section
             payloads in committing AEAD so a .nest file is encrypted-at-rest while staying
             single-file, offline, no_std-friendly, and deterministic given (key, fixed-nonce).
             The committing property binds ciphertext to exactly one key, which is the right
             guarantee for content-addressed citations (nest://content_hash/...).
compression: Indirect but real. BLAKE3-as-hash is not a compressor, but it removes hashing from the
             deterministic-build and content-addressing critical path (5-10x over SHA-256,
             SIMD-parallel on the same AVX2/NEON targets nest already uses), so re-hashing large
             compressed sections during build/validate stops being the bottleneck as nest scales
             to whole datasets/repos/databases. BLAKE3 derive_key/keyed modes also give cheap
             domain-separated subkeys for future per-section keying without extra primitives. The
             AEAD codec is orthogonal to ratio but unlocks compress-then-encrypt single-file
             containers. Net: enables the 'compress AND serve huge corpora' north star by making
             integrity cheap, not by improving ratio.
perf:        Author benchmarks (AMD EPYC 9R45): ChaCha20-BLAKE3 reaches 2,153 MB/s at 64KB and 3,297 MB/s at
             1MB, beating XChaCha20-Poly1305 (~1,650 MB/s) and AES-256-GCM (~1,476 MB/s) on large
             messages; loses on tiny 64B messages (103 vs 540 MB/s for AES-NI GCM) due to fixed
             BLAKE3+KDF overhead. SIMD threshold is 128 bytes before any vector path. BLAKE3
             itself is internally SIMD (AVX-512/AVX2/NEON/SVE) and tree-parallel, so throughput
             scales with SIMD width and multicore for very large inputs - directly relevant to
             nest's large-file content_hash/file_hash. Stream cipher caches the last keystream
             block so chunked unaligned writes cost no re-derivation.
license:     Permissive MIT: the chacha and chacha20-blake3 crates declare license = "MIT" in Cargo.toml;
             repo-root LICENSE.txt is an MIT-style permissive grant 'Copyright (c) 2025 Arcane
             Services'. Safe to learn from with attribution. nest should depend on the upstream
             blake3 crate (also permissive) rather than re-vendoring the from-scratch ChaCha; only
             the construction/dispatch pattern is the reference.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] google/crc32c
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A small C++ library computing CRC32C (Castagnoli/iSCSI polynomial 0x1EDC6F41) with a
             runtime-dispatched tiered backend: hardware SSE4.2 CRC32 intrinsics on x86_64, ARMv8
             CRC32 intrinsics on aarch64, and a portable slicing-by-4 (16 bytes/iteration)
             software fallback.
innovation:  The runtime CPU-feature dispatch pattern: a single Extend() entry point picks the best
             implementation once via lazily-initialized static bools (CanUseSse42 /
             CanUseArm64Crc32 set from CPUID/getauxval), so the same binary runs an accelerated
             kernel where the silicon supports it and silently degrades to a portable scalar
             kernel everywhere else, with byte-identical output across all three paths.
techniques:
  - CRC32C with the Castagnoli polynomial 0x1EDC6F41 (reflected), distinct from zip/zlib CRC32
    0x04C11DB7
  - Three-way parallel hardware CRC: maintains 3 independent _mm_crc32_u64 accumulators over
    disjoint blocks, then folds them with precomputed kBlockNSkipTable lookup tables (XOR of
    polynomial-skip constants) instead of PCLMULQDQ
  - Hierarchical block sizes (Block0/1/2 nested loops) so the skip-table combine cost is amortized
    and cache/prefetch friendly
  - Portable slicing-by-4: 4 stride tables (kStrideExtensionTable0..3) plus a byte table, 256 x
    u32 each (~4KB), 16 bytes/loop iteration
  - ReadUint32LE little-endian reads for deterministic, endianness-stable results regardless of
    host architecture
  - Software prefetch (RequestPrefetch at a fixed kPrefetchHorizon) on large buffers; byte-by-byte
    head handling for unaligned starts
  - Incremental Extend(crc, buf, len) API enabling streaming/chunked checksums that compose across
    calls
  - Lazy one-time static-init feature detection to avoid per-call dispatch overhead
nest steals: Two concrete uses. (1) nest-runtime SIMD dispatcher: nest already ships an AVX2/NEON/scalar
             dot-product dispatcher; crc32c's lazy-static CanUseX pattern is the exact template
             for a single clean cfg(target_arch)+is_x86_feature_detected!/std::arch aarch64
             runtime selector that guarantees byte-identical scalar fallback (critical for nest's
             determinism guarantee). (2) nest-format hashing.rs (the 4 SHA-256 integrity surfaces,
             ~4.9k loc core): add an OPTIONAL fast integrity tier. SHA-256 is overkill and slow
             for the per-section physical-byte checksum on the validate/inspect hot path; a
             hardware CRC32C gives a cheap detect-corruption-fast pre-check (run CRC32C first
             during `nest validate`, only fall through to SHA-256 if you need cryptographic
             strength or it mismatches). Because format v1 is additive-only, this fits as a new
             reserved encoding/metadata field, not a version bump. The slicing-by-4 portable
             kernel is also a ready-made deterministic scalar reference for nest's never-panic,
             repr(C), little-endian, <=300-line-file constraints (the whole CRC32C core is small
             enough to vendor as one file). For nest's North Star (compress-more-than-everyone),
             CRC32C itself does not compress, but a fast checksum lets every codec (zstd/f16/int8)
             verify decode integrity per chunk almost for free, enabling more aggressive lossy
             quantization with cheap corruption detection.
compression: Indirect. CRC32C does not reduce bytes, so it is orthogonal to the compress-more-than-everyone
             goal. Its value is enabling: a near-free hardware integrity check per chunk/section
             lets nest push harder on lossy/aggressive codecs (int8, f16, future entropy coders)
             while still cheaply detecting decode corruption, and the runtime-dispatch +
             deterministic-scalar-fallback pattern directly hardens nest's existing SIMD compute
             paths.
perf:        Hardware SSE4.2 path: 3-way interleaved _mm_crc32_u64 hides the ~3-cycle latency / 1-per-cycle
             throughput of the crc32 instruction, reaching multiple bytes/cycle; combine done by
             table XOR (no PCLMULQDQ dependency, so it works even on hypervisors that hide the
             CLMUL CPUID bit). ARMv8 path uses native __crc32c intrinsics. Portable slicing-by-4
             does 16 B/iter from ~4KB of L1-resident tables with software prefetch. All three are
             streaming/incremental and allocation-free. Note: google explicitly recommends
             HighwayHash for NEW file formats over CRC32C, so use CRC32C only as a fast
             non-cryptographic detect-corruption layer, not as nest's primary content-addressing
             hash (keep SHA-256 content_hash as the stable identity).
license:     BSD-3-Clause (Google), code originally derived from LevelDB

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] ogxd/gxhash
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A non-cryptographic 32/64/128-bit hash that abuses the hardware AES round instruction (x86
             aesenc/AES-NI, aarch64 NEON vaes) as a wide bit-mixer over a 128-bit SIMD state,
             currently the fastest in its class at all input sizes (often >30 GB/s on large
             inputs).
innovation:  Use one hardware AES round (aesenc) as a single-instruction, fully-avalanching 128-bit
             compression/mixing primitive instead of hand-rolled multiply-xor-shift chains.
             Because AES-NI is one ~4-cycle pipelined instruction with near-perfect diffusion, a
             few aesenc rounds over a 128-bit lane give SMHasher-passing quality (low collisions,
             high avalanche) at a fraction of the bytecode of ahash/xxh3, which inlines/caches
             better and saturates ILP.
techniques:
  - aesenc/vaesenc (AES-NI / NEON-AES) as the core 128-bit mixing round, not for encryption
  - 128-bit SIMD State with factor-of-8 loop unrolling (8 independent accumulators) to fill the
    AES-round pipeline and maximize ILP
  - get_partial / get_partial_unsafe: branchless masked load of the trailing <16-byte block so
    every byte influences the hash without OOB reads
  - finalize(): a few terminal aesenc rounds against a fixed KEYS constant array to emit
    32/64/128-bit digests
  - wide-pipe (hybrid VAES+AVX2) construction for multicollision resistance when output <=64 bits
  - seed randomization for DoS resistance with an opt-in deterministic feature flag
  - cross-platform output stability guaranteed within a major version (same hash on x86 and
    aarch64)
nest steals: Two non-overlapping uses. (1) DO NOT touch the four SHA-256 integrity surfaces in
             nest-format/src/reader/decode.rs and chunk.rs (header_checksum, per-section checksum,
             file_hash, content_hash): these are cryptographic identity/citation anchors
             (nest://content_hash/...) and must stay SHA-256. (2) Adopt gxhash only as the
             in-memory BuildHasher for hot-path tables that never persist:
             nest-runtime/src/bm25/index.rs (HashMap<String,TermEntry> built per query and at
             index build, plus per-doc tf maps), bm25/fusion.rs RRF HashMap<usize,f32>, and
             ann/search.rs visited HashSet<u32>. Replacing std SipHash with a gxhash/hashbrown
             BuildHasher cuts BM25 build/query and HNSW traversal CPU with zero format impact. A
             third, format-relevant use: a build-time content-defined chunk dedup pass in the
             nest-python/writer path (writer/encoding_choice.rs) -- gxhash chunk bytes to find
             duplicate chunks_canonical before encoding, storing each unique chunk once with
             refcounting in chunk_ids. Stays additive (build-time decision, frozen v1 layout
             unchanged). Enable gxhash's deterministic (fixed-seed) feature so identical corpora
             still yield byte-identical files.
compression: Indirect but real. gxhash is a hasher, not a compressor, so it shrinks nothing by itself. Its
             leverage is enabling cheap content-defined chunk/block deduplication at build time:
             hash every chunk (and sub-block fingerprints for near-dup detection) fast enough to
             dedup large corpora and whole-repo ingestion before zstd, storing each unique chunk
             once. This dedup-then-compress pipeline (restic/borg/casync style) is the
             highest-yield path to beating everyone on multi-document and repo-scale corpora where
             cross-document redundancy dominates. The hot-table speedups are latency wins, not
             size wins.
perf:        Often >30 GB/s on large inputs on modern x86 with AES-NI; fastest SMHasher-class non-crypto hash
             at every input size. Wins from (a) aesenc being a single pipelined ~4-cycle
             instruction with full diffusion, (b) 8-way unrolled 128-bit accumulators saturating
             ILP, (c) tiny inlinable bytecode. HARD CAVEATS for nest: requires AES+SSE2 (x86) or
             AES+NEON (aarch64) and HAS NO SCALAR FALLBACK -- fails to compile on unsupported
             targets, which collides with nest's scalar-fallback guarantee. Must be feature-gated
             behind target_feature detection with a portable fallback hasher
             (foldhash/ahash-fallback or std SipHash) on non-AES targets, never a hard dep. Uses
             heavy unsafe and aggressive opts (explicitly not for safety-critical use), so it must
             not enter never-panic nest-format library code; confine it to runtime in-memory
             tables and the build-time python/writer dedup pass.
license:     MIT

# focus: SIMD, vectorization, portable intrinsics, fast string ops
