# category: serialization-parsing  (7 repos)
# focus: zero-copy serialization, fast JSON/CSV parsing, mmap-friendly layouts
##################################################################################################

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] simdjson/simdjson
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A branch-free two-stage SIMD JSON parser that classifies 64-byte chunks into structural-character
             bitmasks (stage 1) and materializes a flat 64-bit "tape" or lazily walks values via
             an On-Demand iterator (stage 2), with runtime CPU feature dispatch across
             AVX2/AVX-512/NEON/RISC-V.
innovation:  Separate "find the structure" from "interpret the values": a vectorized branch-free stage-1 pass
             builds a compact reusable structural index (offsets of all JSON tokens) at GB/s while
             validating UTF-8, so the semantic parse is either deferred per-accessed-field
             (On-Demand) or runs as a tight, well-predicted tape build. The structural skeleton is
             cheap, cacheable, and mmap-friendly.
techniques:
  - Two-stage parse: stage 1 = SIMD structural indexing + UTF-8 validation, stage 2 = tape build
    or lazy On-Demand value walk
  - Prefix-XOR quote masking via PCLMULQDQ carry-less multiply by -1: turns a quote-position
    bitmask into an inside-string region mask in one shot; escaped quotes self-cancel by parity,
    cross-chunk state carried in prev_iter_inside_quote
  - Branch-free byte classification: AVX2 PCMPEQB / NEON VCEQ over 64-byte chunks producing
    structural(S)/whitespace(W)/quote(Q)/string-range(R) bitmasks, then S&=~R, W&=~R to drop
    in-string chars; pseudo-structural starts via P=(S|W)<<1 & ~W & ~R
  - Flat 64-bit tape in document order: type tag in high byte ('c'<<56)+payload, bidirectional
    array/object links (open stores closing index + 24-bit child count, close stores opening
    index) for O(1) container skip
  - Separate string buffer: each string is a 32-bit LE length header + UTF-8 bytes, main tape
    holds only an offset, keeping the tape fixed-stride and scannable
  - On-Demand API: defer number/string/atom validation until a field is accessed via get<T>(),
    giving 2-4x speedups when reading a subset of a large document
  - Runtime CPU dispatch: pick fastest compiled kernel (AVX-512/AVX2/SSE4.2/NEON/RISC-V) at load
    via cpuid, single binary, scalar fallback
  - Mandatory input padding (SIMDJSON_PADDING) so 64-byte SIMD loads never read past the buffer
    end
nest steals: Three concrete steals, all additive within nest-format v1. (1) STRUCTURAL-INDEX SECTION for
             JSON/YAML/Parquet-text ingestion: add a stage-1-style SIMD structural indexer in
             nest-format/src/encoding (new module e.g. structure.rs) that, when nest ingests a
             JSON/YAML doc into chunks_canonical, also emits a compact little-endian u32 offset
             index of structural-character positions as an OPTIONAL section (reserved id 0x09+).
             This reusable mmap-friendly skeleton lets the rust runtime re-walk or sub-cite into
             nested spans (nest://content_hash/chunk_id#json_pointer) without re-parsing, and the
             offset index compresses far better than raw text under zstd. (2) TAPE-STYLE OFFSET
             LAYOUT for chunks_original_spans and future nested-document sections: adopt
             simdjson's separate-buffer pattern (fixed-stride u64 tape of {type-tag, offset} +
             side blob with 32-bit LE length headers). Fixed-stride offset tables are
             mmap-scannable with zero allocation and zstd extremely well because offset deltas are
             low-entropy. (3) RUNTIME DISPATCH PATTERN: nest-runtime/src/simd already does
             AVX2/NEON/scalar dot products; lift simdjson's per-kernel-compiled + cpuid-select
             structure as the template if nest adds a SIMD UTF-8 validator or structural scanner,
             keeping one scalar fallback. Implement in pure rust (no PCLMULQDQ dependency
             required; use a portable carry-less/parity scan to stay deterministic and within
             never-panic/typed-error constraints).
compression: Directly central to the "compress AND serve json/yaml/parquet/datasets" north star. The
             structural-index/tape split lets nest store a parsed-document skeleton ONCE
             (low-entropy offset arrays that zstd crushes) while keeping canonical text byte-exact
             for content_hash stability, and serve sub-document citations and search WITHOUT
             re-parsing on the hot path. Fixed-stride offset tapes + separate length-prefixed
             payload buffers are textbook low-entropy, high-zstd-ratio layouts. On-Demand also
             maps to nest's lazy mmap philosophy: decode only the document subtree a query
             touches.
perf:        Stage 1 ~2-6 GB/s; minification ~6 GB/s; NDJSON ~3.5 GB/s multithreaded; ~75% fewer instructions
             than RapidJSON. PCLMULQDQ has latency ~6 but reciprocal throughput 1 on Skylake, so
             schedule independent work to hide latency; a portable rust parity scan trades
             throughput for full determinism. SIMD loads need buffer padding (read-past-end
             safety) which nest must replicate for true 64-byte scans off mmap (pad section or
             bounds-guard the tail chunk). Parser-buffer reuse is the biggest practical win for
             many small documents.
license:     Apache-2.0 (parts also MIT/Boost); permissive, safe to vendor or reimplement the algorithms.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] serde-rs/serde
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A serialization framework that decouples N Rust data structures from M wire formats through a
             fixed 29-type abstract data model, with compile-time monomorphized derive codegen (no
             reflection, no intermediate Value tree) and lifetime-parameterized zero-copy
             deserialization (Deserialize&lt;'de&gt;) that borrows &amp;str/&amp;[u8] directly out
             of the input buffer.
innovation:  The visitor/data-model split that turns the NxM serialization problem into N+M: every type maps
             itself to one of 29 abstract model types via a Serializer trait, and every format
             implements that model once. Crucially for nest, the deserialize side is
             lifetime-parameterized (Deserialize&lt;'de&gt; with visit_borrowed_str returning
             &amp;'de str) so decoded values are borrowed views into the source bytes with zero
             allocation and zero copy when the encoding permits it. This is exactly the discipline
             nest needs: a section decoder that returns borrowed slices straight off the mmap
             rather than owned Vecs.
techniques:
  - 29-type abstract data model (bool, i8..i128, u8..u128, f32/f64, char, string, byte array,
    option, unit, unit_struct, unit_variant, newtype_struct, newtype_variant, seq, tuple,
    tuple_struct, tuple_variant, map, struct, struct_variant) decoupling N types from M formats
    into N+M impls
  - Visitor pattern: Deserializer drives a Visitor by calling exactly one visit_* method; struct
    keys known at compile time avoid map-style key hashing
  - Zero-copy via Deserialize<'de> lifetime + visit_borrowed_str(&'de str)/visit_borrowed_bytes vs
    transient visit_str/visit_string vs owned visit_string; Cow<'de, str> to fall back to owned
    only when escapes force it
  - Monomorphized compile-time codegen (serde_derive): static dispatch only, no vtables, no
    reflection, no intermediate Value tree, no per-field allocation
  - Three-phase compound encoding (serialize_struct/seq/map -> serialize_field/element -> end())
    letting formats stream output without buffering the whole structure
  - DeserializeSeed / stateful deserialization: thread external state (arenas, interners, capacity
    hints) through the deserialize call without globals
  - Length-aware vs length-free distinction: structs/tuples carry compile-time-known arity so the
    format can omit length prefixes; maps/seqs carry runtime length
nest steals: nest-format/src/sections/codec.rs and the per-section reader (crates/nest-format/src/reader/**)
             should adopt a borrowed decode trait: SectionDecode<'a> returning views (&'a [u8],
             &'a str, &'a [f32]) sliced directly from the mmap'd byte range instead of owned
             buffers. This is the single biggest lever for nest's 'serve straight off disk'
             promise -- chunks_canonical(text) and embeddings (raw/f16) can be returned as
             borrowed slices with zero allocation, mirroring nest-runtime/src/materialize.rs which
             already wants to hand back spans. Concretely: (1) add a content_view(section_id) ->
             Cow<'a, [u8]> on the reader where raw/f16/int8 encodings return Borrowed and only
             zstd returns Owned (decompression unavoidably allocates) -- this makes the
             borrow-vs-copy decision encoding-driven exactly like serde's
             visit_borrowed_str-vs-visit_string; (2) refactor encoding/mod.rs Encoding enum into a
             small visitor-style codec registry where each encoding id (raw/zstd/float16/int8)
             implements one decode(&[u8]) -> view method, keeping the additive encoding-id space
             (4-255 reserved) clean and each codec file under the 300-line cap; (3) thread a
             DeserializeSeed-style state param (out-buffer / scratch reused across calls) through
             int8 dequant in encoding/int8.rs so per-query embedding decode reuses one allocation
             instead of allocating per chunk on the hot search path in nest-runtime/src/search.rs.
             Do NOT pull in the serde crate itself for the frozen container -- the format is
             repr(C) little-endian and must stay dependency-light and byte-deterministic; steal
             the design patterns (borrowed-view codec, encoding-driven Cow, the N+M codec/section
             separation), not the dependency. serde_json/serde_yaml/serde_cbor ARE worth a real
             dependency only in nest-python or a build-time ingest path for parsing input
             json/yaml/parquet-adjacent sources before they become canonical chunks.
compression: Indirect but real. serde is not a compressor and adds no entropy coding, so it does not advance
             the 'compress more than everyone' north star directly. Its relevance is to the SERVE
             half of compress-and-serve: zero-copy borrowed decoding lets nest keep aggressive
             encodings (int8, f16, zstd) while still materializing text/vectors at near-zero cost
             off mmap, so compression does not cost runtime allocation. The encoding-driven Cow
             pattern (borrow when raw/f16/int8, copy only when zstd) is the right architectural
             seam to add MORE codecs later (delta/varint/bitpacking for chunk_ids and spans,
             dictionary coding) without rewriting every section reader -- it makes the codec layer
             extensible, which is a prerequisite for pushing the compression frontier.
perf:        serde's performance comes entirely from monomorphization + static dispatch: the derive generates a
             straight-line specialized function per type, so there is no vtable, no boxing, and
             the optimizer can inline the whole serialize/deserialize path. Zero-copy
             deserialization eliminates the dominant cost in real workloads (string/byte
             allocation and memcpy) -- serde_json borrows &'de str directly when no JSON escapes
             are present, falling back to owned only on escaped strings. No SIMD in serde core
             itself (some downstream JSON parsers like simd-json add it). For nest the lesson is:
             a borrowed-slice decode path keeps the SIMD dot-product hot loop
             (nest-runtime/src/simd) reading f32/int8 lanes straight from mmap with zero
             intervening copy, which is exactly the current design intent -- adopting serde's view
             discipline guarantees no accidental owning allocation creeps into the
             rerank/materialize path.
license:     MIT OR Apache-2.0 (dual-licensed)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] google/flatbuffers
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A zero-copy cross-platform serialization library whose buffers are read in place (mmap-friendly)
             via a vtable indirection scheme, plus a schema-less sibling format (FlexBuffers) that
             auto-narrows every scalar/container to the smallest of 8/16/32/64-bit widths and
             pools strings/keys.
innovation:  Random-access read directly off raw bytes (no parse, no allocation, no copy) achieved through a
             layer of relative offsets plus a shared, deduplicated vtable: many records with
             identical field layout point at ONE vtable, so per-record metadata cost amortizes to
             near zero. The complementary FlexBuffers idea - encode each element at the minimum
             bit-width that fits its value and pool repeated strings/keys - is a self-describing,
             mmap-readable layout that is often smaller than a typed format while still allowing
             O(1) indexed access via a parallel width-tagged offset table.
techniques:
  - vtable indirection: each table starts with a soffset to a vtable [vtable_size, object_size,
    field_offset_0..N]; absent field => offset 0 => return schema default (free optionality, no
    presence bitmap)
  - vtable deduplication/sharing: records with the same present-field set reference one identical
    vtable, so N homogeneous rows cost 1 vtable not N
  - three offset widths: uoffset_t (u32 forward to tables/vectors/strings), soffset_t (signed,
    vtable backref), voffset_t (u16 field slot), little-endian, self-relative so buffers are
    position-independent and DAG-shareable
  - shared subobjects via offset reuse: identical strings/vectors/subtables stored once and
    pointed to by multiple parents (string pooling / structural dedup) without a back-reference
    table
  - FlexBuffers minimum-width packing: a 1-byte type+bitwidth tag per value lets each
    int/float/offset use 8/16/32/64 bits, eliminating fixed-width padding on small values
  - FlexBuffers vectors carry a length prefix + a parallel array of equal-width offsets, enabling
    O(1) random indexing into a schema-less, mmap-resident blob
  - size-prefixed buffers + 4-byte file identifier magic for streaming framing and cheap
    type/format sniffing without a full header parse
  - build-backward construction (high to low address) so child offsets are known before parents
    are written, giving single-pass serialization with no fixups
nest steals: Two concrete steals into nest-format. (1) Offset-table layout for variable-length sections: today
             crates/nest-format/src/sections/{chunk_ids,canonical,spans}.rs write a count prefix
             then a flat run of length-prefixed strings, which forces a sequential scan to reach
             chunk N and gives no random access off mmap. Adapt FlatBuffers' uoffset vector:
             prepend a u32 (or width-narrowed) offset array so nest-runtime materialize/cite can
             jump to chunk N in O(1) directly from the mapped page without decoding earlier
             strings, the read-side enabler for nest://content_hash/chunk_id byte-span reopen. (2)
             Apply FlexBuffers minimum-width thinking as a new additive encoding id (reserved
             4-255 space, no version bump) for the structural/metadata sections only: store
             per-chunk offset/length integers and provenance ints at the smallest width that fits
             (delta + varint or fixed 8/16/32-bit pick), shrinking non-embedding overhead at zero
             decode cost on the hot path. Most impactful for chunk_ids: stop storing sha256 as the
             71-byte ASCII string 'sha256:<64 hex>'; store the 32 raw digest bytes (a 2.2x shrink
             before zstd) and reconstruct the prefix on read, a pure entropy win toward 'compress
             more than everyone'. Keep embeddings untouched (still scored from mmap). The
             vtable-default trick also informs provenance: omit absent optional fields and resolve
             to defaults rather than storing nulls. All of this respects single-file, offline,
             deterministic (offset tables are a pure function of inputs) and the additive format
             rule.
compression: Direct. Two distinct wins: (a) the sha256 raw-bytes-vs-hex-string fix is an immediate ~2.2x
             reduction on the chunk_ids section pre-zstd and removes redundant 'sha256:' prefixes,
             pure entropy reduction. (b) FlexBuffers-style minimum-width integer packing trims
             structural/offset/provenance overhead with zero hot-path decode cost, complementing
             zstd which nest already applies to non-embedding sections. The offset-table idea is
             compression-neutral but unlocks O(1) mmap random access that nest's cite/materialize
             path currently lacks, strategically required to serve compressed corpora without
             full-section decode.
perf:        Zero-copy reads: field access is one vtable lookup + one bounds check + one load, no allocation,
             no temp objects, good cache locality, ideal for mmap. All integers little-endian
             aligned to their own size; structs align to largest member (compiler-independent) so
             a memory-mapped struct can be cast and read in place on x86_64/aarch64 without
             misaligned-access penalties, matching nest's repr(C) + LE-unsigned discipline.
             Tradeoffs: FlexBuffers is slower to read than typed FlatBuffers (per-element
             type/width tag dispatch) and FlatBuffers trades some size for alignment padding; nest
             should apply min-width packing only to cold structural sections, never the embedding
             hot path. No SIMD in the format itself, it is a layout/encoding library, not a
             kernel.
license:     Apache-2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] jzombie/rust-simd-r-drive
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A schema-less, append-only, single-file binary storage engine whose payloads are 64-byte-aligned
             for cache/SIMD efficiency, indexed by an in-memory xxh3_64 -> offset map, recoverable
             by a backward-linked metadata chain, and read fully zero-copy straight off mmap.
innovation:  The "metadata-after-payload backward-linked chain" on-disk layout: each entry stores its payload
             first, then a trailing fixed metadata block (key_hash, prev_offset, crc32c). Because
             every entry points back to the previous entry's tail, the whole keyspace and index
             are reconstructible by scanning from EOF backward to byte 0, and any torn/incomplete
             tail write is auto-discarded by stopping at the last unbroken link. This gives
             crash-safe, index-free-on-disk recovery with a single sequential append being the
             only write path, and 64-byte payload alignment so reads are zero-copy reinterpretable
             as typed SIMD slices.
techniques:
  - 64-byte payload alignment via pad = (A - (prev_tail % A)) & (A-1), A=64, so every payload
    start lands on a cacheline/SIMD boundary for safe &[u8]->&[f32] reinterpretation
  - trailing metadata block per entry: 8B xxh3_64 key hash + 8B prev_offset (absolute tail of
    prior entry) + 4B crc32c payload checksum, forming a backward-traversable linked chain
  - index-free-on-disk: in-memory DashMap<u64,u64> (key_hash -> offset) rebuilt by backward chain
    scan at open; newest-wins so updates/deletes are pure appends
  - 1-byte tombstone (0x00 payload) plus same 20B metadata trailer for O(1) logical deletes
    without rewriting
  - torn-write recovery: load stops at last validly-linked+checksummed entry, truncating partial
    tails automatically
  - lock-free parallel reads off a shared Arc<Mmap>, Mutex/RwLock only guards remap-on-grow,
    AtomicU64 tail pointer
  - EntryHandle: zero-copy &[u8] view into the mmap region, optionally reinterpreted as Apache
    Arrow arrays via arrow feature for columnar typed access
  - explicit compaction replays newest-per-key into a fresh file to reclaim dead/tombstoned space
nest steals: Two concrete steals. (1) nest-format reader recovery: nest v1 has 4 SHA-256 surfaces and a
             pre-footer body, but recovery from a truncated/partially-written container is a weak
             spot for a single-file format. Adopt the backward "stop at last valid section" scan
             as an additive validate mode in nest-format/src/reader (surfaced via nest-cli
             `validate`): walk sections from the footer backward, verify each per-section
             checksum, and report the last intact section boundary without trusting the manifest.
             Additive (no format bump), hardens the offline single-file guarantee against torn
             writes. (2) nest-format/src/layout + nest-runtime hot path: enforce and verify
             64-byte alignment on the embeddings section so the mmap'd f32/f16/int8 vectors can be
             reinterpreted as typed SIMD slices with zero bounds/alignment fixups before AVX2/NEON
             dot products. nest scores embeddings directly from mmap and must never zstd them;
             guaranteeing 64-byte payload alignment is the missing precondition that lets the simd
             dispatcher do aligned loads (vmovaps vs vmovups) without a copy. The pad formula
             pad=(64-(off%64))&63 drops straight into the writer's section placement and stays
             deterministic/reproducible.
compression: Indirect but real. The engine does NO compression (raw bytes, schema-less), so it does not advance
             the "compress more than everyone" north star directly. Its compression relevance is
             structural: 64-byte alignment plus zero-copy typed reinterpretation is the
             precondition that lets you keep embeddings UNCOMPRESSED yet serve them at full SIMD
             speed (validating nest's "never zstd embeddings" choice), and the append-only
             newest-wins chain is a substrate for real-time edit (a north-star feature nest lacks)
             without rewriting the whole container. The companion rust-llkv project (Arrow
             columnar chunks over simd-r-drive EntryHandles) is the more compression-relevant
             idea: column-oriented chunking enables per-column encodings (dictionary, RLE, delta)
             which is where extreme compression of json/parquet/datasets actually comes from.
perf:        xxh3_64 key hashing uses SSE2 (default x86_64) / AVX2 / NEON; benchmark cited at ~1M random 8-byte
             seeks in well under 1s. crc32fast (CRC32C, hardware SSE4.2 crc32 instr) for payload
             integrity, NOT SHA, much cheaper than nest's per-section SHA-256 but
             non-cryptographic; nest must keep SHA for its citation/integrity surfaces, but CRC32C
             is a candidate for a cheap fast-path liveness check during streaming edits. Reads are
             lock-free off Arc<Mmap>; only file-growth remap takes a lock. simd_copy accelerates
             the write-side memcpy into the append buffer. Datasets larger than RAM via mmap
             paging.
license:     Apache-2.0 (per crates.io publication of simd-r-drive); verify before vendoring, compatible with
             permissive reuse.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] simd-lite/simd-json
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust port of simdjson that parses JSON at multi-GB/s by splitting parsing into a SIMD stage1
             (branchless structural-character indexing over 64-byte chunks) and a stage2 that
             walks that index to emit a flat u64 "tape", with zero-copy BorrowedValue referencing
             the input buffer.
innovation:  Two-stage parsing: stage1 uses SIMD to classify every byte in bulk (whitespace/structural via
             pshufb lookup tables) and resolves string interiors with a branchless prefix-XOR
             quote mask computed by carry-less multiply (PCLMULQDQ / vmull_p64) of the quote-bit
             vector against all-ones, then ANDs the complement to drop in-string structurals. The
             result is a dense index of "where things are" so stage2 is near-branchless and
             data-driven. The transferable kernel: turn a per-byte stateful scan (classification,
             region-membership, delimiter finding) into a wide bitmask computation, carrying one
             parity bit between chunks.
techniques:
  - prefix-XOR quote masking via carry-less multiply (PCLMULQDQ on x86, vmull_p64 on NEON) of the
    quote-bit vector by all-ones, carrying 1 parity bit across 64-byte chunks
  - pshufb/shuffle as a 16-entry lookup table to classify bytes into whitespace vs structural
    ({}[]:,) categories in parallel, producing bitmasks
  - branchless masking: AND(structural_mask, NOT(in_string_mask)) to exclude delimiters inside
    strings with no branches
  - flat u64 tape: a single Vec where each element packs a type tag in high bits + offset/length
    payload; the tree is indices into one contiguous buffer (no pointer chasing, mmap-friendly)
  - BorrowedValue zero-copy: string and key slices reference the input buffer directly instead of
    allocating per-token
  - SWAR 8-digits-at-a-time number parsing via one 64-bit multiply-add chain behind a feature flag
  - SIMD UTF-8 validation fused into the scan pass instead of a separate post-pass
  - runtime CPU feature detection selecting AVX2/SSE4.2/NEON/simd128/scalar fallback at dispatch
    time
nest steals: Three concrete steals. (1) bm25 tokenizer at nest-runtime/src/bm25/tokenize.rs is today a scalar
             per-char loop (is_alphanumeric + to_lowercase) which is the documented CJK/Thai gap
             and slow on big corpora; adapt simd-json's stage1 byte-classification to build a
             word-boundary bitmask (ASCII alnum vs separator) over UTF-8 bytes for fast Latin
             chunk-splitting with a scalar fallback for non-ASCII codepoints, wired through the
             existing dispatcher in nest-runtime/src/simd/{avx2,neon,scalar}.rs that already does
             AVX2/NEON/scalar selection. (2) The flat-tape idea maps onto a future structured-data
             ingest path (json/yaml/parquet on the north-star roadmap): store parsed docs as a
             contiguous repr(C) u64 tape (tag-in-high-bits + LE offset/length) as a new RESERVED
             section id (0x09+) in nest-format/src/sections, giving mmap-able, pointer-free,
             zero-copy reopen aligned with content_hash determinism. (3) BorrowedValue zero-copy
             discipline reinforces the chunks_original_spans model and the materialize path in
             nest-runtime/src/materialize.rs (parse-by-reference into the mmap, never materialize
             eagerly). The prefix-XOR carry-less-multiply parity trick is also a reusable
             region-detection kernel for future CSV/JSONL delimiter scanning in the SIMD crate.
compression: Indirect to raw ratio: simd-json is a speed play, not a compressor, so it does not shrink bytes
             directly. But it unlocks the north-star "compress AND serve json/yaml/parquet" goal
             by providing a fast, deterministic, mmap-friendly, pointer-free on-disk parse
             representation (the flat u64 tape) that can be zstd-compressed as a non-embedding
             section while remaining cheaply re-scannable. It also accelerates the bm25
             build/query hot path (a known throughput gap), serving the light/cheap pillar even
             though it does not move the size ratio.
perf:        simdjson family hits multi-GB/s on commodity x86; stage1 processes 64 input bytes per iteration as
             bitmasks. PCLMULQDQ is high-latency (~6 cycles on Skylake) but reciprocal-throughput
             ~1, so simd-json hides it by scheduling independent work after it - relevant if nest
             adopts the carry-less-multiply trick. pshufb classification and prefix-XOR are the
             throughput wins; SWAR number parsing avoids per-digit branches. The flat-Vec tape
             avoids per-node heap allocs and pointer chasing, matching the cache/mmap profile nest
             wants. nest constraints: gate any carry-less-multiply behind runtime detection with a
             scalar fallback (mirrors nest's existing avx2/neon/scalar dispatcher) and keep all
             on-disk tape integers little-endian.
license:     Apache-2.0 / MIT dual (Rust ecosystem standard; upstream simdjson is Apache-2.0). Compatible for
             vendoring or learning into nest.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] cloudflare/mmap-sync
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust single-writer/multi-reader IPC library that combines rkyv zero-copy archives with a
             tmpfs-backed mmap double-buffer and a wait-free RCU/Left-Right swap protocol so
             readers parse data in-place off the mmap with no locks and no copy.
innovation:  A 64-bit packed atomic InstanceVersion (1 bit active-instance index, 39 bits data size up to 549
             GB, 24 bits truncated WyHash checksum) lets a writer atomically publish a new mmap'd
             buffer with a single SeqCst store while readers do wait-free zero-copy access;
             per-instance AtomicU32 reader counters plus a grace_duration timeout drain in-flight
             readers off the idle buffer before it is reused (and forcibly reset to handle crashed
             readers), giving lock-free publish-then-swap without ever blocking the read path.
techniques:
  - InstanceVersion: pack {idx:1 | size:39 | checksum:24} into one u64 via mask/shift, swapped
    with a single AtomicU64 SeqCst store (RCU/Left-Right publish)
  - Double-buffer of two mmap data files plus a 3rd state file; writer always writes the idle
    buffer then flips the active index
  - Per-instance [AtomicU32;2] reader counters: rlock=fetch_add(1,SeqCst),
    unlock=fetch_sub(1,SeqCst); writer waits for idle count==0
  - grace_duration drain: poll-sleep until idle reader count hits 0, else store(0) to reclaim from
    crashed/stalled readers (reset=true)
  - rkyv 0.7 zero-copy archives: serialize_value then archived_root::<T>/check_archived_root::<T>
    to read structs straight off mmap with no deserialize step
  - 24-bit truncated WyHash checksum embedded in the version word for cheap optional integrity
    validation on read (40ns checked vs 27ns unchecked)
  - RAII ReadGuard holds the reader-count increment and decrements on Drop, making reader
    bookkeeping leak-proof
  - tmpfs (/dev/shm) backing to drop mmap I/O latency to RAM speed
nest steals: Two distinct steals. (1) nest-runtime: adopt rkyv-style zero-copy archived structs as the
             in-memory view of mmap'd sections (chunk_ids, provenance, search_contract, hnsw/bm25
             index nodes) so the reader casts repr(C) LE bytes to typed structs with zero parse
             cost on open, formalizing nest's existing mmap-cast pattern in nest-format reader.rs
             and nest-runtime materialize; nest's repr(C)/little-endian invariants already match
             rkyv's archived-layout discipline. (2) Bigger steal: the InstanceVersion +
             double-buffer + grace-period drain protocol as the foundation for nest's missing
             real-time edit / hot-reload. Keep the FROZEN .nest v1 container immutable but add an
             optional local atomic-version sidecar in nest-runtime so a rebuilt container is
             published and queried wait-free while old readers finish on the previous mmap; the
             24-bit-checksum-in-version trick gives O(1) integrity gating per swap. Must stay
             offline/single-file: implement as an in-process atomic-swap layer, never a socket,
             and outside the frozen format. The packed-version u64 idiom also informs encoding
             size+checksum+epoch in one LE manifest/footer word.
compression: Low and indirect. mmap-sync does not compress (pins rkyv 0.7 raw aligned archives, no entropy
             coder, no quantization), so it does not advance the 'compress more than everyone'
             north star directly. Its value is to the SERVE half: it shows the canonical zero-copy
             mmap layout that exposes decoded sections (and never-compressed embeddings scored
             straight off disk) as typed structs at tens of nanoseconds, and its version-word
             checksum is a model for compactly embedding integrity metadata. Enables
             fast/real-time edit and concurrent serving (stated mission goals) without touching
             the codec stack.
perf:        i7-13800H: write 250.7 ns (3.96 Melem/s), read with WyHash checksum validation 40.1 ns (24.9
             Melem/s), read without validation 26.7 ns (37.5 Melem/s). All atomics SeqCst
             (conservative; nest could relax to Acquire/Release on the read fast path).
             Single-writer/multi-reader only. tmpfs backing keeps latency at RAM speed; 39-bit
             size field caps a single buffer at 549 GB.
license:     BSD-3-Clause (Cloudflare standard OSS)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] p-ranav/csv2
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A header-only C++17 CSV reader/writer that mmaps the whole file and lazily iterates cells via
             memchr-driven newline scans plus a branchy scalar quote state machine, hitting ~1
             GB/s zero-copy without materializing the dataset.
innovation:  Treat the on-disk byte buffer as the only data structure: never copy, never pre-tokenize.
             Iteration is fully lazy and offset-based -- a RowIterator finds the next row with one
             memchr('\n') call, and CellIterator only computes a cell's [start,end) span at deref
             time, returning a string_view straight into the mmap. The parsed "table" is just two
             cursors over bytes you already paid to map; cell values are decoded (unescape/trim)
             on demand, so unread columns cost nothing.
techniques:
  - memchr-based row scan: locate next \n in the mmap region with one libc SIMD-accelerated memchr
    call instead of a byte loop
  - Lazy offset-only cell boundaries: CellIterator stores start_/end_ and computes the next
    delimiter span on deref, returning string_view (zero allocation, zero copy)
  - Branchy scalar quote state machine: quote_opened flag + last_quote_location==i-1 test detects
    doubled-quote escapes; delimiter ignored while inside quotes
  - Compile-time dialect via templates: delimiter<','>, quote_character<'"'>,
    first_row_is_header<>, trim_policy -- monomorphized so the hot loop has no runtime config
    branches
  - mmap-the-whole-file zero-copy I/O via the bundled header-only mio wrapper (page-aligned,
    cross-platform POSIX/Windows, MIT)
  - Decode-on-demand value path: read_view() returns raw span; read_value() applies trim+unescape
    only when the caller wants the string
  - Naive append-only Writer: ostream_iterator + delimiter concatenation, no quoting/escaping by
    design (caller owns CSV-safety)
nest steals: Two concrete lifts. (1) nest-runtime materialize path + nest-cli: adopt csv2's lazy-offset cursor
             model for any row/columnar section we mmap. Today nest scores embeddings off mmap;
             csv2 proves we can also serve text-table payloads (csv/tsv/dataset rows the
             north-star wants to ingest) as &str slices into the mapped file with zero decode
             until a row is cited, mirroring our chunks_canonical/chunks_original_spans span
             discipline. (2) Ingestion-side tokenizer in nest-python build: replace any
             byte-by-byte CSV/field scanner with the Rust memchr crate (already SIMD on AVX2/NEON,
             deterministic) for row splitting, plus a small monomorphized delimiter/quote state
             machine instead of regex -- speeds import of the PT-BR fake-news corpora and future
             parquet/json/yaml dataset fronts. The decode-on-demand idea also reinforces
             nest-format: store cheap text columns as raw offset spans (like
             chunks_original_spans) so zstd table sections decompress lazily per accessed cell
             rather than whole-section, leaving the embedding hot path untouched.
compression: Low-to-indirect. csv2 is a speed/zero-copy parser, not a compressor -- no entropy coder, no
             quantization, no columnar dictionary encoding, so it does not advance 'compress more
             than everyone' directly. Its value is structural: the lazy-offset, decode-on-demand
             discipline lets a zstd-compressed table section in nest-format be unpacked one
             cell/row at a time rather than wholesale, lowering working-set memory during ingest
             and preserving the mmap-friendly layout all of nest's encodings depend on. It teaches
             layout-for-compression, not a new codec.
perf:        ~0.9-1.0 GB/s sustained cell iteration on an i9-11900KF (16 GB file in 16.5s; 11 GB / 262M rows in
             10.8s). No explicit SIMD intrinsics in csv2 itself -- throughput comes from glibc
             memchr (hand-vectorized SSE/AVX), zero per-row/cell allocation, and template
             monomorphization stripping config branches from the inner loop. Footprint is
             essentially page cache for the mapped file plus two cursor structs; no parse tree, no
             row vector. Quote handling is fully scalar/branchy, so quote-heavy CSVs lose
             throughput -- a ceiling a true SIMD parser (simdcsv / Mison structural-index bitmaps)
             would beat.
license:     MIT (csv2 and the bundled mio mmap header are both MIT)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MUST] facebookresearch/faiss
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        FAISS is a C++/Python library of composable ANN indexes and vector codecs (PQ, additive
             quantizers, scalar quantization, IVF, HNSW) whose distinguishing feature is extreme
             lossy compression of vectors into short codes that remain searchable directly via
             SIMD lookup-table kernels, with a built-in two-stage approximate-then-rerank search
             contract.
innovation:  PQ4 FastScan: quantize each vector into M 4-bit product-quantization codes, lay the codes out in
             interleaved 32-vector blocks (bbs=32, 2 sub-quantizers per 32-byte block), quantize
             the per-query distance lookup tables to 8-bit integers, and evaluate distances with
             SIMD byte-shuffle instructions (AVX2 pshufb / NEON vqtbl) accumulating in 16-bit
             lanes -- a 16-entry LUT lives entirely in a vector register and 32 candidate
             distances are computed per pair of shuffles, with no RAM lookups on the hot path.
             Pair this with a mandatory exact rerank of the top k*k_factor candidates to recover
             the accuracy lost to 4-bit quantization. This is the same
             approximate-then-exact-rerank contract nest already enforces, applied to a far more
             aggressive code than int8.
techniques:
  - PQ4 FastScan: 4-bit product quantization codes (16 centroids/subquantizer) packed in
    interleaved 32-vector blocks for register-resident SIMD shuffle lookups
  - 8-bit-quantized lookup tables with formula A*(dist-B), 16-bit non-saturating accumulators,
    even/odd sub-quantizer split to avoid AVX cross-lane ops (SCANN-derived)
  - Additive quantizers: Residual Quantizer (greedy beam-search encoding) and Local Search
    Quantizer (simulated annealing); x = sum of M codebook entries, beat PQ accuracy at 64-128
    bits
  - use_beam_LUT distance decomposition into precomputable norm/cross-product/query tables to
    avoid explicit residual computation during encoding
  - Scalar quantization tiers SQ8/SQ6/SQ4/SQfp16 as a simple per-dimension uniform codec (nest has
    int8/f16, missing 4-bit and 6-bit)
  - OPQ / random-rotation / Hadamard transform (O(d log d)) preprocessing to decorrelate
    dimensions before PQ, raising recall at fixed code size
  - Composable index-factory string grammar (e.g. OPQ16_64,IVF4096,PQ16x4fs,RFlat) describing
    transform + coarse quantizer + encoding + refine as one declarative recipe
  - RFlat/Refine/RefinePanorama two-stage retrieve-then-rerank with early pruning during the exact
    reranking phase
nest steals: Two concrete steals. (1) Add a PQ codec to nest-format encoding (a new RESERVED encoding id in
             4-255, additive-only, no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump): store learned codebooks plus
             4-bit codes for the embeddings section. Codebooks are tiny (M*16*subdim f32) and
             codes are ~M*0.5 bytes/vector, beating int8's d bytes/vector by 4-16x -- directly
             serving the north-star goal on the largest section. Determinism is preserved by
             fixing k-means seed and Lloyd iteration count and storing codebooks in the manifest,
             so the build stays reproducible (same chunks + model fingerprint => identical
             codebooks => byte-identical file). (2) In nest-runtime's simd dispatcher add a
             pq4_fastscan kernel mirroring nest's existing AVX2/NEON/scalar trifurcation:
             interleaved 32-vector block layout + pshufb/vqtbl LUT shuffle to score PQ codes
             straight off mmap, feeding candidates into the EXISTING mandatory exact-cosine rerank
             (nest already does this for hnsw/hybrid), so search_contract semantics are unchanged
             -- returned score stays real cosine, PQ is only a candidate filter. This makes a new
             'nano' preset feasible (zstd text + pq4 embeddings + hnsw), pushing well below the
             tiny preset's 0.283 ratio while exact rerank holds recall. Keep files under 300 loc
             by splitting codec (nest-format/src/encoding/pq.rs) from kernel
             (nest-runtime/src/simd/pq4.rs).
compression: Directly central. PQ4 takes embeddings (nest's dominant byte cost) from int8's d bytes/vector to
             ~M/2 bytes/vector -- a 4-16x reduction on the section nest deliberately leaves
             uncompressed because it must be SIMD-scored from mmap. FastScan's whole point is that
             the compressed code IS the searchable representation via register-resident LUTs,
             dissolving nest's current tension between compressing embeddings and scoring them
             directly off disk. Additive quantizers (RQ/LSQ) offer an even-higher-accuracy code at
             64-128 bits for a future tier. This is the single most impactful lever toward beating
             competitors on size while keeping the offline/single-file/deterministic guarantees.
perf:        FastScan reaches ~1M QPS without rerank and ~280k QPS with rerank (cited 2x faster than HNSW at
             2.7x less memory since no graph is stored). Critical caveat: the
             4-bit/256-bit-register kernel is AVX2-tuned; ARM NEON needs the
             two-128-bit-registers-bundled-as-256 trick (arXiv:2203.02505, Matsui et al.) to match
             -- relevant since nest targets aarch64 NEON as a first-class path. LUT must be
             quantized to 8-bit because shuffle operates on small ints, and accumulation must be
             16-bit to avoid overflow. Scalar fallback works but emulates vector registers in
             memory and is very slow, so nest's scalar tier should keep a plain unpacked PQ loop
             rather than emulating the block layout.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MUST] antgroup/vsag
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        VSAG is Ant Group's production C++ graph-ANN framework (HNSW/HGraph/IVF/SINDI) whose standout
             component is RaBitQ binary quantization, a randomized 1-bit-per-dimension codec with
             a provably unbiased distance estimator and a popcount-based SIMD hot path.
innovation:  RaBitQ: quantize each L2-normalized, centroid-centered, randomly-rotated vector to its sign bits
             (1 bit/dimension), then estimate the true inner product with an UNBIASED estimator
             (error O(1/sqrt(D)), asymptotically optimal) using only bitwise AND + popcount
             instead of float math or PQ lookup tables. The query is split into Bq bit-planes
             (default 4) so the dot product becomes a weighted popcount sum: result =
             1*popcount(code&q1) + 2*popcount(code&q2) + 4*popcount(code&q3) +
             8*popcount(code&q4). Unlike PQ it needs zero training/codebook learning and has a
             real theoretical error bound, which is exactly the guarantee a deterministic,
             sovereign database needs.
techniques:
  - RaBitQ 1-bit binary quantization: random orthogonal rotation (Johnson-Lindenstrauss) then sign
    extraction -> D-bit code per vector
  - Unbiased distance estimator <o_bar,q>/<o_bar,o> with per-vector precomputed factor <o_bar,o> +
    per-vector norm + dataset centroid stored as f32 side metadata
  - Popcount bit-product distance: AND + popcount over bit-planes weighted by 2^j (AVX512VPOPCNTDQ
    / NEON CNT, 1-cycle ops, ~3x faster than PQ LUT)
  - Bq-bit query decomposition (default 4 bit-planes) trading accuracy vs speed without
    re-encoding the database
  - Extended/multi-bit RaBitQ: binary MSB code first for coarse estimate, ex-code refines
    incrementally; 4/5/7 bits -> 90/95/99% recall
  - FastScan SIMD layout: transposed bit-plane packing for batched quantizer scans
  - SQ4/SQ8 scalar quantization and fp16/bf16 SIMD operators as a tiered quantizer ladder
  - Automatic build-parameter generation from vector dimension and dataset scale
nest steals: Add a new additive encoding id (reserve id=4 in the 4-255 range) `rabitq` in
             nest-format/src/encoding/rabitq.rs, mirroring the existing int8.rs layout: versioned
             u32 prefix, then per-vector side data (f32 norm + f32 <o_bar,o> factor + one shared
             f32 centroid block + the seeded rotation), then packed D-bit sign codes row-major.
             Because nest mandates an exact-cosine rerank in nest-runtime/src/search, RaBitQ only
             ranks the candidate SET; the returned score stays real cosine, so RaBitQ's ANN-proxy
             nature never violates the search contract. Add a dot_rabitq_popcount kernel to
             nest-runtime/src/simd (avx2.rs popcount/LUT, neon.rs vcnt, scalar.rs u64::count_ones)
             dispatched from simd/mod.rs alongside dot_f32_i8. Determinism is preserved by seeding
             the rotation from the model fingerprint (same chunks + model => byte-identical
             rotation => byte-identical codes), matching nest's reproducible-build and
             deterministic-hnsw rules. Wire into writer/encoding_choice.rs and a new bq/micro
             preset.
compression: Directly serves the north star: RaBitQ is 1 bit/dimension = 32x smaller than f32 and 8x smaller
             than nest's current int8 embeddings, the single largest section in any vector
             container. It is the most aggressive embedding compression with a real error bound
             (not heuristic), so it can push nest's tiny preset below its current 0.283 size ratio
             while nest's mandatory exact rerank keeps recall@10 near-lossless. Clearest path to
             compress embeddings more than everyone without sacrificing
             deterministic/sovereign/single-file guarantees.
perf:        RaBitQ distance estimation reduces to AND+popcount, ~1 cycle on Intel IceLake+/AMD Zen4+ via
             AVX512VPOPCNTDQ (NEON CNT on aarch64), benchmarks ~3x faster than PQ RAM table
             lookups at equal accuracy. Codes are D bits (vs PQ 2D default) so smaller AND faster.
             No training pass so encode time is comparable to scalar quantization. Popcount kernel
             is mmap-friendly: codes are dense contiguous bytes scored straight off disk like
             nest's current i8 hot path. Side metadata is tiny (few f32/vector + one shared
             centroid + deterministic rotation).
license:     Apache-2.0 (VSAG); RaBitQ reference impl (gaoj0017 / VectorDB-NTU RaBitQ-Library) is permissive
             academic code from a published SIGMOD 2024 paper, cleanly reimplementable in Rust
             without vendoring C++.

[MUST] zilliztech/knowhere
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Knowhere is the C++ vector-search core of Milvus, a unified index/config factory wrapping a
             Zilliz-optimized FAISS fork plus hnswlib and DiskANN, exposing
             HNSW/IVF/DiskANN/IVF_RABITQ indexes with SQ1-SQ8, PQ, and 1-bit RaBitQ quantization
             over a templated fp32/fp16/bf16/int8/bin1 operand type system with AVX512-VPOPCNTDQ
             and ARM-SVE SIMD kernels.
innovation:  RaBitQ 1-bit quantization: random-orthogonal-rotate a centroid-normalized vector, take the sign of
             each dimension to get a D-bit code (1 bit/dim, no codebook), store 2 small per-vector
             float scalars, and recover a provably-unbiased cosine/distance estimate with
             O(1/sqrt(D)) error bound using only bitwise-AND + popcount against a 4-bit-decomposed
             query, then exact-rerank a refine_k-larger candidate pool with higher precision. This
             is the textbook recipe for "compress harder than int8 yet still return real cosine
             after rerank" that nest already half-implements.
techniques:
  - RaBitQ: random orthogonal rotation + sign() = 1-bit-per-dim code, no stored codebook (1536-dim
    vector -> 192 bytes)
  - Two precomputed per-vector float scalars give an unbiased distance estimator with O(1/sqrt(D))
    error bound
  - Asymmetric distance: data 1-bit, query in Bq=4 bit-planes; estimate =
    1*popcount(code&q1)+2*popcount(code&q2)+4*popcount(code&q3)+8*popcount(code&q4)
  - AVX512 VPOPCNTDQ and ARM SVE popcount kernels for the bitwise hot path; ~3x faster than PQ LUT
    lookups
  - Incremental refinement: split code into MSB binary-code + ex-code, coarse estimate first then
    refine bits on demand
  - DiskANN dynamic PQ+Refine: doubles K each round to emulate range search, switches to PQ+Refine
    when >x% of bits set
  - centroid-relative normalization (global or per-cluster centroid) to improve local 1-bit
    encoding accuracy
  - templated operand type system (fp32/fp16/bf16/int8/bin1/sparse) with compile-time feature-flag
    typeCheck and MockData->fp32 promotion
nest steals: Add a RaBitQ-style 1-bit codec as a new encoding id in nest-format (encoding ids 4-255 are
             RESERVED in v1, so this is additive and does NOT bump NEST_FORMAT_VERSION): apply it
             ONLY to the embeddings section, storing a deterministic seeded random rotation (or a
             fixed Hadamard/structured rotate to avoid storing a dense matrix), the packed D-bit
             sign codes, plus 2 little-endian f32 scalars per vector in a repr(C) struct. This
             becomes a new preset below "tiny" -- call it "atom" -- pushing the 0.283 size ratio
             toward ~0.05-0.08 for the embedding bytes (1 bit/dim vs int8's 8 bit/dim). In
             nest-runtime, the simd dispatcher (currently AVX2/NEON/scalar dot) gains a
             bitwise-AND+popcount path (AVX2 has no native VPOPCNTDQ, so use the SSSE3/AVX2
             nibble-LUT popcount or vcntq_u8 on NEON) to produce the 1-bit estimate cheaply, then
             -- critically -- nest's MANDATORY exact-cosine rerank stays exactly as is: the 1-bit
             codes are pure candidate generation, the returned score is still real cosine read
             from a small materialized f32/f16 refine set, preserving the search_contract
             guarantee that score is never an ann proxy. Keep determinism by seeding the rotation
             and using alphabetical/stable tie-breaks. Each nest-runtime file stays <=300 lines by
             isolating the codec (rabitq_codec.rs) from the popcount kernel (simd/popcount.rs).
compression: Directly central to the north star of "compress data more than everyone." RaBitQ is the single
             most compact published vector encoding that still supports search: 1 bit/dimension
             with NO codebook and a proven error bound, an 8x shrink over nest's current int8
             embedding mode and ~32x over f32. Because nest already mandates an exact-cosine
             rerank, it gets RaBitQ's recall recovery essentially for free -- the risky part of
             1-bit quantization (lossy scores) is already neutralized by nest's architecture. This
             is the highest-leverage compression idea available for the embeddings section.
perf:        Bitwise-AND+popcount distance is ~3x faster than PQ LUT-in-RAM scans and runs near 1 cycle/word on
             VPOPCNTDQ hardware. On nest's targets: x86_64 AVX2 lacks VPOPCNTDQ so use the Mula
             nibble-shuffle popcount (pshufb LUT) for ~16-32 bytes/iter; aarch64 NEON uses
             vcntq_u8 + horizontal add, which is excellent. The 1-bit codes are tiny and
             mmap-friendly (192 bytes for a 1536-d vector), so far more candidates fit in cache,
             improving candidate generation before rerank. Asymmetric 4-bit query keeps full query
             info while data stays 1-bit. Memory: 2 extra f32/vector overhead (8 bytes) is
             negligible vs the bit savings.
license:     Knowhere is Apache-2.0; the vendored FAISS fork is MIT. The original RaBitQ reference
             implementation (VectorDB-NTU/RaBitQ-Library, formerly gaoj0017/RaBitQ) is
             permissively licensed and the algorithm is published (SIGMOD 2024, arXiv:2405.12497)
             -- safe to reimplement from the paper in clean Rust without copying GPL/AGPL code.

[MUST] criteo/autofaiss
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Python wrapper over FAISS that auto-selects and tunes a KNN index (the faiss factory string +
             nlist + PQ subquantizers + nprobe/efSearch) from nb_vectors, dimension, a strict
             memory budget, and a query-latency target, via threshold heuristics plus binary
             search against measured recall.
innovation:  A closed-form memory/compression cost model for Product Quantization (each vector costs pq + 8
             bytes; compression ratio = 4*dim / (pq+8)) that lets you invert a target memory
             budget into a quantization scheme deterministically, then a learned OPQ rotation that
             decorrelates dimensions before PQ so the same byte budget buys higher recall. The
             compression target drives codec choice, not the other way around.
techniques:
  - Product Quantization (PQ): split D-dim vector into m subvectors, each coded to one of 256
    (8-bit) centroids learned by k-means; vector stored as m bytes; distance via asymmetric
    distance computation (ADC) over precomputed lookup tables
  - OPQ: a learned orthonormal rotation matrix applied before PQ (factory 'OPQ{m}_{y}',
    y=(min(dim//pq,6)+1)*pq) that minimizes quantization distortion by aligning subspaces to the
    PQ grid
  - PQ-budget inversion: choose m from pq_values=[256,128,64,48,32,24,16,8,4] such that
    (4*dim)/(pq+8) >= target_compression_ratio = 4*N*dim / max_memory_bytes
  - nlist heuristic: nb_clusters = 4*sqrt(N) rounded to nearest power of 2 (<1M); fixed ladders
    for larger N; switch to IVF{nlist}_HNSW32 coarse quantizer when nlist>=1000
  - k-means train-size heuristic: ~100 points/cluster (floor 31, range 30*nlist..256*nlist),
    2^code_size*1000 for PQ codebooks
  - Two-phase greedy tuning: binary_search_on_param over nprobe/efSearch to find min param hitting
    99% neighbor coverage, then maximize recall under a max_speed_ms wall-clock bound
  - Index-type decision tree by data scale: flat brute force <1000 vectors, no quantization
    <10000, IVF-Flat <1M, IVF-PQ/OPQ above, gated by a should_be_memory_mappable flag
  - Memory-mappable IVF index path: index built and served from disk via mmap with optional
    direct-map for reconstruction (~8 bytes/vec id overhead)
nest steals: Two concrete steals. (1) Add a PQ/OPQ embedding codec to nest-format encoding.rs as a new RESERVED
             encoding id (additive within v1): store per-vector m-byte codes plus a small codebook
             (new section 0x09+ or folded into search_contract). This is nest's path past int8's
             hard 4x ceiling toward 16-100x embedding compression (m=32 on a 768-dim f32 vector is
             ~96x). It does NOT break nest's 'score f32 from mmap on the hot path' rule because
             nest already runs a MANDATORY exact-cosine rerank: use PQ ADC tables in nest-runtime
             as the cheap ANN candidate generator (alongside hnsw), then rerank top-k against
             full-precision vectors so the returned score stays real cosine. PQ codes are
             mmap-friendly: ADC is a gather over a small in-cache codebook. (2) Port the
             budget->codec inversion and nlist=4*sqrt(N) power-of-2 heuristic into nest-python's
             builder so the user passes a target .nest size or recall floor and the builder
             deterministically solves codec+params, replacing the fixed
             exact/compressed/tiny/hybrid presets. The binary-search nprobe/efSearch tuner adapts
             directly to deterministic HNSW efSearch selection feeding search_contract. Keep
             determinism by seeding PQ/OPQ k-means and emitting codebooks in fixed order so same
             chunks+model => byte-identical file.
compression: Directly central. PQ+OPQ is the single highest-leverage embedding compression technique nest
             lacks: it breaks the int8 4x ceiling and reaches 16-100x on the embeddings section,
             which is usually the largest part of a .nest file. nest's mandatory exact rerank in
             search_contract is exactly what makes lossy PQ safe (returned score stays real
             cosine), so this slots into the design instead of fighting it. The budget-driven
             codec inversion turns 'compress more than everyone' into a solvable optimization
             instead of fixed presets.
perf:        PQ ADC computes a distance by summing m table lookups (one per subquantizer) from a 256*m
             precomputed query-distance table that fits in L1/L2; a gather+accumulate that is
             SIMD-friendly (faiss PQ4 fast-scan packs 32 codes and uses shuffle-based lookups).
             Per-vector storage is exactly m bytes (+8 byte id) vs 4*dim for f32. OPQ adds a
             one-time D*D matmul per query (negligible). Build cost is the tradeoff: k-means over
             training subvectors (~100 points/cluster) plus optional Spark distribution for >RAM
             data; autofaiss reports a 10GB index from a 1TB/200M-image corpus at 10ms query in
             ~3h.
license:     Apache-2.0

[MUST] RyanCodrai/turbovec
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust+PyO3 flat-scan ANN index implementing Google Research's TurboQuant: a data-oblivious
             (training-free) quantizer that random-rotates unit vectors so every coordinate
             follows a known Beta/Gaussian marginal, then applies precomputed Lloyd-Max 2/4-bit
             scalar quantization plus a per-vector length-renormalization scalar, scored directly
             in the compressed domain with FastScan-style nibble-LUT SIMD (AVX-512BW/AVX2/NEON).
innovation:  Data-oblivious quantization: because a single fixed random orthogonal rotation makes each
             coordinate's distribution input-independent (Beta marginal converging to N(0,1/d)),
             the quantizer boundaries can be PRECOMPUTED once from (dimension, bit-width) alone --
             no k-means, no codebook training, no per-corpus fit, no rebuild on insert. This
             collapses "good ANN compression" from a stateful trained artifact into a pure
             deterministic function of dim+bits, while a cheap per-vector scalar (||v|| /
             <u,x_hat>) restores inner-product unbiasedness that scalar quantization otherwise
             loses.
techniques:
  - TurboQuant pipeline: normalize (store ||v|| as f32) -> fixed random orthogonal rotation ->
    2/4-bit Lloyd-Max scalar quantization tuned for the Beta marginal -> bit-pack
  - Length-renormalized scoring: store ||v|| / <u, x_hat> per vector to debias the inner-product
    estimate at zero extra runtime cost (adapted from RaBitQ, SIGMOD 2024)
  - TQ+ per-coordinate calibration: fit one shift+scale per coordinate from empirical 5/95%
    quantiles on the FIRST add, then freeze, mapping data onto the canonical Beta marginal
  - FastScan-style SIMD scoring: rotate query once into codebook domain, score directly against
    packed nibbles using nibble-split lookup tables + u16 accumulators at 32-vector block
    granularity, no decompression of DB vectors
  - Runtime SIMD dispatch: AVX-512BW primary, AVX2 (x86-64-v3) fallback gated by
    is_x86_feature_detected!, hand-written NEON on ARM
  - Lazy deterministic init via std::sync::OnceLock for rotation matrix, Lloyd-Max centroids, and
    SIMD-blocked layout (one-time, lock-free reads after)
  - Filtered search honored inside the kernel: ID allowlist / slot bitmask drops non-allowed slots
    at heap-insert per 32-vector block, no over-fetch, no recall penalty
  - IdMapIndex: stable external u64 IDs surviving deletes with O(1) removal, persisted as .tvim
    alongside the flat .tq index
nest steals: Add a new embedding codec in nest-format/src/encoding (alongside raw/zstd/f16/int8), reserving one
             of the additive encoding ids 4-255: a "turboquant2bit"/"turboquant4bit" codec that
             stores, in the embeddings section, (a) a header with dim+bitwidth (rotation+codebook
             fully derived from these, so nothing per-corpus persisted), (b) bit-packed quantized
             codes, (c) a per-vector f32 length-renorm scalar, and (d) per-vector ||v||.
             nest-runtime/src/simd gains a nibble-LUT FastScan kernel beside the existing
             AVX2/NEON dot-product dispatcher, scoring compressed codes straight off the mmap on
             the hot path -- exactly nest's "score embeddings directly from mmap, never decode"
             rule, which today blocks zstd on embeddings. The mandatory exact-cosine rerank in
             nest-runtime search stays unchanged and absorbs the small recall loss, so the
             returned score is still real cosine. This unlocks ~16x embedding compression (6144B
             f32 -> 384B at 2-bit for d=1536) vs int8's 4x, advancing the "tiny" preset and the
             compress-more-than-everyone north star. Determinism holds because the rotation is a
             fixed seeded function of dim+bits (compatible with nest's seeded-hnsw rule), so
             builds stay byte-identical. Keep each new file <=300 loc, typed thiserror, no panics.
compression: Directly central. Embeddings are nest's largest incompressible section (zstd is banned on them
             because they're scored from mmap). TurboQuant breaks that wall: 16x embedding
             compression at 2-bit AND scoring in the compressed domain, so it compresses the one
             section nest currently cannot, without violating the no-decode-on-hot-path
             constraint. At 10M vectors it shrinks 31GB f32 to ~4GB. This is the single biggest
             available lever on nest's "most compact AI database in the world" goal, far beyond
             the current int8 (4x) tiny preset.
perf:        Hand-written kernels beat FAISS IndexPQFastScan by 12-20% on Apple M3 Max NEON and win most 4-bit
             configs by 1-6% on Intel Sapphire Rapids (within ~1% on 2-bit single-threaded).
             Recall at R@1 vs FAISS IndexPQ: +0.4 to +3.4pp on OpenAI d=1536/3072, slightly
             trailing (~1.2pp) on low-dim GloVe d=200 2-bit; high-dim recall converges to 1.0 by
             k=4. Lloyd-Max distortion within 2.7x of the Shannon rate-distortion lower bound.
             Flat scan (no graph/IVF), so O(N) per query -- best paired with HNSW for large N
             (nest already has hnsw_index 0x07; the two compose: HNSW for candidate gen,
             TurboQuant codes for compact storage + fast block scoring, exact rerank on top).
             OnceLock caches make repeated queries lock-free; rayon for parallel scan. Deps:
             ndarray, rayon, faer, rand.
license:     MIT

[MUST] konjoai/vectro
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Mojo-first (with a Rust `squish_quant` extension, MIT-licensed) embedding-compression + HNSW
             library offering 7 quantization codecs (INT8, NF4, PQ, Binary, RQ, learned
             autoencoder, kurtosis-routed AutoQuantize) wrapped in a self-describing VQZ container
             with optional ZSTD/zlib second-pass.
innovation:  A "codec menu + rerank" architecture: store embeddings in one of several aggressive lossy
             quantizations (down to 1-bit binary at 32x), use cheap quantized-space distances
             (Hamming XOR+POPCOUNT, PQ asymmetric distance via precomputed lookup tables) as a
             coarse prefilter, then recover real accuracy with an exact rerank pass. The two
             highest-leverage codecs for nest are NF4 (8x, fixed information-theoretically-optimal
             16-level codebook so it stays deterministic) and Binary+ADC PQ (16-32x), both layered
             UNDER a generic ZSTD second pass.
techniques:
  - NF4: 4-bit normal-float with a fixed 16-value codebook at equiprobable N(0,1) quantiles
    (-1.0,-0.6962,...,0,...,0.723,1.0), per-block absmax scale, two codes packed per byte, decoded
    via a branchless decision tree
  - PQ with ADC (asymmetric distance computation): 96 subspaces x 256 centroids = 96 bytes/768-dim
    vec (32x); query distances precomputed into a 96x256 lookup table then summed per candidate
  - Binary 1-bit packing: sign(v) -> 8 dims/byte, Hamming distance via XOR+POPCOUNT (>=50M vec/s),
    used as coarse prefilter then exact-rerank to recover ~0.95 recall@10
  - Residual Quantization: 3-pass cascade where each pass quantizes the residual of the previous,
    ~0.98 cosine at 10x
  - NF4-mixed / outlier-aware: outlier dims stored as FP16, remaining dims as NF4
  - Kurtosis-routed AutoQuantize: pick codec (INT8/NF4/PQ/Binary) from per-tensor data statistics
    to hit a target_cosine/target_compression
  - VQZ container: 64-byte header (magic, version, blake2b checksum) + optional ZSTD/zlib
    second-pass OVER the already-quantized payload (INT8 4x then ZSTD ~1.6x ~= 6.4x)
  - assume_normalized fast path + fused single-pass NEON/AVX2 kernel: skip absmax scan for
    L2-normalized embeddings (~1.4x), eliminate the second L2 traffic pass for d<=4096
nest steals: nest-format: add NF4 as encoding id 4 (PQ as id 5, binary as id 6) to nest-format's encoding codec
             set, applied ONLY to the embeddings section. Today nest stops at int8 (0.283 ratio,
             tiny preset); NF4 at 8x and PQ at 32x would push past competitors on the embedding
             bytes, the dominant payload. NF4's codebook is a FIXED constant table, so
             encode/decode stays byte-deterministic and additive (ids 4-255 reserved-in-v1, no
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump). nest-runtime: vectro proves embeddings can be scored in
             quantized space without full dequant -- add a binary-Hamming or PQ-ADC prefilter in
             nest-runtime's simd dispatcher to cut candidates cheaply, slotting UNDER nest's
             mandatory exact-cosine rerank (rerank already guarantees the returned score is real
             cosine, so a lossy prefilter changes recall, never the contract). The fused
             single-pass / assume_normalized trick directly optimizes the existing AVX2/NEON
             kernels. Boundary to re-examine: nest's rule 'never zstd the embeddings (scored off
             mmap on the hot path)' -- vectro's ADC/Hamming path scores the QUANTIZED bytes
             directly, so a quantized-but-not-zstd'd embeddings section keeps mmap-direct scoring
             while still hitting 8-32x.
compression: Directly central. Embeddings are nest's largest section; nest tops out at int8 (4x) while vectro
             ships NF4 (8x), PQ (32x), Binary (32x), RQ (10x), then stacks a generic ZSTD second
             pass (~6.4x compound for INT8 alone). Adopting NF4+PQ as embedding codecs is the
             single biggest lever to 'compress data more than everyone.' Codebooks are
             fixed/deterministic and the lossy distortion is absorbed by nest's mandatory exact
             rerank, so extreme compression is nearly free against the recall contract.
perf:        Claimed INT8 ~12M vec/s (Mojo SIMD, d=768, '4.85x FAISS'); Python/NumPy fallback ~167-210K vec/s;
             Binary Hamming >=50M vec/s; VQZ save/load >=2GB/s. SIMD: NEON 32-wide unroll, AVX2
             fused kernels, AVX-512-VNNI/SME2 stubbed, Apple AMX/Accelerate (vDSP_vsmsa) for
             d>=256, Rayon block-coarsening BLOCK=64 rows/task. Numbers are author marketing
             claims, unverified; the Hamming >=50M figure is plausible (XOR+POPCOUNT) and most
             relevant to a nest prefilter. Accuracy/size: INT8 4x cos>=0.9999, NF4 8x cos>=0.985,
             PQ-96 32x cos>=0.95, Binary 32x ~0.80 cos but >=0.95 recall@10 with rerank.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] nmslib/hnswlib
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Header-only C++ implementation of HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World) graphs with a flat
             byte-packed single-allocation graph layout, runtime-dispatched SIMD distance kernels
             (SSE/AVX/AVX512), and incremental insert/update/delete with deleted-slot reuse.
innovation:  The level-0 graph is stored as ONE contiguous flat byte array where each node is a fixed-stride
             record interleaving [linklist | vector | label] (size_data_per_element_ = maxM0*4 + 4
             + data_size + sizeof(label)), so a neighbor lookup, a vector fetch, and a label read
             are all a single base+offset index into mmap-able memory with zero pointer chasing --
             the antithesis of nest's current Vec<Vec<u32>> adjacency. Combined with a reusable
             per-query visited-list pool (epoch-tagged array reset by bumping a generation tag
             instead of clearing) this gives allocation-free graph traversal.
techniques:
  - Flat single-malloc level-0 layout: fixed-stride record per node = [maxM0*tableint links +
    linklistsizeint count][data_size vector bytes][labeltype], offsets offsetLevel0_=0 /
    offsetData_=size_links_level0_ / label_offset_ computed once
  - Separate linkLists_ char** for upper layers (sparse: only allocated when getRandomLevel>0,
    size_links_per_element_=maxM*4+4 per level), keeping the hot level-0 array dense
  - VisitedListPool: epoch-tagged visited array (visited_array_tag bumped per query, full reset
    only on overflow) eliminates per-query HashSet allocation and clearing
  - Runtime SIMD dispatch by CPU capability AND dim divisibility: InnerProductSIMD16Ext (AVX512
    _mm512_fmadd_ps + _mm512_reduce_add_ps / AVX _mm256_fmadd_ps / SSE) plus *ExtResiduals
    variants that SIMD the aligned prefix then scalar-tail the remainder
  - Exponential level assignment: level = floor(-ln(U)*mult_), mult_=1/ln(M); deterministic if RNG
    seeded
  - Neighbor selection heuristic (Algorithm 4 / getNeighborsByHeuristic2): keep a candidate only
    if it is closer to query than to any already-selected neighbor (diversity prune), not just
    top-M nearest
  - In-place deletion via DELETE_MARK bit in the linklistsizeint, plus allow_replace_deleted to
    reclaim slots without realloc
  - writeBinaryPOD serialization: fixed header (offsets, max_elements, cur_count, stride,
    maxlevel, enterpoint, M, mult_, ef_construction) then raw level-0 block then per-node variable
    upper-layer linklists
nest steals: Target nest-runtime/src/ann (codec.rs + search.rs + mod.rs) and nest-format section 0x07. Three
             concrete steals: (1) Replace the Vec<Vec<u32>> Node adjacency and variable codec with
             a flat fixed-stride level-0 byte block (count:u32 + maxM0 neighbor u32s) plus a
             separate sparse upper-layer block, so the 0x07 payload becomes directly
             mmap-traversable -- read neighbor ids as &[u32] slices off mmap with zero
             decode/alloc, matching nest's score-off-disk philosophy and shrinking the index by
             dropping per-Vec capacity overhead. This is an ADDITIVE bump of HNSW_PAYLOAD_VERSION
             inside 0x07 (allowed; section payload version moves, not NEST_FORMAT_VERSION). (2)
             Adopt the VisitedListPool epoch-tag trick in search.rs to kill the per-query
             HashSet<u32> in layer_search (replace with a reusable Vec<u16>+generation counter) --
             pure latency win, stays deterministic. (3) Route
             greedy_search/layer_search/cosine_dist through nest's existing simd dispatcher
             (avx2.rs/neon.rs) instead of the scalar dot loop, since vectors are L2-normalized so
             distance is 1-dot -- reuses the kernel already on the rerank hot path. Also steal
             getNeighborsByHeuristic2 to replace the _simple top-m selection (mod.rs already flags
             this as planned Phase 2) for higher recall at fixed M, letting the tiny preset lower
             M (fewer neighbor ids per node = smaller index) while holding recall@10. CAUTION: do
             NOT copy hnswlib's habit of interleaving the raw vector into the graph record -- nest
             embeddings live in the separate scored-off-mmap section; steal the layout for graph
             ids only.
compression: Indirect but real. The flat byte-packed graph removes Rust Vec<Vec> overhead (each inner Vec
             carries 24B ptr/len/cap plus heap slack) and lets 0x07 store neighbor lists as raw
             little-endian u32 blocks the zstd codec (non-embedding sections) compresses well; the
             heuristic neighbor selection raises recall per edge, so the tiny preset can lower M
             (fewer u32 neighbor ids per node = smaller index) without losing recall -- directly
             serving smallest-container-at-fixed-recall@10.
perf:        VisitedListPool avoids malloc/free and cache-cold HashSet rehash per query (nest currently
             allocates a fresh HashSet<u32> every layer_search). SIMD16Ext processes 16
             floats/iter with FMA and one horizontal reduce; *ExtResiduals lets non-16-multiple
             dims still hit SIMD on the aligned prefix. Flat level-0 array is cache/prefetch
             friendly: neighbor count and ids sit in one region, mmap pages fault in linearly.
             Graph is dtype-independent (links are u32 regardless of f16/i8 embeddings), matching
             nest building the graph on an f32 snapshot.
license:     Apache-2.0
