--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MUST] qdrant/qdrant
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust vector database whose differentiating engine is a stack of rescore-backed quantization
             codecs (scalar int8, binary 1/1.5/2-bit, product, and the new data-oblivious
             TurboQuant) under an HNSW graph, where compressed vectors live hot and full-precision
             originals stay on disk purely for a two-stage rescore.
innovation:  TurboQuant (Google Research, ICLR 2026, as extended by qdrant): a DATA-OBLIVIOUS quantizer needing
             NO trained codebook. Apply a fast randomized Hadamard rotation (seeded deterministic
             sign flips + Walsh-Hadamard transform, O(d log d)) to each vector, which provably
             redistributes energy uniformly so every coordinate becomes ~Beta-distributed
             regardless of the embedding model. Then quantize every coordinate with ONE global
             precomputed Max-Lloyd (1-D k-means) codebook baked in at compile time. A per-vector
             scalar length-renormalization factor (orig_length/reconstruction_length, borrowed
             from RaBitQ) corrects the systematic length-shrink bias. Scoring is asymmetric:
             stored vectors pack to 1-4 bits, the query stays full precision and is rotated the
             same way, so one SIMD pass over packed codes yields a real distance estimate.
techniques:
  - Randomized Hadamard rotation: seeded sign-flip (LCG) + fast Walsh-Hadamard transform in O(d
    log d), making any embedding distribution coordinate-uniform so a single global codebook
    suffices (no training, deterministic)
  - Compile-time baked Max-Lloyd codebooks: precomputed optimal 1-D k-means quantizers for
    Beta-distributed coords at 1/1.5/2/4-bit; data-oblivious so reproducible
  - Per-vector length renormalization (RaBitQ trick): store one f32 scale = orig_len/quantized_len
    to cancel quantization's systematic length-shrink, applied at score time
  - Asymmetric quantization: compress stored vectors to k bits, keep query full precision ->
    higher recall at same storage; query rotated then encoded into a SIMD scoring form
  - Scalar int8 with algebraic dot decomposition: expand (alpha*i8+offset)*(alpha*i8'+offset) so
    offset/min cross-terms precompute per-vector, leaving ONLY raw int8.int8 dot at query time ->
    AVX-512 VNNI vpdpbusd / NEON sdot
  - Binary quantization via sign bit + XOR/popcount Hamming; 1.5-bit and 2-bit variants explicitly
    encode near-zero values to fix binary's weakest case
  - Two-stage retrieve-then-rescore: oversample 2x-4x candidates on cheap quantized distance, then
    rescore the small set against full-precision originals via async IO (all ids known, no
    random-access penalty) -> recall 0.98-1.0
  - Quantile-calibrated bounds (e.g. 0.99) to clip outliers before linear int8 mapping
nest steals: Add a TurboQuant codec to nest-format/src/encoding alongside int8/float16, applied ONLY to the
             embeddings section (new encoding id from the reserved 4-255 range, additive, no
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump). Build pipeline (python side, nest-python): per vector ->
             normalize for metric -> pad to power of 2 -> seeded sign-flip + Walsh-Hadamard
             rotation -> assign coords against a codebook baked as a rust const -> pack to
             1/2/4-bit -> store one f32 renorm scale per vector. Compile-time const codebook keeps
             builds byte-identical/deterministic (nest's hard constraint) and needs NO corpus
             stats. nest-runtime gets a new asymmetric SIMD scorer in the simd dispatcher: rotate
             the f32 query once, dot against packed codes (AVX2/NEON/scalar fallback already
             exist). This PRESERVES nest's mandatory exact-cosine rerank: TurboQuant is the cheap
             oversample stage, and the search-contract rerank reads the original f32 embeddings
             (kept raw/mmap, never zstd) so the returned score stays true cosine. Unlocks a preset
             below the int8 'tiny' floor: TurboQuant-4bit ~halves embedding bytes vs int8 at
             comparable recall; 1-bit/1.5-bit go smaller than binary while beating it on recall --
             directly advancing 'compress more than everyone'. The Hadamard rotation is a
             self-contained, seeded, reversible ~one-file util respecting <=300-line and
             never-panic rules.
compression: Directly central. TurboQuant is the single best lever for nest's 'compress more than everyone'
             goal on the embeddings section -- the largest, least-compressible part (zstd is
             forbidden there since vectors are scored from mmap). 4-bit gives ~2x over the current
             int8 'tiny' preset at similar recall; 1-bit/1.5-bit beat binary quantization on
             recall at smaller size. Being data-oblivious with compile-time codebooks, it is the
             ONLY high-end quantizer that keeps nest's deterministic/reproducible-build and
             no-training guarantees, and asymmetric full-precision query scoring plus the existing
             exact-cosine rerank make the compression lossless for the FINAL returned score.
perf:        Hadamard rotation O(d log d) per vector (one-time at build for stored vectors; once per query at
             search). int8 path: only raw int8.int8 dot at query time -> AVX-512 VNNI vpdpbusd
             (x86) / NEON sdot (aarch64), ~4x lane density vs f32, ~75% RAM cut, <0.3% recall
             loss, 28-60% latency win on qdrant benchmarks. Binary: XOR+popcount Hamming, up to
             40x speedup. TurboQuant 4-bit: scalar-quality recall at 2x compression;
             SIMD-accelerated for cosine/dot/L2 (L1 needs full reconstruction, slow). Rescore
             needs no random access -- all candidate ids known, originals streamed from disk, so
             cheap mmap/network disks suffice. nest already has AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatch, so the
             asymmetric scorer slots into existing infra.
license:     Apache-2.0 (qdrant core). TurboQuant algorithm is from a Google Research paper (arXiv:2504.19874)
             -- reimplement from the paper to stay clean; do not copy qdrant's exact source.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MUST] milvus-io/milvus (core vector engine: zilliztech/knowhere; storage: milvus-io/milvus-storage)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Distributed vector database whose single most transferable kernel is Knowhere/ScaNN's FastScan:
             4-bit product quantization scored by SIMD in-register lookup tables, combined with
             anisotropic quantization and a mandatory full-precision refine stage.
innovation:  FastScan = 4-bit product quantization where each sub-vector maps to one of 16 centroids (4
             bits/subspace), and the per-query distance lookup table is held entirely in SIMD
             registers so distance computation becomes register byte-shuffles (in-register ADC)
             instead of memory-bound table loads. Paired with anisotropic quantization, which
             trains the PQ codebook to penalize the residual component PARALLEL to each vector
             more than the orthogonal component, preserving inner-product/cosine ranking accuracy
             instead of minimizing raw reconstruction error. Both are made safe by over-fetching
             candidates and re-ranking the top reorder_k with exact full-precision distance.
techniques:
  - FastScan 4-bit PQ: 16 centroids per subspace packed at 4 bits, distance LUT kept in SIMD
    registers (in-register ADC) eliminating the memory-load bottleneck of classic PQ table lookups
  - Anisotropic vector quantization (Guo et al 2020 MLR): score-aware loss weighting parallel
    residual error higher than orthogonal, optimizing for MIPS/cosine ranking not L2
    reconstruction
  - Runtime SIMD dispatch via Google Highway generating AVX2/AVX512 from one source; AVX2 pshufb /
    AVX512 vpermb / NEON vqtbl1q for the 16-entry table gather
  - Mandatory exact refine stage (reorder_k / with_raw_data / FP32 refiner): over-fetch from lossy
    index then recompute true distance on raw vectors -- identical to nest's mandatory exact
    cosine rerank
  - SQ8 scalar quantization: per-dimension f32->uint8 affine map storing min/scale (~4x), with IVF
    clustering as a coarse quantizer
  - JSON shredding: classify keys into typed columns (frequent+consistent type), dynamic columns
    (frequent+mixed type, path-named like /user/name), and a shared binary-JSON column with an
    inverted index for sparse keys
  - Per-field columnar binlog: each column is its own file with a magic-number header + typed
    event stream and an ExtraBytes slot for additive format evolution without breaking
    compatibility
  - Storage Format V2 adaptive layout: 'narrow column merging + wide column independence'
    co-locates small scalar columns, keeps large vector columns independent, enabling random point
    lookups without Parquet I/O amplification
nest steals: Add a PQ codec to nest-format as a new encoding id (reserved 4-255 space, additive within v1)
             applied ONLY to the embeddings section -- the one place nest currently refuses to
             compress because it scores from mmap. FastScan flips that: the 4-bit PQ codes ARE the
             mmap'd hot-path data and the per-query LUT is built once into registers, so you keep
             zero-copy scoring while reaching 8-32x compression vs raw f32 (beyond today's int8 4x
             and zstd's no-op on embeddings). Concretely: (1) nest-format gains a pq codec module
             -- write side runs deterministic seeded k-means per subspace, stores tiny codebooks
             in a sub-section plus 4-bit codes. (2) nest-runtime simd adds a FastScan kernel
             building the 16-entry-per-subspace LUT per query, then AVX2 pshufb / AVX512 vpermb /
             NEON vqtbl1q in-register gather to accumulate distances -- mirrors the existing
             AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatch shape, stays <=300 lines, typed errors. (3) Search path
             needs ZERO contract change because nest ALREADY mandates an exact cosine rerank: PQ
             becomes another approximate first stage, over-fetch then rerank against existing
             raw/f16 vectors so returned scores stay real cosine. (4) New preset 'micro'
             (zstd/pq/hnsw) targeting <0.1 size ratio below 'tiny'. Anisotropic codebook training
             is a pure build-time python change to the k-means loss (deterministic, no format
             change, better recall at same bytes). Separately, JSON shredding maps onto nest's
             roadmap to compress json/yaml/datasets: a future shredding codec for a
             structured-data section turns repetitive nested docs into typed columns that zstd
             compresses far better than raw text.
compression: Directly central to 'compress more than everyone'. Embeddings are nest's largest,
             currently-incompressible section (zstd skips them, int8 reaches only 4x). FastScan
             4-bit PQ reaches 8-32x on exactly that section while staying mmap-scored on the hot
             path, and anisotropic training recovers the recall PQ would otherwise lose -- the
             single biggest available win on nest's dominant byte cost. The exact-rerank safety
             net nest already enforces is precisely what makes aggressive lossy quantization
             acceptable.
perf:        In-register LUT turns PQ scoring from memory-bound to compute-bound; ScaNN reported ~5x QPS over
             IVF_FLAT on Cohere-1M. 4-bit codes pack 2 subspaces/byte: a 768-d vector with 96
             subspaces = 48 bytes vs 3072 bytes f32 (~64x raw, before tiny codebook overhead).
             AVX2 pshufb is a 16-byte shuffle (exact fit for 16 centroids); AVX512 vpermb; aarch64
             NEON vqtbl1q. Refine cost scales with reorder_k only, not corpus size, so the
             exact-rerank tax stays bounded.
license:     All components Apache-2.0 (milvus core, knowhere, milvus-storage, google-research/scann). Clean to
             study and reimplement the algorithms in nest's Rust core; do not vendor C++ --
             reimplement the FastScan kernel and anisotropic loss from the public MLR 2020 paper
             and PQ literature.

[MUST] chroma-core/chroma
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Chroma is a log-structured, Rust-core embedded/distributed vector DB whose index crate has
             converged on rotation-based binary/low-bit quantization (RaBitQ 1-bit/4-bit and
             TurboQuant 3-bit) over a SPANN inverted index, with an Arrow-RecordBatch blockfile
             storage layer and HNSW centroid routing.
innovation:  RaBitQ-family quantization: a random orthogonal rotation decorrelates coordinates so each
             dimension can be stored in 1 bit (sign of the rotated, centroid-residual, normalized
             vector), and distance is recovered via a provably UNBIASED inner-product estimator
             (error O(1/sqrt(D))) computed as bitwise AND + popcount plus two precomputed
             corrective scalars (centroid distance, and inner product between the quantized and
             normalized vector). This gives ~28x compression over f32 (vs nest's current 8x int8)
             with no codebook training, no iterative optimization, and a binary-only hot loop; an
             exact rerank then fixes residual error. TurboQuant extends this to a tunable 3-bit
             point (PolarQuant Lloyd-Max 2-bit per coordinate + QJL 1-bit sign-of-residual
             correction scaled by Sigma|e[i]|/dim) for a higher-recall operating point.
techniques:
  - RaBitQ 1-bit quantization: random rotation + store sign per dim -> nearest hypercube vertex
    (144B/1024-dim, 28.4x vs f32)
  - Unbiased inner-product/distance estimator from binary codes with two corrective scalars per
    vector (centroid dist + <quantized,normalized> dot)
  - Bitwise asymmetric distance kernel: AND + popcount (~111ns/code) instead of float dot products
    on the hot path
  - Random orthogonal rotation as decorrelation preprocessing so per-coordinate scalar
    quantization becomes near-MSE-optimal
  - TurboQuant 3-bit = PolarQuant (Lloyd-Max MSE-optimal 2-bit scalar quantizer per coord) + QJL
    (1-bit sign of PolarQuant residual, scaled by Sigma|e[i]|/dim)
  - 24-byte per-code header of precomputed factors (norm, radial, correction, qjl_scale) for fast
    decode/estimate
  - SPANN inverted index: in-memory HNSW over centroids routes to on-disk posting lists (nprobe),
    residuals quantized -> tiny disk footprint
  - Two-stage search: cheap quantized estimate for candidate generation, then exact full-precision
    rerank for true scores
nest steals: Add a new embeddings codec in nest-format/src/encoding/ (rabitq.rs) as a v1-additive encoding id
             (reserved 4-255 range; next free after int8=3), mirroring int8.rs layout: a payload
             header (rotation seed + dim), per-vector corrective scalars (f32 norm/centroid +
             estimator constant), then 1 bit/dim packed sign codes. Determinism is preserved by
             SEEDING the random rotation and storing the seed in the header so build stays
             byte-identical/reproducible (same chunks + model fingerprint => same file),
             satisfying nest's hard constraint. nest-runtime/src/simd dispatches a new
             AND+popcount kernel (trivial AVX2/NEON/scalar, popcnt is one instruction) to produce
             the unbiased estimate straight off mmap, feeding candidates into the EXISTING
             mandatory exact-cosine rerank in nest-runtime search; that is the perfect home
             because nest already guarantees the returned score is real cosine, so the quantizer
             only needs to be a good RANKER not a good scorer. Unlocks a new 'nano' preset (zstd
             non-embedding sections + rabitq embeddings + hnsw) targeting a size ratio well below
             the current 0.283 'tiny' preset while keeping recall@10 high via rerank. nest-python
             builder unchanged except for exposing the preset.
compression: Directly central to 'compress more than everyone': embeddings are the largest binary section nest
             cannot zstd (scored from mmap on the hot path), so they are the single biggest lever.
             RaBitQ takes embeddings from 8x (current int8) to ~28x compression while nest's
             mandatory exact rerank absorbs the accuracy loss; highest-leverage compression win
             available, attacking the one section currently uncompressable.
perf:        Binary kernel ~111ns/code (AND+popcount) vs ~3.94us scalar path -> ~35x faster candidate scoring;
             popcount is a single CPU instruction so AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatch is trivial for
             nest's existing simd module. RaBitQ needs NO training/codebook (rotation + sign
             only), so build stays deterministic and cheap (~1us/vector encode at 1-bit). 1-bit
             codes keep far more vectors resident in page-cache, improving mmap locality.
             TurboQuant 3-bit is the higher-recall knob (cosine P95 error 0.96% vs RaBitQ 1-bit
             3.56%) at 10x compression for presets needing better pre-rerank recall, but encode is
             ~12x slower (13.7us vs 1.1us).
license:     Apache-2.0 (chroma-core/chroma). RaBitQ (SIGMOD 2024) and TurboQuant (ICLR 2026) are published
             algorithms; reimplement from the papers rather than copying Chroma's Apache code to
             keep nest's licensing clean and the rust core small.

[MUST] weaviate/weaviate
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Go-based vector database whose flagship compression is 8-bit Rotational Quantization (RQ): a
             RaBitQ-inspired scheme that applies a fast pseudorandom Walsh-Hadamard rotation to
             each vector so per-vector min/max scalar quantization to int8 becomes near-lossless,
             then guarantees recall via a mandatory full-precision rescore stage on HNSW
             candidates.
innovation:  Rotate-then-quantize: a deterministic fast pseudorandom rotation (three rounds of blocked Fast
             Walsh-Hadamard Transform with seeded element swaps + sign flips, O(D log D)) makes
             any embedding's coordinates approximately normally distributed before scalar
             quantization. This smooths the min-max interval and spreads similarity information
             uniformly across all dimensions, so naive per-vector min/max int8 quantization
             becomes near-lossless (98-99% recall@10) at 4x compression WITHOUT any trained
             codebook or k-means, so it stays fully deterministic and data-independent.
techniques:
  - 8-bit Rotational Quantization (RQ): per-vector store = D int8 codes + l_x (f32 min) + delta_x
    (f32 step) + squared-norm (f32), ~96 bits metadata overhead per vector
  - Fast pseudorandom rotation = 3 rounds of blocked Fast Walsh-Hadamard Transform, padded to
    multiple of 32/64, with seeded random element permutation and random sign flips per round (O(D
    log D), ~7us for D=1536)
  - Decomposed quantized inner product: <q,x> approx = d*lq*lx + lq*dx*sum(cx) + lx*dq*sum(cq) +
    dq*dx*<cx,cq>, where only the int8 dot product term needs the hot SIMD kernel (~2.3x faster
    than f32)
  - 1-bit RQ variant: sign-bit binary codes after rotation giving ~32x compression as D grows,
    Hamming distance via XOR+popcount
  - Binary Quantization (BQ): sign threshold to 1 bit/dim, distance via bitwise XOR + SIMD
    popcount, ~5% of f32 search latency
  - Mandatory two-stage rescore (rescoreLimit): HNSW walks on compressed distances, then top
    candidates get exact full-precision distance recompute for the returned score
  - Output dimension rounded up to nearest multiple of 64 so FWHT blocks and the SIMD int8 dot
    product stay aligned
nest steals: Upgrade crates/nest-format/src/encoding/int8.rs from naive per-section int8 to a rotation-aware
             per-vector RQ codec, registered as a NEW additive encoding id (4-255 reserved range)
             so the FROZEN v1 format and existing int8 stay byte-compatible. Store per embedding
             row: D int8 codes plus three f32 scalars (l_x, delta_x, sq_norm). Determinism is
             preserved because the rotation seed derives from the model fingerprint already in the
             manifest (no trained codebook, no k-means -- fits nest reproducible builds exactly,
             unlike PQ). Add a small FWHT helper (well under the 300-line cap, pure
             repr(C)/little-endian, no panics) plus the decomposed int8 inner-product kernel into
             the nest-runtime SIMD dispatcher (AVX2/NEON/scalar) beside the existing dot-product
             path. Near-perfect fit for nest's 'hnsw + mandatory exact cosine rerank' contract: RQ
             does the cheap candidate walk and nest's already-mandatory exact cosine rerank IS the
             rescore stage, so the returned score stays real cosine. Net: the 'tiny' preset int8
             recall@10 should climb from 0.992 toward 0.998+ at the same 4x size, and a 1-bit RQ
             mode unlocks a new ultra-tiny preset at ~32x embedding compression. nest-cli
             benchmark/stats surface the recall delta; nest-python needs no change.
compression: Directly central. RQ is the rare quantizer that is simultaneously (a) data-independent and
             deterministic (seeded rotation, no trained codebook), satisfying nest's
             reproducible-build and additive-format constraints where Product Quantization fails,
             and (b) near-lossless at 4x with a 1-bit path at ~32x. It pushes the 'tiny' preset
             toward best-in-class embedding compression at fixed recall, the literal core of the
             compress-more-than-everyone mission, while embeddings stay directly scorable from
             mmap on the hot path (int8 codes read raw, no entropy decode).
perf:        Rotation ~7-10us for D=1536 (O(D log D), three blocked-FWHT rounds, 64/256-element block kernels).
             Distance estimate ~2.3x faster than f32 since the dominant cost is an int8 inner
             product (Apple M4 NEON ~182 M ops/s for 64-dim 8-bit RQ vs ~145 M ops/s f32). Memory:
             1M x 1536-dim drops 5859 MB -> 1465 MB (4x). 1-bit RQ and BQ use XOR+popcount, ~5% of
             f32 latency. Fixed 96-bit/vector metadata overhead is negligible at typical D but
             favors higher-dimensional embeddings.
license:     BSD-3-Clause (Weaviate core). RQ is an original Weaviate implementation inspired by the RaBitQ
             paper (RaBitQ academic code is MIT). The math is published in their blog and is not
             patent-encumbered, so a clean-room Rust reimplementation is safe.

[MUST] supervc-stack/VectorChord (mirror of tensorchord/VectorChord)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A PostgreSQL extension (vchordrq) that indexes vectors as IVF posting lists where each vector is
             stored as a tiny RaBitQ-quantized bit-code, scanning compressed codes with binary
             popcount arithmetic and deferring full-precision math to an adaptive rerank stage.
innovation:  RaBitQ quantization: apply a random rotation (Johnson-Lindenstrauss / Fast Hadamard Transform) to
             the residual of a vector around its cluster centroid, take the sign bits to get a
             1-bit-per-dim code, and store just two corrective float scalars per vector (centroid
             distance + the inner product between the quantized and normalized vector). The
             cosine/L2 estimate is reconstructed from a single binary dot product plus those two
             scalars, and crucially it is an UNBIASED estimator with a provable O(1/sqrt(D)) error
             bound (asymptotically optimal, Alon-Klartag). That is what separates it from product
             quantization, which is biased and silently fails on some datasets. The binary dot
             product is popcount(code AND query_bits); the multi-bit variant (RaBitQ4/8) reuses
             the same machinery by quantizing the QUERY into bit-planes q1,q2,q3,q4 and summing
             weighted popcounts: 1*popcount(code&q1) + 2*popcount(code&q2) + 4*popcount(code&q3) +
             8*popcount(code&q4) -- the data stays 1 bit/dim while query precision is tunable.
techniques:
  - RaBitQ 1-bit quantization: random-rotation + sign-bit code + 2 corrective scalars
    (centroid_dist, <quantized,normalized>) giving an unbiased estimator with O(1/sqrt(D)) error
    bound
  - Residual quantization: quantize each vector relative to its IVF centroid and reformulate
    <o,q-c>=<o,q>-<o,c> so the query is rotated/quantized once per probe (~20% QPS)
  - Random rotation via Fast Hadamard Transform (O(D log D), no dense DxD matrix to store) to
    spread information before sign-bit extraction
  - Binary distance estimator = popcount(code AND query_bits); multi-bit = weighted-popcount over
    query bit-planes keeping data at 1 bit/dim
  - SIMD Fast Scan: bit-pack/transpose codes to estimate 32 vectors per kernel pass; single-vector
    popcount path beats PQ table-lookups ~3x
  - IVF posting lists instead of a global graph: insert=append code, delete=clear code, no
    cascading graph repair, deterministic and update-friendly
  - Adaptive two-stage search: scan all codes with cheap integer math, then full-precision rerank
    only a small top subset sized by the error bound
  - DiskANN + 2-bit RaBitQ (vchordg) alternate graph index trading update cost for higher QPS at
    high recall
nest steals: Add a RaBitQ-style codec as a new RESERVED encoding id (4-255, additive within v1, no
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump) in nest-format/encoding, targeting the embeddings section.
             Per embedding store a 1-bit-per-dim sign code plus two f32 corrective scalars
             computed against a small set of seeded deterministic k-means centroids serialized
             alongside (seeded => reproducible, honors the determinism guarantee). In nest-runtime
             add a binary-popcount estimator kernel to the simd dispatcher (popcount is one
             instruction on AVX2/NEON, trivial scalar fallback) as the cheap first pass; nest
             already mandates an exact-cosine rerank on the hot path, which is precisely RaBitQ's
             two-stage model, so the search_contract is unchanged and the returned score stays
             real cosine. This unlocks an embeddings encoding far smaller than int8 (1 bit/dim vs
             8 => ~8x on the dominant section) and the provable error bound lets you bound rerank
             candidate count instead of guessing. The Fast Hadamard rotator (O(D log D), nothing
             dense to persist) is the only new primitive and fits as one <=300-line module. Longer
             term, the IVF posting-list layout (centroid id -> contiguous code run) is a
             deterministic, cheap-to-build alternative to hnsw_index.
compression: Directly central to 'compress more than everyone'. Embeddings are the largest, least-compressible
             section in a .nest file (zstd is banned on them because they are scored from mmap).
             RaBitQ is the rare scheme that compresses embeddings 8-32x WHILE keeping them
             scoreable in place via popcount on the hot path -- no decode step before scoring,
             unlike a generic entropy coder. Combined with nest's existing mandatory exact rerank,
             recall is recovered for free, making this the highest-leverage way to shrink the
             dominant section without breaking the score contract.
perf:        Single-vector RaBitQ via popcount is ~3x faster than PQ in-RAM table lookups at equal accuracy;
             batch Fast Scan estimates 32 vectors per pass; estimate is integer/bitwise only,
             full-precision floats touch only the reranked subset. Storage claims: ~6x more
             vectors than Pinecone optimized storage, ~26x vs pgvector at equal cost; 1M 960-dim
             indexed in ~1.5 min, 100M under 20 min with precomputed centroids; residual
             reformulation ~20% QPS; Fast Hadamard rotation O(D log D) with no dense matrix to
             load, important for an mmap/disk design.
license:     tensorchord ships VectorChord under a source-available license (AGPL/ELv2-style); verify exact
             terms before vendoring. The underlying RaBitQ reference (gaoj0017/RaBitQ, now
             VectorDB-NTU/RaBitQ-Library, SIGMOD 2024 paper) is independently published and is the
             cleaner source to reimplement from to avoid the extension's license.

[MUST] sqliteai/sqlite-vector
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A pure-C SQLite extension that does ANN over plain BLOB columns with zero index build, pairing a
             runtime-dispatched SIMD distance table (SSE2/AVX2/NEON/RVV) with TurboQuant:
             rotation-based data-oblivious 2/3/4-bit scalar quantization scored via SIMD nibble
             lookup tables without vector reconstruction.
innovation:  TurboQuant scalar quantization (Google Research, ICLR 2026, arXiv 2504.19874): apply a fast
             structured random rotation (Hadamard-based, reproduced here as a reversible-LCG +
             Fisher-Yates Hadamard rotation) to each vector so per-coordinate variance is
             redistributed to a concentrated near-iid Beta/Gaussian distribution, THEN apply a
             fixed per-coordinate Lloyd-Max scalar codebook (16 centroids at 4-bit, 4 at 2-bit)
             with a per-vector scale+shift calibration. This is data-oblivious (no
             training/codebook fitting, no centroid storage), gives near information-theoretically
             optimal distortion (within ~2.7x of the proven lower bound), and at query time scores
             ASYMMETRICALLY: the full-precision query is rotated once and folded with 1/scale, so
             dot(q,x) is recovered as a lookup-table sum over the stored 2/3/4-bit codes with zero
             per-vector decompression. The decisive part for nest is that this beats plain int8 SQ
             at half the bytes (4-bit ~8x compression matches/beats nest's current int8 4x; 2-bit
             hits 16x; 1-bit 32x) while preserving recall.
techniques:
  - TurboQuant: fast structured random rotation (Hadamard/FFHT-style, O(d log d)) before
    quantization so coordinates become near-iid and a single fixed scalar codebook applies
    per-coordinate
  - Per-coordinate fixed Lloyd-Max codebook from standard normal (16 centroids for 4-bit, 4 for
    2-bit) plus per-vector (scale,shift) calibration x -> (x+shift)*scale
  - Asymmetric scoring: dot(q,x) = <q/scale, x_codes> - <q, shift>, query rotated and folded with
    1/scale ONCE per query; stored vectors never decompressed
  - SIMD nibble-split LUT kernel: codebook quantized to int8, pshufb does parallel 16-byte table
    lookup, query split into low/high nibbles fed to _mm_maddubs_epi16 (or VPDPBUSD on VNNI / NEON
    tbl / AVX-512BW)
  - Two-stage PROD mode: MSE-optimal quantizer + 1-bit QJL (Quantized Johnson-Lindenstrauss)
    transform on the residual to remove inner-product estimation bias at ~1 bit/vector extra cost
  - Index-free architecture: runtime dispatch_distance_table[metric][dtype] function-pointer array
    selected by CPU feature detection (SSE2/AVX2/NEON/RVV), brute-force full scan over BLOBs,
    zero-cost inserts/updates with no graph rebuild
  - On-disk quantized row = rowid(8) + scale(4) + packed_codes(ceil(dim*qbits/8)); shadow table
    vector0_{table}_{column}; streaming scan when k omitted (progressive WHERE/LIMIT)
  - Native multi-format storage: float32/float16/bfloat16/int8/uint8/1-bit BLOBs with Hamming
    distance for the 1-bit path
nest steals: Add a TurboQuant codec as a new additive encoding id in nest-format/src/encoding/ (e.g.
             turboquant.rs alongside int8.rs), reserved-range ids 4-6 for tq4/tq2/tq1, NO
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump (additive only). The embeddings section stores per-vector
             [scale:f32][shift:f32][packed_codes] plus a section-level header carrying the
             rotation seed (deterministic: seeded LCG + Fisher-Yates Hadamard, fits nest's
             seeded-determinism constraint exactly) and qbits. Critically nest's hard rule is
             embeddings are scored DIRECTLY from mmap on the hot path and never zstd-compressed;
             TurboQuant respects this perfectly because codes are scored in place via LUT with no
             decompression, so it slots straight into nest-runtime/src/search.rs and the SIMD
             dispatcher in nest-runtime/src/simd/ (add a tq nibble-LUT kernel: pshufb+maddubs for
             avx2.rs, NEON tbl for neon.rs, scalar fallback in scalar.rs). The mandatory
             exact-cosine rerank stays unchanged and actually gets cheaper to justify because
             TurboQuant is the ANN proxy. This unlocks a new preset 'nano' (tq2/hnsw) targeting a
             size ratio well under the current tiny preset's 0.283 (2-bit is 16x vs int8's 8x
             effective), and tq1 (32x, Hamming) for an extreme-compression tier, directly
             advancing the compress-more-than-everyone north star. nest-cli stats/inspect surface
             the qbits and rotation seed; nest-python build path picks tq presets.
compression: Directly central to the north star. nest's smallest preset today is int8 (8x payload reduction,
             0.283 ratio). TurboQuant 4-bit matches that at half the bits with equal-or-better
             recall (it beats plain int8 SQ by 1-2 to ~5 recall points on real embeddings like
             dbpedia-openai-ada: 0.93 vs 0.88), 2-bit reaches 16x, 1-bit 32x. It is the single
             highest-leverage quantization upgrade available, is data-oblivious (no training, fits
             offline+deterministic), is provably near-optimal distortion, and crucially scores
             from packed codes with no decompression so it does not violate nest's
             never-decompress-embeddings-on-hot-path rule.
perf:        sqlite-vector benchmarks (1M x 768d, dot, k=10): tq4 218ms vs 3248ms full scan (14.9x) at 0.84
             recall@10; tq3 188ms (9.2x) 0.74; tq2 85ms (38x) 0.48 on synthetic, but on real
             embeddings recall is far higher (Fashion-MNIST 10k tq4 = 0.948). Preload of quantized
             codes into RAM gives 4-5x. The SIMD win comes from pshufb 16-byte LUT +
             _mm_maddubs_epi16 (VPDPBUSD on VNNI, NEON tbl on aarch64) processing 2/4-bit codes
             without float ops; per-query cost is one rotation (O(d log d) Hadamard) plus a folded
             1/scale, amortized across the whole scan. Memory per quantized row ~ 8 + 4 +
             ceil(dim*qbits/8) bytes; baseline footprint 30MB.
license:     sqlite-vector is source-available under the Elastic License 2.0 (ELv2) per sqliteai repos; verify
             before vendoring code. The TurboQuant ALGORITHM is from a public Google Research
             paper (arXiv 2504.19874) and is freely reimplementable - nest should reimplement from
             the paper, not copy ELv2 C source.

[MUST] asg017/sqlite-vec
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A pure-C, zero-dependency SQLite extension (vec0 virtual table) that stores float32/int8/bit
             vectors in chunked shadow-table BLOBs and runs brute-force SIMD KNN with
             metadata/partition filtering, optionally using 1-bit binary quantization plus a
             coarse-search-then-float-rescore pattern.
innovation:  Binary (1-bit) quantization as a coarse pre-filter: sign-bit-threshold each dimension into a
             packed bitmap (32x smaller than f32), score candidates with Hamming distance via
             popcount (integer-only, branchless, SIMD-friendly), then rerank the small candidate
             set against full-precision vectors. This is the exact two-stage pattern nest already
             mandates (ANN/coarse retrieve then mandatory exact cosine rerank), so it slots in as
             a new ultra-compact embedding codec without weakening nest's "returned score is real
             cosine" contract.
techniques:
  - binary quantization: sign-bit threshold (>=0 -> 1, <0 -> 0) packing 8 dims/byte = 1 bit/dim,
    32x smaller than f32
  - Hamming distance via XOR + popcount (count_ones) as the coarse metric for bit vectors
  - two-pass coarse+rescore: retrieve k*8 candidates on bit vectors, rerank top-k with
    vec_distance_L2 on stored float vectors
  - per-vector int8 scalar quantization (1 byte/dim), 4x smaller than f32, selectable per column
  - chunked contiguous storage: vectors packed into one BLOB per chunk per column (default 128
    vectors/chunk) for SIMD locality, no per-row overhead
  - validity bitmap per chunk for O(1) tombstone deletes (clear bit, zero rowid) without
    rewriting/shifting the index
  - shadow-table separation: _chunks (bitmap+rowids), _vector_chunksNN (packed vectors),
    _metadatachunksNN (packed fixed-width metadata), _auxiliary (un-indexed + columns)
  - partition keys + per-chunk packed metadata blobs to prune whole chunks before the distance
    kernel runs
nest steals: Add a binary/bit embedding codec to nest-format/src/encoding (new encoding id in the reserved
             4-255 range, e.g. encoding=5 'bit1', additive-only so NEST_FORMAT_VERSION stays v1)
             alongside the existing int8.rs: quantize each L2-normalized f32 vector to a sign-bit
             bitmap (dim/8 bytes, deterministic, repr(C) little-endian). In nest-runtime/src/simd
             add a hamming kernel (XOR + u64 count_ones; NEON vcntq_u8 on aarch64, AVX2 on x86_64,
             scalar fallback) parallel to the dot-product dispatcher. In
             nest-runtime/src/search.rs wire it as a coarse stage that over-fetches candidates
             (e.g. k*8) ranked by Hamming, then feeds them into the EXISTING mandatory
             exact-cosine rerank using full-precision (or int8) embeddings from the embeddings
             section, so the search contract is unchanged and the returned score stays real
             cosine. Expose a new preset (e.g. 'nano': zstd metadata / bit embeddings / hnsw) in
             nest-cli targeting the smallest embeddings section. The chunked validity-bitmap
             tombstone idea also transfers to nest's future real-time-edit goal: a per-section
             validity bitmap marks deleted chunk_ids without rewriting embeddings, preserving
             determinism by treating tombstones as content.
compression: Directly central to the north-star 'compress more than everyone' for vectors: 1-bit binary
             quantization is 32x smaller than f32 and 8x smaller than nest's current int8 floor,
             dropping a 768-dim embedding from ~3KB to 96 bytes. Because nest already enforces an
             exact-cosine rerank, nest can adopt the most aggressive quantization (which would
             normally wreck recall) and recover full-precision accuracy at query time: nest's
             architecture is uniquely positioned to ship binary embeddings WITHOUT the usual
             recall penalty, since the bit vectors are only a coarse pre-filter, not the scored
             answer. This is the single biggest embedding-size win available.
perf:        Pure C, no dependencies, compiles everywhere SQLite runs; SIMD via AVX (x86_64) and NEON
             (Apple/Android). Brute-force KNN over packed chunk BLOBs is cache-friendly because
             vectors are contiguous (default 128/chunk), the same mmap-contiguous property nest
             relies on for its hot-path dot products. Hamming/popcount is integer-only and
             branchless, far cheaper per candidate than f32 cosine, which is why it works as a
             coarse filter ahead of an expensive rerank. int8 path is 4x smaller than f32 with
             minimal accuracy loss on normalized vectors; bit path is 32x smaller. Project
             tradeoff: chunked OLTP writes are slow per-insert (transaction + chunk assignment) vs
             a static read-only blob mode; nest's frozen-file build model sidesteps this entirely
             since nest writes once, like sqlite-vec's faster static mode.
license:     Apache-2.0 OR MIT (dual-licensed)

[HIGH] lancedb/lancedb (built on lancedb/lance)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        LanceDB is a disk-based, zero-copy embedded vector database built on the Lance columnar file
             format, which uses adaptive per-buffer structural+compressive encodings
             (miniblock/fullzip, FSST, bit-packing, RLE, byte-stream-split) and decomposes every
             vector index into the three composable parts IVF clustering + sub-index (FLAT/HNSW) +
             quantization (PQ/SQ/RaBitQ) with residual encoding and an uncompressed-vector refine
             rerank.
innovation:  The single most transferable idea is Lance's per-buffer compression cascade with a
             transparent-vs-opaque encoding distinction: each column buffer is independently
             probed through an ordered decision (constant -> dictionary -> bitpack -> RLE -> FSST
             -> byte-stream-split -> general zstd/lz4 -> raw), and encodings are tagged
             transparent (a single value is extractable at a known offset without decoding
             neighbors, e.g. bitpack/FSST/dict) vs opaque (zstd block, needs full decompress).
             This transparency tag is exactly what lets nest pick aggressive entropy coders for
             cold sections while keeping the embeddings hot path random-accessible from mmap.
techniques:
  - Compression cascade: ordered per-buffer selector
    constant->dict->bitpack->RLE->FSST->byte-stream-split->general(zstd/lz4)->flat, chosen by
    cheap stats (cardinality budget num_values/2, dict-size-ratio<=0.8, RLE
    run_count/num_values<0.5, FSST when max_len>=5 and total>=32KB)
  - Transparent vs opaque encoding taxonomy: transparent (bitpack, FSST, dictionary, flat)
    supports O(1) random access to a single value at known offset/length; opaque (zstd/lz4 block)
    forbids it
  - Byte-stream-split (BSS): de-interleave f32/f64 by byte position so each plane has lower
    entropy, then general-compress; entropy-auto-detected, the key trick for compressing float
    embeddings
  - FSST (Fast Static Symbol Table): one symbol table per page, substring->1-byte-code,
    transparent string compression for chunks_canonical text
  - Miniblock structural layout for narrow types: ~4-8KiB power-of-two chunks, 8-byte aligned,
    tiny metadata (24-41 bytes/chunk) tuned to control random-access read amplification
  - Fullzip structural layout for large values (>=256B, embeddings/blobs): control-word + size +
    value zipped into one buffer with a u64 repetition index for O(1) row access on scans
  - Vector index decomposition IVF x sub-index x quantization with PQ residual encoding (quantize
    vector-minus-centroid) and transposed __pq_code uint8 codes
  - refine_factor rerank: ANN/quantized search returns candidates, then re-scores against
    uncompressed full-precision vectors -- identical contract to nest's mandatory exact-cosine
    rerank
nest steals: Three concrete steals. (1) In nest-format/encoding, add a byte-stream-split codec for the
             embeddings section before the int8/f16 step: de-interleave f32 by byte plane (4
             planes) so high-order exponent bytes RLE/zstd-compress hugely; but since nest scores
             embeddings directly from mmap on the hot path, expose it ONLY as a new RESERVED
             encoding id (4-255 space) used for a cold/archival frozen-embeddings variant, never
             the scored path -- this respects the never-compress-scored-embeddings rule while
             still cutting file size. (2) In nest-format/encoding for chunks_canonical(text), add
             FSST as a transparent codec id so text stays single-value random-accessible for
             citations (nest://content_hash/chunk_id must reopen an exact span) -- FSST gives ~2x
             over raw while preserving the O(1) span access that block-zstd destroys; directly
             advances compress-more-than-everyone on the text section. (3) Borrow the
             transparent-vs-opaque tag as a one-bit manifest field per section/encoding so
             nest-runtime knows whether a section permits zero-copy span extraction vs needs a
             decode buffer -- formalizes the constraint nest enforces by convention. The
             PQ-residual + transposed-codes scheme is a future hnsw_index(0x07) upgrade path (add
             a quantized-storage sibling), and Lance's refine_factor validates nest's exact-rerank
             contract as prior art.
compression: Directly central to the compress-more-than-everyone north star. Byte-stream-split + general
             compression is the standard high-ratio trick for float embedding archives and is
             missing from nest's encoding set (nest has raw/zstd/f16/int8 but no byte-plane
             split). FSST gives transparent text compression that, unlike nest's current
             section-level zstd, preserves the O(1) citation-span access nest's
             content_hash/chunk_id citations require. The transparent-vs-opaque taxonomy is the
             conceptual framework nest needs to safely add aggressive codecs without breaking the
             mmap hot path.
perf:        Disk-first design: Lance is reportedly the only vector DB where all vector indexes are
             disk-resident, read zero-copy via the columnar format; embeddings and PQ codes are
             mmapped/paged, not RAM-loaded. Miniblock targets 4-8KiB chunks to bound read
             amplification; an LRU search cache preloads page locations, encodings, miniblock
             metadata, dictionaries, and repetition index, amortizing init cost over reader
             lifetime. BSS and entropy auto-detection add encode-time cost but are
             statistics-driven. PQ training (k-means) is noted as slow and memory-intensive -- a
             caution for nest deterministic builds (k-means seeding must be fixed for
             byte-identical reproducibility). f32 BSS miniblock caps at 1024 values/chunk, f64 at
             512, aligning with SIMD batch sizes; the uncompressed-vector refine step keeps
             returned scores exact like nest's rerank.
license:     Apache-2.0 (both lancedb/lancedb and lancedb/lance)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] pgvector/pgvector
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A PostgreSQL extension implementing exact + approximate (HNSW, IVFFlat) vector search over four
             vector types (float32 vector, float16 halfvec, binary bit, sparsevec) with
             scalar/binary quantization codecs, distance operators for
             L2/cosine/inner-product/L1/Hamming/Jaccard, and a
             binary-quantize-coarse-filter-then-exact-rerank retrieval pattern.
innovation:  The binary-quantize-then-exact-rerank pipeline.
techniques:
  - binary_quantize(): sign-bit threshold at zero (x>0 -> 1, x<=0 -> 0), packing each dim into 1
    bit -> dimensions/8 + 8 bytes, scored via Hamming distance (popcount of XOR)
  - halfvec scalar quantization: IEEE float16 (2 bytes/dim) giving 2.0-3.0x shrink with
    near-identical recall because the most significant bits dominate distance
  - sparsevec compressed encoding: store only non-zeros as (index,value) pairs, 8 bytes per nnz +
    16 byte header, format {1:0.1,3:0.2}/dim
  - HNSW on-disk layout: 36-byte HnswMetaPageData (magic 0xA953A953, m, efConstruction, entry
    blkno/offno/level, insertPage) on page 0; element tuples carry vector inline + heaptids[10]
    dedup array + 6-byte neighbortid pointer; neighbor tuples sized (level+2)*m with 6-byte
    ItemPointerData refs
  - two-stage retrieval: over-fetch K' (e.g. 800) by quantized distance, then ORDER BY exact
    distance LIMIT K (e.g. 10)
  - heaptids[10] dedup: identical vectors share one graph node, collapsing duplicate embeddings
  - expression/cast indexing: index embedding::halfvec or binary_quantize(embedding)::bit so the
    same source column drives multiple quantized indexes
  - iterative index scans (strict_order/relaxed_order) to keep filtering past the first ef_search
    batch without recall collapse
nest steals: Add a binary quantization tier to nest-format encoding (next reserved encoding id, additive within
             v1): a binary codec that sign-bit-thresholds each embedding dim at 0 into a packed
             bitmap section, dimensions/8 bytes per vector vs 4 bytes/dim raw = ~32x shrink on the
             embeddings section, which is nest's single largest section and the one its zstd
             policy refuses to touch. In nest-runtime, add a Hamming popcount kernel to the simd
             dispatcher (AVX2 _mm256 popcnt / NEON vcnt / scalar count_ones) that scores the bit
             vectors straight off mmap, used ONLY as a coarse candidate generator that
             over-fetches K*~50-80x, then feeds nest's already-mandatory exact cosine rerank,
             materializing real f32 from a sidecar full-precision embeddings section (or from
             int8). This yields a new "nano" preset below tiny that beats 0.283 size ratio while
             the search_contract guarantee (returned score is always real cosine) is preserved
             unchanged. Separately, adopt the heaptids-style dedup in nest-format writer: chunks
             whose embeddings are byte-identical collapse to one stored vector, helping the
             deterministic-build/compress-more goal. The sparsevec (index,value) encoding maps
             directly onto nest's bm25_index(0x08) and any future sparse-embedding section. Skip
             pgvector's page/TOAST machinery entirely (Postgres-coupled, irrelevant to a single
             mmapped file).
compression: Directly central. Binary quantization is the highest-leverage missing codec for nest's "compress
             more than everyone" north star: the embeddings section is the dominant volume and is
             deliberately excluded from zstd, so a 1-bit-per-dim packed bitmap (~32x over raw f32,
             ~8x over int8) is the biggest single win available, and pgvector proves with real
             recall numbers (91-99% at 1536-dim with rerank) that the exact-rerank step nest
             already mandates is exactly what makes it safe. halfvec confirms nest's float16
             choice; sparsevec gives a concrete encoding for sparse sections.
perf:        halfvec doubles vectors-per-page and gives identical-or-better QPS/latency vs float32. Binary
             quantization stacked with halfvec gave 67x HNSW build speedup (0.7.0 vs 0.5.1).
             Hamming distance is a single XOR + popcount per vector, far cheaper than f32 dot
             product, so the coarse stage is near-free; recall depends on bit-diversity (fails on
             low-dim sift-128/gist-960, excels at high-dim 1536). Over-fetch ratio (e.g. 800-to-10
             = 80x) is the recall/cost knob. HNSW graph must fit in RAM in pgvector; nest
             sidesteps this by mmapping the whole file and letting the OS page it.
license:     PostgreSQL License (permissive, BSD/MIT-like; compatible with vendoring or clean-room
             reimplementation)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] sqliteai/sqlite-columnar
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A self-contained SQLite loadable extension that adds column-oriented virtual tables backed by
             shadow tables, fixed 65,536-row chunks, and per-chunk min/max zone maps so analytical
             scans/aggregations skip non-matching rowid ranges and aggregate natively in C,
             hitting up to 130,000x speedups without patching SQLite's pager/btree/VDBE.
innovation:  Zone-map chunk-skipping: partition a column into fixed-size chunks (65,536 rows), store a tiny
             precomputed min/max summary per chunk, and at query time prune entire chunks whose
             [min,max] cannot satisfy the predicate before touching any data; paired with an
             incremental dirty-chunk invalidation model where writes only mark their chunk dirty
             and re-analyze rebuilds just those chunks (no-op analyze ~2ms vs ~66s cold build).
techniques:
  - Fixed 65,536-row chunk partitioning per column (power-of-two, alignment-friendly)
  - Per-chunk zone maps (min/max) for range-filter pruning before any scan (3 of 153 chunks
    selected in their range query)
  - Shadow-table layout: separate tables for rowids, column values, global stats, chunk zone maps,
    dirty-chunk set, table metadata
  - Incremental dirty-chunk invalidation: writes mark chunks dirty, analyze rebuilds only dirty
    chunks, valid+no-dirty short-circuits
  - Native C hash aggregation over only the required column shadow tables (skips row-store VDBE
    overhead)
  - Result-hash equivalence harness: every columnar helper auto-verified against the equivalent
    plain-SQLite query
  - Variance bench methodology: deterministic datasets, warm-once, repeat, report median and p95
nest steals: Steal the zone-map/chunk-skipping idea into nest-runtime/src/search.rs and the embeddings section
             of nest-format. (1) nest-format: add an OPTIONAL additive per-block summary sidecar
             to the embeddings section (RESERVED section id 0x09+, or a metadata block), storing
             per fixed-size block of N vectors (e.g. 1024) a coarse summary: a per-block centroid
             plus a max-norm/radius bound giving a cheap IVF/triangle-inequality upper bound on
             achievable cosine. Additive within v1, deterministic if centroids are computed in
             fixed order. (2) nest-runtime search.rs: today the exact path is an unconditional
             linear SIMD dot-product scan over all n_embeddings; before that loop, compute the
             per-block upper-bound cosine vs the query and skip whole blocks that cannot beat the
             current top-k threshold. This turns the exact path into pruned-exact that still
             returns TRUE cosine, preserving the search-contract guarantee (scores are real
             cosine, never an ann proxy), and also accelerates the MANDATORY exact rerank after
             HNSW/BM25. (3) nest-cli stats: report blocks-scanned vs blocks-pruned, mirroring
             their zone-map effectiveness output. The dirty-chunk model is the blueprint for the
             north-star real-time-edit feature: a re-embedded chunk only recomputes its own block
             centroid/bound.
compression: Indirect but real. Do NOT copy their on-disk layout for compression: their columnar store is ~1.2x
             LARGER than row store (7.0 GB vs 5.9 GB on 10M rows) because the value is
             scan-pruning, not byte reduction. Transferable compression-adjacent angle: zone-map
             block summaries are tiny overhead (one centroid + scalar bound per ~1024 vectors)
             that unlocks skipping work, letting nest push more aggressive lossy quantization
             (int8/float16) on bulk vectors while keeping a small exact summary to bound error and
             prune. Pairs with the tiny preset to hold recall at smaller size, and the per-block
             centroid can double as a residual-quantization anchor (vector = centroid + small
             residual) for a future codec that compresses embeddings structurally rather than just
             float16/int8.
perf:        Up to 130,582.95x on simple precomputed aggregates (sum/avg/count read straight from global stats,
             not scanning), 6-14x on grouped hash aggregation, 248-273x on zone-map-pruned
             clustered range queries. The durable generalizable win for nest is the 248-273x
             range-pruning class, not the headline aggregate numbers. Chunk size 65,536; range
             query touched only 3 of 153 chunks. No SIMD or entropy coding on their side;
             aggregation is plain C hash aggregation. Incremental analyze: cold 66,763ms, no-op
             2.114ms.
license:     Elastic License 2.0 (source-available, NOT OSI-open; commercial license required for
             managed-service/production use from SQLite Cloud, Inc). Do NOT vendor or copy their
             code into nest. Re-implement the algorithmic idea (zone maps / chunk-skipping is
             decades-old DB prior art, unencumbered) from scratch; never lift EL2.0 source.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[REFERENCE] asg017/sqlite-vss
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A SQLite extension (vss0 virtual table + vector0 codec module) that bolts Facebook Faiss onto
             SQLite by serializing an entire Faiss index into a single BLOB inside an fts5-style
             shadow table, with index type chosen declaratively via Faiss factory strings.
innovation:  The clean separation of a storage-agnostic vector codec layer (vector0: a 1-byte-type-header +
             float32-LE compact BLOB encoding plus a JSON ingest path, exposed as
             vector_to_blob/vector_from_blob) from the index/search layer (vss0), where the index
             type is selected declaratively through a Faiss "factory string" DSL (Flat,IDMap2 vs
             IVF4096,Flat,IDMap2). The codec and the index are independent contracts.
techniques:
  - vector0 compact BLOB codec: leading type byte + raw float32 little-endian payload, dual ingest
    from JSON text or raw bytes
  - fts5-style virtual-table design with two shadow tables: xyz_data (rowid bookkeeping, no-op
    column) + xyz_index (one row holding the whole serialized Faiss index BLOB)
  - Faiss factory-string DSL to declaratively compose index pipelines (Flat exhaustive vs IVF4096
    inverted-file with k-means centroids requiring an explicit operation='training' pass)
  - delegating only math kernels to Faiss via knn_L2sqr_by_idx / range_search_L2sqr while keeping
    storage outside Faiss (proposed non-Faiss 'incremental' storage format)
  - per-table selectable distance metrics (L2, inner product, L1, Linf, Canberra, BrayCurtis,
    JensenShannon) via metric_type
  - vss_search_params() to pass nprobe-style runtime ANN knobs into the KNN call
nest steals: Two adaptations. (1) Codec/index separation validates nest-format's existing split: keep the
             embeddings section (float32-LE payload, analogous to vector0) and the optional
             hnsw_index(0x07) section (analogous to the Faiss index) as fully independent section
             contracts so an index can be rebuilt or dropped without touching embeddings. (2)
             Generalize the Faiss factory-string DSL into nest: turn nest-cli presets
             (exact/compressed/tiny/hybrid) plus the search_contract section into a small
             declarative index-spec string (e.g. 'hnsw(m=16,ef=200)+bm25(rrf)') persisted in
             nest-format so a .nest file self-describes deterministically how to search it.
             CRITICAL NEGATIVE LESSON for nest-format/nest-runtime: sqlite-vss's single-BLOB
             whole-index serialization forces a hard ~1GB ceiling, full-index rewrite
             write-amplification on every commit, and forbids mmap (must fit in RAM). nest's
             mmap-off-disk SIMD scoring and per-section physical layout already defeat all three;
             encode this as a guardrail that hnsw_index(0x07) must stay an independently-paged,
             mmap-scannable section, never one monolithic compressed blob.
compression: Low-to-negative for extreme compression. sqlite-vss does NO vector compression in its default
             path: vector0 stores raw float32-LE; the only size reduction is from optionally
             choosing a Faiss PQ/IVF factory string (product quantization), which then cannot mmap
             and pays full-rewrite cost. The transferable compression insight is purely
             cautionary: a monolithic serialized-index BLOB is the wrong on-disk shape. nest's
             per-section encodings (zstd on non-embedding sections, f16/int8 on embeddings, hnsw
             as a paged section) are strictly superior for both compactness and serve-from-mmap.
             Use as a counter-example, not a source.
perf:        CPU-only (no GPU). Indices must fit entirely in RAM - no mmap support. Every INSERT/DELETE +
             COMMIT rewrites the ENTIRE Faiss index BLOB (O(N) write amplification: inserting 1
             vector into a 1M-vector index rewrites 1,000,001 vectors). Hard ~1GB index ceiling
             from SQLite's single-row BLOB limit. IVF training data read fully into memory. No
             UPDATE support (INSERT/DELETE only). SIMD comes only indirectly from Faiss internal
             kernels, not from sqlite-vss code.
license:     MIT (depends on Faiss, MIT)

# category: graph-db  (15 repos)
# focus: graph databases, engines, and traversal
