[HIGH] kuzudb/kuzu
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Kuzu is an embedded, single-file columnar graph DBMS (Cypher, ACID, archived Oct 2025) that stores
             nodes as Parquet-style compressed node groups and relationships/HNSW topology as
             bit-packed disk-resident CSR adjacency lists, with a native NaviX HNSW vector index
             that runs SIMD distance kernels directly on buffer-manager pages.
innovation:  Lossless floating-point compression (ALP - Adaptive Lossless floating-Point) makes it viable to
             compress embedding-grade float arrays WITHOUT giving up exact, in-place SIMD scoring:
             ALP maps doubles/floats that originated as decimals to integers via two
             multiplications then bit-packs to the minimum bit width (ALPRD splits the bit pattern
             into a dictionary-coded left part + bit-packed right part), all in 1024-value vectors
             using auto-vectorizing scalar code. This breaks nest's current hard assumption that
             "embeddings can never be compressed because they are scored straight from mmap."
techniques:
  - ALP / ALPRD lossless float compression: doubles->int via 2 multiplications + bitpack to min
    bits, or split bitwise into dict-coded left part + bitpacked right part, in 1024-value vectors
    (FastLanes-style auto-vectorized scalar kernels)
  - Per-column-chunk adaptive codec selection driven by node-group min/max zone maps (constant,
    RLE, integer bitpacking with chosen bit width, string dictionary, ALP for floats)
  - Integer bitpacking with per-chunk bit-width chosen from observed value range (INT8/16/32/64),
    incl. bitpacked InternalIDs and var-list offsets
  - Disk-based CSR adjacency lists with automatic bit compression, packing multiple adjacency
    lists into a single 4KB page for cache efficiency
  - NaviX two-level HNSW: small upper layer (GU) pinned in memory, large lower layer (GL) stored
    as on-disk CSR relationship table, vectors stored separately via buffer manager
  - Zero-copy in-buffer-manager distance computation: SIMD (SimSIMD) kernels run directly on page
    bytes with no copy, ~1.6x latency win
  - Predicate-agnostic filtered ANN via node semimasks + adaptive-local heuristic picking
    onehop-s/directed/blind per candidate using local selectivity sigma_l
  - In-memory metadata file mapping (node-group id, column name) -> compressed column-chunk
    location
nest steals: Three concrete steals. (1) nest-format encoding codec: add ALP/ALPRD as a new RESERVED encoding id
             (4-255 space, additive within v1) for the embeddings section, plus per-section
             adaptive codec choice driven by min/max stats. This is the big unlock: lossless f32
             embedding compression that still decodes to exact bits, so the search contract's
             exact-cosine-rerank stays byte-true while shrinking the largest section. Decode must
             be a tiny auto-vectorizing scalar kernel to stay single-file/offline/deterministic.
             (2) nest-format hnsw_index(0x07): store HNSW neighbor lists as bit-packed CSR
             (offsets + min-bit-width neighbor ids) instead of fixed-width arrays, and bitpack the
             var-length offset array - cuts the index section size, directly improving the 'tiny'
             preset's 0.283 ratio. (3) nest-runtime simd dispatcher + search: formalize the
             zero-copy 'distance on mmap bytes, never copy' discipline nest already relies on, and
             if ALP lands, fuse on-the-fly ALP decode + AVX2/NEON dot-product per 1024-lane block
             so embeddings decompress in-register on the hot path instead of being materialized.
             The adaptive-local semimask heuristic is a reference design for a future
             filtered/graph search path.
compression: Directly central. ALP is the single most relevant external idea found: it is the state-of-the-art
             lossless float codec (beats Gorilla/Chimp/Patas/Elf and matches/beats Zstd while
             being far faster) and it specifically targets FLOAT/DOUBLE arrays - nest's
             embeddings, the section nest currently refuses to compress and the dominant byte cost
             of every container. Landing ALP-decode-then-SIMD-score lets nest compress its biggest
             section losslessly without breaking exact in-place scoring, advancing the 'compress
             more than everyone' north star past today's int8/f16 (which are LOSSY) by adding a
             LOSSLESS float path. Bit-packed CSR further shrinks the hnsw_index section the 'tiny'
             preset already ships.
perf:        ALP decodes via scalar code that auto-vectorizes (FastLanes blocks of 1024) - fast enough for a
             query hot path, unlike Gorilla/Chimp. NaviX zero-copy in-buffer distance compute gave
             ~1.6x latency improvement vs copying; uses SimSIMD for SIMD distance kernels. HNSW
             lower layer GL ~7.8GB vs upper GU ~200MB on Wikipedia dataset (upper stays resident).
             Disk-based ANN costs ~2 buffer-manager lookups per adjacency access (vs DiskANN
             co-locating vector+neighbors in one page) - a tradeoff nest could avoid by
             co-locating. Vectors up to 1024 dims; efs tunes recall (~95% target). Multiple
             bit-packed adjacency lists per 4KB page improve cache locality.
license:     MIT License

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] FalkorDB/FalkorDB
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A property-graph database built on Redis modules that represents every relationship type as a
             GraphBLAS sparse adjacency matrix and executes openCypher traversals as sparse linear
             algebra (matrix multiply / masked semirings) rather than pointer-chasing adjacency
             lists.
innovation:  Model a graph as a set of sparse boolean adjacency matrices and let an adaptive storage layer
             (SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS) pick the most compact of four formats per matrix at runtime
             -- hypersparse (DCSC) / sparse (CSC) / bitmap / full -- plus an "iso" mode that
             stores a single scalar instead of a value array when all stored entries are identical
             (e.g. unweighted edges = all-true). Adjacency is thus compressed by structure
             (sparsity-tier selection) AND by value (iso de-duplication), and the entire k-hop
             traversal collapses to A^k matrix products, so the relationship topology is both the
             index and the query plan.
techniques:
  - Adaptive 4-tier sparse format auto-selection per matrix: hypersparse DCSC (only nonempty
    columns get a pointer), sparse CSC, bitmap (1 bit/cell), full -- chosen by density so storage
    tracks actual occupancy
  - Iso / uniform-valued matrices: when every stored value is identical, store ONE scalar instead
    of an N-length value array (ideal for boolean adjacency / unweighted graphs)
  - Doubly-compressed sparse column (DCSC/hypersparse): a second index level over column pointers
    so empty columns cost zero, scaling to billions of dimensions with few nonzeros
  - Delta-Matrix write buffering: M (committed) + delta-plus (pending adds) + delta-minus (pending
    deletes); reads merge the three, writes batch until DELTA_MAX_PENDING_CHANGES then a single
    GrB_wait flush, avoiding per-write resort
  - Traversal-as-linear-algebra: friends-of-friends = F^2, pattern (A)->(B)->(C)<-(A) =
    A*B*transpose(A); masked matrix-multiply with semirings prunes the result during the multiply
  - One matrix per relation type + one type-agnostic adjacency matrix + symmetric diagonal label
    matrices, so labels/edge-types are selected by matrix choice not by per-row filtering
  - Transpose matrices maintained alongside forward matrices so reverse traversal is also a
    multiply, never a scan
nest steals: Add an optional graph/relations section to nest-format (reserved section id 0x09+, additive within
             v1) that stores adjacency as a DCSC/hypersparse triple (column-pointer array,
             row-index array, optional iso-scalar value) with all integers little-endian unsigned
             per the repr(C) rule -- this lets a .nest container carry the edge structure of a
             corpus (citation links, chunk-to-chunk references, knowledge-graph edges) at
             near-zero extra bytes when iso/boolean. The biggest steal is the FORMAT-SELECTION
             HEURISTIC for nest-format's encoding layer: today encodings (raw/zstd/f16/int8) are
             chosen per-preset, but FalkorDB's per-matrix density-tiered pick (bitmap when dense,
             hypersparse when ultra-sparse, iso when constant) is exactly the discipline nest
             needs to choose the smallest codec PER SECTION based on measured statistics,
             deterministically. In nest-runtime, a small CSC/DCSC reader behind the existing
             mmap+SIMD dispatcher enables graph-search (1-2 hop neighbor expansion as sparse
             mat-vec straight off mmap, scalar-portable, no GraphBLAS dependency) which is a named
             north-star gap. The delta-plus/delta-minus buffer pattern is the clean blueprint for
             nest's planned real-time edit: keep the frozen mmap'd section immutable and merge a
             tiny pending-add/pending-delete overlay at read time, flushing to a new deterministic
             container on commit -- preserving the single-file/deterministic guarantees.
compression: High and direct. Iso (single-scalar) storage plus density-tiered sparse formats is a textbook way
             to compress structured/relational data far below generic zstd: a boolean adjacency
             with N nonzeros costs ~N row-indices + column pointers and ONE value byte, versus a
             dense or text edge list. This is precisely the 'compress whole databases/repos and
             graphs' part of nest's north star, and the per-section format-selection heuristic
             raises the compression ceiling for every section, not just graphs.
perf:        GraphBLAS uses OpenMP-parallel saxpy-vs-dot meta-algorithm for masked matmul and exploits AVX
             where available; the 256-variant masked C<M>=A*B kernel dispatch is overkill for
             nest. For nest, the relevant takeaway is the access pattern, not the parallelism:
             DCSC/CSC are pure flat arrays (col-ptr, row-idx, vals) that mmap and scan linearly,
             so a sparse mat-vec for k-hop neighbor expansion is cache-friendly and SIMD-amenable
             with nest's existing scalar/AVX2/NEON dispatcher -- no need to vendor SuiteSparse
             (heavyweight C, would blow the small-core and offline constraints). Iso matrices also
             make integrity hashing cheaper since the value array is a single scalar.
license:     FalkorDB core is Server Side Public License (SSPL) / source-available (not OSI-permissive);
             SuiteSparse:GraphBLAS itself is Apache-2.0. Do NOT vendor FalkorDB code; reimplement
             the DCSC/iso/delta ideas from scratch (they are public algorithms, CSC/DCSC predate
             FalkorDB) to keep nest's licensing clean.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] surrealdb/surrealdb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        SurrealDB is a Rust multi-model (document/graph/vector/full-text) database that layers SurrealQL
             over pluggable sorted KV backends (RocksDB/SurrealKV/TiKV/FDB), built on two reusable
             in-house encoding crates: `revision` (revision-tolerant binary serialization for
             schema evolution) and `storekey` (order-preserving lexicographic key encoding).
innovation:  The `revision` crate: a `#[revisioned(revision = N)]` derive macro that prefixes every serialized
             struct/enum with a varint revision number, then uses per-field attributes (`start =
             N`, `end = N`, `convert_fn`, `default_fn`) so bytes written by an older schema
             deserialize losslessly into the newest in-memory type via declared migration/default
             functions. This makes binary records forward/backward compatible WITHOUT a
             format-version bump and without a self-describing schema in each record -- the
             version tag is one varint, fields stay positional. It separates a cheap additive
             field-add (default_fn) from a true breaking change (convert_fn), which is exactly
             nest's v1-additive vs v2-breaking distinction, but expressed at per-field granularity
             instead of per-file.
techniques:
  - revisioned macro: leading varint u16 revision tag per record + positional
    (non-self-describing) field layout, so the schema lives in code not in bytes
  - per-field lifecycle attributes start=N / end=N with convert_fn (old->new migration) and
    default_fn (fill new fields) -- additive vs breaking encoded per field
  - optimised envelope: u16 revision varint | u32_le payload_length | optional field offsets |
    fields, enabling O(1) skip of whole records
  - SkipRevisioned / WalkRevisioned traits: consume-without-allocate skip and per-element walk to
    seek one field/blob without decoding the rest (lazy partial decode off a buffer)
  - specialised-vectors bulk encoding for Vec<primitive>/Vec<Uuid>/Vec<Decimal> (contiguous, no
    per-element framing) vs per-element framing for sets/heaps
  - storekey: order-preserving binary key codec where lexicographic byte order == logical order
    (sign-flip signed ints, IEEE754 float bit-twiddle, null-escaped strings), aiming for fewest
    bytes
  - hierarchical key construction (ns -> db -> tb -> id) so every logical scope is a contiguous
    byte-range prefix scan; prefix()/suffix() range mappers per item type
  - HNSW/DiskANN low-precision storage TYPE clause (F32/F16/I8/U8) -- quantization is a per-index
    storage-type choice decoupled from the distance metric
nest steals: Adopt the `revision` pattern in nest-format for the manifest and future binary sub-records,
             augmenting today's JCS canonical-JSON manifest
             (crates/nest-format/src/manifest/canonical.rs). Concretely: give each reserved
             section (ids 0x09+) and each future manifest field a `start = N` revision tag so a v1
             reader can skip-or-default unknown additive fields deterministically -- turning
             nest's prose rule ('additive-only within v1; bump NEST_FORMAT_VERSION only on meaning
             change') into machine-checked per-field metadata, and crucially distinguishing a
             default_fn add (stays v1) from a convert_fn change (forces a true v2). The
             SkipRevisioned/WalkRevisioned traits map onto nest-runtime's mmap hot path: lazily
             walk a section to one chunk's span without decoding neighbors, complementing
             reader/parse.rs. Separately, steal `storekey` order-preserving encoding for
             nest-format/src/sections/chunk_ids.rs and any future sorted index (e.g. a
             chunk_id->offset table or a graph-edge section): sign-flipped/escaped lexicographic
             keys enable prefix range-scans and let monotonic ids be delta+varint compressed far
             tighter than raw u64 arrays, directly serving 'compress more than everyone'. Both are
             pure codecs: deterministic (positional layout + canonical order) and offline (no
             socket), respecting nest's hard constraints; keep each new rust file <=300 lines.
compression: Real but layered. `revision`'s positional non-self-describing layout plus a one-varint version tag
             is far more byte-frugal than nest's JSON manifest and gives compatibility for free,
             removing per-record schema overhead. `storekey`'s order-preserving + minimal-byte key
             encoding is the directly compression-relevant piece: sorted, sign-flipped, escaped
             keys make chunk_ids and any index section delta-encodable and varint/zstd-friendly
             (monotone ids compress near-entropy), and the prefix-scan hierarchy avoids storing
             redundant high-order key bytes. DiskANN's per-index F16/I8/U8 storage type confirms
             nest's tiny-preset int8 quantization is mainstream.
perf:        revision uses bincode under the hood; specialised-vectors gives contiguous bulk encode/decode for
             Vec<primitive> (fast, cache-friendly) but its walk path rejects each element before
             reading length (must use DeserializeRevisioned/SkipRevisioned there). The u32_le
             payload_length + optional offset table enables O(1) record skip and selective field
             decode -- good for mmap partial reads. storekey targets minimal byte count, keeping
             key sections small and zstd-friendly. SurrealDB 3.0 added a streaming/batched
             execution engine and concurrent HNSW writes; DiskANN (DEGREE 64, L_BUILD 100, ALPHA
             1.2) targets larger-than-memory graphs -- not needed by nest's single-file in-memory
             model but validates int8 quantization.
license:     SurrealDB core: BSL 1.1 (converts to Apache-2.0 after ~4 years). The reusable sub-crates
             `revision` and `storekey` are permissive (Apache-2.0 / MIT) -- safe to vendor or
             depend on for nest-format. Verify exact SPDX before vendoring.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] alibaba/GraphScope
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A distributed property-graph compute stack (GAE/GIE/GLE on the vineyard zero-copy in-memory store)
             whose most transferable artifact is GraphAr: a chunked, columnar, CSR/CSC-semantic
             on-disk graph format that delta-encodes adjacency to reach ~27% of naive plain+offset
             storage.
innovation:  Store graph topology as sorted CSR split into a per-vertex offset table plus a destination
             adjacency column, then delta-encode the monotone internal vertex ids and offsets so
             columnar payload reaches ~27.3% of a plain id+offset baseline; dense contiguous
             internal ids are the enabler for both delta coding and zero-copy mmap.
techniques:
  - sorted CSR/CSC: edges ordered_by_source (CSR) or ordered_by_dest (CSC), with an offset table
    whose first entry is 0 recording each vertex's edge run start
  - delta encoding of monotone columns (continuous internal vertex ids + offset arrays) before
    columnar compression -> 27.3% of plain+offset size
  - vineyard compact_edges = variant (varint) + delta encoding of the CSR adjacency
  - dense contiguous internal vertex ids (= row index within a label) enabling a single zero-copy
    mmapped id space and O(1) offset lookup
  - chunking: vertex chunk 2^18, edge chunk 2^22, each physical chunk = one file, enabling
    selective/partial reads of one vertex or one chunk without reading the whole graph
  - self-descriptive YAML metadata that fully determines how to resolve the binary so CSR is
    stored directly with no serialize/deserialize round-trip
  - separation of topology (2-column adjList) from properties (separate columnar property groups)
    so a traversal reads only the adjacency, never property bytes
  - multiple coexisting adjacency orderings (CSR + CSC copies) selected per access pattern
nest steals: Add a reserved section to nest-format (section id 0x09, graph_adjacency) plus a delta+varint codec
             in crates/nest-format/src/encoding/ (a new delta_varint.rs sitting beside
             int8.rs/float16.rs/zstd_codec.rs, registered as a new encoding id in the reserved
             4-255 range, additive so NEST_FORMAT_VERSION stays 1). Layout: store a per-chunk
             offset array (u32/u64 LE, monotone) zig-zag delta-varint encoded, and a
             destination-id adjacency column where dst ids are delta-coded within each source's
             run; vertex ids are nest's existing dense chunk_ids row index, so no extra id map is
             needed. In nest-runtime, add ann::graph (a graph_search path next to ann::HnswIndex)
             that mmaps the offset+adjacency arrays and walks neighbors with O(1) offset lookups
             -- decoding varints lazily per row, never materializing the whole graph -- and feeds
             candidates into the existing mandatory exact-cosine rerank so graph traversal stays a
             candidate generator, not a score source (mirrors the hnsw contract). This unlocks the
             mission's stated 'graph search' goal while keeping single-file, offline,
             deterministic (sorted CSR + alphabetical/seeded order = byte-identical builds), and
             content_hash stable because the decoded adjacency is canonical regardless of varint
             packing.
compression: Directly on-mission. Delta+varint CSR is the canonical way to compress graph adjacency and is
             proven here at 27.3% of plain+offset. It is the missing codec for nest's 'graph
             search' north-star item and generalizes: the same monotone-column delta-varint codec
             also shrinks nest's own chunk_ids and chunks_original_spans (offset/span arrays are
             monotone), so it pays off even before any graph section ships. Unlike zstd it is
             decode-cheap and seekable straight off mmap, fitting the 'score/traverse directly
             from disk, never inflate' hot-path rule.
perf:        Columnar topology/property split means a traversal touches only the 2-column adjList, not property
             bytes (the dominant data). Offset table gives O(1) per-vertex neighbor-run lookup;
             varint decode is sequential and branch-light. Recommended chunk sizes (vertex 2^18,
             edge 2^22) balance file-count overhead vs parallelism; for nest's single-file model
             use one logical chunk per section so there is one mmap region, not thousands of
             files. Delta+varint is far cheaper to decode than general entropy coders, so it suits
             scoring-from-mmap; vineyard demonstrates the CSR can be mapped and shared zero-copy
             with no deserialize step.
license:     Apache-2.0 (GraphScope and the spun-out Apache GraphAr are both Apache-2.0); format spec and
             encoding ideas are freely adoptable, clean-room reimplementation in Rust is trivial
             since the technique is just delta+varint over sorted CSR.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] blazegraph/database
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Blazegraph (formerly bigdata) is a Java RDF/SPARQL graph database built on a key-range-partitioned
             B+Tree over a single-file journal (RWStore), where every triple/quad is materialized
             into multiple sorted "covering" statement indexes (SPO/POS/OSP) whose unsigned-byte[]
             keys are compressed at the page level with front-coding and order-preserving
             canonical Huffman raba coders.
innovation:  The "raba coder" page model: B+Tree leaves store keys as a logical sorted byte[][] and compress it
             with FrontCodedRabaCoder using a ratio parameter -- every `ratio` keys one full key
             is emitted, the rest store only (shared-prefix-length, suffix) -- AND the codec is
             searchable in place (binary search over the periodic full keys, then linear scan
             inside the bucket), so you decompress nothing to look up. A sibling
             CanonicalHuffmanRabaCoder compresses while strictly preserving lexicographic order,
             so even Huffman-coded keys remain range-scannable. This is order-preserving,
             search-on-compressed, tunable-ratio prefix compression for sorted byte strings.
techniques:
  - Front coding with tunable ratio: every Nth sorted key stored in full, intervening keys as
    (shared_prefix_len, suffix); binary-search the full keys then linear-scan the front-coded
    bucket -- no full decompression to search
  - Order-preserving canonical Huffman coder for byte[][] (CanonicalHuffmanRabaCoder) that keeps
    lexicographic order of coded values, so range scans work on compressed keys
  - Covering/clustered indexes: same triples materialized in multiple key orderings (SPO,POS,OSP
    triples; 6 for quads, YARS-style) so every access pattern is a prefix range-scan with locality
  - Value inlining: small RDF values encoded directly INTO the statement-index key with
    order-preserving encoding to skip dictionary indirection
  - Two-way dictionary (TERM2ID forward, ID2TERM reverse) mapping terms to compact numeric
    term-ids so statement keys are fixed-width integer tuples
  - BLOBS index: large literals pulled into a separate hash-keyed index so dictionary index stride
    stays small and cache-friendly
  - RWStore fixed-size slot allocators + free-lists with small-slot optimization (~1024B), page
    split when a leaf exceeds 8k after compression
  - Unsigned big-endian byte[] keys so numeric/temporal order == lexicographic byte order, which
    makes prefix range-scans and front coding correct
nest steals: Adopt front-coded, search-on-compressed encoding for nest's sorted-string sections in nest-format.
             (1) chunk_ids section: encode as a FrontCodedRabaCoder-style block -- sort the ids,
             store every Nth id in full plus (prefix_len:varint, suffix) for the rest, plus a
             small offset table to binary-search the full keys. New encoding id in the RESERVED
             4-255 space (additive, no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump), applied to chunk_ids only, never
             to embeddings (which stay raw/f16/int8 for mmap SIMD scoring). (2) bm25_index(0x08):
             its term dictionary is already required to be alphabetical for determinism -- ideal
             front-coding input, so the vocabulary shrinks with zero loss and stays
             binary-searchable on compressed bytes (no decompress on the search path, preserving
             offline-mmap discipline). The codec lives in crates/nest-format/.../encoding as a
             deterministic pure function (sorted input => byte-identical output), respecting the
             <=300-line single-responsibility rule and typed thiserror (never panic).
             Order-preserving canonical Huffman is a phase-2 entropy upgrade for the same
             dictionaries. The covering-index idea informs nest-runtime: if nest adds graph
             search, materialize edge tuples in multiple sorted orderings so traversals become
             prefix range-scans, not full scans.
compression: Directly on the north star ("compress data more than everyone"). Front coding plus
             order-preserving entropy coding gives large lossless reduction of sorted string
             columns (chunk_ids, bm25 term dictionary) while keeping them searchable on the
             compressed bytes -- nest compresses these harder than a generic zstd pass without
             paying decompression on the hot search path. Textbook technique for the
             sorted-identifier and sorted-vocabulary data nest carries, and it composes with
             existing zstd (front-code first, then zstd the residual).
perf:        Single-file journal mmap-friendly model parallels nest's mmap design. Front coding's key property
             for nest is search-on-compressed: O(log) binary search over periodic full keys +
             O(ratio) linear scan in a bucket touches a few hundred bytes, not a decompressed
             buffer -- keeps the search path allocation-light and cache-resident. The `ratio` knob
             is a direct compression-vs-lookup-latency dial. B+Tree pages split past an 8k
             boundary after compression; RWStore small-slot ~1024B, branching factor 256 --
             evidence ~1-8k compressed blocks are the cache/IO sweet spot, a useful block-size
             hint for nest's encoded sections. No SIMD in the Java codec, so a Rust prefix-compare
             kernel can beat it.
license:     GPLv2 (SYSTAP historically offered a separate commercial license); Java codebase, Amazon acquired
             SYSTAP in 2018 and the OSS project is effectively abandoned -- treat as
             reference/learn, not vendor (GPL + Java rule out direct linking into nest's Rust
             core).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] JanusGraph/janusgraph
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A distributed graph database that maps an adjacency-list graph onto any BigTable-model store
             (Cassandra/HBase/Bigtable), serializing each incident edge as one sorted cell whose
             bytes are sort-key-ordered and shrunk with variable-length integer encoding plus
             delta-encoded neighbor ids.
innovation:  The edge-as-cell encoding: each edge is one byte string laid out so that lexicographic byte order
             equals the logical sort order (label id with direction bit stolen into the low bit,
             then sort-key property bytes, then neighbor id, then edge id), and the neighbor
             vertex id is stored as a signed DELTA against the row-owner vertex id rather than its
             absolute 64-bit value. Combined with locality-aware id assignment (co-accessed
             vertices get numerically close ids), the deltas stay small and the whole edge
             collapses to a few varint bytes. Compression AND traversal-order indexing fall out of
             the SAME byte layout, with no separate index structure.
techniques:
  - Adjacency-list-as-row: vertex = row keyed by 64-bit id, each edge/property = one sorted cell,
    enabling range-scan retrieval of edge subsets
  - Variable-length integer (varint) encoding of label ids, sort keys, neighbor ids, edge ids so
    small numbers cost 1-2 bytes
  - Delta encoding of the adjacent vertex id relative to the owning row's vertex id (store
    difference, not absolute)
  - Direction bit packed into the low bit of the label id (bit-stealing) instead of a separate
    byte
  - Order-preserving serialization: column bytes arranged so lexicographic order == edge-label
    sort order, making the sort the index (vertex-centric indices are column-range scans)
  - Locality-aware id allocation: ids assigned so co-accessed entities have small absolute id
    difference, partitioning data and minimizing deltas
  - Column/value split: structural+sort bytes in the sorted column, signature property values get
    compressed serialization in the value, ad-hoc properties uncompressed in the value
nest steals: Two targeted lifts, both in nest-format (crates/nest-format/src/sections + encoding). (1) Add a
             graph-search section (reserved id 0x09+, additive within v1) when nest grows graph
             search: encode each node's adjacency list as a sorted run of (varint label,
             delta-to-owner neighbor index, varint edge-id) triples with the direction bit stolen
             into the label low bit. chunk_ids are already a dense 0..N index space, so
             delta-to-owner makes neighbor refs 1-2 bytes; a new codec, not a format break. (2)
             Useful even without graph search: apply varint+delta to the existing
             chunks_original_spans section (offset/length pairs are monotonic, locally small) and
             to chunk_ids, as a new encoding id (4-255 reserved) selectable per-section in the
             manifest, scored off mmap with zero-copy varint decode in nest-runtime.
             Order-preserving bytes also reinforce nest's deterministic build: a canonical sort +
             delta layout is inherently reproducible and content-hash-stable.
compression: High for non-embedding sections. Delta+varint is the classic way to compress
             monotonic/locally-clustered integer streams (spans, ids, future adjacency) and stacks
             under zstd for further gains, advancing 'compress more than everyone'. It does NOT
             touch embeddings (kept raw for SIMD hot-path scoring per nest's contract), so it
             complements the tiny/compressed presets rather than conflicting. The delta-to-owner
             trick specifically unlocks compact graph-search payloads later.
perf:        Varint decode is branchy but cache-friendly and zero-copy off mmap; delta decode is a single
             running-sum (prefix-sum) pass, trivially fast and SIMD-able for bulk neighbor-id
             reconstruction (NEON/AVX2 prefix sum). Order-preserving layout means traversal/range
             queries are memcmp-ordered scans, no separate index load. Main cost is encode-time
             sorting and id assignment, a build-time (python) concern, not runtime.
license:     Apache License 2.0 (permissive; safe to learn from and reimplement the encoding ideas in nest's
             own Rust codecs).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] TuGraph-family/tugraph-db
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C++ high-performance property-graph database (Ant Group, LDBC SNB #1) that builds a graph
             storage layer on top of LMDB's memory-mapped B+tree, packing CSR-like adjacency
             topology and compact-encoded properties into single key-value pairs with an adaptive
             mixed/split mapping scheme.
innovation:  Adaptive mapping: a vertex's entire neighborhood (vertex props + out-edges + in-edges + edge
             props) is packed CSR-style into ONE key-value blob when small (mixed mapping), but
             auto-splits into separate VERTEX/OUTEDGE/INEDGE key-value records once the blob
             exceeds a size threshold (merge under ~2KB, split over ~4KB). The split records all
             share the same VertexUid key prefix, so the KV store keeps them physically adjacent
             and a single prefix scan plus two binary searches still serves sequential traversal
             at full speed, while bounding the per-write repack cost. The threshold is
             deliberately tuned to fit hot blobs in L1 cache and to amortize SSD ~150us page
             latency.
techniques:
  - Adaptive mixed/split mapping: single packed KV blob below ~2KB, auto-split into
    VERTEX/OUTEDGE/INEDGE records above ~4KB threshold, sharing a common VertexUid key prefix for
    locality
  - CSR-like topology packing: VertexUid header followed by inline sorted OutEdgeUid[] and
    InEdgeUid[] arrays in one contiguous value, giving pure-sequential adjacency scans
  - Composite EdgeUid key = (SrcVid, LabelId, TemporalId, DstVid, Eid) ordered so same-label edges
    cluster; enables vertex-centric range scans by label and time window
  - Length-based varint compression of EdgeUid: LabelId/DstVid/Eid are small auto-incrementing
    integers from 0, stored in minimal byte length; optional TemporalId omitted entirely when
    absent
  - Compact property packing: [Nullable bitmap | FixedData | VariableOffset table | VariableData]
    so fixed fields are O(1) indexed and variable fields located via offset table, zero pointer
    overhead
  - External-sort bulk import: assign monotonically increasing VertexUid, hold only the vertex
    primary-key->VertexUid map in memory, sort edges externally (sorted insert ~10x faster than
    random)
  - Threshold sizing tuned to hardware: value size bounded to fit L1 cache and amortize SSD
    page-fault latency
  - Optimistic query-level concurrent writer plus WAL durability layered over LMDB's single-writer
    B+tree
nest steals: Two distinct steals. (1) For a future graph_index section (reserved 0x09+, additive within v1):
             adopt the CSR-like adjacency layout verbatim in nest-format -- one contiguous,
             mmap-friendly array of sorted neighbor ids per source node with an offset table,
             which is exactly the SIMD-scannable, zero-pointer, deterministic shape nest already
             uses for embeddings. nest-runtime would traverse it straight off mmap with no
             deserialization, matching the existing hot-path discipline. (2) The bigger,
             immediately-relevant steal for compression is the length-based varint encoding of
             small monotonic integers: nest's chunk_ids section and any id/offset arrays
             (chunks_original_spans offsets, future graph neighbor ids) are dense ascending
             integers ideal for delta+varint or frame-of-reference encoding. Add this as a new
             encoding id (4-255 reserved space) in nest-format/encoding alongside
             raw/zstd/float16/int8, applied to NON-embedding id/offset sections only -- preserving
             the rule that embeddings stay raw for mmap dot-products. The adaptive mixed/split
             threshold idea also maps onto nest's section sizing: keep tiny sections inline, split
             only when a size threshold is crossed, tuned to mmap page (4KB) boundaries.
compression: Directly relevant on the id/offset axis, not the embedding axis. The length-based varint trick
             (exploiting that ids are small auto-incrementing integers from 0) plus delta encoding
             can shrink nest's chunk_ids and span-offset arrays substantially with zero accuracy
             loss and full determinism -- a cheap, lossless win that compounds the tiny preset's
             0.283 ratio. The CSR layout is a compactness win specifically for the eventual
             graph-search capability in nest's north star (graph search over whole
             repos/databases), giving the smallest possible on-disk adjacency representation.
             Neither touches the embedding hot path, so both respect the never-compress-embeddings
             rule.
perf:        LMDB is copy-on-write mmap'd B+tree: zero-copy reads straight from the page cache, exactly nest's
             mmap model. TuGraph targets a 20:1 read:write ratio and chooses B+tree over LSM
             precisely to avoid LSM background-merge latency jitter -- validation for nest's
             static single-file read-mostly stance. Threshold tuned so hot blobs land in L1 and
             writes amortize ~150us SSD page latency. Sorted bulk import is ~10x faster than
             random insert. Single-writer LMDB is the bottleneck they patched with optimistic
             concurrency plus WAL; nest sidesteps this entirely by being build-once/serve-many.
license:     Apache-2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] typedb/typedb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust-rewritten (3.0) typed-hypergraph database that flattens an n-ary entity/relation/attribute
             model with inheritance onto RocksDB's LSM key-value engine using fixed-prefix,
             type-grouped, sort-order-preserving key encodings.
innovation:  The attribute-value IID encoding: a value is packed into a fixed-width, sort-stable key where
             small values inline directly and large/variable-length values (strings) switch to an
             [8-byte prefix][8-byte hash][1-byte tag] form. The high bit of the final tag byte
             flags inline-vs-hashed, the low 7 bits double as either a length (inline) or a
             collision disambiguator counter (hashed, up to 128 colliding values per prefix+hash
             before overflow). This gives a content-addressed, bounded-width handle for
             arbitrarily long values while keeping the first 8 bytes as a sort-meaningful prefix
             so range scans and prefix iteration still work.
techniques:
  - Fixed 1-byte prefix kind tag (e.g. 0x1e entity, 0x1f relation) + TypeID + counter forming a
    self-describing IID that encodes the instance's direct type
  - Type-and-connection-grouped key layout so edges/vertices of the same type/relation sort
    adjacently in the LSM, giving locality and prefix-iterable traversal without pointer chasing
  - Inline-vs-hash attribute encoding: strings <=16 bytes stored raw with a [0b0|7-bit length] tag
    byte (17 bytes total); longer strings as [8B prefix][8B u64 hash][0b1|7-bit disambiguator]
  - Caller-provided &[u8]->u64 hash with snapshot-checked collision disambiguation via a 7-bit
    counter (128 variants per prefix+hash)
  - Struct values always hashed: 1-byte value-type-category + 7-byte hash-with-disambiguator (8
    bytes total)
  - Fixed-width-per-type value encoding (bool/int/double/decimal/date/datetime/duration all 8
    bytes) so keys are memcmp-comparable and sortable
  - Centralized value comparators in one encoding module so traversal predicates (>, <) and sort
    share one byte-order definition (no comparator drift)
  - Disabling vertex/edge caching on read paths so multi-million-element scans stay O(1) memory;
    only written vertices get cached
nest steals: Steal the inline-vs-hash bounded-width value encoding for nest-format. Today nest stores
             chunks_canonical text and chunk_ids as separate sections; adopt TypeDB's
             [prefix][hash][tag] scheme as a new optional codec/representation for chunk_ids and
             any future fixed-width id/key section, so a chunk_id or content-key is a bounded
             17-byte (or 16-byte) handle whose first 8 bytes stay sort-meaningful and whose
             tag-byte high bit distinguishes inline-short vs hashed-long. This is
             content-addressed by construction, aligning with nest's existing content_hash/SHA-256
             surfaces; reusing a truncated content_hash as the 8-byte hash field would let
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id citations resolve via a memcmp prefix scan over a sorted
             id section instead of a hashmap, and keeps the format additive (new encoding id in
             the reserved 4-255 range, deterministic since hashing is seedless). The
             fixed-width-per-type, memcmp-sortable value packing is also directly applicable if
             nest adds a structured/json/parquet column section: pack scalar columns as 8-byte
             sort-stable keys so range filters run as ordered scans straight off mmap. Lower
             relevance to raw embedding compression (those stay raw f32/f16/int8 for SIMD), but
             high relevance to compressing/serving id/key/metadata sections and to the
             graph-search and structured-data items on the north star.
compression: Medium-high for nest's metadata/id sections, low for embeddings. The bounded 17-byte handle
             replaces storing full variable-length keys inline, compressing id/key columns and any
             high-cardinality string column to fixed width while preserving range-scan ability.
             The transferable extreme-compression idea is that the SAME bytes serve as both a sort
             key and a content-addressed handle (prefix stays meaningful, hash gives uniqueness, 1
             tag byte multiplexes mode+length+collision-counter) at zero extra bytes for the
             discriminator. Does not help the embedding hot path (kept raw for SIMD dot products),
             so it complements rather than competes with nest's zstd/f16/int8 codecs.
perf:        RocksDB LSM gives graceful degradation past RAM (TypeDB ~25% throughput drop 100->400 warehouses
             vs Neo4j ~51%), the payoff of memcmp-sortable type-grouped keys over pointer-chasing
             property graphs. Tradeoff: ~24GB vs Neo4j 17GB on disk because TypeDB auto-builds
             bidirectional/ad-hoc indices. Read paths deliberately skip caching vertices/edges to
             scan millions without OOM. Query pipeline is synchronous (non-async) Rust, coupled to
             RocksDB. No SIMD in the encoding path; the win is branch-light fixed-width
             comparisons, not vectorization.
license:     Mozilla Public License 2.0 (MPL-2.0) for the typedb engine; TypeQL grammar Apache-2.0. MPL-2.0 is
             file-level copyleft, so vendoring TypeDB source files into nest would carry MPL
             obligations on those files. Safe path: reimplement the encoding scheme (ideas are not
             copyrightable) rather than copy source.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] apache/tinkerpop
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Apache TinkerPop is a vendor-neutral graph computing framework whose core transferable assets are
             the Gremlin traversal language compiled to language-agnostic bytecode optimized by
             rewrite strategies, and GraphBinary, a compact self-describing length-prefixed
             type-coded binary serialization for graph elements (vertices, edges, paths,
             properties) that round-trips deterministically without an external schema.
innovation:  Two separable ideas. (1) GraphBinary's wire model: every value is `{type_code:1
             byte}{type_info}{value_flag:1 byte}{value}`, integers are fixed-width big-endian, and
             strings/lists/maps/composites are length-prefixed (4-byte length, so each value is
             capped at 2^32-1) so a reader can skip or materialize any value with zero schema
             lookup. GraphBinary4 adds "TinkerPop Defined Types" carrying explicit per-field
             numbers so new fields are additive and old readers skip unknown fields, plus a single
             canonical null replacing per-field null flags. (2) Gremlin's bytecode-as-IR: a query
             is lowered to a language-agnostic serializable instruction list, then a pipeline of
             pure rewrite strategies (FilterRankingStrategy reorders filters cheapest-first,
             IncidentToAdjacentStrategy fuses outE+inV into one adjacency step,
             IdentityRemovalStrategy deletes no-ops) transforms the IR before it compiles to
             executable steps -- optimization happens on a serializable IR, not on live objects.
techniques:
  - GraphBinary fully-qualified value layout {type_code}{type_info}{value_flag}{value} for
    schema-free self-describing decode
  - 4-byte length-prefixing of strings/lists/maps/composites (value capped at 2^32-1, full object
    must be in memory to compute length)
  - GraphBinary4 'TinkerPop Defined Types' with explicit per-field numbers: additive evolution,
    old readers skip unknown fields, single canonical null
  - Graph elements (Vertex/Edge/Path/VertexProperty) as nested composite typed values,
    references-only (parent serialized as null) to avoid cycles
  - IO test-suite-as-spec: golden byte files every serializer must round-trip read+write to prove
    conformance
  - Gremlin bytecode: query lowered to a language-agnostic serializable instruction-list IR
  - Traversal strategy rewrite pipeline on the IR: FilterRankingStrategy,
    IncidentToAdjacentStrategy (outE+inV fusion), IdentityRemovalStrategy
  - Vertex-centric index pattern: outE().has(label).inV compiled to one VertexQuery against a
    log(m) per-vertex edge index
nest steals: Graph search is a stated nest north-star gap; TinkerPop gives two ready blueprints. (1) Add a
             frozen graph section to nest-format (reserved section id 0x09+, additive within v1)
             using GraphBinary4's per-field-number Defined-Type scheme as the encoding so
             adjacency/node-attribute records evolve additively and old readers skip unknown
             fields -- nest's additive-only rule expressed as a wire format. It pairs with
             content_hash because GraphBinary's deterministic byte layout (fixed-width ints,
             sortable map keys) gives reproducible byte-identical output. CRITICAL adaptation:
             mandate little-endian unsigned (GraphBinary is big-endian, so adapt do not copy).
             Adjacency lists as length-prefixed neighbor blocks are mmap-skippable and
             zstd-friendly (topology is a non-embedding section so zstd is allowed), advancing
             compression of whole databases/repos. (2) In nest-runtime add a small graph::plan
             module (under the 300-line cap): represent a graph query as a tiny instruction list
             and run deterministic rewrite strategies (filter-cheapest-first; fuse
             edge-then-vertex hops into one adjacency scan) before executing against the mmapped
             adjacency section. The IncidentToAdjacentStrategy fusion is the highest-value steal:
             it turns two index hops into one sequential mmap scan, stays
             offline/deterministic/never-panics. nest-cli gains a graph-search subcommand
             mirroring search-ann. Skip Gremlin-the-language, Gremlin Server, and OLAP/Spark --
             they violate the no-socket, small-core, offline constraints.
compression: Indirect but real. GraphBinary's length-prefixed fixed-width schema-free layout is a denser
             graph-topology encoding than JSON/GraphSON (TinkerPop docs note a significantly
             smaller wire footprint), and because it carries no inline schema and orders fields by
             number it is highly zstd-compressible -- directly serving the goal to compress
             json/datasets/whole-databases more than everyone. The per-field-number additive
             scheme also lets a graph section shrink over versions without a format bump. It never
             touches embeddings (the hot SIMD path), so no conflict with the
             never-compress-embeddings rule.
perf:        GraphBinary trades streaming for size determinism: length must be known upfront so the full object
             is serialized in memory to compute its 4-byte length, raising peak serialization
             memory and capping any single value at 2^32-1 bytes (fine for nest's
             Python-build/Rust-serve split since the heavy build side is Python). Decode is
             allocation-friendly: length prefixes let the reader pre-size buffers and validate
             bounds, and unknown field numbers are skipped by length without parsing -- ideal for
             mmap. On traversal, TinkerPop benchmarks show strategy application (the optimizer)
             costs ~5-6x bytecode construction, so the rewrite pass is the expensive phase; keep
             nest's strategy set tiny and run it once at plan time, not per-traverser, to respect
             the boot-overhead budget.
license:     Apache License 2.0

# category: semantic-rdf  (7 repos)
# focus: RDF triple stores, semantic web, graph query languages

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] apache/jena
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Apache Jena is a Java RDF/SPARQL framework whose TDB2 store persists triples as three permuted
             B+tree indexes (SPO/POS/OSP, six for quads) over 8-byte NodeIds resolved through a
             dictionary node table, with scalar values inlined directly into the NodeId to skip
             the dictionary entirely.
innovation:  NodeId tagged-union inlining: an 8-byte (64-bit) identifier uses bit 63 as a discriminator - 0
             means the low 63 bits are a pointer (file offset) into the dictionary node table; 1
             means the value is self-contained in the id. A second tag bit then selects an
             encoding: doubles (62-bit double with exponent range reduced 2 bits), or a 6-bit
             XSD-type field + 56-bit value carrying signed integers, decimals (8-bit scale +
             48-bit signed mantissa), dates/datetimes at ms precision, and booleans. The win:
             common scalar literals carry their full value in the index key itself, so they never
             allocate a dictionary entry, never trigger a hash lookup, and sort/compare correctly
             inline. The dictionary is only paid for strings/URIs/IRIs that genuinely need it.
techniques:
  - NodeId tagged-union: bit63 ptr-vs-value, bit62 sub-encoding select, 6-bit xsd type + 56-bit
    payload
  - Inline scalar encodings: 56-bit signed int, 8-bit-scale+48-bit decimal, ms-precision
    date/datetime, boolean, range-reduced 62-bit double
  - Dictionary node table: bidirectional Node<->NodeId via 128-bit MD5 hash key plus sequential
    value file for reverse lookup
  - Permuted multi-index storage: 3 B+trees SPO/POS/OSP (6 for quads) each holding full tuple,
    enabling index-only scans for any bound prefix
  - Fixed-length-key/fixed-length-value custom B+tree (keys are packed NodeId tuples, no
    variable-length records)
  - Rule-based cardinality estimation (stats.opt) reordering patterns by selectivity tokens
    VAR/TERM/URI/LITERAL
  - xloader bulk build: emit binary tuples, external merge-sort via unix sort(1), compress
    intermediate sort runs to shrink peak temp space, build each index as a separate streamed
    phase
  - Copy-on-write MVCC B+tree blocks for single-writer/multi-reader without blocking readers
nest steals: Two concrete steals. (1) Tagged-NodeId inlining maps onto nest-format as a typed-scalar codec for
             structured payloads (json/yaml/parquet columns, dataset cells): instead of a flat
             chunks_canonical text section, add a future column/value section where each cell is
             an 8-byte tagged word - bit63 = inline-vs-span, lower bits = a 4-bit encoding tag +
             payload (i56 int, 8-bit-scale i48 decimal, ms-epoch date, bool, f16/range-reduced
             double). Numbers/dates/bools cost zero dictionary bytes and zero chunk-span
             indirection; only strings keep a chunks_original_spans pointer. This is a new
             RESERVED section id (0x09+) and a new encoding id (4-255) - fully additive within v1,
             no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump. Directly advances compress-more-than-everyone for
             tabular/json corpora where today every integer round-trips through text+zstd. (2) The
             xloader pattern - stream tuples, external-merge-sort, compress intermediate runs - is
             the blueprint for nest-python's deterministic builder when corpora exceed RAM: sort
             chunk_ids/terms with a stable alphabetical external sort (matching the bm25
             deterministic-term-order constraint), zstd the spill files, build
             hnsw_index(0x07)/bm25_index(0x08) as separate streamed passes; keeps builds
             deterministic/reproducible while removing the in-RAM ceiling. (3) Reference-only:
             SPO/POS/OSP permuted-index idea informs a future graph-search section - store an edge
             tuple in multiple sort orders so any bound prefix is an index-only range scan, the
             on-disk substrate for the north-star graph search.
compression: High for structured/tabular data. The inline-NodeId trick is a domain-specific entropy win
             orthogonal to zstd: it removes dictionary entries and span-pointer indirection for
             the most common literal types (ints, dates, bools, decimals), packing each into <=8
             self-describing bytes that also sort correctly. For json/yaml/parquet/dataset
             payloads (explicitly in nest north-star scope) this beats text+zstd because zstd
             still pays for the textual representation and a per-value span offset. The embedding
             hot path and mandatory exact-cosine rerank are untouched - this is a non-embedding
             section, so it can even be zstd-wrapped on top.
perf:        NodeIds are fixed 8 bytes, so B+tree keys are fixed-length tuples - comparisons are word-aligned
             integer compares, ideal for branchless/SIMD-friendly scanning and mmap-resident range
             scans (matches nest mmap + scalar/AVX2/NEON dispatch). Inline values avoid a random
             dictionary read (MD5-keyed hash lookup) per scalar, a large latency saving on
             selective queries. xloader compresses intermediate sort runs to cut peak temp disk
             substantially. MVCC copy-on-write blocks give lock-free readers; for nest
             (read-mostly, mmap, offline) the takeaway is fixed-width keys + permuted sort orders,
             not the transaction machinery.
license:     Apache License 2.0 (permissive; safe to vendor/learn from, no copyleft concern)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] eclipse-rdf4j/rdf4j
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Java RDF quad store whose embedded backends (NativeStore B-tree, LMDB store) split storage into
             a value dictionary mapping every RDF term to a compact integer ID plus B-tree/B+tree
             permutation indexes (spoc, posc, ospc) that store only those IDs, with the LMDB store
             adding an order-preserving variable-length integer (Varint) codec for the IDs.
innovation:  Dictionary-encode every repeated term to a small integer ID once, then store and index ONLY the
             IDs (never the bytes), and encode those IDs with an order-preserving variable-length
             integer whose length is recoverable from the first byte alone, so sorted key storage
             stays correct while small/common IDs cost 1-2 bytes instead of a fixed 4-8.
techniques:
  - order-preserving varint: smaller values use fewer bytes, total length decodable from the first
    byte, and byte-wise lexicographic order equals numeric order so it doubles as a sort key
  - value-type tag (URI/BNode/Literal) packed into the low 2 bits of the integer ID, avoiding a
    separate type column
  - two-level dictionary: ValueStore maps each distinct term to a sequential integer ID; indexes
    store 4 IDs (s,p,o,c) not the terms; ID 0 reserved as null/default-graph context
  - IRI factoring: split each IRI into namespace + local-name, give the namespace its own ID,
    store only [type 1B][namespaceID 4B][localname UTF-8] so shared prefixes are paid once
  - literal layout [type 1B][datatypeID 4B][langLen 1B][langTag<=255B][label UTF-8], referencing
    datatype by ID not inline
  - fixed-slot B-tree page: 16B header (magic btf, version, blockSize, valueSize, rootID), nodes
    one block each, slotSize=4+valueSize, branchFactor=1+(blockSize-8)/slotSize, direct offset
    math with no per-node scan
  - configurable index permutations (spoc/posc/ospc/psoc) chosen per query shape so the most
    selective access pattern is a sorted range scan
  - dictionary-scoped WAL for crash recovery leaving primary .dat files authoritative; mmap + OS
    page cache makes the store behave in-memory when RAM suffices
nest steals: nest-format: add a dictionary-encoded section family plus an order-preserving Varint codec (new
             RESERVED encoding id 4-255, additive within v1) for any section that is a column of
             monotone or repeated integers, principally chunk_ids and future graph/edge sections.
             The biggest concrete win is provenance and repeated metadata: factor repeated source
             strings/URIs into one per-file string/namespace table and store only small IDs
             (mirroring RDF4J ValueStore), which beats zstd-on-raw because redundancy becomes
             integer locality. nest-runtime: when nest adds the promised graph search, store edges
             as (src_id, rel_id, dst_id) Varint triples in spo/ops permutation orders so adjacency
             is a sorted range scan straight off mmap; RDF4J permutation indexes are the proven
             blueprint, and nest's mandated deterministic alphabetical/seeded ordering maps
             cleanly onto preserve-lexicographic-order so the index stays byte-identical and
             reproducible. nest-cli: the cite path can reuse the namespace/localname split so
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id URIs are stored factored.
compression: Directly central. Dictionary encoding + order-preserving Varint is the textbook way to push the
             non-embedding sections nest already zstd-encodes (chunk_ids, provenance, future graph
             edges) BELOW what a generic entropy coder reaches on raw bytes, by removing
             structural redundancy (repeated terms, monotone IDs, shared IRI prefixes) before the
             entropy stage rather than relying on the coder to rediscover it. It never touches
             embeddings (correctly, since those are scored from mmap on the hot path), so it stays
             fully inside nest's never-compress-embeddings rule while lowering the size ratio on
             everything else.
perf:        mmap + OS page cache lets it behave like an in-memory store when RAM suffices (matches nest mmap
             model). Fixed-slot B-tree pages give O(1) in-node offset addressing, no scan. Varint
             decode is branch-light (first byte gives length), good for a tight Rust loop; order
             preservation means you can binary-search encoded keys WITHOUT decoding. Reported LMDB
             footprint ~120-130 bytes/quad with 3 permutation indexes, so the cost of graph search
             is redundant permutations: nest should keep permutation count minimal (e.g. spo plus
             one inverse) to stay compact.
license:     Dual Eclipse Distribution License 1.0 (BSD-3-Clause style) and Eclipse Public License 2.0;
             permissive enough to study and reimplement. nest should clean-room the
             Varint/dictionary ideas in Rust rather than vendor Java.
