[HIGH] oxigraph/oxigraph
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust embedded RDF triple/quad store and SPARQL engine that encodes every term as a 17-byte
             fixed-width (type-byte + 16-byte value) record, inlining small strings and all XSD
             numeric/date literals as native bytes while hashing only large strings via
             SipHash-2-4-128 into an id2str side table, so the six SPOG-permuted RocksDB indexes
             never store raw strings.
innovation:  Hash-or-inline content-addressed typed-value encoding: a 1-byte kind tag plus a <=16-byte value
             that inlines small strings and native numerics/dates and replaces only large strings
             with their content hash, eliminating the forward string->id dictionary index and
             making identical values produce identical deterministic ids.
techniques:
  - Type-byte + <=16-byte value fixed-width term encoding (17 bytes total, big-endian for range
    scans)
  - Inline-small-string rule: UTF-8 strings <16 bytes stored verbatim, no dictionary entry
  - Native typed-literal encoding: i64 integers, i128 fixed-point decimals (x10^18), dateTime as
    decimal timestamp + i16 offset, 1-byte booleans, raw f32/f64 bits
  - SipHash-2-4 128-bit content hashing for large strings -> id2str side table, no forward
    string->id index
  - Six SPOG permutations (SPOG/POSG/OSPG/GSPO/GPOS/GOSP) so any triple pattern maps to a prefix
    range scan
  - Big-endian key layout so lexicographic key order = numeric/range order for range queries
  - MVCC memory backend: single quad hashset + per-quad 'last inserted matching S/P/O/G' pointers
    + selectivity counters for greedy join ordering
  - Volcano iterator evaluation with greedy join reordering by triple-pattern selectivity
    (sparopt: filter pushdown, join reorder)
nest steals: Steal the hash-or-inline typed-value encoding for nest-format as a new optional section codec on
             chunk metadata / provenance / structured-payload sections (NOT embeddings, which stay
             raw f32 on the mmap hot path). Concretely: in crates/nest-format/src/encoding add a
             'typed-dict' encoding (claim a reserved encoding id 4-255) where each metadata field
             value is emitted as a 1-byte kind tag + <=16-byte value;
             integers/timestamps/booleans/decimals go native (i64/i128-fixed-point/i16-offset)
             instead of as JSON text, small UTF-8 strings inline, and any string >=16 bytes is
             replaced by its SHA-256-prefix id (nest already computes SHA-256 everywhere, so reuse
             content_hash machinery instead of SipHash to stay within nest's
             four-integrity-surface story) pointing into a per-file id2str dictionary section.
             This is directly content-addressed and deterministic -- identical metadata yields
             byte-identical encoding, preserving nest's reproducible-build guarantee. It unlocks
             (a) large compression wins on repetitive structured metadata (datasets, parquet
             schemas, json/yaml provenance) where the same IRIs/keys/timestamps repeat across
             millions of chunks, deduped to one dictionary entry; (b) a future graph-search
             section (the mission's 'graph search' north-star) -- adopt the six-permutation SPOG
             index layout in nest-runtime/src as a new section (reserved 0x09+) so relationship
             triples over chunks become prefix-range scans on the mmap with no separate forward
             index; (c) native typed columns for the 'compress whole databases/repos' goal. The
             i128 fixed-point decimal trick (value x 10^18) and dateTime-as-decimal+offset give
             nest exact, range-scannable numeric columns without floats.
compression: High and direct. The encoding is a compression scheme in disguise: it turns variable-length text
             literals into fixed 17-byte content-addressed ids and inlines anything under 16 bytes
             so it never costs a dictionary slot, then dedupes everything larger into a single
             id2str table. For nest's repetitive structured payloads (per-chunk provenance,
             dataset schemas, json/yaml/parquet keys, timestamps), encoding values natively
             instead of as text plus deduping long strings to a content hash can beat zstd on
             highly repetitive structured metadata because dedup happens at the semantic-value
             level before the byte entropy coder ever runs -- and it stacks with zstd (nest can
             zstd the resulting dictionary section since it is non-embedding). The native
             i64/i128/i16 numeric encoding also removes JSON text bloat for numbers/dates
             entirely. This squarely serves 'compress data more than everyone' for the
             structured/dataset/database payload classes nest wants to swallow.
perf:        Fixed 17-byte records mean term comparison and index scans are branch-light and cache-friendly;
             big-endian keys make range queries pure prefix scans (no comparator). Inlining
             sub-16-byte strings and native numerics removes a pointer-chase to id2str on the
             common path -- only large strings pay a lookup. SipHash-2-4-128 is fast and
             collision-free on a full 2019 Wikidata dump per their docs (nest would substitute its
             existing SHA-256 to keep one hash family; SHA-256 is slower but nest already pays it
             at build time, not on the search hot path, so no runtime cost). RocksDB LSM gives
             write throughput but nest does not want RocksDB -- nest must reimplement the layout
             as flat mmap'd sorted sections, which is actually simpler and faster to read than an
             LSM and matches nest's zero-server, mmap-and-scan model. Six permutations cost ~6x
             index storage; nest should make graph/triple indexing an OPTIONAL section so default
             builds stay tiny.
license:     Dual-licensed Apache-2.0 OR MIT (permissive; safe to read and reimplement the encoding scheme; do
             not vendor RocksDB which is GPLv2/Apache-2.0 and brings a C++ LSM dependency that
             violates nest's small single-file offline core).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] terminusdb/terminusdb (storage core: github.com/terminusdb/terminusdb-store)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Git-for-data document/graph database whose Rust storage core (terminus-store) stores RDF triples
             as immutable, content-addressed delta layers built from HDT-derived succinct data
             structures (front-coded string dictionaries, bit-packed integer arrays, and
             rank/select bit vectors over adjacency lists), reaching ~13 bytes per triple.
innovation:  Decompose data into integer-ID triples and store the whole thing as a stack of immutable,
             content-addressed "delta layers", where each layer is built entirely from
             query-time-no-decompression succinct structures: a Plain-Front-Coded (PFC) string
             dictionary that assigns implicit integer IDs by sorted position, bit-packed
             fixed-width LogArrays for the ID columns, and a two-level block/superblock
             rank-select bit index over adjacency-list bit sequences. Compression and the
             queryable index are the SAME bytes, so you search directly on the compressed form,
             and new data is just another append-only layer addressed by the hash of its content.
techniques:
  - Plain Front Coding (PFC) string dictionary: lexicographically sort strings, fixed-size
    buckets, store shared-prefix-length + suffix per entry; ID = implicit sorted position;
    bidirectional id<->string via bucket binary search (Martinez-Prieto INFOSYS 2016)
  - LogArray: bit-packed array of fixed-width integers using ceil(log2(max+1)) bits per slot
    instead of fixed u32/u64, with explicit on-disk width header for portability
  - Two-level rank/select BitIndex: 64-bit blocks grouped into superblocks (SBLOCK_SIZE=52),
    precomputed block+superblock popcount tables stored as LogArrays; rank = sblock_rank +
    block_rank + masked popcount; select via binary search over superblocks then a 64-bit scan
  - Adjacency-list triple encoding: SP and SPO orderings expressed as bit sequences + LogArrays
    (HDT bitmap-triples model) so subject->predicate->object navigation is rank/select with no
    pointers
  - Content-addressed immutable delta layers: every commit is a new layer named by a 20-byte hash;
    additions+removals stored separately; queries resolve current state by traversing the layer
    stack newest-wins
  - Delta rollup compaction: periodically squash the layer stack into a fresh base layer to bound
    read amplification and in-memory footprint
  - Single-archive on-disk format (v0.20+): all primitive structure files for a layer bundled into
    one mmap-friendly archive blob, ~13 bytes/triple on billion-triple sets
  - Buffer-oriented zero-copy structures: every structure is parsed as a view over an existing
    (mmappable) byte buffer rather than heap-constructed, so reads operate straight off disk
nest steals: Two concrete steals into nest-format. (1) Add a PFC codec (encoding id in the reserved 4-255
             range) for the non-embedding string sections, primarily chunk_ids and provenance, and
             for nest's future json/yaml/dataset payloads: front-coding crushes
             URI/path/repeated-prefix data far below generic zstd while staying queryable.
             Implement as crates/nest-format/src/encoding/pfc.rs plus a bucketed FrontCodedDict
             reader in nest-runtime so id<->string lookups run zero-copy off mmap. (2) Add a
             LogArray + two-level rank/select BitIndex primitive
             (crates/nest-runtime/src/succinct/ logarray.rs + bitindex.rs, each <300 lines) and
             use it to shrink the HNSW index section (0x07): replace u32 neighbor-id arrays with
             ceil(log2(N))-bit LogArrays and represent level/neighbor occupancy bitsets with
             rank/select. This directly attacks the 'tiny' preset size ratio (0.283) by removing
             wasted high bits in every adjacency list while keeping deterministic, seekable
             layout. The content-addressed delta-layer idea maps onto nest's existing content_hash
             + nest://content_hash/chunk_id citation model: it is the blueprint for nest's
             real-time-edit goal, append an immutable additive layer-section (reserved id 0x09+)
             carrying added/removed chunk_ids + embeddings, resolved newest-wins at query time,
             with periodic rollup to a fresh base file, all without breaking the FROZEN v1
             single-file determinism.
compression: Directly central to nest's north star of compress-more-than-everyone. PFC is a domain-specific
             entropy win on exactly the structured/prefix-heavy data nest wants to ingest (paths,
             URIs, JSON keys, dataset columns) that generic zstd misses, and it stays
             query-without-decompress. LogArray + rank/select removes the largest source of slack
             in nest's ANN index (fixed-width 32-bit IDs over small N), shrinking the index
             section that dominates the 'tiny' preset footprint. The ~13 bytes/triple result is
             the proof that succinct-but-queryable beats compress-then-decompress for the search
             hot path, the same principle nest already applies by never zstd-compressing
             embeddings.
perf:        Succinct structures are queried with no decompression: rank/select are O(1) (block+superblock
             table lookup plus one 64-bit popcount); select is O(log superblocks) binary search
             then a single-word scan. All structures are buffer-views (Bytes) over a single
             mmappable archive, so reads happen straight off disk with zero heap copy,
             architecturally identical to nest's mmap+SIMD hot path. Reported ~13 bytes/triple
             in-memory on billion-triple datasets. Two-level index overhead is small (blocks every
             64 bits, superblocks every 52 blocks, a few percent). Cost: read amplification grows
             with layer-stack depth, mitigated by periodic delta rollup, a tradeoff nest must
             budget if it adopts append-only edit layers.
license:     Apache-2.0 (both terminusdb and terminusdb-store)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] terminusdb/terminusdb-store (succinct structures in terminusdb-labs/tdb-succinct)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A tokio-backed immutable triple store whose entire on-disk format is built from succinct data
             structures (front-coded dictionaries, minimum-bit-width packed integer arrays, and
             two-level rank/select bit indexes) queried directly from mmapped bytes with no
             decompression step, reaching ~13.6 bytes/triple on 17B-triple graphs.
innovation:  Store integer columns and string dictionaries in succinct, query-in-place encodings so the file IS
             the index: data stays compressed on disk and in the page cache yet supports O(1)
             random access (LogArray.entry) and O(1) rank/select navigation (BitIndex) without
             ever materializing a decompressed copy. This is the missing middle for nest between
             "raw f32 (fast, big)" and "zstd block (small, but must decompress to read one
             element)".
techniques:
  - LogArray: pack N unsigned ints at W = ceil(log2(max+1)) bits each into 64-bit words plus a
    trailing control word holding N (u32) and W (u8); entry(i) reads at most two words and
    shifts/masks W bits
  - BitIndex two-level rank/select: per-64-bit-block popcount counts in a LogArray plus
    per-superblock (52 blocks) cumulative counts in a second LogArray; rank1 = sblock_rank -
    block_rank + count_ones(masked word); select1 = binary search over superblocks then linear
    block scan
  - use_linear_bitscan cache-line shortcut: if start and end share a 512-byte cache line,
    popcount-scan directly instead of consulting the index
  - Plain Front Coding (PFC) dictionary: sort strings, group in blocks of 8, store block head
    verbatim, each successor as vbyte(common-prefix-len)+nul-terminated suffix; prefix compression
    plus binary-searchable string<->id both ways
  - AdjacencyList = LogArray (right-hand values) + BitIndex (1-bit marks last pair of each left
    id); traverse a subject's predicates via rank/select with zero stored pointers
  - vbyte (HDT-style, msb=last-byte flag) for prefix-length and gap encoding inside dictionary
    blocks
  - WaveletTree over a u64 sequence (log2(alphabet) bitarray layers) for
    positional/range/reverse-traversal queries
  - TFC typed dictionary: order-preserving per-XSD-datatype byte encodings (sign-magnitude varint
    integers, decimals, datetimes) so numeric values stay sorted and front-codable in one
    dictionary
nest steals: Add a succinct integer/string codec family to nest-format under crates/nest-format/src/encoding
             using reserved v1 encoding ids 4-255 (additive, no version bump). (1) Encode
             chunk_ids and monotone offset arrays (chunks_original_spans, per-section length
             tables) as a LogArray: ~64k chunks need ~17 bits not 32/64, cutting those sections
             ~2x with O(1) mmap random access and zero decode on cite/materialize. (2) Add a PFC
             front-coded dictionary codec for repetitive string metadata (provenance fields,
             source URIs, tags, BM25 vocabulary in bm25_index 0x08); prefix sharing beats
             per-field zstd for sorted short strings and stays binary-searchable from mmap. (3)
             Port BitIndex rank/select into nest-runtime for sparse presence/tombstone bitmaps
             (optional-field presence, deleted chunks for real-time edit) at ~1.05 bits/bit with
             O(1) navigation. (4) The two-level rank/select plus cache-line bitscan upgrades BM25
             posting lists and any future graph-search adjacency without pointer overhead. Keep
             deterministic (sorted/seeded), but flip terminusdb's big-endian words to
             little-endian per nest's constraint; each module fits the <=300-line
             single-responsibility rule.
compression: Directly central to "compress more than everyone": succinct structures give near-entropy size
             while keeping random access, which is exactly nest's bind since embeddings must be
             scored from mmap and block compressors are banned on the hot path.
             LogArray/BitIndex/PFC compress every surrounding integer/string/offset/index section
             without the full-block decode that zstd forces, complementing zstd (cold sections
             only) and the f16/int8 quantizers. They do not shrink the dense embedding matrix
             itself.
perf:        Block=64 bits (one register), superblock=52 blocks. rank1 is O(1): two LogArray lookups + one word
             read + hardware popcount; select1 is O(log #superblocks) binary search then linear
             block scan; use_linear_bitscan skips the index when a range fits a 512-byte cache
             line. LogArray.entry(i) touches at most 2 contiguous words (cache-friendly). PFC
             blocks of 8 bound the suffix walk to 7 strings. All structures are
             immutable/read-only: lock-free, trivially parallel, matching nest's mmap-and-serve
             model. Reported density ~13.57 bytes/triple at 17B triples with a full 6-way index.
license:     Apache-2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] RDFLib/rdflib
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A pure-Python RDF toolkit whose in-memory Store indexes triples in three redundant permutation
             dictionaries (SPO/POS/OSP) plus context maps, and evaluates SPARQL 1.1 via an algebra
             of lazy nested-loop joins with dynamic most-bound-first triple-pattern reordering.
innovation:  The "triple of permutations" index: store every edge three times under SPO, POS, and OSP
             nested-dict orderings so that any of the eight subject/predicate/object binding
             patterns resolves to a direct prefix walk (O(1) descent then iterate), giving
             symmetric fast lookup no matter which graph terms are known. This is the canonical
             compact way to make a graph adjacency structure queryable from any direction without
             a query planner.
techniques:
  - Three permuted nested-dict indices SPO/POS/OSP, each leaf=presence bit, so any bound-prefix
    (s, sp, p, po, o, os, spo, none) maps to one permutation's descent
  - Term-as-interned-string: all terms subclass Python str (URIRef/BNode/Literal) so identity
    reuses CPython string interning; Literal hash = str_hash XOR hash(lang.lower()) XOR
    hash(datatype) to fold the 3-tuple into one dict key
  - Context/quad layering: triple->{context:quoted_bool} map plus inverse context->set(triple) map
    sit on top of the shared triple indices so named graphs share one physical edge (dedup across
    graphs)
  - SPARQL BGP evaluation as recursive algebra with dynamic triple-pattern reordering by count of
    already-bound variables (most-selective-first heuristic), no cost statistics needed
  - Lazy nested-loop join (evalLazyJoin) with push-down of bound variables into the next pattern
    and FrozenBindings immutable solution mappings instead of materializing intermediate relations
  - Pluggable Store API (add/remove/triples(pattern)/contexts) decoupling the graph algebra from
    physical storage (memory, BerkeleyDB, remote SPARQL)
nest steals: Adopt the three-permutation triple index as a new OPTIONAL nest-format section (next reserved id
             0x09) to deliver the north-star "graph search" capability over chunk relationships.
             Concretely: in nest-format add a graph_index section storing edges as three sorted
             little-endian u32 arrays (SPO, POS, OSP) over integer-densified term ids, plus a term
             dictionary section mapping term-id->canonical string (reuse the existing chunk_ids
             interning pattern). Because each permutation is monotonically sorted, store it
             delta+varint or just sort and let zstd(non-embedding section) crush it -- graph edges
             are highly compressible, which directly serves "compress more than everyone." In
             nest-runtime add a graph::TripleIndex that mmaps the three arrays and does
             binary-search prefix walks (no dict, no allocation) to answer any bound-pattern, then
             port RDFLib's most-bound-first BGP reordering as a deterministic seeded join order
             (alphabetical/id-order tiebreak) so multi-hop graph queries stay reproducible and
             never panic (typed thiserror). nest-cli gains a search-graph subcommand; nest-python
             exposes pattern queries. The XOR-folded term hashing and str-interning idea informs
             how to dedup term strings in the dictionary section. Files stay <=300 loc by
             splitting graph/term_dict.rs, graph/index.rs, graph/join.rs.
compression: High for the graph modality: the three-permutation layout looks 3x redundant but each permutation
             is a sorted integer sequence, so delta-coding + zstd makes the redundancy near-free
             while unlocking direction-agnostic queries; term-id densification + a string
             dictionary turns long repeated URIs/strings into small ints (dictionary compression),
             and cross-graph edge sharing (one physical edge, many contexts) is exactly the dedup
             nest wants when compressing whole databases/repos into one .nest container.
perf:        Pure Python, dict-of-dict-of-dict: fast for interactive use but memory-heavy (every edge stored 3x
             as Python objects, ~hundreds of bytes/triple) and not mmap-able; scales to ~low
             millions of triples in RAM, not billions. The lazy nested-loop join has no hash-join,
             so large joins are O(n*m) in the worst case. nest should keep the algorithmic shape
             (3 permutations, most-bound-first reorder) but replace Python dicts with sorted
             mmap'd u32 arrays + binary search to get zero-allocation, SIMD-free but
             cache-friendly prefix scans straight off disk.
license:     BSD-3-Clause

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[LOW] opencypher/openCypher
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        openCypher is the open specification of the Cypher property-graph query language -- an XML-sourced
             grammar that generates ANTLR4/EBNF/railroad artifacts, plus a Cucumber-based
             Technology Compatibility Kit that defines engine conformance via
             canonically-serialized, order-aware query result tables.
innovation:  The TCK's canonical, encoding-independent result model: every query outcome (primitives, lists,
             maps, nodes, relationships, paths) has a single deterministic string serialization
             plus an explicit ordered-vs-unordered-set comparison semantics, so two conforming
             engines are declared equivalent by comparing normalized outputs rather than internal
             representations.
techniques:
  - TCK canonical result-table serialization: one normalized string form per value (single-quoted
    strings, [v0,v1] lists, {k:v} maps, (:Label {p:v}) nodes, [:Type] rels)
  - Explicit unordered-multiset vs ordered-sequence result comparison (ORDER BY + 'in order'
    asserts sequence; otherwise set equality)
  - Side-effect accounting as observable deltas only (nodes/rels/properties/labels added/removed),
    transient state ignored
  - Typed errors tagged with compile-vs-runtime phase (SyntaxError/SemanticError/TypeError raised
    at parse|runtime)
  - Single-source XML grammar deterministically generating ANTLR4 .g4 + ISO-14977 EBNF + railroad
    diagrams via Java/XSLT, with --INCLUDE_LEGACY gating
  - Reusable named deterministic fixture graphs (binary-tree-1/2) shared across conformance
    scenarios
nest steals: See detailed field; two adaptations -- a golden-file search-conformance harness (nest-cli/tests)
             that machine-checks the search_contract (ann results rerank to identical real-cosine
             top-k across exact/tiny/hybrid presets, proving query-layer determinism the way
             content_hash proves storage-layer determinism), and a single-source format-spec
             codegen pattern in nest-format so the FROZEN .nest layout and its diataxis docs
             cannot drift. Both are test/codegen scaffolding only; the additive v1 format is
             untouched.
compression: Essentially none. openCypher is a language specification (grammar + formal semantics + conformance
             tests). It contains no entropy coder, quantization, columnar layout, delta encoding,
             or any storage/compression machinery. The only tangential link is that a
             Cypher-subset query AST could one day be the textual surface for nest's aspirational
             graph-search feature, but that touches query ergonomics, not byte-level compaction.
             For the north-star goal of compressing data more than everyone, this repo offers
             nothing directly.
perf:        No runtime performance characteristics relevant to nest: this is a build-time specification and
             test corpus (Maven, Scala 2.12/2.13 cross-compile, Java/XSLT grammar generation,
             Cucumber execution). No SIMD, no memory-mapped data, no hot-path concerns. Value is
             entirely in determinism/conformance methodology, not throughput.
license:     Apache License 2.0 (with an additional grant-of-rights / no-derivative-grammar-name clause per the
             openCypher project terms). Permissive enough to learn from and adapt the methodology;
             nest would only borrow ideas/conventions, not code or grammar.

# category: embedded-db  (8 repos)
# focus: embedded / datalog / temporal / kv / column storage engines (edit + real-time)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] replikativ/datahike
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A durable, immutable Datalog database that ports DataScript onto a content-addressed Hitchhiker
             tree (a fractal B+ tree with buffered write-logs) over the konserve pluggable KV
             store, giving append-only temporal facts, snapshot/time-travel, and
             structural-sharing dedup.
innovation:  Content-addressed persistent index nodes: every tree node is stored under an ID that is a
             cryptographic hash (hasch, SHA-512) of the node's own contents, so identical
             sub-structures across versions/snapshots automatically collapse to the same physical
             block (structural sharing + dedup for free), and a whole database version is named by
             a single root hash. This is the same idea nest already uses for content_hash, but
             pushed down to per-block granularity so that incremental edits only write the changed
             nodes while unchanged nodes are shared by reference.
techniques:
  - Hitchhiker (fractal) tree: B+ tree whose internal index nodes carry a bounded append-log of
    pending insert/retract ops, flushed downward in batches only when the buffer overflows,
    amortizing write amplification across many mutations
  - Content-addressed nodes: node UUID = hash(node contents) via hasch SHA-512, yielding
    deterministic IDs, automatic dedup, and verifiable integrity (analogous to nest per-section
    SHA-256)
  - Datom = [entity, attribute, value, tx] fact tuples stored in three sorted covering indexes
    EAVT / AEVT / AVET, giving column-store-like locality without a separate columnar layout
  - Append-only fact log: retractions are new datoms with an assertion flag, so history is never
    overwritten; time-travel (as-of / history) is a filter over tx ids, with snapshots sharing
    structure
  - Block granularity in KV: leaf data nodes hold a sorted vector of many datoms (a block), not
    one-datom-per-key, cutting KV overhead and improving scan locality
  - konserve KV abstraction decoupling logical index from physical store (File/LMDB/S3/JDBC), with
    only the root-id map mutated in place
  - GDPR 'excision': because facts are addressable, specific historical values can be purged while
    keeping the rest of the immutable log intact
nest steals: Two concrete steals into nest-format and nest-runtime. (1) In nest-format hashing.rs: extend
             nest's content_hash model to PER-BLOCK content-addressed chunk nodes so that when
             nest gains real-time edit, an edit rewrites only the touched blocks and shares
             unchanged ones by their SHA-256 id inside the single .nest file -- this is exactly
             the structural-sharing dedup that lets a v2 'append region' grow without rewriting
             the whole file, while keeping byte-determinism. (2) In nest-runtime, model
             edits/retractions as an append-only fact log (a reserved section 0x09+, additive
             within v1) carrying [chunk_id, op, tx] tuples plus a small fractal-style
             buffered-flush index, so temporal 'as-of' queries and undo become a filter over tx
             ids without mutating frozen sections -- gives nest the 'real-time edit' and
             'temporal' pillars of the north star while preserving the
             frozen-format/offline/deterministic guarantees. The EAVT/AEVT/AVET
             sorted-covering-index trick informs how to lay out a future graph-search section
             (chunk-relations) as sorted tuple blocks scored directly off mmap.
compression: Medium-high for compression specifically: the per-block content-addressed structural sharing IS a
             compression mechanism -- duplicate chunks/embeddings/index-nodes across versions or
             across similar corpora deduplicate to a single physical block, so a multi-version or
             multi-corpus .nest pays once. This directly serves 'compress more than everyone' when
             nest stores whole evolving databases/repos with overlapping content, and the fractal
             write-buffer keeps that dedup cheap under real-time edit instead of forcing full
             re-encode.
perf:        Hitchhiker tree trades a small read cost (must merge a node's pending log on read) for large
             write-amplification savings: O(buffered) flushes instead of per-insert rebalances,
             ideal for high-ingest append workloads. Block-granular leaves give sequential scan
             locality similar to a column store. Content-addressing makes reads cache-friendly and
             integrity-checkable but adds a hash computation per node write (SHA-512 there; nest
             would reuse its existing SHA-256 surfaces, no new dependency). All structures are
             persistent/immutable so reads are lock-free -- aligns with nest's mmap read path.
license:     Eclipse Public License 1.0 (EPL-1.0) -- copyleft-ish per-file; safe to LEARN/adapt the algorithm
             and on-disk ideas, do NOT vendor Clojure source into nest's Rust core.

[HIGH] tonsky/datascript
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        see above
innovation:  see above
techniques:
  - see array above
nest steals: see above
compression: Indirect but real. The structural-sharing copy-on-write B+ tree is the cheapest known way to make
             an append-only single-file container support edits without rewriting (or duplicating)
             the whole file: an edit costs ~3 dirty nodes, so versioned/edited corpora compress
             dramatically vs naive re-serialization. Node arenas of packed sorted keys are also
             delta/front-coding friendly (shared prefixes between adjacent keys), and the index
             itself is a non-embedding section so it is eligible for nest's zstd codec. It does
             NOT compress embeddings, but it directly unlocks the 'real-time edit' north-star
             while preserving single-file size discipline.
perf:        In-memory range scans ~3x faster than Clojure's built-in sorted-set due to contiguous-array nodes
             and cache-friendly binary search within a node. Bulk insert via transients is 2-3x
             faster than persistent conj. Per-edit persistence touches only ~log_b(N) nodes (~3
             for millions of elements at depth 3). Lazy restore means read working set ~ query
             reach, not total size - ideal pairing with mmap demand paging. No SIMD; the win is
             allocation/IO economy, not raw scan throughput, so it complements rather than
             competes with nest's SIMD dot-product hot path (which must stay on raw mmap
             embeddings).
license:     Eclipse Public License 1.0 (EPL-1.0) for DataScript and persistent-sorted-set. Note: EPL is
             weak-copyleft and not a clean fit to vendor into MIT/Apache Rust crates -
             re-implement the B+ tree + IStorage idea from the documented design rather than
             copying code; the algorithm/format ideas are not themselves licensable.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] xtdb/xtdb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        XTDB v2 is a bitemporal SQL database whose primary index is a distributed LSM tree of immutable
             Apache Arrow files, sharded by a 2-bit (branching-factor-4) HAMT trie over entity-id,
             where each file publishes min/max + bloom-filter metadata and a single collapsed
             "recency" scalar so the query engine can prune whole files before touching their
             columnar buffers.
innovation:  Collapse a multi-dimensional temporal coordinate (valid_time, system_time) into ONE monotone
             sortable scalar ("recency" = max T at which the row was believed valid for both
             valid-time=T and system-time=T), publish it as per-file metadata alongside min/max
             ranges and bloom filters, then prune entire immutable files at query time without
             decoding their payload. This is a generic "skip-index on a derived sort key + cheap
             per-file summary statistics" pattern, decoupled from temporality.
techniques:
  - 2-bit HAMT trie (branching factor 4) over entity-id hash; trie path [3,2,1,0] = recursive
    prefix buckets that double as deterministic file shard names (210/211/212/213) and keep ~100MB
    files
  - LSM tree of append-only immutable files; compaction merge-sorts L0->L1->L2 and CONCATENATES
    bitemporal events instead of dropping versions; a file is 'superseded' only when its compacted
    output commits, making cache invalidation trivial
  - Per-file zone-map metadata: min/max for numeric columns + Bloom filters for non-numeric
    columns, used for predicate pushdown / file skipping before reading Arrow buffers
  - Recency heuristic: collapse (valid_time, system_time) into one sortable scalar so 'give me
    data newer than T' elides whole historical files
  - Survivor vs historical split at L1: current versions sharded by entity-id only; superseded
    versions sharded by entity-id AND recency, so hot current-state scans never touch cold history
  - Apache Arrow columnar layout chosen because remote/immutable storage already forbids in-place
    update, so columnar scan locality comes for free
  - Deferred leaf sorting: HAMT leaf stays unsorted until it exceeds 1024 bytes and is promoted to
    a branch, amortizing sort cost
nest steals: Two steals, both anchored in nest-format. (1) ZONE-MAP/SUMMARY metadata per section, additively:
             extend the manifest entry in crates/nest-format/src/manifest and the section
             descriptors in crates/nest-format/src/sections so each section (chunks_canonical,
             provenance, future edit-log) carries cheap precomputed summaries -- for embeddings,
             per-block min/max per dimension or a coarse centroid; for text/provenance, a
             deterministic Bloom filter over tokens/source-ids. Then
             crates/nest-runtime/src/search.rs and bm25 skip blocks whose Bloom filter cannot
             contain a query term and whose summary cannot beat the current top-k cosine bound,
             cutting SIMD dot products and mmap page faults without changing scored bytes (exact
             cosine rerank stays ground truth). Implement as a new RESERVED optional section
             'section_zonemaps(0x09)' or new optional manifest fields -- additive, never a v2
             bump. (2) RECENCY-SCALAR for nest's future real-time edit dimension: model each chunk
             version's (valid_from, ingested_at) as one monotone u64 'recency' stored in chunk_ids
             or a new edit-index section, kept sorted so 'serve current state' and 'as-of T'
             queries prune superseded chunk versions deterministically; the LSM
             survivor/historical split gives a coordination-free, single-file, append-only edit
             model that stays deterministic (seeded, alphabetical term order) and offline by
             construction.
compression: Indirect but real. The zone-map/Bloom skip-index does not shrink the file but lets nest keep MORE
             data in one .nest container while staying fast, because cold blocks are never paged
             in or decoded -- it makes large-corpus single-file serving viable, the scaling axis
             of the 'compress AND serve whole databases' north star. The recency-collapse +
             LSM-concat compaction is the canonical compact way to store full edit history: one
             append-only sorted run that compacts and dedups instead of N snapshots, so historical
             versions migrate to deeper denser blocks that zstd (nest's existing non-embedding
             codec) compresses well. Bloom filters are themselves a compact (~10 bits/elem)
             probabilistic substitute for an exact term dictionary.
perf:        Index lives on object storage and is cached across nodes (irrelevant to nest's single-file offline
             model -- adapt the file-skip idea, drop the distribution). Pruning works because
             Arrow buffers are columnar and contiguous, so a passed zone-map check yields a tight
             sequential scan; nest's mmap + SIMD dot-product hot path benefits identically --
             skipping a block avoids both page faults and AVX2/NEON work. The ~100MB shard size
             and 1024-byte leaf-promotion threshold are tuning knobs worth mirroring as nest block
             sizes. Bloom false positives cost one wasted block scan, never a wrong answer; nest's
             mandatory exact rerank preserves correctness regardless.
license:     Mozilla Public License 2.0 (MPL-2.0), file-level copyleft. Safe to read and reimplement the ideas,
             but do not vendor MPL source files into nest's permissive crates; reimplement the
             trie/zone-map/recency logic from scratch in Rust.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] tidwall/buntdb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An embeddable pure-Go in-memory key/value store that is ACID-durable via a single text append-only
             file (AOF) replayed on open, with copy-on-write B-tree indexes, pluggable Less
             comparators, GJSON path indexing, and an R-tree for spatial/k-NN queries.
innovation:  Durability and real-time edit on a single file without a WAL+pages engine: writes are serialized
             as a RESP/text command log appended to one AOF, live state is an in-memory B-tree,
             and crash recovery is just deterministic replay of the log in commit order.
             Compaction is a Shrink() that rewrites the file as the minimal set of `set` commands
             representing current state (an idempotent, replay-equivalent snapshot), triggered
             when the log grows past AutoShrinkPercentage. Transaction rollback needs no MVCC:
             each Tx records a rollbackItems map of the prior value (or tombstone) for every key
             it touches, and on abort re-inserts those prior values into the B-tree.
techniques:
  - AOF as sole durability surface: each committed write appended as a RESP/text command
    (set/del/SETEX); recovery = replay in order, no page cache or WAL checkpoints
  - Shrink/compaction: rewrite AOF as minimal `set` snapshot of live state (idempotent,
    replay-equivalent), gated by AutoShrinkPercentage + AutoShrinkMinSize
  - Per-Tx rollbackItems map: store previous dbItem (or nil tombstone) per touched key; rollback
    re-applies them, commit flushes one buffered byte slice then fsyncs per SyncPolicy
  - Single-writer / multi-reader: one write Tx, many concurrent read Tx over stable snapshots, no
    read-side lock contention
  - Copy-on-write B-tree with path-hint Get/Set/Delete (SetHint/GetHint) to amortize repeated
    nearby lookups during bulk insert/replay
  - Pluggable Less comparator secondary indexes (IndexString/Int/Float/JSON/Rect/Desc), each a
    B-tree selected by key glob pattern (user:*)
  - GJSON zero-allocation path indexing of a JSON field (name.last) without deserializing the
    whole document; R-tree for Intersects() bbox and Nearby() k-NN
  - Configurable SyncPolicy (Never/EverySecond/Always) trading fsync frequency against a bounded
    data-loss window
nest steals: nest's hard gap is that the .nest v1 container is frozen/read-only (mmap + SIMD scoring) with no
             edit path, yet the North Star demands real-time edit. Steal BuntDB's AOF+Shrink model
             as nest's mutation layer WITHOUT touching the frozen v1 body. Add a new optional
             binary append-only edit journal of typed records
             (add_chunk{content_hash,chunk_id,canonical_bytes,embedding}, del_chunk{chunk_id})
             written by nest-runtime in a new journal.rs (<300 loc, single responsibility,
             little-endian repr(C), append-only -> matches additive-only + offline constraints).
             The reader overlays journal deltas in memory on top of the mmap base sections: base
             served from mmap, deltas merged at open. Adopt rollbackItems for transactional edits
             (record prior dbItem per touched chunk_id, re-apply on abort) staying
             typed-thiserror, never panicking. Adopt Shrink() as a nest-cli `compact` subcommand
             that rewrites base+journal into a fresh deterministic .nest v1 holding only live
             chunks, recomputing content_hash/file_hash so same live set => byte-identical output
             (matches nest determinism law). Separately steal the pluggable-Less secondary-index
             pattern for nest-runtime metadata filtering: a deterministic sorted index over
             provenance fields (source/lang/timestamp), mirroring bm25's alphabetical-order
             determinism rule.
compression: Indirect but real. BuntDB does no entropy coding, so it is not a direct compression win. The value
             is that AOF+Shrink is the only way nest gains real-time edit while preserving
             single-file/deterministic guarantees, and the Shrink/compaction step is exactly where
             extreme compression re-enters: compaction is the moment to re-run zstd on
             non-embedding sections, re-quantize embeddings (int8/f16), drop dead chunks, and
             rebuild hnsw/bm25 to the smallest preset (tiny, 0.283 size ratio, recall@10 0.992).
             Without an edit-then-compact cycle nest can only compress at build time; with it nest
             can absorb edits cheaply (append) and periodically re-derive a minimal
             maximally-compressed container. Shrink idempotency (replay-equivalent minimal
             snapshot) is the conceptual twin of nest's reproducible build.
perf:        In-memory B-tree O(log n) ops; reads lock-free across concurrent read Tx. Writes serialized
             (single writer), dominated by AOF append: EverySecond (default) buffers in memory and
             fsyncs once/sec (<=1s loss window), Always fsyncs per commit, Never defers to OS.
             Path-hint ops cut repeated comparisons during bulk replay. Cost: full dataset
             resident in RAM (pure in-memory) -- contrast with nest, which mmaps and scores off
             disk, so nest should keep base sections mmap-resident and hold only journal deltas in
             RAM, avoiding BuntDB's whole-dataset-in-RAM cost. GJSON indexing is zero-allocation
             over raw JSON bytes.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] tigerbeetle/tigerbeetle
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A statically-allocated, fully deterministic, single-file financial accounting database (Zig) whose
             entire on-disk state is a hash-chained tree of fixed 0.5 MiB grid blocks protected by
             out-of-band 128-bit AEGIS checksums, with an LSM "forest" over a WAL ring buffer and
             a 4-copy superblock root.
innovation:  Byte-for-byte physical determinism turns integrity into content-addressing: every block is
             referenced by {address, 128-bit checksum} in its parent, so corruption recovery is
             "fetch a block matching checksum X" with no error path, and repair is single-block
             (physical) rather than recomputing whole structures.
techniques:
  - EWAH (Enhanced Word-Aligned Hybrid) run-length bitmap compression for the grid free-set:
    marker words encode runs of clean (all-0/all-1) words + literal dirty words,
    scannable/operable in place, guaranteed never >0.1% larger than raw bitset
  - Two-bitset free-set design (blocks_acquired + blocks_released) so deletions are deferred until
    a checkpoint is durable, giving crash-consistent reclamation without a full rewrite
  - AEGIS-128L as a (non-cryptographic-strength but fast) 128-bit checksum built on AES round
    instructions to saturate hardware AES on x86-64/aarch64; software fallback exists but is
    assumed unused
  - Out-of-band checksums: a block's checksum lives in its parent reference, not the block,
    defeating misdirected/torn writes and decoupling the checksum from the bytes it covers
  - Hash-chaining at every layer (WAL prepares chain, manifest log is a linked list of blocks,
    superblock chains predecessors) so a single root checksum authenticates all reachable state
  - 4-redundant superblock root with a per-copy `copy` field excluded from the checksum, so all
    copies share one checksum yet stay individually addressable; recovery picks the newest root
    with >=2 matching copies
  - Static allocation from CLI-derived worst-case upper bounds: zero alloc/free after startup, so
    memory and on-disk sizing are an explicit deterministic function of declared limits
  - Deterministically scheduled, evenly-amortized LSM compaction (fixed work-per-op) so layout and
    latency are reproducible from a seed; LSM forest = many fixed-value-size trees keyed by stable
    tree_id
nest steals: 1) nest-format hashing: TigerBeetle's out-of-band, content-addressed checksum model directly
             validates and sharpens nest's four-checksum scheme. Adopt the "checksum stored in the
             parent reference, not the bytes" rule for the manifest's per-section entries
             (layout.rs/manifest.rs/hashing.rs), and treat content_hash as a Merkle root over
             per-section content_hashes so a citation nest://content_hash/chunk_id can verify a
             single chunk against the root (partial verification without rehashing the whole
             file). 2) nest-format encoding: add an EWAH/word-aligned-hybrid bitset codec as a new
             RESERVED encoding id (>=4) or as an internal representation for sparse/boolean
             metadata -- e.g. a deletion/tombstone bitmap for real-time edit, an hnsw "live node"
             mask, or bm25 doc-presence sets -- giving near-free compression of sparse id-sets
             while staying scannable in place off mmap; deterministic by alphabetical/ascending id
             order, fits the <=300-line single-responsibility codec module. 3) nest-format
             additive section: the two-bitset (acquired/released) free-set is the blueprint for
             nest's missing edit story -- an additive section (0x09+) holding an EWAH live-set +
             released-set lets nest mark chunks deleted/superseded and reclaim space at the next
             deterministic rebuild without bumping to v2, preserving append-only +
             reproducible-build guarantees. 4) nest-runtime simd: evaluate AEGIS-128L (AES-NI /
             NEON AES) as an optional fast integrity check on the mmap hot path where SHA-256 is
             too slow for per-query re-verification -- keep SHA-256 as the canonical sovereign
             hash, expose AEGIS only as an internal fast-path detector of mmap corruption.
compression: Directly relevant via EWAH: extreme, in-place-scannable compression of the sparse boolean id-sets
             nest will need for edit/tombstones/hnsw-liveness/bm25-presence, with a hard "never
             materially larger than raw" guarantee -- ideal for the smallest-on-disk mission. The
             deterministic/content-addressed-block model also reinforces nest's reproducible-build
             and partial-verification story, which underpins trustworthy aggressive compression.
             AEGIS is a perf, not a size, lever (fast re-verification of compressed mmap'd bytes).
perf:        AEGIS-128L checksumming targets >GB/s by mapping onto AES round instructions on both x86-64
             (AES-NI) and aarch64 (NEON crypto); software fallback exists but is assumed never
             used (an assert guards non-AES CPUs). Direct I/O (O_DIRECT) bypasses the page cache
             to avoid fsync/EIO cache-coherency bugs and to manage its own cache; fixed 0.5 MiB
             blocks double as network messages (zero re-encode between disk and wire). Static
             allocation means no GC/alloc jitter; compaction work is evenly spread per op to bound
             tail latency. EWAH bitsets are operated on in compressed form (no decompress step)
             which is the property nest needs for off-mmap scanning.
license:     Apache-2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] facebook/rocksdb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An LSM-tree embedded key-value engine whose on-disk SST "block-based table" format combines
             prefix/delta-encoded sorted blocks with restart points, full/partitioned Bloom and
             Ribbon probabilistic filters, and trained-dictionary block compression to hit
             aggressive space-amplification targets.
innovation:  Decoupling the compressor's statistical model from the unit of access: RocksDB trains ONE ZSTD
             dictionary by sampling across many small blocks (buffer up to target_file_size,
             sample uniformly, run the ZDICT trainer to max_dict_bytes) and stores that single
             digested dictionary in a meta-block, so each 4KB block compresses against cross-block
             redundancy a per-block compressor can never see. The complementary idea is the Ribbon
             filter: replacing per-key Bloom hashing with a solved banded linear system over GF(2)
             that hits the same false-positive rate at ~30% fewer bits/key, trading one-time
             construction CPU for permanent space savings on long-lived data.
techniques:
  - ZSTD trained dictionary: sample across blocks, run ZDICT trainer to max_dict_bytes, store one
    shared digested dictionary in a meta-block, compress each block against it
  - Ribbon filter: banded linear system over GF(2) solved by on-the-fly Gaussian elimination
    (Dietzfelbinger-Walzer), ~7 bits/key vs Bloom ~10 for equal FP, 128-bit band
  - Prefix/delta key encoding with restart points: store (shared_len, non_shared_len,
    non_shared_bytes), full key only every block_restart_interval=16, binary search over restarts
  - format_version=4 index delta encoding: reconstruct offset as prev_offset+prev_size+trailer,
    store only the size delta, ~4-5x smaller index blocks
  - Full/partitioned filter blocks: one filter per whole SST file instead of per-data-block,
    removing index lookup and address jumping on the query path
  - Footer-anchored layout parsed backwards from a fixed-size footer pointing to meta-index and
    index blocks
  - Cost-based filter selection: Ribbon for long-lived data, Bloom for short-lived, hybrid
    per-level
  - optimize_filters_for_memory: round filter sizes to reduce cache-line/page waste
nest steals: Two steals into nest-format plus one into nest-runtime. (1) ZSTD trained-dictionary codec in
             crates/nest-format/src/encoding/zstd_codec.rs: today it calls plain zstd::encode_all
             at level 19 with NO dictionary. Add a build-time pass that trains ONE ZDICT
             dictionary over all text sections (chunks_canonical, chunks_original_spans,
             provenance) and compresses each against it; persist the digested dictionary in a new
             RESERVED section id 0x09+ or in the manifest. This attacks the exact pain point: many
             small, highly-similar chunks (the PT-BR fake-news corpus repeats names/boilerplate
             across articles) compress far better against a shared cross-chunk model than
             independently. Determinism holds because ZDICT is deterministic given identical
             sampled input and a pinned zstd version, so same chunks => byte-identical dictionary
             => byte-identical file. Purely additive via a new encoding id (4-255 reserved); old
             readers fail closed. Embeddings stay untouched for SIMD mmap scoring. Biggest single
             lever on 'compress more than everyone'. (2) Delta + restart encoding
             (format_version=4 trick) for chunk_ids and chunks_original_spans sections
             (crates/nest-format/src/sections/chunk_ids.rs, spans.rs): these are sorted u64 ids
             and (offset,len) spans that delta-encode almost perfectly; store only the size delta
             and reconstruct offsets, shrinking metadata overhead in the smallest 'tiny' preset.
             (3) Deterministic seeded Ribbon filter in nest-runtime alongside ann::HnswIndex and
             bm25 (crates/nest-runtime/src/bm25/): ~30% smaller membership filter than Bloom at
             equal FP, stored in the optional bm25_index (0x08) region, as a fast offline
             pre-filter before the mandatory exact-cosine rerank, never violating the search
             contract.
compression: Directly on-mission. Trained-dictionary ZSTD is the highest-ROI compression upgrade for nest: it
             converts cross-chunk redundancy (invisible to per-section zstd today) into bytes
             saved on every text section, improving every preset that uses zstd
             (compressed/tiny/hybrid) at zero recall cost since embeddings are excluded. Ribbon
             filters and delta/restart encoding of id and span sections cut index and metadata
             overhead, shrinking the smallest 'tiny' ratio (today 0.283) further. All three
             respect the FROZEN additive format (new reserved encoding/section ids only) and stay
             deterministic and offline.
perf:        Ribbon construction ~140ns/key (4-5x Bloom ~32ns) with ~230 bits/key temporary build memory, but
             query only ~600ns vs Bloom ~500ns and steady-state ~7 vs ~10 bits/key; one-time
             offline build cost is fine for nest (python builds). ZSTD dictionary: in-memory
             digested form is larger than on-disk but decompresses faster; dictionary pin is one
             small meta-block. nest compresses whole sections (not 4KB blocks), so the dictionary
             gain is maximal precisely on many-small-similar-chunk corpora. Level 19 plus a
             trained dictionary stacks cleanly.
license:     Apache-2.0 and GPLv2 dual-licensed. nest must NOT vendor RocksDB code: reimplement Ribbon
             clean-room from the public Dietzfelbinger-Walzer paper and use the zstd crate's
             native ZDICT bindings, to avoid GPL contamination of the small rust core.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] duckdb/duckdb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An in-process columnar analytical database with a single-file, block-based storage engine that
             applies per-segment adaptive lightweight compression (RLE, bitpacking, FOR, delta,
             dictionary, FSST for strings, ALP for floats) chosen automatically by a
             sampling-based Analyze phase.
innovation:  Per-segment adaptive compression cascade: each fixed-size column segment is independently analyzed
             by cheaply sampling the data, every candidate codec estimates its own compressed size
             via a uniform Analyze/Compress interface, and the smallest-estimate codec wins -- so
             the engine picks the best lightweight scheme per data block rather than committing to
             one global codec, all while keeping the data scannable in place without a heavyweight
             LZ pass.
techniques:
  - FSST string compression: a 255-entry static symbol table mapping frequent 1-8 byte substrings
    to single-byte codes, ~2-3x on text with random-access per-string decode (no block
    decompression); dictionary offsets stored via delta+bitpacking
  - ALP float compression: PseudoDecimal path uses one shared exponent e + factor f per 1024-value
    vector to map doubles to ints then Frame-of-Reference + bitpacking; falls back to cutting
    low-variance front bits and bitpacking the remainder; outliers stored as exceptions
  - FastLanes 1024-value bitpacking with the Unified Transposed '04261537' layout over a virtual
    MM1024 register so scalar code auto-vectorizes to any SIMD width (AVX2/AVX512/NEON) with no
    intrinsics
  - Frame-of-Reference (FOR): store min value + bitpacked offsets per 1024-value block; ideal for
    dates/timestamps/clustered ints
  - Delta encoding feeding into FOR+bitpacking for monotonic sequences
  - Constant + RLE encoding for low-cardinality / repeated runs (store value once, or
    value+run-length pairs)
  - Uniform CompressionFunction interface (InitAnalyze/Analyze/FinalAnalyze size estimate then
    InitCompress/Compress/Finalize) enabling cheap sampling-based codec selection per segment
  - Block-based single file: 256KB blocks, main header + two alternating DatabaseHeaders for
    atomic ping-pong checkpoint, free list of reclaimable blocks, metadata blocks pointing to row
    groups/segments
nest steals: Two distinct steals into nest-format/src/encoding. (1) FSST as a new RESERVED encoding id (4-255
             slot) for the canonical text sections (chunks_canonical, chunks_original_spans,
             provenance): 2-3x on text like zstd but with per-string random-access decode straight
             off mmap, so nest can cite/materialize a single chunk's bytes without inflating a
             whole zstd frame; pairs naturally with nest://content_hash/chunk_id byte-span reopen.
             (2) ALP + FastLanes-style transposed bitpacking as the principled successor to nest's
             crude int8/float16 embedding quantization: ALP is lossless and the FastLanes 04261537
             layout means one scalar decode kernel auto-vectorizes to AVX2 and NEON, collapsing
             nest-runtime's three SIMD paths into one portable kernel. Most important
             architecturally: adopt DuckDB's per-section Analyze-and-pick pattern in the nest
             writer so each section's codec is chosen by a deterministic sampling estimate
             (seeded/ordered to stay reproducible) instead of fixed per-preset encodings --
             directly serving 'compress more than everyone' by letting tiny/compressed presets
             pick the smallest codec per section while keeping byte-identical determinism.
compression: Directly central: DuckDB is the reference design for cheap, scannable, lossless lightweight
             compression. FSST gives random-access text compression (better fit than zstd for
             nest's cite/materialize path), ALP gives lossless float compression that could shrink
             embeddings better than naive float16/int8 without recall loss, and the per-segment
             adaptive selection is exactly the 'pick the smallest encoding automatically'
             machinery nest's north star needs. Caveat: nest scores f32 directly from mmap on the
             hot path, so any embedding codec must keep cheap/random decode -- FastLanes
             bitpacking decodes fast enough to fit, plain zstd does not (which is why nest already
             bans zstd on embeddings).
perf:        FSST ~= LZ4 ratio (3x on TPCH l_comment) but decodes individual strings; analyze samples 25% of
             row-group vectors to estimate size. ALP is 2-4x faster decompress than older
             Patas/Chimp at ~2x better ratio, avg 2.08x on FCBench vs 1.47x for Bitshuffle+Zstd.
             FastLanes claims >100 billion ints/sec decode; the transposed interleaved layout
             speeds even scalar decode by exposing ILP, and the same source auto-vectorizes across
             Intel/AMD/Apple/AWS. All codecs operate on 1024-value vectors keeping working sets in
             L1/registers.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] cruppstahl/upscaledb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An embedded, page-based B+Tree key-value/analytical engine (single-file, multi-database, mmap'd)
             whose headline innovation is SIMD-accelerated integer-key compression inside B+Tree
             leaf KeyLists, plus analytical aggregates that run directly over compressed pages.
innovation:  Store sorted integer key columns as delta-coded, block-bit-packed streams (SIMD-BP128 / VarIntGB /
             StreamVByte / MaskedVByte) and operate on them WITHOUT full decompression: binary
             search, range scans, and analytical aggregates (SUM/COUNT/AVG) run directly over the
             packed blocks. Decode kernels are vectorized via per-control-byte SSE shuffle-mask
             lookup tables (StreamVByte) and per-block fixed-bit-width unpacking (SIMD-BP128),
             hitting 4-8 billion ints/sec. This is the 'compress columnar data AND serve it from
             the same compact bytes' pattern, co-authored by upscaledb's own Christoph Rupp with
             Daniel Lemire.
techniques:
  - SIMD-BP128 binary packing: split a sorted column into blocks of 128 ints, compute max
    bit-width b for the block, pack 128 ints into b 128-bit SSE words; decode via fixed shift/mask
    SIMD kernel (best case 1 bit/key, ~32x shrink)
  - Delta (differential) coding of sorted keys before packing so only small gaps are stored;
    reconstruct via vectorized prefix-sum (cumulative add)
  - StreamVByte: separate control stream (2 bits per int = byte length 1/2/3/4) from data stream;
    decode 4 ints/iteration via a 256-entry SSE pshufb shuffle-mask table indexed by the control
    byte (co-authored by Rupp)
  - VarIntGB / Group Varint: one control byte per group of 4 ints for branch-free length lookup;
    MaskedVByte for vectorizing classic varint streams
  - Frame-of-Reference (FOR) and patched PFOR / SIMD-FastPFOR: subtract a per-block reference
    value, bit-pack residuals, store rare exceptions out-of-band for fast random access
  - Per-block bit-width header lets binary search skip to the target block, decode only that
    block, then scan in-register
  - Analytical aggregates (SUM/COUNT/AVG) computed in B+Tree context across many packed keys at
    once without materializing records (millions/call)
  - Transparent record/journal compression (snappy/zlib/lzf) plus CRC32 page verification and AES
    page encryption layered under one page abstraction
nest steals: Add a fourth integer codec family to nest-format/src/encoding alongside raw/zstd/float16/int8: a
             delta+bit-packing codec (SIMD-BP128-style blocks of 128, or StreamVByte) for INTEGER
             sections, specifically chunk_ids and chunks_original_spans (offset/length pairs are
             monotone or near-monotone and delta-code to ~1-4 bits each, far below zstd on
             small-int arrays). Reserve a new encoding id in the additive 4-255 v1 range (no
             version bump). The decode kernel slots into nest-runtime's existing SIMD dispatcher
             (AVX2/NEON/scalar fallback already present) as a new vectorized unpack path: NEON
             tbl, AVX2 pshufb shuffle-mask tables. Critically for the hot path, StreamVByte's
             'operate-in-register' property lets the runtime reconstruct span offsets during
             materialize() and resolve nest://content_hash/chunk_id citations without
             decompressing whole sections, mirroring upscaledb's binary-search-on-compressed
             trick. Keep it deterministic (fixed block size 128, sorted input nest already
             mandates) so reproducible-build byte-identity holds. Do NOT apply to embeddings (they
             stay raw/f16/int8 scored straight from mmap), exactly as nest already forbids zstd on
             embeddings.
compression: Directly central to 'compress more than everyone'. nest leans on zstd for non-embedding sections,
             but zstd is weak on arrays of small/monotone integers, which is exactly what
             chunk_ids and chunks_original_spans (span offset+length pairs) are. Delta + SIMD
             bit-packing reaches 1-4 bits/integer (vs zstd's byte-granular framing overhead on
             short int runs), shrinking structural sections while DECODING faster than memcpy. It
             is a true compress-and-serve codec: the runtime searches the packed bytes directly
             off mmap, preserving the offline/single-file/no-materialize hot path. Rare codec that
             improves both size and speed at once, which is precisely nest's mission.
perf:        Reference simdcomp decodes binary-packed ints at ~0.3 cycles/int (>8 billion/sec on Skylake);
             StreamVByte decode can exceed memcpy (up to 2x faster than varint-G8IU). SIMD-BP128
             saves up to 2 bits/int over prior vectorized schemes; whole-DB benchmark showed ~40%
             faster queries AND ~10x compression vs uncompressed B+Tree. Decode cost scales with
             bit-width (fewer bits = faster), so delta-coded near-sorted data is both smallest and
             fastest. Blocks of 128 ints map onto 128-bit registers (SSE/NEON 128-bit lanes),
             portable to nest's AVX2/NEON dispatch; AVX2 runs two 128-bit lanes. Per-block
             bit-width metadata is tiny (1 byte/block).
license:     upscaledb itself is GPLv3 / commercial dual-license (AVOID vendoring its code). The underlying
             algorithm libraries are permissive and safe to learn/port: lemire/simdcomp and
             lemire/streamvbyte are Apache-2.0, and StreamVByte is explicitly patent-free;
             permissive Rust ports exist (streamvbyte by Marshall Pierce / Trevor McCulloch,
             bazhenov/svbyte). Reimplement from the papers and Apache refs, not from the GPL
             upscaledb source.

# category: fulltext-search  (16 repos)
# focus: full-text and lexical search engines (inverted index, BM25, edge cases)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MUST] quickwit-oss/quickwit
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Distributed full-text/analytics search engine over object storage, built on the Tantivy Rust
             library, whose immutable "split" files pack an FST term dictionary plus 128-doc-block
             SIMD-bitpacked posting lists and bitpacked columnar fast fields, all mmap-readable
             and decompressed on the fly.
innovation:  An mmap-first, fully block-structured inverted index where the term dictionary (FST), postings
             (128-doc delta+SIMD-bitpacked blocks with a skip list), and field norms (1-byte
             quantized) are all self-describing and decoded lazily off disk, enabling block-max
             WAND pruning and branchless in-block search.
techniques:
  - FST term dictionary mapping term -> ordinal -> TermInfo, shared-prefix compressed, O(term-len)
    lookup with free prefix/range scans
  - 128-doc posting blocks: delta-encoded SIMD-bitpacked doc ids + separately bitpacked term
    freqs, each block at its own optimal bit width
  - VInt remainder for tails and rare terms (doc_freq<128) to avoid fixed block/skip overhead
  - Per-block skip list (last_doc_id, bit widths, fieldnorm_id, max_term_freq) driving block-max
    WAND top-K pruning
  - Branchless unrolled binary search over fixed &[u32;128] yielding cmove-only code (~9.55
    cycles/seek)
  - 1-byte saturating field-norm quantization exploiting BM25 length-saturation
  - Bitpacked columnar fast fields: min_value + fetch_bits, single-fetch random access
  - Roaring-derived indexable bitset (dense 1.25 bits/doc popcount-rank vs sparse 16-bit) with
    O(1) rank/select
nest steals: Rebuild nest's bm25_index (section 0x08, built in nest-python, served in nest-runtime/src/bm25) as
             an mmap-native block layout instead of an in-memory map. Concretely: (1) store the
             term dictionary as an FST via the `fst` crate (pure-rust, MIT/Apache,
             no_std-friendly) inside section 0x08 -- this replaces the alphabetical-term-order
             map, keeps determinism (FST build over sorted terms is canonical), gives prefix/range
             scan for free, and compresses the vocabulary; (2) encode each term's postings as
             delta-encoded 128-doc blocks bitpacked with the `bitpacking` crate (Tantivy's own
             subcrate, scalar+SIMD, falls back cleanly to scalar for nest's portability rule),
             with a VInt remainder for rare terms; (3) add a per-block skip list with last_doc_id
             + max_term_freq + fieldnorm to do block-max WAND so search-text only decodes blocks
             that can affect top-K; (4) quantize doc lengths to 1 byte (BM25 saturation) and store
             as a bitpacked column. Each of these is byte-deterministic (seeded/canonical
             ordering) and additive within format v1 (0x08 is already reserved, internal layout is
             free to evolve). The whole index becomes a mmap-and-score artifact matching nest's
             "no socket, decode off disk" model, and shrinks the bm25 section dramatically vs raw
             text -- directly serving the "compress more than everyone" north star. nest-cli
             search-text then reads postings off mmap, killing most of the 300-500ms python boot
             path because no python is needed to query.
compression: Very high. The 128-doc delta+bitpack block format with per-block optimal bit widths is a proven,
             deterministic, mmap-friendly way to compress posting lists far below raw text, and
             the FST term dictionary compresses the vocabulary via shared prefixes. The
             1.25-bit/doc Roaring-style bitset and 1-byte field-norm quantization are concrete
             near-entropy-minimal encodings. All bitpacking-based, so it composes with nest's
             existing zstd-on-non-embedding-sections without fighting the hot path (postings
             decode is cheap integer SIMD, not entropy decode).
perf:        Posting blocks decompress on the fly from mmap via SIMD bitpacking; in-block seek is branchless
             (LLVM unrolls a fixed-size 128-element binary search into 7 cmove iterations, ~9.55
             CPU cycles). Fast-field reads are a single memory fetch (min + bitpacked slice).
             Dense bitset rank is ~5ns via popcount on 64-bit mini-blocks (1.25 bits/doc).
             Block-max WAND skips entire 128-doc blocks that cannot enter top-K, so query cost
             scales with surviving blocks not corpus size. The `bitpacking` crate provides both
             SSE/AVX2 and scalar paths, satisfying nest's NEON/scalar-fallback portability
             constraint.
license:     MIT (Tantivy, the fst crate, and the bitpacking crate are all MIT licensed; Quickwit itself is
             AGPLv3, but the reusable building blocks nest would vendor -- tantivy, fst,
             bitpacking -- are MIT, so safe to depend on/adapt)
