[MUST] meilisearch/meilisearch
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust lexical+hybrid search engine whose term dictionary is a byte-level minimal-acyclic FST
             (BurntSushi `fst`), whose docid sets are roaring bitmaps with a custom small-set
             serialization, and whose typo/prefix matching is a Levenshtein-automaton intersected
             against the FST in one streaming pass.
innovation:  Store the entire sorted term dictionary as a single minimal acyclic finite-state transducer (FST):
             the FSA shares both common prefixes AND common suffixes across all terms, compressing
             the dictionary to a fraction of the concatenated-string size while staying
             mmap-friendly and zero-copy. The same structure that compresses the terms also serves
             prefix lookup and fuzzy lookup by intersecting it with a Levenshtein DFA in a single
             streaming pass (no per-term scan).
techniques:
  - FST term dictionary (BurntSushi `fst` crate): minimal acyclic FSA compressing shared
    prefix+suffix of sorted keys, mmap zero-copy, byte-level transitions
  - Levenshtein automaton x FST streaming intersection: DFA from query term + max edit distance
    intersected with FST to enumerate all in-distance terms without scanning; unify prefix + typo
    on the last query term in one pass
  - Roaring bitmaps for docid sets (array/bitmap/run containers per 64k block) with a custom
    small-set serialization that writes raw integers when smaller than the standard roaring header
  - Variable-length integer term ids: intern words as ~2-byte varints instead of 5-6 byte strings;
    store dictionary once, reference by id
  - Database reshape (word,fid)->docids => word->[(fid,docids)] to kill per-field word
    duplication; positional bucketing capped at first N positions to bound posting growth
  - Damerau-Levenshtein typo budget keyed to term length (5 chars=>1 typo, 9=>2) plus
    word-boundary concatenation/split candidates each counted as 1 typo
  - Bucket-sort cascade of independent ranking rules (words, typo, proximity, attribute, sort,
    exactness) applied sequentially over shrinking candidate sets
  - charabia: script-detection-driven segmentation + normalization (lowercase, diacritic strip,
    CJK/Thai dictionary segmentation) shared identically at index and query time
nest steals: Rebuild nest-runtime/src/bm25/codec.rs section 0x08. Today nest serializes the term dictionary as
             raw `(u32 len, utf8 bytes)` per term (alphabetical) and postings as fixed `(doc u32,
             tf u32)` pairs with zero compression. Steal three layers: (1) replace the term-string
             blob with an FST map (term -> u32 term-id) built from the already-alphabetical terms;
             the FST is deterministic given sorted input (satisfies nest's alphabetical-term-order
             rule) and shrinks the dictionary dramatically on natural-language corpora, a pure win
             for the compression north star and compatible with the zstd-on-non-embedding-sections
             policy. (2) Delta+varint encode postings: docids are sorted ascending so store first
             docid as varint then gaps as varints, tf as varint, cutting the 8-byte fixed pair to
             ~2-3 bytes typical and shrinking the tiny/hybrid presets. (3) Optionally swap
             per-term Vec<u32> for a roaring bitmap docid set with the small-set raw-int trick for
             rare terms. The FST also closes nest's two known bm25 gaps:
             Levenshtein-automaton-over-FST intersection gives typo tolerance and
             prefix/search-as-you-type, and FST byte-level transitions make CJK/Thai bigram tokens
             compress well. All additive: new internal layout under the existing reserved 0x08 id;
             bump only BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION (per-section payload version), never
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION, since section meaning is unchanged. Split fst-dict and
             posting-codec into separate modules to stay under the 300-line cap.
compression: Directly central. The FST term dictionary is one of the highest-leverage text-index compression
             structures known (shared prefix+suffix minimization beats simple front-coding), and
             delta+varint postings plus roaring bitmaps are the standard inverted-index size wins.
             Together they make nest's bm25 section (0x08) a fraction of its current size,
             advancing the compress-more-than-everyone mission while staying deterministic,
             mmap-friendly, and offline.
perf:        FST lookup is O(query length) with zero-copy mmap reads (no whole-dictionary decompression).
             Levenshtein-DFA x FST intersection enumerates in-distance terms in a single streaming
             pass; BurntSushi notes the bundled Levenshtein construction is proof-of-concept
             (~2.5ms for distance 2 on `Levenshtein`), while the
             tantivy-search/levenshtein-automata algorithm builds the same DFA in ~0.09ms if query
             latency headroom is needed (FST intersection dominates anyway). Roaring bitmaps give
             SIMD-friendly union/intersection across array/bitmap/run containers. nest's current
             HashMap<String,TermEntry> is fast but uncompressed and non-mmap; FST trades a tiny
             constant lookup cost for large on-disk and zero-copy gains.
license:     MIT (meilisearch core); BurntSushi `fst` is Unlicense/MIT (public-domain-friendly, safe to
             vendor); roaring-rs is Apache-2.0/MIT; charabia is MIT.

[HIGH] apache/lucene
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Java full-text search library whose default codec compresses inverted-index postings with
             delta-coded, block-bit-packed integers (FOR/PForDelta over 128-doc blocks), stores
             term dictionaries as a shared-prefix/suffix FST (now Trie), and accelerates top-k
             BM25 with Block-Max WAND skipping over impact-block skip lists.
innovation:  Sorted integer lists (doc ids, freqs) are never stored raw: they are delta-encoded then bit-packed
             in fixed 128-value blocks using Frame-Of-Reference (FOR for docs,
             Patched-FOR/PForDelta for freqs/positions), with a VInt/group-varint tail. This
             single idea -- decompose a monotone integer column into delta + per-block
             minimal-bit-width packing -- is the workhorse that shrinks postings 4-10x while
             keeping SIMD-friendly fixed-width decode.
techniques:
  - Delta (gap) encoding of sorted doc-id postings before any packing
  - FOR / PForDelta: 128-value blocks bit-packed with PackedInts at the minimal bitsPerValue, with
    outliers patched separately
  - byte-aligned bitsPerValue rounding (to 8/16/32) tuned by acceptableOverheadRatio for fast
    random-access decode
  - VInt / group-varint tail encoding for the sub-128 remainder of each postings list
  - FST (minimal acyclic subsequential transducer, Mihov/Maurel) for term dict with BOTH prefix
    and suffix sharing; recently a byte-keyed Trie in blocktree
  - BlockTree / burst-trie term dict: in-memory FST/Trie index over on-disk variable-length term
    blocks (25-48 terms) sharing a common prefix
  - Block-Max WAND: per-block max impact (pruned (tf,dl) pairs) in hierarchical skip lists
    (8/64/512/4096) to skip decoding whole blocks during top-k
  - int8/int4 scalar quantization with per-vector confidence-interval calibration, two int4 values
    packed per byte
nest steals: Target nest-runtime bm25/codec.rs and bm25/index.rs (the 0x08 section). Today to_bytes() writes
             each posting as raw little-endian (u32 doc, u32 tf) -- 8 bytes/posting, zero
             compression -- and search() does exhaustive-OR over every posting. Adapt three layers
             additively: (1) replace per-term postings encoding with delta-coded doc ids +
             deterministic bit-packing of 128-doc blocks at minimal byte-aligned bitsPerValue plus
             a varint tail; tf gets its own bit-packed block. This is a pure encoding change to
             section 0x08 (bump only BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION, NOT NEST_FORMAT_VERSION, since the
             section is optional/additive), cutting the bm25 section several-fold -- directly
             serving the compress-more-than-everyone north star and shrinking the hybrid preset.
             (2) Replace the raw length-prefixed alphabetical term list with shared-prefix
             front-coding (store common-prefix-len + suffix per term; already alphabetical so
             deterministic) -- a small no-new-dependency win before a full FST. (3) Add per-block
             max-impact metadata so search() does Block-Max WAND skipping instead of exhaustive
             OR, cutting search-text latency; the mandatory exact-cosine rerank in nest's search
             contract is unaffected since BMW only reorders/skips candidate generation. All three
             preserve repr(C) little-endian, determinism (alphabetical terms, seeded), and
             never-panic typed errors.
compression: Directly central: postings delta+block-bitpacking is the canonical lossless compression for the
             exact integer column nest currently stores raw (u32 doc, u32 tf) in section 0x08,
             plus front-coding/FST for term strings. Both shrink bm25_index and the hybrid preset
             with zero recall loss and zero new dependencies -- squarely the
             compress-more-than-everyone mandate.
perf:        FOR/PForDelta in 128-value blocks is SIMD-friendly: fixed-width packing lets a vectorized unpack
             decode a whole block branch-free (Lucene uses its vector API); nest can decode with
             scalar shifts now and add an AVX2/NEON unpack later via its existing simd dispatcher.
             Byte-aligned bitsPerValue trades a few bits for faster random access -- relevant
             since nest mmaps and decodes on the hot path. Block-Max WAND cuts top-k query time to
             ~10-20% of exhaustive OR in production reports but degrades faster as k grows. int4
             quantization halves int8 storage (8x vs f32) at ~30% slower scoring when bit-packed.
license:     Apache License 2.0 (permissive; format/algorithms free to clean-room reimplement in Rust, no
             copyleft contamination)

[HIGH] quickwit-oss/tantivy
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Lucene-class Rust full-text search library whose inverted index stores posting lists as
             interlaced 128-doc blocks of delta-encoded, SIMD-bitpacked doc ids and bitpacked term
             frequencies, fronted by an FST term dictionary and a Block-Max-WAND skip list.
innovation:  128-doc block posting lists with delta+SIMD-bitpacking and a Block-Max-WAND skip list, fronted by
             an FST term dictionary.
techniques:
  - 128-doc block posting layout: delta+bitpacking on doc ids, bitpacking on term frequencies,
    interlaced to avoid pointer chasing
  - minimal per-block bit-width b = ceil(log2(max+1)); value = min + fetch_bits(num_bits*i..)
  - VInt fallback for the incomplete trailing block (1-127 docs)
  - SIMD bitpacking (BitPacker4x SSE3 128-int, BitPacker8x AVX2 256-int, scalar fallback) at ~5-7
    billion int/s
  - FST term dictionary (BurntSushi fst): term -> TermOrdinal, ordinal mod block size locates one
    TermInfo block to decode
  - skip list with per-block fieldnorm_id + max_term_freq enabling Block-Max-WAND top-k pruning
  - fast-field columnar codecs: bitpacking + GCD divisor + linear/blockwise-linear interpolation
    over a min_value baseline
  - LZ4/Zstd block-compressed document store kept separate from the index
nest steals: Rewrite nest-runtime/src/bm25/codec.rs (and the Posting/TermEntry model in index.rs) to replace
             the current raw fixed-width u32 (doc, tf) pair encoding with tantivy-style 128-doc
             blocks: per term, delta-encode the already-doc-sorted posting doc ids and bitpack at
             minimal per-block width, bitpack the parallel tf block, and VInt the trailing partial
             block. This is a pure section-0x08 payload codec change behind BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION
             (bump it; section id and additive v1 format are untouched), so it respects the FROZEN
             container. Term order stays alphabetical (already deterministic) -- optionally front
             the term-string region with an fst-crate FST to shrink it and get prefix lookups.
             Keep a scalar bitpacking kernel in nest-runtime/src/simd to honor the
             offline/repr(C)/LE/no-panic constraints (only vendor the bitpacking crate if its
             scalar fallback is acceptable). Add a per-block max_term_freq plus doc_length-derived
             bound to enable Block-Max-WAND in bm25/index.rs::search, cutting the full-posting
             scan that today touches every Posting. Keep files under the 300-line cap by leaving
             the block codec in codec.rs and putting WAND in a new wand.rs.
compression: High and direct. Today nest spends a flat 8 bytes per posting (u32 doc + u32 tf) plus u32
             doc_lengths, the dominant cost in the bm25_index section. Delta+bitpacking typically
             collapses doc ids to 4-8 bits each on dense corpora and tf to 2-4 bits (most tf are
             1-2), a 4-8x shrink of the dominant posting array, advancing the 'compress more than
             everyone' north star for the hybrid/tiny presets. The zstd wrapper over the
             now-bitpacked section adds little extra -- bitpacked integers are already near
             entropy-optimal, so this is real structural compression, not just a general codec
             pass.
perf:        bitpacking crate hits ~5.3-7 billion int/s compress and ~5.5-6.5 billion int/s decompress
             (SSE3/AVX2); delta decode ~5 billion int/s. A scalar fallback exists and is selected
             at runtime, so determinism + portability hold (nest can ship a scalar kernel matching
             its existing SIMD-dispatch pattern). Block-Max-WAND turns BM25 from O(all postings)
             into early-terminating top-k, helping amortize the ~300-500ms search-text python boot
             overhead by making the scan negligible. Bitpacked blocks decode straight off mmap
             (fetch_bits over a block) with no per-value branching, matching nest's mmap-and-score
             hot path.
license:     MIT

[HIGH] typesense/typesense
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A from-scratch C++ in-memory typo-tolerant lexical search engine whose inverted index is an
             Adaptive Radix Tree (ART) over tokens with Frame-of-Reference bit-packed posting
             lists, pointer-tagged compact lists for short postings, incremental trie-Levenshtein
             fuzzy traversal, and relevance encoded as a single sortable 64-bit integer.
innovation:  Posting lists are compressed with Frame-of-Reference (FOR) bit-packing: each block stores a min
             base m, then packs (value - m) deltas at the minimal bit-width b = required_bits(M -
             m) for that block. This is a self-contained, branch-light integer codec that
             compresses sorted doc-ids and token positions to near entropy while still allowing
             random access (at(i)) and binary search (lower_bound) directly on the packed bytes
             without full decompression -- exactly the "compress AND still serve from the packed
             form" property nest needs on its scoring hot path.
techniques:
  - Frame-of-Reference (FOR) bit-packing: per-block min base + adaptive bit-width
    b=required_bits(M-m) over delta-from-min, sized via for_compressed_size_bits() (lib for.h)
  - Block-chained posting_list_t with id_block_map = std::map<last_id_t, block_t*> as a skip index
    to jump to the right block for range/AND-intersection without scanning the chain
  - compact_posting_list_t: interleaved [num_offsets, off1..offn, id] records in a single uint32
    flex-array for short lists, avoiding per-list block overhead
  - Low-bit pointer tagging (ptr|1) to distinguish compact vs full posting list at zero extra
    storage; IS_COMPACT/RAW_PTR/COMPACT_PTR macros mask the LSB
  - Relevance packed into one 64-bit int via bit-shifts (word-presence high bits, then unique-word
    count, typo cost, proximity, exact match, position, synonym) so ranking is a single integer
    compare
  - Adaptive Radix Tree node4/node16/node48/node256 upgrading node type as fanout grows, leaves
    holding sorted_array ids + offset_index + raw offsets
  - Trie-Levenshtein fuzzy search: descend ART carrying parent's DP cost-row, compute child row
    from insert/delete/substitute min, prune subtrees by max-word-length and length-delta bound;
    O(k*nodes) not O(terms)
nest steals: Two concrete drops. (1) nest-format: add a FOR bit-packed codec for the bm25_index (section 0x08)
             postings -- doc-ids delta-from-min + adaptive bit-width per block, positions
             likewise. Additive (new encoding id in reserved 4-255 applied only to 0x08, never
             embeddings), deterministic (bit-width is a pure function of the block min/max), and
             supports at()/lower_bound on packed bytes so nest-runtime bm25::Bm25Index keeps O(log
             n) AND-merge directly off mmap with no decompress on the hot path -- same discipline
             nest uses scoring embeddings from mmap. (2) nest-runtime: implement trie-Levenshtein
             incremental cost-row fuzzy matching over the bm25 term dictionary for typo tolerance
             and, via a byte/grapheme trie, a path to fix the known CJK/Thai word-segmentation gap
             (match character n-gram paths instead of whitespace words). The compact-vs-full
             interleaving idea maps to a small-posting inline format cutting overhead for the many
             low-DF terms in nest's PT-BR corpus. The 64-bit packed score gives a deterministic,
             alphabetical-tie-broken single-integer sort key fitting nest's deterministic bm25
             contract before the mandatory exact-cosine rerank.
compression: High and direct: FOR bit-packing of delta-from-min postings is a textbook extreme-compression win
             for the bm25_index doc-id and position lists (the inverted index is often a large
             fraction of a lexical index), and it preserves random access + binary search on the
             packed bytes, so it compresses WITHOUT giving up serve-from-mmap speed -- exactly
             nest's north-star constraint. It stacks under nest's existing zstd on non-embedding
             sections (FOR first to shrink integer entropy, zstd second for residual redundancy).
perf:        In-memory, read-optimized C++; ART chosen for cache locality. Iterators decompress a FOR block
             into a raw uint32 array on demand for tight loops, but random access works on the
             packed form. FOR decode is branch-light bit-unpacking amenable to SIMD (nest's
             existing AVX2/NEON dispatch could vectorize the unpack). Single-integer relevance
             compare avoids multi-field comparator branches in top-k. Skip index (id_block_map)
             makes AND-intersection sub-linear. Trie-Levenshtein is O(k*nodes) with length-based
             subtree pruning, far cheaper than scanning the dictionary.
license:     GPL-3.0 (Typesense core is GPLv3). Clean-room reimplement the FOR codec and trie-Levenshtein from
             public literature; do NOT vendor or copy Typesense C++ into nest's permissive Rust
             core. The algorithms (FOR, delta coding, trie edit-distance) are public ideas; the
             specific code is GPL.

[HIGH] manticoresoftware/manticoresearch (+ manticoresoftware/columnar, the MCL library)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C++ full-text + columnar search engine whose Manticore Columnar Library (MCL) compresses
             attributes with per-block adaptive encoding (const / table-dictionary / delta+bitpack
             / generic) plus min-max zone maps and a PGM learned secondary index, while full-text
             postings use VLN (variable-length number) delta encoding and the docstore stores
             original text in LZ4-compressed fixed-size blocks.
innovation:  Per-block adaptive codec selection: data is cut into fixed-size blocks (a few hundred to ~1024
             rows) and for EACH block independently the encoder picks the cheapest scheme from a
             small menu - const (1 value when all equal), table/dictionary (store a <256-entry
             value table + small indices when low cardinality), delta+bitpack (FastPFOR-style:
             subtract a base, bitpack residuals to minimal bit-width), or generic raw fallback -
             and writes a tiny per-block min-max header so a scan can skip the whole compressed
             block without decoding when the filter range cannot match. This decouples compression
             choice from the global column and lets locally-uniform regions collapse to near-zero
             bytes while keeping random block access O(1).
techniques:
  - Per-block adaptive encoding menu: const, table/dictionary (<256 uniques), delta+bitpack,
    generic raw - chosen independently per block by cost
  - Dictionary/table encoding: store value table once + minimal-width index per row when block
    cardinality is low
  - Delta + FastPFOR-style binary bitpacking: subtract base/min, pack residuals to
    ceil(log2(max-min)) bits, SIMD-friendly (SSE4.2)
  - Min-max zone maps per block enabling skip-without-decode (analyzers reject blocks on range
    filters)
  - VLN (varint) delta encoding of doc-ids and positions in inverted postings (.spd doclists, .spp
    hitlists)
  - PGM-index (Piecewise Geometric Model): learned piecewise-linear key->position index, dominates
    B-tree space by orders of magnitude
  - Docstore: original text in fixed-size (default 16KB) blocks, LZ4 / LZ4HC compressed,
    decoded-block LRU cache, tunable block size trades ratio vs latency
  - Cost-based codec selection driven by cheap precomputed block statistics (min, max, distinct
    count)
nest steals: Two concrete steals. (1) nest-format encoding layer (crates/nest-format/src/encoding + sections):
             add a per-block adaptive codec for non-embedding sections, especially
             bm25_index(0x08) postings and chunk_ids/spans, as a NEW additive encoding id in the
             reserved 4-255 range. Cut each section's integer streams into fixed blocks; per block
             pick const / dict(<256 uniques) / delta+zigzag+bitpack / raw by a deterministic cost
             compare; prepend a small repr(C) little-endian block header (scheme tag, base,
             bit-width, min, max). Beats whole-section zstd on structured integer columns
             (doc-ids, term freqs, span offsets) and stays deterministic (alphabetical term order
             already yields sorted, delta-friendly streams). (2) nest-runtime bm25 + search path
             (crates/nest-runtime/src/bm25, search): use per-block min-max zone maps to skip
             entire posting blocks during candidate gathering before the mandatory exact-cosine
             rerank, cutting decode work on selective queries with zero recall change. Keep
             embeddings exempt (scored directly from mmap), matching Manticore's rule that
             columnar stream-compression is wrong for hot random-access data. PGM-index is a
             reference idea for a future deterministic seekable offset map over chunk_ids/term
             dictionary if a 0x09+ section is ever spent on it.
compression: Directly central. Per-block adaptive encoding (const/dict/delta-bitpack) is exactly the 'compress
             more than everyone' lever for nest's structured integer sections: it exploits local
             uniformity a single global zstd pass misses, and const/dict blocks collapse
             low-entropy regions to a handful of bytes. The min-max zone map is a free
             compression-adjacent win (skip decode). FastPFOR bitpacking gives
             near-entropy-minimal integer storage with SIMD decode, matching nest's AVX2/NEON
             dispatcher philosophy.
perf:        MCL requires SSE>=4.2; delta/bitpack codecs are SIMD binary-packing (FastPFOR family, same lineage
             Lucene adopted) decoding ~128 ints per vectorized pass. Block skipping via min-max
             avoids decompressing whole blocks - the dominant cost saver on selective scans.
             Docstore default 16KB LZ4 blocks with a daemon-wide 16MB decoded-block cache; bigger
             blocks raise ratio, smaller blocks lower random-access latency (a tunable nest can
             mirror per preset). Empty-instance footprint ~40MB RSS; columnar keeps only tiny
             per-block metadata resident, serving the rest from filesystem cache rather than
             mmap-resident pages.
license:     GPL-3.0 (manticoresearch daemon); MCL columnar library is Apache-2.0; FastPFOR Apache-2.0;
             PGM-index Apache-2.0. Reimplement the published/Apache-licensed techniques rather
             than copying GPL daemon code to keep nest licensing clean.

[HIGH] RediSearch/RediSearch
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A C full-text/secondary-index engine for Redis whose inverted index is a custom block-structured
             posting list using delta+varint gap coding with pluggable per-schema encoder
             variants, backed by a HyperLogLog-driven adaptive numeric range tree.
innovation:  Posting-list entries are encoded with delta (gap) coding of monotonically-increasing internal
             32-bit doc-ids plus variable-byte (varint) packing, and the engine selects the
             MINIMAL encoder variant (DocIdsOnly / FreqsOnly / FieldsOnly / Full) per index based
             on which metadata the schema actually needs, so a doc-id-only posting collapses to
             ~1-2 bytes/hit instead of carrying dead frequency/offset fields.
techniques:
  - Delta/gap encoding of internal monotonic 32-bit doc-ids so postings stay sorted and gaps stay
    small
  - Variable-byte (varint) integer coding: 1 byte for <255, 2 bytes for <16383 (~75% index shrink,
    ~6 bytes best-case hit)
  - Pluggable encoder family by IndexFlags: DocIdsOnly, FreqsOnly, FieldsOnly, Full - write only
    fields the schema needs
  - Variable record layout: docId_delta(1-4B) + freq(1-2B) + fieldMask(1-16B) + offsetsLen(1-2B) +
    varint offset vector
  - Block-structured posting lists: each IndexBlock stores firstId/lastId for binary-search
    skip/seek across blocks
  - TF*docRank pre-folded into stored hit score so only IDF applied at query time (cheaper
    BM25/TF-IDF)
  - Numeric range tree: binary tree of range nodes each owning its own inverted index, adaptive
    median split via double-heap
  - HyperLogLog (6-bit precision ~13% error) for per-node cardinality, depth-dependent split
    threshold 16<<(depth*2) capped 2500
nest steals: Target the optional bm25_index section (0x08) in nest-format and the bm25::Bm25Index reader in
             nest-runtime. nest's bm25 today is word-segmented with alphabetical-term-order
             layout; replace its posting storage with the delta+varint block scheme. (1) In
             nest-format encoding/sections, store each term's postings as gap-coded internal
             chunk-ordinals (already monotonic) packed with a LEB128/varint codec, plus a
             minimal-encoder selector so a boolean/presence index ships DocIdsOnly (1-2 B/hit)
             while a scoring index adds a varint term-freq; purely additive, no
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump, fits encoding-id/section reservation rules. (2) Add a
             fixed-size repr(C) LE block header per posting list carrying first_id/last_id so bm25
             iterators binary-search the right block and skip across blocks, speeding
             AND-intersection without a server. (3) Preserve determinism: fixed block size, terms
             in existing alphabetical order. This advances 'compress more than everyone':
             varint-delta postings are typically 3-5x smaller than naive ones, and since
             bm25_index is a non-embedding section, zstd may layer on top for compounding gains.
             The numeric range tree + HLL split is a blueprint for a future numeric/metadata
             filter section (reserved id 0x09+) to compress and range-query provenance/json
             columns.
compression: High and direct. Delta+varint gap coding plus minimal-encoder selection is the canonical way to
             shrink inverted indexes 3-5x; it is the lever for nest's bm25_index(0x08). Because
             bm25_index is a non-embedding section, nest can layer zstd on the already-compact
             varint stream for compounding gains. Block firstId/lastId skip lists keep the compact
             form fast to query off mmap. The HLL-driven adaptive range tree shows how to compress
             numeric/metadata columns (future sections) while staying queryable.
perf:        Best-case ~6 bytes/posting hit, ~1-2 bytes for doc-id-only; varint claimed to cut index size ~75%
             vs fixed 32-bit. Block firstId/lastId enables O(log n) skip to a block then linear
             varint decode, avoiding full posting scans during intersections. TF*rank pre-folded
             so query-time scoring only multiplies IDF. Byte-aligned varint is SIMD-decodable
             (Stream-VByte/Masked-VByte family) - relevant to nest's SIMD dispatcher if posting
             decode becomes hot, though embeddings stay the f32 hot path. HyperLogLog at 6-bit
             precision keeps numeric cardinality tracking near-free.
license:     RSALv2 / SSPLv1 (Redis Source Available License v2 + Server Side Public License) -
             source-available, NOT OSI-open. Treat as reference/learn-from only; do NOT vendor or
             copy code. The underlying techniques (delta+varint, range tree, HLL split) are
             standard public-domain IR algorithms, safe to reimplement from scratch.

[HIGH] xapian/xapian-docsprint
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        The docsprint repo is only the Sphinx user-guide source for Xapian, a mature C++ probabilistic IR
             engine whose real value here is its documented glass backend on-disk format: chunked,
             delta+varint-encoded BM25 posting lists with a separated document-length stream and
             order-preserving numeric encodings.
innovation:  Decouple per-posting payload from the inverted list: store docids as varint deltas inside
             fixed-key chunks, and crucially do NOT repeat document length in every posting entry
             -- keep document lengths in one separate compressed stream indexed like a posting
             list. nest currently has no such separation and can shrink its bm25_index section
             dramatically by adopting it.
techniques:
  - chunked posting lists: each term's list split into B-tree chunks keyed by
    pack_string_preserving_sort(term)+pack_uint_preserving_sort(first_docid) so chunks sort and
    allow skip-ahead
  - docid delta encoding inside a chunk: store (next_docid - prev_docid - 1) instead of absolute
    ids, plus per-posting wdf
  - pack_uint variable-byte (7-bit continuation) integer codec; redundant encodings deliberately
    left to encode runs of equal-wdf docids compactly
  - separated document-length stream: doclen stored once per doc in its own postlist-like
    structure, never repeated per term-posting
  - sortable_serialise / pack_uint_preserving_sort: encode ints and IEEE doubles to byte strings
    whose bytewise order equals numeric order, enabling range filters and sorted scans without
    decoding
  - prefix compression within value chunks: store count of shared-prefix bytes with previous entry
    instead of full value
  - weight-tiered posting-list splitting (matcher optimization): partition postings into high/low
    BM25-weight tiers so top docs are read first -- requires freezing k1/b at index time
  - out-of position encoder + separate position table: term positions stored relative to a known
    max in a dedicated table, off the scoring hot path
nest steals: Target nest-format's bm25_index section (0x08) and nest-runtime bm25::Bm25Index. (1) Restructure
             on-disk postings to varint-delta docids per term with a SEPARATE doclen stream rather
             than embedding doclen per posting -- biggest size win, fits nest's additive format
             (new internal layout inside an existing reserved-payload section, no version bump).
             (2) Add a small order-preserving varint codec (pack_uint_preserving_sort analogue) in
             nest-format/encoding so the bm25 term dictionary and numeric metadata are
             byte-sortable and range-scannable straight off mmap, reinforcing the deterministic
             alphabetical-term-order rule already in place. (3) Adopt prefix compression for the
             alphabetically-sorted term dictionary (store shared-prefix length vs previous term)
             -- nearly free since terms are already sorted, helps 'compress more than everyone'.
             (4) Optionally add weight-tiered split as early-termination for bm25, only under a
             frozen-params contract consistent with the deterministic search_contract and
             mandatory exact rerank. Keep all of this on the cold/text path only -- embeddings
             stay raw f32 for SIMD scoring per nest's hot-path rule.
compression: Direct: separated-doclen-stream + delta-varint posting layout and sorted-term prefix compression
             are exactly the structural redundancy elimination that shrinks nest's bm25_index
             (0x08) without any entropy coder, complementing zstd-on-non-embedding-sections.
             Order-preserving encodings let compressed term dictionaries be searched without full
             decode, keeping the single-file mmap-and-scan model intact.
perf:        Glass optimizes for ascending-docid streaming reads (the matcher's access pattern); chunk
             skip-ahead via sortable first-docid keys avoids scanning whole lists.
             Prefix-compressed chunks need linear decode within a chunk but chunks are small so it
             is cheap. pack_uint trades a few wasted bits for branch-light decode. Maps cleanly
             onto nest's mmap-and-scan, no-server runtime; nothing requires a socket or background
             process, satisfying offline-by-construction.
license:     GPL-2.0-or-later (Xapian core); docs under GPL/doc terms. Implication: do NOT vendor Xapian code
             -- reimplement the on-disk encoding ideas (formats/algorithms are not copyrightable)
             from scratch in nest's Rust core to keep licensing clean.

[HIGH] izihawa/summa
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An IPFS-friendly, WASM-compatible full-text search server built on a fork of Tantivy (Rust
             Lucene), whose key trick is a network-backed caching Directory that resolves BM25
             queries over a remote, immutable, byte-range-addressable segment format by
             downloading only the few 32KB blocks a query actually touches.
innovation:  The whole search index is laid out as immutable, locality-optimized files where every structure
             (FST term dictionary, bitpacked skip-list postings, block-max metadata) is byte-range
             addressable, so a query is resolved by fetching only the handful of small blocks it
             touches instead of loading the index. This decouples "index size on disk" from "bytes
             needed to answer a query" - the same property nest already exploits by scoring
             embeddings straight off mmap, but Summa extends it to the lexical/inverted-index
             side.
techniques:
  - FST (finite-state transducer) term dictionary via the fst crate: shares common prefixes AND
    suffixes into a minimal acyclic automaton, compresses term keys better than a trie or general
    compressor while keeping O(key-len) random access plus wildcard/range lookup
  - Posting-list compression: delta-encode sorted doc-ids then SIMD-bitpack in fixed blocks of 128
    docs (each block uses the minimum bit-width for its deltas), with term-frequencies bitpacked
    in a parallel block stream
  - Skip-list over posting blocks storing per-block partial sums of deltas, giving O(log L) seek
    to a target doc-id without decoding the whole list
  - Block-Max WAND / MaxScore dynamic pruning: per-block upper-bound score lets the scorer skip
    whole posting blocks that cannot enter the top-k
  - Immutable append-only segment model enabling content-addressing and aggressive caching
  - Network/caching Directory (CachingDirectory + HotCache): turns internal file reads into
    chunked 32KB HTTP/IPFS range requests with speculative sequential prefetch and indefinite
    chunk caching
  - HotCache: a precomputed bundle of the hottest header/dictionary bytes shipped up front to cut
    first-query round trips
nest steals: Target crate nest-runtime, the bm25 module (crates/nest-runtime/src/bm25/codec.rs + index.rs)
             feeding the optional bm25_index section 0x08. Today the codec writes raw uncompressed
             postings: per term it emits len-prefixed UTF-8 then a flat list of (u32 doc, u32 tf)
             pairs (codec.rs lines 28-37), plus raw u32 doc_lengths. Steal three additive,
             deterministic pieces: (1) delta+varint or SIMD-bitpack the doc-ids within each
             posting list (docs are already sorted by doc-id at index.rs line 69) and zigzag the
             tf stream - shrinks the bm25 section several-fold with zero recall loss, staying
             inside layout 0x08 by bumping only BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION, NOT NEST_FORMAT_VERSION. (2)
             Replace the flat alphabetical term list with an FST built from the already-sorted
             keys (fst crate is pure-Rust, MIT/Apache) - compresses the dictionary and unlocks
             prefix/wildcard term queries for search-text. (3) Adopt per-block upper-bound
             (Block-Max WAND) so search-text early-terminates instead of scanning every posting -
             directly attacks the known search-text latency gap. The remote/IPFS Directory is OUT
             of scope (nest is offline-by-construction, never opens a socket), but its
             block-addressable philosophy validates nest's mmap-and-skip design and could later
             inform a zstd block index for the canonical text section so one chunk decompresses
             without inflating the whole section.
compression: Directly on-mission for "compress more than everyone": bm25_index (0x08) is nest's
             least-compressed section - raw 8 bytes per posting plus uncompressed UTF-8 terms.
             Delta+bitpacked postings typically cut posting bytes 3-6x and an FST term dictionary
             cuts dictionary bytes further while adding prefix/range query capability. Because
             postings are read by the lexical path (not the SIMD dot-product hot path), they can
             be compressed freely, unlike embeddings - pure upside for the hybrid and tiny
             presets.
perf:        SIMD bitpacking decodes fixed 128-doc blocks branch-free, pairing with nest's existing
             AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatcher in nest-runtime; the same dispatch pattern can host a
             bitpack decode kernel. Block-Max WAND turns full-posting scans into skips, cutting
             the per-query work that currently dominates search-text. The 32KB-chunk + prefetch
             caching is irrelevant to nest (the OS page cache already demand-pages the mmapped
             file), so skip it. FST lookups are O(query-term-length) automaton walks over far less
             memory than a HashMap, friendly to mmap-off-disk.
license:     Summa itself is AGPL-3.0 (do not vendor its source). The reusable building blocks are permissive
             and re-implementable: the fst crate (BurntSushi) is MIT/Apache-2.0;
             delta/varint/zigzag, SIMD bitpacking, and Block-Max WAND are public algorithms and
             Tantivy itself is MIT. Reimplement from algorithm descriptions, do not copy AGPL
             code.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] toshi-search/Toshi (thin REST/Raft server over the Tantivy search library; the transferable codecs live in Tantivy + its tantivy-columnar / bitpacker / sstable / fst subcrates)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Toshi is an Elasticsearch-style HTTP/Raft frontend over Tantivy, whose value to nest is Tantivy's
             segment codec stack: FST term dictionaries, BP128 delta+bitpacked posting blocks with
             VInt remainders, 1-byte log-quantized fieldnorms, and an auto-selecting columnar
             fast-field codec (constant/GCD/bitpacked/blockwise).
innovation:  Block-wise posting-list compression: split each posting list into fixed 128-doc blocks,
             delta-encode doc_ids within a block, bitpack each block to the minimum bit-width
             needed (num_bits derived from the block max), and VInt-encode the final partial
             block; pair this with a per-term skip header so you can binary-search blocks and
             decode only the block you need. Tantivy generalizes the same min+num_bits bitpacking
             idea to columnar data and auto-picks the cheapest codec (constant when all values
             equal, GCD when they share a divisor, plain bitpacked otherwise) at serialize time,
             so the file always carries the smallest representation without the reader needing to
             know which was chosen.
techniques:
  - BP128: per-block (128 docs) delta-encode doc_ids then bitpack at block-local num_bits =
    bits(max_delta); store one num_bits byte + block min
  - VInt (LEB128-style variable-byte) for the trailing partial block under 128 elements, avoiding
    a wasted full-width block
  - Per-term skip list / block headers (first doc_id + byte offset per block) enabling block-skip
    and future block-max WAND without decoding the whole list
  - FST (finite-state transducer, fst crate) term dictionary: term -> TermOrdinal -> TermInfo,
    giving shared-prefix term compression and ordered prefix/range iteration
  - 1-byte log-quantized fieldnorm encoding: doc length compressed to a single byte (small values
    exact) instead of a raw u32 doc_length
  - Columnar auto-codec selection: probe constant / GCD / bitpacked, emit whichever is smallest,
    recorded in column metadata so decode stays uniform
  - min_value + bitpacked-residual columnar layout giving O(1) random access (min +
    fetch_bits(num_bits*id..)) directly off mmap
  - BlockedBitpacker: bitpacking in 128-element blocks so each block adapts its bit-width to local
    value range
nest steals: Target nest-runtime/src/bm25/codec.rs + index.rs, which today serialize section 0x08 as raw
             fixed-width pairs (Posting{doc:u32, tf:u32} = 8 bytes/posting, no deltas) plus
             doc_lengths as raw u32. Adapt three Tantivy codecs, all deterministic and additive
             within format v1 (no NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump since the 0x08 payload is internal,
             gated by BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION 1->2): (1) replace each term's postings with sorted-doc
             delta + BP128-style block bitpacking (block num_bits byte + bitpacked deltas + VInt
             tail), cutting the dominant posting cost roughly 2-6x on real corpora; (2) replace
             per-doc doc_lengths: Vec<u32> with a single min+num_bits bitpacked column (the
             columnar trick), typically 1-2 bytes/doc instead of 4, or go further with the 1-byte
             log-quantized fieldnorm; (3) optionally replace the alphabetical term list + 4-byte
             length prefixes with a front-coded / shared-prefix term block (the FST idea in its
             simplest sorted-prefix form) to shrink the dictionary. Keep tf as a parallel
             bitpacked stream. All structures stay seeded/sorted so builds remain byte-identical,
             and the existing exact-cosine rerank means BM25 stays a candidate generator so lossy
             fieldnorm quantization is safe. A small bitpacker (min + num_bits + LE u64 word
             packing) is ~60-100 LOC, under the 300-line file cap, and reusable later for HNSW
             neighbor-id lists (section 0x07) and future columnar metadata.
compression: Directly on the north star. The BM25 index is currently one of nest's least-compressed sections (8
             bytes/posting, 4 bytes/doc, fully raw). Block delta+bitpacking plus a bitpacked
             doc-length column is a proven, lossless, mmap-friendly win that shrinks section 0x08
             several-fold while preserving exact-rerank correctness; the same min+num_bits
             columnar bitpacker is the reusable primitive for compressing any integer-id section
             (HNSW edges, chunk-id maps) under nest's hot-path mmap constraint.
perf:        BP128 / blocked bitpacking is SIMD-friendly: Tantivy decodes 128-doc blocks with SSE2-vectorized
             bitpacking (the bitpacking crate has AVX2/SSE paths; a scalar word-at-a-time unpack
             is the portable fallback, matching nest's existing AVX2/NEON/scalar dispatch
             philosophy). Decode is branch-light and cache-friendly because each block is
             contiguous; random access via min+fetch_bits is a single memory fetch per value.
             Bitpacking touches far fewer bytes than raw u32 arrays, so the mmap/page-cache
             footprint and cold-read I/O both drop, which matters more than raw CPU for nest's
             off-disk model. Determinism holds: bit-width is a pure function of the sorted data,
             so identical input yields byte-identical packed output.
license:     Toshi: MIT. Tantivy and subcrates (bitpacker, columnar, sstable, fst): MIT. fst crate (Andrew
             Gallant): Unlicense/MIT. All permissive and vendorable; nest can cleanly reimplement
             the algorithms without pulling the heavy tantivy dependency, keeping the rust core
             small and offline.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] SeekStorm/SeekStorm
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A sub-millisecond, sharded, real-time full-text + vector search engine in Rust (Apache-2.0) whose
             lexical core stores posting lists as roaring-bitmap-compressed doc-id sets with
             delta+VINT-encoded term positions, ranked by BM25F/BM25F_Proximity via
             Document-at-a-Time traversal with improved Block-Max WAND, MaxScore, galloping and
             SIMD set intersection, served from one on-disk format in either RAM or mmap mode.
innovation:  A production lexical index whose byte layout is identical in RAM and on mmap'd disk (switchable
             via an AccessType flag), so the inverted index is served straight off the file
             exactly like nest's embeddings already are. The winning combination of
             compact-AND-fast: roaring-bitmap posting lists (doc-id sets) + a separate
             delta-compressed VINT position stream + Block-Max WAND skip metadata, traversed
             Document-at-a-Time so no large intermediate result sets ever materialize.
techniques:
  - Roaring bitmaps for posting lists: per-65536-doc-chunk hybrid array/bitmap/run containers,
    giving compressed doc-id sets that allow hardware SIMD AND/OR directly on the compressed form
    (no full decode)
  - SIMD vector intersection and union of compressed bitmap posting lists on both x86-64 (AVX2)
    and AArch64 (NEON), the same two-arch dispatch nest already runs for dot products
  - Galloping (exponential) intersection for skewed posting-list size ratios, complementing SIMD
    merge for balanced lists
  - Improved Block-Max WAND + MaxScore: per-block max-impact upper bounds let top-k pruning skip
    whole blocks whose best possible BM25 cannot enter the heap
  - Document-at-a-Time (DAAT) traversal scoring one doc fully across all query terms, bounding
    memory vs Term-at-a-Time accumulators
  - Term positions delta-compressed then VINT (variable-byte) encoded as a SEPARATE stream from
    doc-ids, enabling phrase/proximity (BM25F_Proximity) without bloating the doc-id bitmaps
  - Zstandard-compressed JSON document store (docstore.bin) decoupled from index files (index.bin
    doc-ids+positions, index.json meta, delete.bin tombstones)
  - Tombstone-based real-time delete (delete.bin, recoverable until compaction) so every doc is
    searchable immediately, before/during commit
nest steals: nest already ships an optional bm25_index section (0x08) in nest-runtime::bm25::Bm25Index. Steal
             three layers into it additively (0x08 internals can evolve without a
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump): (1) in nest-format/src/sections + encoding, replace any
             plain doc-id list inside 0x08 with roaring-bitmap-encoded posting lists plus a
             SEPARATE delta+VINT position stream; this is the single biggest size win for the
             lexical section and keeps it serveable straight off mmap, matching nest's
             read-from-disk discipline. (2) in nest-runtime::bm25, add Block-Max WAND + MaxScore
             top-k pruning with per-block max-impact bounds stored alongside each posting list;
             this slashes query cost and is safe because bm25 only needs to surface candidates for
             reciprocal-rank fusion before nest's mandatory exact-cosine rerank. (3) reuse
             nest-runtime's EXISTING simd dispatcher (AVX2/NEON/scalar) for bitmap AND/OR
             intersection, so adding integer set-ops is incremental, not a new subsystem. Preserve
             determinism by keeping alphabetical term order and a single non-sharded global-stats
             index. The zstd JSON docstore maps onto nest's already-zstd-encoded chunks_canonical,
             so that piece is already solved and serves only as validation.
compression: Directly on-mission for "compress more than everyone" on the lexical/text path. Roaring bitmaps
             are a near-optimal compressed encoding for sparse-to-dense doc-id sets (array
             containers for sparse terms, bitmap for dense, run containers for contiguous runs)
             and routinely beat plain VINT/delta posting lists while STILL supporting SIMD set ops
             without full decode, so compression and serve-speed are not in tension, exactly
             nest's requirement. The separate delta+VINT position stream keeps proximity ranking
             from inflating the doc-id bitmaps. For nest's bm25_index (0x08) this is the
             highest-leverage size reduction for the text index, complementing (not touching) the
             embeddings hot path which stays raw f32/f16/int8 for direct SIMD scoring.
perf:        Claims sub-millisecond queries and ~20x Lucene throughput; reported ~40,000 docs/sec indexing on
             5M-doc Wikipedia, and 4-6x faster indexing / 3x shorter query latency from
             intra-query shard parallelism. SIMD intersection runs on x86-64 AVX2 and AArch64
             NEON, the same targets nest already supports. Index latency and size grow linearly
             with doc count while RAM stays roughly constant in mmap mode, enabling billion-scale
             indices. nest should take the per-document deterministic single-index path, not the
             shard topology.
license:     Apache-2.0 (permissive). Roaring bitmap is itself a published standardized format with permissive
             Rust crates (e.g. roaring-rs), so nest can reimplement or vendor without GPL
             contamination. WARNING: do NOT adopt SeekStorm's sharding; it makes BM25 scores
             non-deterministic and non-comparable across shards (per-shard avg doc length, df,
             posting-list length, load-balanced non-deterministically), which would break nest's
             byte-identical reproducible builds. Keep a single deterministic index with global
             stats and alphabetical term order; steal the encoding, pruning, and SIMD set-ops
             only.

[HIGH] valeriansaliou/sonic
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A lightweight schema-less search backend in Rust that pairs a RocksDB inverted index (term ->
             packed 32-bit IID arrays) with a per-bucket memory-mapped BurntSushi-fst Finite-State
             Transducer for typo-tolerant autocomplete, returning external identifiers rather than
             documents and running in a few MB of RAM.
innovation:  Decouple the ranked inverted index from the lexical/fuzzy lookup layer: postings live in a compact
             KV index (term -> packed u32 ids), and a separate immutable memory-mapped FST holds
             only the vocabulary for prefix/Levenshtein expansion, with all term/document identity
             collapsed to 32-bit ids so raw strings never sit on the hot path.
techniques:
  - OID<->IID 32-bit id indirection so postings are u32 arrays not strings (30-40% vs 64-bit)
  - 9-byte radix key [idx<1B>|bucket<4B>|route<4B>] of XxHash32 hashes, little-endian
  - Term-To-IIDs postings as raw little-endian u32 arrays; IID-To-Terms reverse map for GC
  - Immutable mmap'd BurntSushi fst::Set per bucket, lexicographic insertion required
  - FST consolidation tasker: buffer word push/pop, periodic sorted-merge rebuild to fresh on-disk
    FST
  - fst_levenshtein automaton for typo tolerance + FST prefix scan for autocomplete, as
    exact-match fallback
  - Language-aware lexer: stopword-count for long text / n-gram guess for short text, ISO-639-3
    stopword stripping, word-level only
  - RAM write-buffering + RocksDB atomic delete_range() flush to minimize SSD write amplification
nest steals: nest-format gets a redesign target for the optional bm25_index (0x08) section: store postings as
             the term -> sorted u32 chunk-ordinal arrays exactly like Sonic's Term-To-IIDs, with
             terms keyed by a deterministic hash (nest already mandates alphabetical term order,
             which doubles as the FST/lexicographic-insertion guarantee). The chunk-ordinal
             indirection mirrors OID<->IID: nest already has chunk_ids, so the bm25 section only
             needs to carry u32 ordinals + per-term doc-freq, never raw strings, and these arrays
             are highly zstd-friendly (zstd is allowed on non-embedding sections, so delta+zstd
             over sorted u32 postings is a direct extreme-compression win). nest-runtime's
             bm25::Bm25Index can mmap these postings straight off disk (Sonic proves mmap'd
             lexical lookup at a few MB RAM) and feed the existing reciprocal-rank-fusion +
             mandatory cosine rerank path. Crucially, nest can add an OPTIONAL FST/vocabulary blob
             (a new RESERVED section id 0x09+, additive-only) built with the BurntSushi fst crate
             to deliver typo-tolerant and prefix SUGGEST over chunk vocabulary, run as a
             Levenshtein/prefix automaton in nest-runtime, then resolve expanded terms back into
             the BM25 postings before rerank. This addresses nest's known CJK/word-segmentation
             gap path (language-aware lexer + ISO-639-3 stopword tables) and gives nest-cli
             search-text a fuzzy mode. The immutable consolidation model (rebuild FST from sorted
             merge) fits nest's frozen/deterministic-build ethos perfectly: build it once in
             python, freeze it, mmap in rust.
compression: Direct: collapsing terms and documents to 32-bit hashed ids and storing postings as sorted
             little-endian u32 arrays (Sonic's 30-40% saving over 64-bit ids) is exactly the kind
             of dictionary+integer-indirection that makes the bm25_index section tiny; sorted u32
             postings are ideal for delta-encoding + zstd (already permitted on non-embedding
             sections), so this could shrink the lexical index dramatically while staying
             mmap-scorable. The FST itself is a state-of-the-art compressed vocabulary
             representation (shared prefixes/suffixes via minimal DFA), aligning with nest's
             compress-more-than-everyone north star.
perf:        ~1M messages indexed in 20MB KV + 1.4MB FST on disk, peak 28MB RAM; ~4000 PUSH/s (275us avg) and
             ~1000 QUERY/s (880us avg) single-thread, queries parallelize across CPUs. FST is
             mmap'd so vocabulary access is page-cache served, not heap-resident. Achilles heel:
             FST is immutable and must be fully rebuilt on vocabulary change (consolidation
             batched at intervals), which is fine for nest's frozen build-once model. Word-level
             only (no positional/phrase index) to keep the FST shallow.
license:     MPL-2.0 (Mozilla Public License 2.0)

[HIGH] tinysearch/tinysearch
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A static-site full-text search engine that replaces a traditional inverted index with one
             approximate-membership Xor filter per document, serialized with bincode and compiled
             into a tiny dependency-free WASM blob.
innovation:  Invert the cost model of lexical search: instead of one global term->postings inverted index,
             store one tiny per-document approximate-set-membership filter (Xor filter) keyed on
             that document's token set, then answer a query by probing every document's filter for
             each query term and ranking by the count of term hits. This trades exact recall and
             term-frequency scoring for a drastically smaller, append-friendly index where each
             document's lexical footprint is a fixed ~9-10 bits per unique token rather than full
             postings lists.
techniques:
  - Xor8 filter (xorf crate): ~9.84 bits per key, ~0.39% FPR, fingerprint = u8; membership test is
    f == B[h0(x)] XOR B[h1(x)] XOR B[h2(x)]
  - 3-hash peeling construction: assign each key to one of three slots via reverse topological
    order over a hypergraph so the xor of three table cells reproduces the fingerprint (single
    static build, no incremental inserts)
  - BinaryFuse8 successor filter: ~9.0 bits/key, denser slot packing, faster build than classic
    Xor8, same xor-of-three-fingerprints probe
  - per-document filter granularity: one filter per doc instead of a global inverted index; query
    = AND of terms, rank by number of filters containing each term (score = hit count)
  - bincode binary serialization of the Vec of (metadata, filter) tuples, with serde derive on the
    filter structs
  - build-time stop-word stripping to shrink the keyspace plus brotli/gzip post-compression
    (~1KB/doc compressed)
  - explicit rejection of tries (10x larger) in favor of probabilistic filters; abandoned prefix
    search because it bloated the binary
nest steals: Add an optional ultra-compact lexical mode to nest-runtime's bm25::Bm25Index path and a new
             encoding inside the existing bm25_index section 0x08 (still v1, additive). Build a
             deterministic Xor8/BinaryFuse8 filter per chunk over the chunk token set (seeded
             hashing + alphabetical term order to preserve determinism), serialize the filter
             arrays as little-endian repr(C), stored one filter per chunk_id parallel to
             chunk_ids. nest-format gains a 'bm25-membership' codec variant: where full BM25
             postings+tf are too big, the membership-only filter gives lexical pre-filtering at ~9
             bits/unique-token/chunk. In nest-runtime search-text, run the Xor probe over all
             chunk filters as a cheap candidate generator (AND query terms, score by hit count)
             feeding RRF, then the mandatory exact-cosine rerank still produces real scores, so
             approximation is confined to candidate generation and never leaks into returned
             scores, matching nest's search_contract. Unlocks a 'tiny+lexical' preset where
             lexical search adds only kilobytes.
compression: Directly on-mission. A per-chunk Xor8/BinaryFuse8 filter encodes lexical membership at a fixed
             ~9-10 bits per unique token with no length-dependent postings or varint gaps, an
             order of magnitude smaller than full BM25 postings+term frequencies, and it compounds
             with nest's existing zstd on non-embedding sections. It is the smallest way to keep a
             lexical/keyword channel alive in the 'tiny' preset, shrinking the bm25_index
             footprint while still enabling hybrid search.
perf:        Xor/BinaryFuse probes are three random table reads plus two XORs and one compare per term:
             branch-light, cache-bound, data-parallel across chunks. Construction is O(n) via
             peeling but build-time only (python side), so the Rust hot path stays read-only over
             mmap. Filters can be probed in place from the mmapped 0x08 section with zero
             deserialization if laid out as flat little-endian arrays. Xor8 FPR ~0.39% is
             acceptable because the mandatory exact-cosine rerank removes false positives.
license:     MIT (tinysearch); xorf crate MIT/Apache-2.0; BinaryFuse algorithm (Graf/Lemire) permissively
             published

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] nextapps-de/flexsearch
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An in-memory full-text search library whose "contextual index" replaces query-time TF-IDF with a
             pre-scored lexical dictionary that buckets postings by an integer relevance score
             (resolution), making multi-term intersections near-constant-time.
innovation:  Pre-score at index build, not query time: instead of storing one posting list per term and
             computing BM25/TF-IDF on the fly, FlexSearch stores `map[score_bucket][term] =
             [doc_ids]` where the bucket index IS the quantized relevance. Relevance is collapsed
             into a small integer in [0, resolution) computed from three distances (document
             position, partial/token distance, and contextual term-pair distance within a
             bidirectional window of depth d). Querying becomes: walk buckets from highest score
             to lowest and intersect/union the small doc-id arrays, so scoring is "already done"
             and ranking is just bucket order. This trades a tiny, bounded score space for
             eliminating per-hit float math, and it makes the score field itself compressible (a
             4-bit/1-byte ordinal instead of an f32).
techniques:
  - Resolution-bucketed inverted index: term -> array of `resolution` buckets, each holding doc-id
    lists; bucket index = quantized integer relevance (default resolution 9), so score is an
    ordinal not a float
  - Contextual pre-scoring: bidirectional moving 2-pointer window of configurable `depth`
    pre-computes term-pair proximity scores at build time; bidirectional flag collapses
    (a,b)/(b,a) pairs to halve memory
  - Three-distance scoring model (document distance + partial distance + contextual distance)
    deliberately replacing TF-IDF, yielding an integer score
  - complexity = resolution - threshold: a `threshold` floor prunes low-score buckets entirely
    from storage and from intersection, bounding both index size and query cost
  - Latin encoder ladder (Exact -> Normalize -> Balance -> Advanced -> Extra -> Soundex) giving
    ~7% to ~70% term-dictionary shrinkage via progressive phonetic normalization, collapsing many
    surface forms to one key
  - Tokenizer memory-factor taxonomy (strict 1x, forward nx, reverse 2n-1x, full n(n-1)x, tolerant
    2(n-2)+2x) making the size/recall tradeoff explicit and selectable
  - fastupdate secondary register for O(1) delete via reverse doc->term map, plus cleanup() to
    compact empty slots
  - Fast-boot serialization: export/import the already-pre-scored dictionary so rehydration does
    zero recompute
nest steals: Apply to nest-runtime crates/nest-runtime/src/bm25 (index.rs + codec.rs) and section 0x08. (1)
             CODEC WIN, additive: today codec.rs writes `Posting{doc:u32, tf:u32}` (8
             bytes/posting) and search.rs recomputes BM25 floats at query time. Adopt FlexSearch's
             bucketing: precompute each posting's BM25 contribution at build time, quantize it
             into N ordinal buckets (e.g. 16, 4 bits), and store the inverted list grouped by
             bucket. The 4-byte tf becomes a 4-bit ordinal, shrinking section 0x08 roughly 30-40%,
             and doc-id lists per bucket are monotonically sortable so they zstd-delta-compress
             far better than interleaved (doc,tf). This stays within the 0x08 section and is gated
             by BM25_PAYLOAD_VERSION (bump the payload version, not NEST_FORMAT_VERSION, since
             0x08 is optional and additive). (2) SPEED WIN: search becomes bucket-descending
             iteration with early termination once top-k is filled from high buckets, removing the
             per-posting idf/denom float loop in index.rs:106-114; nest's mandatory exact-cosine
             rerank then dominates less. Keep determinism via alphabetical term order (already
             done) plus fixed bucket boundaries. (3) TOKENIZER/CJK GAP: port the Latin
             normalization ladder into bm25/tokenize.rs as deterministic encoder presets (raw ->
             normalize-diacritics -> phonetic) to collapse surface forms and shrink the term
             dictionary; this directly attacks the documented bm25 tokenizer gap. (4) RRF fusion
             in fusion.rs already wants ranks, not raw scores, so an ordinal bucket score is a
             natural fit. Optional `context.depth` proximity could later be a reserved 0x09+
             section.
compression: Directly relevant. Quantizing the per-posting score from an f32/u32 into a small integer ordinal
             (resolution buckets) is the same move nest already makes for embeddings (f32 ->
             int8); applied to the bm25 0x08 section it cuts posting size ~30-40% before zstd, and
             grouping doc-ids by bucket produces monotonic, delta-friendly runs that zstd
             compresses much better than interleaved (doc,tf) pairs. The threshold prune further
             shrinks the index by dropping low-relevance postings entirely. This advances the
             north-star "compress more than everyone" on the lexical-index section specifically,
             with no recall loss on the top buckets and the exact-cosine rerank still guaranteeing
             real scores.
perf:        FlexSearch reports ~50M single-term queries/sec vs 3.9k-23k for competitors, attributed entirely
             to pre-scored buckets eliminating query-time scoring and shrinking intersection sets.
             Memory scales mainly with context depth (depth 1 ~12.5MB, depth 2 ~21.5MB on
             Gulliver's Travels corpus); bidirectional pair-collapsing reduces it (17.9MB vs
             19.0MB at depth 2). Pure JS, single-thread hot path, with optional Web Worker
             sharding. For nest the relevant takeaway is that ordinal scores make both storage
             smaller and the scan branch-predictable; no SIMD needed since the hot path becomes
             integer bucket iteration plus sorted-list intersection.
license:     Apache-2.0

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] Michael-JB/bm25
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A pure-Rust Okapi BM25 toolkit (MIT, ~1.2M downloads) that turns text into precomputed
             sparse-vector embeddings via a pluggable tokenizer, with three composable layers:
             Embedder (text to sparse vec), Scorer (inverted-index query scoring), and
             SearchEngine (in-memory CRUD search).
innovation:  Treat BM25 as a precomputed sparse embedding so query-time scoring is a sparse dot product reusing
             the same vector primitive as dense embeddings.
techniques:
  - Okapi BM25 with k1/b/avgdl params; b=0 cleanly disables length normalization (good for short,
    uniform chunks)
  - avgdl auto-fit from corpus statistics (with_fit_to_corpus) so the only stateful param is
    computed deterministically from the data
  - Sparse embedding format: TokenEmbedding { index, value } pairs, only non-zero dims stored,
    directly ingestible by Qdrant/Pinecone/Milvus
  - Hash-based token IDs (token hashed to u32/u64) instead of a term dictionary, eliminating the
    string->id map at the cost of possible collisions
  - Pluggable Tokenizer trait; DefaultTokenizer pipeline = deunicode normalization +
    unicode-segmentation word boundaries + stop-words removal + rust-stemmers Snowball stemming
  - Three-layer split Embedder/Scorer/SearchEngine, each usable independently, generic over
    token-id (TokenEmbedder trait) and doc-id (Document<T>)
  - Optional feature flags: language_detection (whichlang), parallelism (rayon batch fit),
    default_tokenizer toggle to drop text-proc deps
nest steals: nest-runtime/src/bm25.rs (Bm25Index) should adopt the precomputed-sparse-weight model: store, in
             the 0x08 bm25_index section, per-chunk postings as (term_id:u32_le,
             weight:f16_or_quantized) sorted by alphabetical term order (nest's determinism rule
             already requires this), plus a small header of {k1,b,avgdl,doc_count}. This converts
             BM25 scoring into a sparse dot product that nest's existing SIMD dispatcher can run
             straight off mmap, exactly mirroring the dense f32 cosine hot path, so hybrid preset
             RRF fusion needs no separate scoring engine. Steal the Tokenizer trait shape into
             nest-runtime so the word-segmented tokenizer becomes swappable (addresses the known
             CJK/Thai gap) without an additive format change. Borrow
             deunicode+unicode-segmentation+rust-stemmers+stop-words as the default pipeline. Do
             NOT adopt hash-based token IDs: nest needs deterministic alphabetical term ordering
             and reopenable citations, so keep an explicit sorted term dictionary in-section (a
             sorted Vec<&str> with delta/front-coded prefix compression) to stay collision-free
             and byte-reproducible.
compression: Medium-high: storing BM25 as precomputed sparse weights lets the 0x08 section be zstd-compressed
             (it is a non-embedding section, so nest's codec rules permit it) and the postings
             quantized (f16 or int8 weights) and front-coded/delta-coded on term_id, shrinking the
             lexical index dramatically versus a full inverted index with float positions. The
             hash-id trick is a compression idea (drop the term dictionary entirely) but conflicts
             with nest's determinism/citation guarantees, so nest should instead front-code the
             term dictionary to recover most of that space without collisions.
perf:        Pure-Rust, no_std-friendly enough to compile to WebAssembly (proven by bm25-demo). Sparse
             representation keeps memory proportional to non-zero terms. Optional rayon
             parallelism only for batch corpus fitting; query scoring is single-pass over
             postings. No SIMD kernel of its own (nest would add SIMD via its existing
             dispatcher). In-memory only, no on-disk serialization in the upstream crate, so nest
             must design the on-disk 0x08 layout itself.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] jameslittle230/stork
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust+WebAssembly static-site full-text search engine that builds a single self-contained binary
             `.st` index file (MessagePack-serialized inverted "prefix map") which a WASM blob
             loads in the browser to do instant prefix search with positional excerpt
             highlighting, no server.
innovation:  The entire search index is a single self-describing versioned binary artifact that bundles BOTH
             the inverted index AND the displayable content (excerpts + char offsets) so the
             search runtime needs nothing but the one file to return rendered, highlighted results
             offline. The index is keyed by BTreeMap<String, Container> (a "prefix map") where
             each Container is self-sufficient: from one container you derive every result for a
             query, and prefix queries with no exact container resolve through aliases (BTreeMap
             of alias-target -> score) that redirect to the nearest indexed term. The on-disk
             structure directly serves the query without a separate scoring pass over the corpus.
techniques:
  - Inverted index as BTreeMap<String, Container> 'prefix map' (deterministic, alphabetically
    ordered keys) instead of a hashmap, so iteration/serialization is stable
  - Self-contained Container: results: BTreeMap<EntryIndex, SearchResult> + aliases:
    BTreeMap<AliasTarget, Score>, so prefix/partial matches alias to indexed terms with a
    discounted score
  - Excerpts stored INLINE in the index with word offset, field (title vs contents), and
    char-range annotations, so the runtime returns ready-to-render highlighted snippets without
    touching original documents
  - MessagePack binary serialization via rmp-serde (compact tag-length-value, no JSON key
    repetition) for the .st payload
  - Self-describing versioned container: length-prefixed version string (e.g. 'stork-3.0.0')
    precedes payload; VersionedIndex::try_from dispatches to V2/V3 parsers for additive format
    evolution
  - Scoring via MATCHED_WORD_SCORE base plus configurable title_boost multiplier and
    exact-vs-prefix discount, precomputed at build time and stored, not recomputed at query time
  - rust-stemmers (Snowball) + unicode-segmentation + stopword normalization at BUILD time so
    query-time matching is cheap and the index conflates 'tries'->'try', 'Power'->'power'
  - excerpts_per_result=0 build switch that strips inline excerpts to shrink the index 20-50%
    (filter-only mode), trading display payload for size
nest steals: Three steals for nest-format's optional bm25_index section (0x08). (1) Adopt Stork's
             BTreeMap<term, Container> postings layout: nest already mandates 'bm25 alphabetical
             term order' for determinism, so a BTreeMap-backed self-contained per-term container
             is a drop-in that satisfies determinism by construction and serializes stably. (2)
             Adopt the alias/prefix-redirect trick in nest-runtime/bm25 to add cheap prefix and
             stemmed-variant matching to nest's word-segmented tokenizer (a known gap) without
             storing full substring postings: store only canonical terms plus an alias table
             mapping prefixes/stems to canonical term ids with a discount score; this directly
             mitigates nest's CJK/Thai tokenizer degradation by letting non-exact segments alias
             to the nearest indexed term. (3) Precompute per-doc term scores (Stork's build-time
             MATCHED_WORD_SCORE + boost model) so the bm25 candidate pass in nest-runtime is a
             pure lookup before the mandatory exact-cosine rerank, keeping the hot path
             allocation-light. Keep nest's own serialization (repr(C), little-endian, zstd on
             non-embedding sections) -- borrow the data-structure shape, NOT rmp-serde. For
             extreme compression: Stork's excerpts_per_result=0 'filter-only index' maps to a nest
             preset flag that drops any cached snippet/offset payload from bm25_index and
             reconstructs spans on demand from chunks_original_spans, shrinking the optional
             section 20-50% while citations stay intact.
compression: Indirect but real for nest's bm25 section, not the embedding hot path. Two transferable size
             ideas: (a) the alias/prefix-redirect table avoids materializing substring/prefix
             postings (Stork's docs note full substring indexing bloats the file), giving prefix
             search at a fraction of postings size; (b) the excerpts_per_result=0 'strip display
             payload, keep only the filter' mode is a clean precedent for a nest preset that drops
             cached snippets and reconstructs spans from chunks_original_spans, a 20-50% cut on
             bm25_index. Stork's actual codec (MessagePack, no entropy coder, no compression crate
             -- relies on the host server's gzip) is BELOW nest's bar: nest already
             zstd-compresses non-embedding sections, which beats raw MessagePack, so nest should
             NOT adopt rmp-serde. Borrow index shape and the two size tricks; nest's existing zstd
             + repr(C) encoding dominates Stork's on-disk format.
perf:        Stork's claim to fame is sub-millisecond browser-side search by loading the whole index into WASM
             linear memory and doing direct BTreeMap lookups (O(log n) key lookup + container
             iteration), no network round-trips after the initial index download. No SIMD, no
             bitpacked postings, no roaring bitmaps -- deliberately simple: build-time
             precomputation (stemming, scoring, excerpt extraction) trades index size for
             query-time CPU. Entire index resident in WASM heap, which is why >~megabyte corpora
             and <3-char queries get slow and the 2.0 line moved to sharded indexes (load one
             chunk at a time) to bound resident memory. Lesson for nest: precompute at build, keep
             the query path a pure lookup; but Stork's lack of bitpacked/delta-encoded postings is
             exactly where nest can do better.
license:     Apache-2.0

# category: embeddings-models  (15 repos)
# focus: embedding and representation models (text + image, including tiny/static)
