[MEDIUM] jina-ai/jina
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Jina is a gRPC/HTTP/WebSocket microservice framework for multimodal AI serving whose real reusable
             asset is its data layer DocArray, with a columnar DocVec store that stacks all
             per-document tensors into one contiguous tensor and serializes via a typed protobuf
             schema (DenseNdArrayProto = raw buffer bytes + shape + dtype).
innovation:  The DocVec columnar layout: instead of storing N documents row-wise (each carrying its own
             embedding object), DocVec transposes the array so every schema field becomes a
             column, and all tensor fields across documents are stacked into ONE contiguous
             backing tensor; each Document becomes a zero-copy view (like tensor[i]) into that
             column. This is exactly nest's "embeddings as one packed matrix scored straight off
             mmap" idea, generalized into a data model with a typed protobuf wire format
             (DenseNdArrayProto: bytes buffer + repeated uint32 shape + string dtype) that cleanly
             separates the dense numeric payload from its shape/dtype metadata.
techniques:
  - DocVec columnar transpose: per-field columns, all tensor fields stacked into one contiguous
    tensor for batch/SIMD-friendly access
  - Document-as-view: BaseDoc holds no data, it is a numpy/torch-style view[i] into the shared
    column buffer (zero-copy slicing)
  - DenseNdArrayProto encoding: split tensor into raw `bytes buffer` + `repeated uint32 shape` +
    `string dtype`, decoupling dense payload from metadata
  - DocVecProto with 4 typed column maps (tensor_columns / doc_columns / docs_vec_columns /
    any_columns) routing dense tensors, nested docs, doc-arrays, and heterogeneous scalars to
    format-appropriate codecs
  - Deferred tensor backend selection: dtype/shape metadata lets the same bytes deserialize to
    numpy/torch/tf at read time without re-encoding
  - Pluggable protocol x compression matrix (protobuf|pickle) x (lz4|bz2|lzma|zlib|gzip) via
    self-describing `name.$protocol.$compress` filenames
  - Recursive nesting: a BaseDoc field becomes a nested DocVec column, enabling columnar storage
    of structured/multimodal records
nest steals: Two concrete adaptations. (1) nest-format: adopt DenseNdArrayProto's split-metadata discipline for
             any future non-embedding tensor section (image-similarity feature matrices, audio
             frames) -- store raw little-endian buffer + explicit (shape, dtype/encoding-id)
             descriptor in the section manifest, never interleaving metadata into the hot dense
             bytes; this keeps the AVX2/NEON dot-product kernel pointed at a clean contiguous
             matrix like nest's existing embeddings section, and the deferred-backend trick maps
             onto nest's float16/int8 encoding ids (same bytes, reader picks decode path). (2)
             nest-format sections + manifest: borrow DocVec's 4-way column routing as the design
             rule for the planned multi-modal expansion (text/json/yaml/parquet/pdf/audio/video)
             -- a typed-column manifest where each logical column declares its codec (zstd for
             text/json columns, raw-mmap f32/f16/int8 for dense tensor columns, bm25 for tokenized
             text), so the writer routes each column to its best codec while the reader still
             mmaps tensor columns directly. This is the columnar analogue of nest's current
             per-section encoding, uses RESERVED section ids 0x09+ additively, and unlocks
             "compress structured datasets/parquet/repos" without breaking
             single-file/offline/deterministic guarantees. nest-runtime hot path is untouched
             because tensor columns stay raw contiguous f32/f16/int8.
compression: Medium-indirect. Jina/DocArray does NOT do extreme compression -- embeddings hit the wire as a raw
             f32 buffer (DenseNdArrayProto.buffer), and "compression" is just an outer
             general-purpose codec pass (lz4/zlib/lzma) over the serialized blob, the opposite of
             nest's policy of never general-compressing embeddings. The transferable
             compression-relevant idea is structural, not entropic: the columnar transpose groups
             like-typed, like-dtype values together, the precondition for high-ratio compression
             of NON-embedding data (delta/dictionary/RLE on an id column, zstd on a homogeneous
             text/json column) and for keeping dense tensor columns in one clean contiguous block
             that int8 quantization and mmap-scoring exploit. Informs nest's dataset/parquet
             column-compression roadmap; offers no new entropy coder.
perf:        DocVec's contiguous column tensor is built for batch inference and vectorized ops -- the same
             memory shape nest relies on for SIMD dot products; zero-copy Document views avoid
             per-doc allocation. DocArray got ~240% faster JSON serialization by moving the
             validation core to Pydantic v2 (Rust pyo3), confirming the
             build-in-python/serve-in-Rust split nest already uses via nest-python. Jina/DocArray
             ships no SIMD distance kernels of its own; vector search is delegated to external
             Document Indexes (HNSWLib/Qdrant/Weaviate/Elastic), so there is nothing to steal on
             the ANN/SIMD side -- nest's hand-rolled HnswIndex + mandatory exact cosine rerank is
             already ahead.
license:     Apache-2.0 (both jina and docarray)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] supermemoryai/smfs
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A Rust daemon that exposes a remote Supermemory container as a real mountable POSIX directory
             (FUSE on Linux, a pure-Rust loopback NFSv3 server on macOS) backed by a SQLite
             write-back cache, a Semaphore(8) bidirectional sync engine, and a transparent grep
             shim that turns flagless `grep` inside the mount into a hybrid semantic-search call.
innovation:  "Retrieval-as-a-filesystem": present an opaque memory/vector store as a navigable directory tree
             so standard Unix tools (ls, cat, tree, grep) become the query interface. The
             signature trick is that flagless `grep` inside the mount is transparently rerouted to
             semantic/vector search while any flag (or `grep -F`) falls back to literal substring
             matching; mount context is detected via a hidden `.smfs` marker file so the shim is a
             no-op everywhere else. This makes a vector DB consumable by any agent/tool that can
             read a file, with zero bespoke SDK.
techniques:
  - Pure-Rust NFSv3 loopback server bound to 127.0.0.1:<auto>, mounted via /sbin/mount_nfs, no
    kernel extension (macOS path)
  - Zero-setup FUSE mount via the `fuser` crate (Linux path)
  - SQLite as a persistent write-back cache that survives daemon restarts and serves offline reads
    (replaced an in-memory store)
  - Bidirectional sync engine: pull on cache-miss/TTL-expiry, push dirty writes with
    exponential-backoff retry, parallelized by a Semaphore(8) fan-out with independent pull/push
    sides
  - Transparent grep interception keyed on a hidden `.smfs` marker: flagless grep => semantic
    search, any flag / `grep -F` => literal grep
  - Scoped indexing via path globs (trailing slash = recursive folder, no slash = exact file)
    decide which writes get embedded vs stored as inert bytes
  - Virtual synthesized file `profile.md` at mount root: never stored, regenerated on read from
    all memories via an API endpoint (computed-on-read pseudo-file)
  - VFS hot-path optimization (v0.0.4) that short-circuits local-only reads/writes to skip
    sync/IPC overhead
nest steals: Two adaptable patterns that keep nest's offline/single-file/no-socket guarantees. (1) A NEW
             optional crate nest-fuse, deliberately kept OUT of nest-runtime so the socketless
             core is untouched: mmap a .nest file and expose chunks_canonical as virtual read-only
             files (e.g. /chunks/<chunk_id>.txt) plus a synthesized /search/<query>.txt
             computed-on-read pseudo-file that runs the existing exact-cosine search path and
             emits ranked `nest://content_hash/chunk_id` citations -- SMFS's profile.md-on-read
             pattern applied to nest's deterministic search, so any agent can grep/cat a .nest
             with no SDK. FUSE opens no network socket, preserving 'offline by construction'; the
             loopback-NFSv3 variant must be REJECTED because it binds a socket. (2) The
             flagless-vs-flagged grep split maps onto a thin nest-cli addition: `nest grep
             <pattern>` where a bare pattern => semantic (embed query + mandatory exact-cosine
             rerank over nest-runtime's search) and `-F`/regex => literal scan of
             chunks_canonical, giving the same dual-mode UX inside the existing clap surface. The
             SQLite write-back cache is a REFERENCE only for nest's future real-time-edit roadmap
             (stage dirty chunk edits in a sidecar, flush via a deterministic rebuild) since the
             FROZEN additive container forbids in-place mutation.
compression: n/a -- SMFS discloses no compression, entropy coding, quantization, or on-disk vector format; it
             is a filesystem/sync facade that delegates all retrieval to a remote hybrid-search
             API. It offers nothing for the 'compress more than everyone' north star. Its value to
             nest is purely ergonomic (retrieval-as-filesystem) and architectural
             (computed-on-read virtual files, dual-mode grep), not byte size.
perf:        Sync concurrency capped at Semaphore(8); default 30s pull interval; 30s unmount drain-timeout;
             exponential-backoff push retry. macOS pays NFSv3-over-loopback marshalling cost;
             Linux FUSE pays a kernel<->userspace context switch per syscall. v0.0.4 added a VFS
             hot path to skip sync overhead on local-only ops. No SIMD, no vector-scan kernels, no
             mmap'd embedded index -- all retrieval is remote, so there are no on-device
             throughput numbers to steal.
license:     Not confirmed from README; supermemory-ecosystem repos are typically MIT or Apache-2.0 but treat
             as unverified. Only design patterns (FUSE mount, grep shim, on-read virtual file)
             would be reimplemented from scratch, not vendored, so license exposure is minimal.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] weaviate/Verba
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Verba ("The Golden RAGtriever") is a modular Python RAG orchestrator over Weaviate, built around
             swappable Manager stages (Reader/Chunker/Embedder/Retriever/Generator) whose
             retrieval-path innovations (WindowRetriever neighbor expansion, document-level score
             grouping, Weaviate semantic answer cache, format-aware chunkers) are orchestration
             logic layered on a remote vector DB.
innovation:  WindowRetriever: after a top-k hybrid (vector + BM25) search, every chunk whose score clears a
             configurable threshold (1-100) is expanded to its N neighboring chunks fetched by
             contiguous chunk index within the same parent document, then chunks are grouped per
             document and re-scored at the document level. This decouples embedding/retrieval
             granularity (small, precise chunks) from generation granularity (wide, coherent
             context) with zero extra index storage, since neighbors are addressed by
             deterministic positional id rather than re-searched.
techniques:
  - WindowRetriever: positional neighbor-chunk expansion by chunk index within a document, gated
    by a score threshold and window-size N
  - document-level score aggregation: group retrieved chunks by parent document and compute a doc
    score to rank/merge fragmented hits
  - hybrid search via Weaviate: parallel dense-vector + BM25 sparse search fused by Reciprocal
    Rank Fusion (RRF)
  - semantic answer cache: embed both query and generated answer, serve cached answers on
    cosine-similar repeat queries (skip retrieval+generation)
  - format-aware chunkers: Markdown/HTML/Code/JSON/Recursive/Semantic/Sentence/Token splitters
    preserving structural boundaries vs blind fixed-size splits
  - spaCy-driven token and sentence segmentation as chunk-unit primitives
  - SemanticChunker: group adjacent sentences by embedding similarity into variable-length
    coherent chunks
  - Manager/component plug-in registry: each pipeline stage is a swappable interface selected by
    config
nest steals: nest-runtime search path: add a 'window expansion' post-step in crates/nest-runtime/src/search
             after the exact-cosine rerank. nest already has zero-cost ingredients: chunk_ids
             (deterministic, ordered) + chunks_original_spans (byte span per chunk) + provenance
             (parent-doc grouping). Implement: for each reranked hit above a configurable
             threshold, materialize chunk_ids[i-w .. i+w] from the same provenance source by id
             adjacency, dedup, and merge their original_spans into one contiguous citation range
             so nest://content_hash/chunk_id citations widen to a coherent passage with no
             re-search. NO format change (additive, reads existing FROZEN sections), determinism
             preserved (seeded order). Document-level aggregation maps to a new nest-cli flag
             (search --group-by-doc) over provenance. The format-aware chunkers + SemanticChunker
             belong in nest-python (builder writing chunks_canonical + chunks_original_spans):
             boundary-aware markdown/code/json chunking improves recall AND compression. The
             Weaviate semantic-answer-cache is the one piece nest must NOT steal: it needs online
             state and a network DB, violating offline/single-file/no-socket constraints.
compression: Indirect but real, via two levers. (1) Decoupling retrieval granularity from context granularity
             (WindowRetriever) lets nest embed/store smaller, more uniform chunks that quantize
             (int8) and dedup/zstd more predictably, shrinking embeddings + chunks_canonical,
             while window expansion restores context at query time for free from chunk_ids
             adjacency. (2) Structure-aware chunkers produce boundary-aligned chunks_canonical
             that zstd-compress better (more repeated structural tokens, fewer mid-token splits)
             and reduce chunks_original_spans fragmentation. Neither adds a new on-disk section;
             both reuse existing FROZEN sections, advancing 'compress more than everyone' without
             format risk.
perf:        WindowRetriever expansion is O(hits * window) id lookups plus span merges, no extra vector math,
             so it adds negligible cost on nest's mmap hot path and stays off the SIMD dot-product
             loop. nest's known ~300-500ms search-text python boot overhead is unrelated. Verba
             itself is pure-Python orchestration with no SIMD/quantization of its own; all vector
             math, RRF fusion, and HNSW live inside Weaviate (Go), so there is no kernel or codec
             to vendor, only the retrieval-flow algorithm to reimplement in Rust.
license:     BSD-3-Clause

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[LOW] supermemoryai/opensearch-ai
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A ~80-line TypeScript/Next.js edge-runtime "personalized Perplexity" that fuses Brave/Bing web
             results with user-scoped memories pulled from the hosted Supermemory engine and
             streams them through gpt-4o-mini; the real retrieval IP lives in Supermemory (a
             vector-graph memory engine), not in this thin client.
innovation:  The orchestration treats personalization as a single namespace-scoped memory search whose results
             are returned at the SMALLEST SUFFICIENT GRANULARITY via a fallback ladder (summary >
             title > concatenated chunks), rather than always shipping raw chunks. Memory is
             extracted facts with temporal supersession (isLatest flag) instead of stateless
             verbatim RAG chunks.
techniques:
  - containerTags: per-tenant namespace partitioning of a single shared store (scope =
    [user.email, 'opensearch']) so one index serves many isolated views without separate files
  - Summary-first retrieval fallback ladder: return mem.summary ?? mem.title ?? chunks.join('\n')
    -- pick the most compressed representation that still answers, instead of always returning raw
    spans
  - isLatest temporal-supersession flag in the memory graph: contradictions resolved by
    timestamp/update-pattern so stale facts are deprioritized while history is preserved (not
    deleted)
  - Memory-vs-RAG: ingest-time fact extraction into a 'facts-on-facts' vector-graph with
    ontology-aware edges, vs storing verbatim chunks (note: academic counterpoint says extraction
    discards the verbatim substrate, capping recall ~20pts below a verbatim ceiling)
  - Hybrid retrieval: parallel dense-vector + keyword/BM25-style lexical match, merged then
    context-aware reranked with temporal filtering, all under ~400ms
  - Two-tier user profile: stable static facts + dynamic episodic context, assembled at query time
    (~50ms) and injected as a compact system-prompt preamble
  - Edge-runtime stateless orchestration: no session/state in the app layer because the memory
    store is the durable state primitive
nest steals: nest-format: add OPTIONAL additive per-chunk summary section (id 0x09+, no field-meaning change so
             still v1) feeding nest-runtime materialize a compressed representative. nest-runtime:
             implement summary>canonical>raw fallback ladder in search/materialize; add
             deterministic version/isLatest u32 in provenance to collapse superseded duplicate
             chunks at build time before encoding (shrinks tiny/compressed presets); layer a
             deterministic provenance-timestamp 'temporal rerank' boost ON TOP of the mandatory
             exact-cosine rerank (cosine stays ground truth). containerTags -> optional per-chunk
             tag set in provenance for multi-tenant filtering inside one .nest file, exposed as
             nest-cli search --tag.
compression: Indirect but real. The one transferable compression idea is the summary-first granularity ladder:
             storing and serving a small synopsis per chunk lets nest return a representative far
             smaller than the canonical span (good for context packing and citation previews), and
             build-time supersession/dedup of contradicting facts removes redundant chunks before
             encoding, directly improving size ratios of the tiny/compressed presets. Everything
             else (knowledge graph, fact extraction, hosted reranker) is orchestration-layer and
             orthogonal to nest's byte-level codec goals; nest's exact-cosine-rerank guarantee is
             actually stronger than supermemory's ANN-proxy scores.
perf:        Client is Vercel edge-runtime, streaming via Vercel AI SDK; no local compute. Supermemory engine
             reports profile assembly ~50ms, full hybrid+rerank+temporal-filter retrieval <400ms,
             with no caller-configured embedding infra. Benchmarks (vendor-reported): 85.4%
             LongMemEval-S, #1 LoCoMo P@1 59.7%, #1 ConvoMem; an arXiv counterpoint measured
             ~65.4% citing the verbatim-discard weakness of extraction-first memory. No
             SIMD/quantization relevance -- all heavy lifting is remote and opaque.
license:     MIT (opensearch-ai repo); Supermemory engine is a hosted closed/proprietary API behind the client

# category: data-pipeline  (3 repos)
# focus: ML data lakes, dataset tooling, embedding benchmarks

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] activeloopai/deeplake
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Deep Lake is a columnar "tensor storage format" data lake that stores ML data as chunked,
             natively-compressed arrays and keeps a separate, run-length-encoded triplet of index
             maps (ChunkIdEncoder, BytePositionsEncoder, ShapeEncoder) mapping each sample index
             to its chunk, byte range, and shape without loading the bulk payload.
innovation:  Sidecar index maps are stored as 2D integer arrays of "runs" where each row holds a value plus the
             CUMULATIVE last-sample-index that run covers; consecutive identical values (same
             chunk, same shape, same per-sample byte size) collapse into a single row, and lookups
             are a binary search over the cumulative-index column. This run-length +
             cumulative-prefix-index hybrid makes per-sample sidecar metadata grow only when a
             value actually changes (a fixed-shape tensor of 1B samples needs one shape row;
             ~150MB chunk-id encoder per 1PB), while staying O(log n) random-addressable.
techniques:
  - Run-length + cumulative-index encoders: rows = (value, last_index_covered); identical
    consecutive samples collapse to one row
  - Binary search over the cumulative last-index column for O(log n) sample->location lookup
  - Triplet split of sidecar metadata: ChunkIdEncoder (sample->chunk), BytePositionsEncoder
    (per-sample byte size, offsets derived by prefix sum), ShapeEncoder (sample shape)
  - Metadata fully separated from bulk data so shape/offset queries never decode payload chunks
  - Native sample-level compression: store jpeg/png/mp4 bytes verbatim, copy-without-recode when
    raw codec matches tensor codec
  - Chunk-size bounds (lower/upper) packing variable-length samples into ~8MB chunks instead of
    static per-sample chunking
  - Tiling: samples larger than a chunk split across multiple chunks via a tile encoder
  - TQL + optimized hnswlib HNSW with exact linear-scan fallback under ~100k rows
nest steals: Adopt the run-length + cumulative-index encoder in nest-format for the chunks_original_spans
             (0x03) section and optionally chunk_ids (0x01). Today
             crates/nest-format/src/sections/spans.rs writes the full source_uri string plus two
             raw u64 (byte_start, byte_end) for EVERY chunk; when 5k chunks come from one document
             this is ~5k repeated URIs and ~80KB of offsets that are almost always contiguous
             (end[i]==start[i+1]). Replace with: (a) a deduplicated source_uri dictionary plus a
             per-chunk uri_id run-length encoded (one run per source document), and (b)
             delta/cumulative encoding of spans where contiguous chunks store only a run-length
             plus per-chunk length, reconstructing absolute offsets by prefix sum exactly like
             BytePositionsEncoder. This is an internal encoding of a non-embedding (cold-path)
             section, so it is zstd-eligible and never touches the SIMD scoring hot path. It fits
             the frozen v1 format as a NEW encoding id in the reserved 4-255 range (or a
             transparent re-encode within the existing section that decodes identically) with no
             NEST_FORMAT_VERSION bump. content_hash stays stable because it hashes DECODED
             canonical sections, and determinism holds via an insertion-stable dictionary. It
             shrinks provenance/spans metadata that currently scales O(num_chunks) with redundant
             strings, directly serving the compress-more-than-everyone north star.
compression: High. nest's metadata sections (spans, chunk_ids, provenance) currently scale O(num_chunks) with
             verbatim repeated source URIs and raw 8-byte offsets. The run-length +
             cumulative-index scheme collapses these to O(num_distinct_runs), often near-constant
             for single-document corpora, removing structural redundancy that zstd alone leaves on
             the table (entropy coding shrinks bytes but not the inherent redundancy of contiguous
             64-bit offsets, which is better eliminated before compression). It targets exactly
             the non-embedding part of the .nest file, complementing int8/f16 embedding
             quantization.
perf:        Encoders are tiny: ~150MB ChunkIdEncoder per 1PB tensor; a fixed-shape tensor needs a single
             ShapeEncoder row. Lookups are O(log n) binary search over the cumulative column using
             only integer ops, with no payload decompression, so they stay off any SIMD hot path.
             For nest the span/uri encoder is read once during materialize/cite (not during
             scoring), so binary-search cost is irrelevant to query latency; the win is on-disk
             size plus faster section parse (fewer length-prefixed strings). Deep Lake's hnswlib
             HNSW handles ~35M embeddings in <1s with exact linear scan under ~100k rows,
             mirroring nest's mandatory-exact-rerank philosophy.
license:     Apache-2.0 (re-implement the idea from the CIDR 2023 paper rather than vendoring code; the encoder
             concept is freely transferable)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] rom1504/img2dataset
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A multiprocess/multithreaded downloader that turns billion-scale image-URL lists into sharded
             WebDataset tar files paired 1:1 with lightweight columnar parquet sidecars and
             order-aligned embedding .npy arrays, all addressed by a deterministic positional
             composite key.
innovation:  The "shadow sidecar" split: heavy opaque payload (jpg bytes in tar) is stored separately from a
             parallel lightweight columnar metadata file (parquet) that mirrors it 1:1, so all
             filtering, statistics, and validation happen against the small columnar file without
             ever touching the heavy payload, while a deterministic positional composite key
             (SSSSSIIII = 5-digit shard id + 4-digit in-shard index) preserves exact order
             alignment across every parallel artifact (tar payload, parquet metadata, npy
             embeddings) and makes builds resumable.
techniques:
  - composite positional key SSSSSIIII: zero-padded shard-id prefix + in-shard index, encoding
    both location and order in a sortable integer string
  - 1:1 ordered alignment invariant: input urls -> tar payload -> parquet metadata -> npy
    embeddings all in identical sample order, addressable by offset
  - shadow columnar sidecar (parquet) mirrors heavy tar so stats/filtering run without decoding
    payloads (41455 tars <-> 41455 parquets, ~1.6MB vs ~270MB)
  - WebDataset key convention: files sharing a basename before the first dot form one logical
    sample; extension selects type (.jpg/.txt/.json)
  - incremental/resumable builds keyed on shard completion (incremental | overwrite | extend),
    retry up to max_shard_retry
  - multi-tier hash integrity: optional md5/sha256/sha512 per sample, verify_hash drops mismatches
    vs externally-supplied checksums
  - sequential-I/O-first tar packaging: 3-10x over random access, collapses millions of file
    handles to single digits
  - post-hoc small-file consolidation: pyspark compacts hundreds of thousands of parquet shards
    into ~32 large files
nest steals: Three concrete steals. (1) nest-format: formalize the "shadow columnar sidecar" inside the single
             .nest container. Today provenance + search_contract carry coarse metadata; add an
             optional additive section (reserved id 0x09+, never bump v1) holding a compact
             columnar SoA of per-chunk filterable scalars (source_id, lang, byte_len, token_count,
             dataset tag) so nest-cli stats/filtered-search and nest-runtime can pre-filter
             candidate chunks WITHOUT decoding chunks_canonical text or touching embeddings on the
             hot path -- a pure metadata scan, mirroring parquet-without-tar. The scalar columns
             are tiny, delta/zstd-friendly (zstd already allowed on non-embedding sections), and
             let search skip-list before SIMD scoring. (2) nest-format hashing/layout: adopt the
             1:1 ordered-alignment invariant explicitly as a frozen guarantee -- chunk_ids[i],
             chunks_canonical[i], chunks_original_spans[i], embeddings[i] and any new scalar
             column[i] all share index i, so nest://content_hash/chunk_id resolves to one offset
             across every section; this is the on-disk analog of tar<->parquet<->npy alignment and
             strengthens the deterministic contract. (3) nest-cli: borrow the composite positional
             key idea for chunk_ids -- a deterministic sortable key (source-shard prefix +
             monotone index) makes builds reproducible and enables future incremental/extend
             rebuilds (append new chunks as a new logical shard range) without rewriting prior
             sections, aligning with the additive-only rule. The downloader/distributed/fsspec
             machinery is out of scope (nest is offline-by-construction, never opens a socket);
             only the on-disk layout discipline transfers.
compression: Medium-high but indirect. img2dataset is a downloader, not a codec, so it offers no entropy coder
             or quantization scheme to beat. The transferable compression lever is the
             shadow-sidecar layout: separating tiny filterable scalar columns from the heavy
             text/embedding payload lets nest (a) zstd-compress the scalar columns extremely well
             via columnar SoA + delta coding (homogeneous columns compress far better than
             row-interleaved metadata), and (b) prune candidates before scoring so less of the
             file is touched on the hot path. It reinforces nest's existing principle that
             embeddings stay raw/mmap-scored while everything else is squeezed.
perf:        Reference numbers: ~1350 images/s/machine (~116M/24h) via K=10000 shards, N=cpu-count processes, M
             threads each, one tar per process to avoid disk contention. Parquet sidecar in
             LAION-400M is ~1.6MB vs ~270MB tar (>150x smaller) -- the quantitative case for
             metadata-only scans. Sequential tar I/O is cited at 3-10x faster than random access
             on local drives. None of this involves SIMD or a custom codec; it is process/thread
             orchestration plus I/O layout. For nest the takeaway is purely architectural: keep
             the scored array contiguous and order-aligned, keep filterable metadata in a separate
             tiny columnar block.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] embeddings-benchmark/mteb
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        MTEB is a Python embedding/retrieval evaluation harness that standardizes datasets
             (corpus/queries/relevant_docs/top_ranked), runs chunked top-k similarity search over
             multiple metrics, scores with pytrec_eval (nDCG@k, MRR@k, Recall@k, MAP), and
             persists results as content-addressed per-(model,revision,task) JSON in a tiered
             remote/disk/in-memory cache.
innovation:  A standardized, reproducible retrieval-quality measurement protocol: a fixed task schema (corpus,
             queries, relevant_docs as query_id->corpus_id->relevance, top_ranked candidate lists)
             feeding a chunked exact top-k search that computes cosine/dot/euclidean and reports
             the best, scored by an established trec_eval metric set (nDCG@k, MRR@k, Recall@k,
             capped recall), with results stored under deterministic content-keyed paths so an
             identical (model, revision, task) never recomputes.
techniques:
  - RetrievalEvaluator: chunked exact top-k via corpus_chunk_size, sub-batch similarity matmul,
    per-query (corpus_id, score) collected then descending sort, avoiding O(N) memory blowup
  - Multi-metric scoring: compute cosine, dot, and euclidean and report the best; cosine = inner
    product on L2-normalized vectors
  - pytrec_eval (trec_eval bindings) for nDCG@k / MRR@k / Recall@k / MAP, plus custom recall_cap
    (cap by #relevant) and calculate_pmrr (MRR delta under instruction change)
  - BEIR-derived task schema: dataset[subset][split] = {corpus, queries, relevant_docs:
    qid->cid->rel, top_ranked: qid->[cid]}
  - Content-addressed result cache: composite key = hash(benchmark name + language tuple); layout
    results/{model_path}/{revision}/{task}.json with experiments/{name}/ subtree
  - TaskResult validation on load: required-split checks (test/train) reject stale cache that no
    longer matches task requirements
  - SearchProtocol index()/search() interface accepting encode_kwargs so custom indexes (e.g.
    PLAID) plug into the same eval loop
  - Tiered cache: HuggingFace Hub remote -> local filesystem JSON -> in-memory object cache,
    rooted at MTEB_CACHE / ~/.cache/mteb
nest steals: Three steals. (1) nest-cli benchmark subcommand: adopt MTEB's retrieval-task schema
             (corpus/queries/relevant_docs/top_ranked) and pytrec_eval-equivalent metrics (nDCG@k,
             MRR@k, Recall@k) as a deterministic Rust quality harness over a .nest file, turning
             today's anecdotal 'tiny preset recall@10 0.992' into a reproducible seeded per-preset
             report; pure compute, no socket, fits offline/deterministic constraints and the
             <=300-line single-responsibility rule (one metrics module per metric). (2)
             nest-runtime search path: MTEB's chunked exact top-k (corpus_chunk_size sub-batches,
             descending sort) mirrors nest's mandatory exact-cosine rerank; formalize a
             corpus_chunk_size loop in the SIMD dispatcher so the exact rerank streams over mmap'd
             embedding sub-blocks (bounded working set, cache-friendly) rather than one big pass,
             hardening the search_contract guarantee. (3) nest-format: an optional additive
             eval-results section (reserved id 0x09+) recording metric scores lets a .nest
             container self-certify its recall vs the exact f32 baseline without re-running;
             nest's existing content_hash already delivers MTEB-style 'same inputs never
             recompute' for free.
compression: Low-to-indirect. MTEB has no entropy coder, quantization codec, or on-disk compression to steal;
             it stores results as plain JSON. Its value to nest's compress-more-than-everyone goal
             is as a rigorous quality yardstick: it lets nest prove that aggressive compression
             (int8, f16, hnsw in the tiny preset) preserves retrieval recall (nDCG@k/Recall@k vs
             the exact f32 baseline), so compression can be pushed harder with measured,
             non-regressing quality rather than guesswork.
perf:        RetrievalEvaluator's corpus_chunk_size keeps the similarity matmul working set bounded so corpora
             larger than RAM are scored in sub-batches; cosine is implemented as inner product on
             L2-normalized vectors (one normalization, then BLAS/SIMD dot), matching nest's mmap
             SIMD dot kernels (AVX2/NEON/scalar). Scoring throughput is dominated by pytrec_eval
             (C trec_eval), not Python. MTEB itself ships no SIMD or quantization; it relies on
             the model and FAISS/numpy for heavy linear algebra.
license:     Apache-2.0


##################################################################################################
# category: content-addressed  (4 repos)
# focus: content-addressed, reproducible, and distributed storage
##################################################################################################

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] ipfs/kubo
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Kubo is the reference Go implementation of IPFS: a content-addressed distributed store that chunks
             files, wraps them in a UnixFS Merkle DAG of hash-linked blocks (each block addressed
             by a CID = multibase + multicodec + multihash), and deduplicates at the block level
             via a multihash-indexed blockstore.
innovation:  Content-defined chunking (CDC): block boundaries are chosen by a rolling hash over the byte stream
             (boundary when the top/low N bits of the fingerprint hit a target value), NOT at
             fixed offsets. This makes chunk boundaries content-aligned, so a local edit only
             perturbs the one or two chunks it touches instead of re-flowing every downstream
             boundary, yielding stable hashes and high cross-version deduplication. Pair this with
             a content address (multihash) that indexes storage independently of the on-disk
             encoding/wrapper, so identical content collapses to one stored block regardless of
             how it was transported.
techniques:
  - Rabin fingerprint CDC: 64-byte rolling polynomial window, boundary when low bits == target;
    Kubo exposes rabin-[min]-[avg]-[max]
  - Buzhash CDC: cyclic-polynomial rolling hash (left-rotate + xor over window), ~3x faster than
    Rabin, judge on top bits
  - Gear/FastCDC family: single left-shift+add rolling hash, zero-padded mask to widen effective
    window, cut-point skipping below min-size, normalized chunking for tight size distribution,
    ~1350 MB/s
  - CID = multibase + cidv1 + multicodec(0x55 raw / 0x70 dag-pb / 0x71 dag-cbor) + multihash(0x12
    sha2-256,len,digest); multihash alone indexes the blockstore
  - UnixFS Merkle DAG with selectable layout: balanced (fast random seek) vs trickle
    (append-optimized); high fanout enables parallel/partial fetch and incremental verification
  - raw-leaves: leaf blocks stored as raw bytes (codec 0x55) with no protobuf wrapper, so block
    multihash equals plain content hash
  - HAMT (hash array mapped trie) sharding for large directories/maps via max-hamt-fanout
  - block-level dedup in the blockstore: same-content chunks across files/versions store once,
    keyed by multihash
nest steals: Add a content-defined chunker to nest-python's build pipeline (the stage producing
             chunk_ids/chunks_canonical) as an alternative to current token/sentence segmentation,
             used for large blob inputs (pdf/json/parquet/datasets/whole repos on the roadmap).
             Implement a small deterministic Gear/FastCDC kernel in nest-format under a new
             encoding-neutral chunking module (rolling hash + seeded mask, seeded determinism to
             satisfy the deterministic-build constraint, <=300 loc, typed thiserror errors,
             repr(C) little-endian if any state is serialized). This unlocks edit-local rechunking
             for nest's promised real-time edit: editing one paragraph re-emits only the touched
             chunk(s), so embeddings + chunks_canonical for unchanged chunks stay byte-identical
             and the file diff stays tiny. Second, formalize a block-level dedup pass in
             nest-format writer: hash each canonical chunk (already done via content_hash
             machinery) and store duplicate chunks once, with chunk_ids referencing the shared
             physical block. Third, nest's content_hash (STABLE across encodings) and
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id citation already mirror Kubo's multihash-indexes-storage
             / encoding-is-a-wrapper split; cite as prior-art validation and consider chunk-level
             addressing so a citation resolves a single deduped block.
compression: Directly central. CDC + block dedup is the single biggest lever for 'compress more than everyone'
             on repetitive/edited corpora: it removes redundancy ACROSS chunks and versions before
             any entropy coder (zstd) runs, which nest currently does not do (it chunks by
             tokens/sentences and zstd-compresses sections independently). Edit-local boundaries
             also make incremental/append builds cheap, supporting real-time-edit and 'whole
             databases/repos' goals. Applies to non-embedding sections (canonical text, original
             spans, future binary blob sections) since embeddings are intentionally never
             compressed; the win is on text/json/span and blob payloads with zero recall cost.
perf:        Gear/FastCDC reaches ~1350 MB/s single-thread (Rust gearhash/fastcdc crates), ~3x Rabin; rolling
             hash is a left-shift+add per byte (or 2 bytes/step in FastCDC), branch-predictor and
             SIMD friendly, fits nest's scalar+SIMD dispatch philosophy. Dedup ratio of Gear is
             within ~1% of Rabin typical (up to ~6% worse worst-case); FastCDC normalized chunking
             closes the gap while staying fast. Memory is O(window) constant. Boundary probability
             set purely by mask width (1/2^N), giving deterministic expected chunk size with no
             separate average parameter.
license:     Kubo is dual-licensed Apache-2.0 and MIT (permissive); standalone Rust gearhash and fastcdc crates
             are MIT/Apache-2.0, so the algorithm and a vendored/clean-room kernel are safe to
             adopt.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] NixOS/nix
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A purely-functional package manager whose store is a content-addressed object graph: artifacts are
             identified by SHA-256 hashes of a canonical, metadata-stripped serialization (NAR)
             plus a structured "fingerprint" string, enabling deterministic builds and distributed
             substitution from untrusted binary caches.
innovation:  Decoupling logical-content identity from physical-byte identity via a canonical serialization
             (NAR) hashed independently of its on-disk compressed encoding: the narinfo carries
             BOTH NarHash/NarSize (uncompressed canonical content) and FileHash/FileSize
             (compressed artifact), and signatures/addresses are computed only over the
             uncompressed canonical form. This makes the content identity stable across any
             compression codec while still verifying the physical bytes you downloaded.
techniques:
  - NAR canonical serialization: 64-bit LE length-prefixed strings padded to 8-byte boundaries,
    magic 'nix-archive-1', directory entries sorted by name, all timestamps/metadata stripped
    except executable-bit and symlink target -> byte-identical output for identical trees
  - Structured hash 'fingerprint' string: type ':sha256:' inner-digest ':' store ':' name,
    SHA-256'd then truncated/compressed from 256 to 160 bits and rendered in a custom base-32
    alphabet (omits e,o,u,t, reverse byte order)
  - Dual-identity addressing: NarHash/NarSize (logical, uncompressed) vs FileHash/FileSize
    (physical, compressed .nar.xz/.zst) tracked separately in narinfo
  - Hash-modulo-self-references: replace the object's own not-yet-known store path with a fixed
    sentinel/scratch placeholder before hashing, so a self-referential CA digest is computable and
    placement-independent
  - Ed25519 signing over a fingerprint '1;StorePath;NarHash;NarSize;refs' covering only
    uncompressed content, so recompression never invalidates signatures
  - Minimal HTTP substitution protocol: GET /<storehash>.narinfo (metadata keyed by content hash)
    + GET /nar/<filehash>.nar.<ext> (content-addressed blob), trivially
    mirrorable/offline-cacheable
  - Content-defined chunking lesson (nix-casync/Tvix): whole-NAR dedup is weak (one changed bit
    busts the whole file); CDC into a chunk-store + index-store (caibx) enables sub-file dedup,
    and compression must sit OUTSIDE the chunking layer because payloads are chunked decompressed
  - Fixed-output derivations: declare expected flat-or-recursive hash up front, decoupling content
    address from build inputs (intrinsic vs extrinsic addressing)
nest steals: nest-format already has the right skeleton (content_hash = decoded canonical sections, stable
             across encodings; per-section checksum = physical bytes) -- Nix validates this split
             is the correct primitive and supplies hardening details. Concretely: (1) In
             crates/nest-format/src/hashing.rs, adopt NAR's structured-fingerprint discipline --
             prefix the content_hash preimage with a typed, length-delimited domain string
             (version + section-id + logical-length) rather than concatenating raw section bytes,
             eliminating canonicalization ambiguity and giving the same domain-separation Nix gets
             from its 'type:sha256:...:store:name' fingerprint. (2) In sections/encoding,
             formalize that content_hash is computed over the NAR-style canonical decoded form
             while per-section checksum stays over compressed physical bytes -- making
             content_hash provably codec-invariant (raw==zstd==f16 for non-embedding sections),
             the exact NarHash-vs-FileHash separation; this lets nest re-encode a .nest (raw->tiny
             preset) and prove identical content_hash, a killer sovereign re-packaging guarantee.
             (3) For citation layer nest://content_hash/chunk_id, borrow
             length-prefixed+8-byte-padded framing for chunk_ids/spans so the canonical form is
             unambiguous and deterministic across platforms. (4) Future additive section id 0x09+:
             a content-defined-chunking 'chunk_store'+'index' pair (FastCDC, gear-hash rolling)
             for the chunks_canonical/text section, giving sub-document dedup across many .nest
             files or repeated boilerplate inside one corpus -- directly serving 'compress more
             than everyone' by deduplicating shared substrings before zstd, with the Nix-learned
             constraint that CDC runs on DECOMPRESSED bytes and compression is applied per-chunk
             afterward. (5) nest-cli could gain a Nix-style 'verify' checking both surfaces and a
             'cite'/export emitting a narinfo-like sidecar for distributed mirroring -- all
             offline, no socket.
compression: High. The content-defined-chunking + chunk-store/index model is a concrete path to
             sub-file/cross-file deduplication that compounds with nest's existing zstd (dedupe
             shared substrings BEFORE entropy coding). The NarHash/FileHash separation is the
             formal license to aggressively re-encode (zstd/f16/int8) while keeping a stable
             content identity -- you can ship the smallest preset and still prove it carries the
             same content_hash as the exact preset. The Nix lesson 'compression outside the
             chunking layer, CDC on decompressed bytes' prevents a classic mistake that would
             otherwise destroy nest's dedup ratio.
perf:        NAR/store-path hashing is single-threaded SHA-256 over a streaming serialization; cheap relative
             to builds. Binary cache supports multi-threaded xz and zstd compression of NARs (only
             xz/zstd are MT-capable); cache.nixos.org defaults to xz (smallest) with zstd as the
             faster-decompress alternative. The 256->160-bit truncation keeps store-path digests
             short (32 base-32 chars) at negligible collision risk for the namespace size. CDC
             (nix-casync) adds chunk-lookup overhead but turns O(file) re-uploads into
             O(changed-chunks); reassembly is sequential concatenation of zstd-decompressed
             chunks.
license:     LGPL-2.1-or-later. Use as design reference / clean-room reimplementation only -- do NOT vendor
             code into nest's permissively-licensed Rust crates; the algorithms (NAR framing,
             fingerprint scheme, CDC) are ideas/specs, not copyrightable, so reimplementing from
             the published protocol spec is safe.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[HIGH] NixOS/nixpkgs (Nix store / NAR model, implemented in NixOS/nix)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        Nix is a content-addressed package store whose substrate is NAR (Nix Archive): a fully
             deterministic, metadata-stripped, length-prefixed binary serialization of filesystem
             trees, hashed with SHA-256 and truncated to a 160-bit nix-base32 digest to form
             immutable, reproducible content-addressed store paths and derivations.
innovation:  A canonical, reproducible serialization (NAR) that strips all non-reproducible metadata (mtime,
             atime, uid/gid, all permission bits except the single executable bit) and sorts
             directory entries by name, so "same logical content => byte-identical archive =>
             identical hash" holds across machines and time. This is exactly the discipline nest
             aspires to with content_hash; NAR proves the pattern at planet scale (all of
             nixpkgs).
techniques:
  - NAR serialization: magic str("nix-archive-1"), recursive (...) framing of
    regular/symlink/directory nodes, only the executable bit preserved, no timestamps
  - str(s) framing: 64-bit little-endian length prefix + raw bytes + zero-pad to next 8-byte
    boundary (the load-bearing canonicalization primitive)
  - Directory entries serialized sorted-by-name so the archive is independent of source filesystem
    ordering
  - Two-level hashing: SHA-256 over NAR contents -> inner-digest (hex), then SHA-256 over typed
    fingerprint 'type:sha256:inner:store:name', truncated to first 160 bits
  - nix-base32: custom 32-char alphabet (omits e/o/u/t to avoid confusables and dirty words)
    encoding the 160-bit digest into the store path
  - Content-addressed derivations + deriving paths: refer to an output before its hash is known,
    resolved post-build; flat/nar/text/git addressing methods
  - Store-wide dedup via nix store optimise: hash every regular file, hard-link identical
    NAR-content files into /nix/store/.links/<checksum> (25-35% savings, no correctness loss
    because store is immutable+CA)
  - Binary cache: NARs transported as independent zstd (or xz) compressed, content-addressed blobs
    enabling distributed substitution without a central DB
nest steals: nest-format already content_hashes decoded canonical sections; steal NAR's exact canonicalization
             to harden it. In crates/nest-format/src/hashing.rs + sections.rs: (1) adopt NAR's
             str() framing as the content_hash preimage -- u64-LE length prefix + payload +
             zero-pad to 8 bytes per section, sections in fixed ID order, any maps/sets
             (chunk_ids, bm25 terms) sorted -- so content_hash is provably encoding-independent,
             making the 'STABLE across encodings' claim airtight and matching the bm25
             alphabetical-term-order rule already in spec. (2) Port the store-optimise dedup
             pattern into nest-format/encoding: before zstd, SHA-256 each canonical chunk and
             store duplicate chunks once with a back-reference table -- a new additive encoding id
             (4-255 reserved) 'dedup' collapsing repeated text/json/yaml fragments, directly
             serving 'compress more than everyone' on boilerplate-heavy corpora (legal, PT-BR news
             templates, repeated JSON keys). Apply to non-embedding sections only, never
             embeddings (hot-path rule). (3) Use nix-base32 (160-bit, confusable-free) as a
             shorter, copy-paste-safe citation token in nest-cli cite for
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id. All of this respects repr(C), little-endian, additive,
             deterministic, offline constraints.
compression: High and direct. NAR is not itself a compressor, but two ideas feed extreme compression: (a)
             canonical content-addressing is the precondition for safe dedup -- you can only
             collapse byte-identical regions if you can prove identity reproducibly, which nest
             needs before any cross-chunk dedup codec; (b) Nix store-optimise hard-link dedup
             yields real 25-35% reduction on redundant corpora purely by content-hash equality,
             before any entropy coding. nest can layer a dedup pre-pass under its existing zstd
             path on non-embedding sections, compounding with float16/int8 quantization in the
             tiny preset.
perf:        NAR's 8-byte-aligned length-prefixed framing makes parsing fast and unambiguous (no
             escaping/quoting) but causes many tiny 8-byte reads in naive streaming readers -- a
             non-issue for nest since sections are mmapped and reads are length-bounded. Two-level
             SHA-256 (content then fingerprint) is cheap vs I/O. Store-wide dedup is I/O-bound
             (10-30 min on full Nix store) and the giant .links dir slows GC; nest's bounded
             single-file scope avoids that -- dedup is a one-shot build-time pass in nest-python
             before freezing. zstd over NAR is ~8-12x faster than xz at competitive ratio (xz
             18.8s vs zstd 2.35s on a sample), validating nest's zstd-over-heavier-coder choice on
             the build path.
license:     MIT

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] jvm-repo-rebuild/reproducible-central
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A crowd-sourced verification corpus that rebuilds Maven Central artifacts inside pinned Docker/JDK
             environments and proves byte-for-byte reproducibility by comparing SHA-512
             fingerprints of every output file against the published artifact, recording the
             verdict in declarative .buildspec/.buildcompare/.buildinfo text files.
innovation:  A two-layer reproducibility proof split into (1) a tiny declarative recipe (.buildspec:
             groupId/artifactId/version + gitTag + jdk-major + newline-mode + exact build command)
             that is the minimal pinned input needed to regenerate bytes, and (2) a
             per-output-file SHA-512 manifest (.buildinfo: outputs.N.filename / outputs.N.length /
             outputs.N.checksums.sha512 lowercase) that is content-addressing of the build result;
             equality of the manifest is the reproducibility certificate, and diffoscope is
             invoked only on mismatch to localize the divergence. Reproducibility is engineered,
             not hoped for: ~92% of Maven non-determinism came from embedded archive timestamps,
             killed by zeroing ZIP/JAR mtimes (SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH / project.build.outputTimestamp)
             plus enforcing a deterministic sorted entry order inside the archive.
techniques:
  - Declarative minimal build recipe (.buildspec): groupId/artifactId/version + gitRepo/gitTag +
    jdk MAJOR version only + newline mode (lf/crlf) + exact build command -- the smallest
    deterministic input set
  - Per-output-file content manifest (.buildinfo): indexed records outputs.N.filename /
    outputs.N.length / outputs.N.checksums.sha512 in lowercase; manifest equality ==
    reproducibility proof
  - SHA-512 (not SHA-256) lowercase hex as the canonical artifact fingerprint, with length
    recorded alongside as a cheap fast-reject pre-check
  - Verdict file (.buildcompare) decoupled from hashes: ok/ko/ignored counts plus space-separated
    okFiles/koFiles/ignoredFiles name lists + reference_java_version/reference_os_name provenance
  - Archive normalization for determinism: zero/pin all ZIP/JAR entry timestamps
    (SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH, project.build.outputTimestamp, java.properties.date) which removed ~92% of
    failures, plus enforced sorted/canonical entry order
  - Environment pinning at coarse granularity: only JDK MAJOR version pinned (minor/vendor proven
    irrelevant to bytecode), minimizing the recipe while preserving reproducibility
  - diffoscope as mismatch explainer only: recursively unpacks JAR -> class -> bytecode to
    localize diverging bytes, run in a pinned container, never on the happy path
  - Containerized rebuild (pinned Docker image per JDK) as environment-as-data substrate for
    independent re-verification
nest steals: nest-format already has content_hash (decoded canonical sections, stable across encodings) and
             file_hash (physical pre-footer body). Steal the .buildspec/.buildinfo split to make
             REPRODUCIBILITY a first-class, externally verifiable artifact. (1) In
             nest-format/manifest, emit a deterministic build recipe alongside provenance: model
             fingerprint, chunker version, encoding preset, section ordering -- the minimal pinned
             inputs guaranteeing 'same chunks + same model fingerprint => byte-identical file'.
             This is the .buildspec analogue and turns nest's determinism claim into something a
             third party can re-derive offline. (2) Adopt the per-section SHA manifest in
             nest-format/hashing: nest already has per-section physical checksums; expose them as
             a flat, sorted, lowercase-hex manifest (section_id, decoded_length, content_checksum)
             so two independently-built .nest files compare by manifest equality without
             byte-diffing 100MB embeddings, with decoded_length as a free fast-reject before
             hashing. (3) Add a nest-cli 'verify-reproducible' subcommand (sibling to validate)
             that takes a recipe + corpus and asserts manifest equality, emitting an ok/ko/ignored
             verdict like .buildcompare -- cheap, offline, deterministic, additive. (4) The
             archive-normalization lesson maps to extreme compression: canonicalize entry order
             and strip incidental timestamps/non-canonical ordering BEFORE hashing/encoding,
             because canonical ordering both guarantees reproducibility AND maximizes zstd ratio
             on non-embedding sections (sorted bm25 terms + sorted chunk_ids already do this;
             extend the discipline to every section). Cross-file relevance: content-addressed
             manifests enable dedup -- identical chunks/embeddings across many .nest files share
             one content_hash, so a future distributed/pack format stores each unique decoded
             section once and references it (Maven's 'same coordinates => same bytes' applied to
             vector data).
compression: Indirect but real. The direct payload (SHA-512 manifests, .buildspec recipes) is verification
             infrastructure, not a codec, so it does not shrink bytes itself. Two transferable
             ideas serve compress-more-than-everyone: (1) canonical/sorted entry ordering before
             encoding is simultaneously the reproducibility guarantee AND a zstd ratio win on
             non-embedding sections; (2) content-addressed per-section manifests are the
             prerequisite for cross-file dedup -- store each unique decoded section once across a
             corpus of .nest files and reference by content_hash, which is how Maven's 'same
             coordinates => same bytes' becomes 'same chunk => stored once'.
perf:        No SIMD/throughput angle -- offline batch verification (docker rebuild + hash). Two transferable
             perf ideas: record decoded length next to each hash so file comparison fast-rejects
             on length mismatch before computing the expensive hash; and compare-by-manifest
             (hash-list equality) instead of byte-diffing large payloads, so verifying two
             multi-GB artifacts is O(number of sections) over precomputed hashes, not O(bytes).
             diffoscope-style deep diff is reserved strictly for the mismatch path so the common
             case stays cheap.
license:     Apache-2.0 (project content); per-artifact recipes follow upstream project licenses. Safe to learn
             from; no code vendored.


##################################################################################################
# category: factcheck-datasets  (12 repos)
# focus: fact-checking / misinformation resources and datasets (nest demo domain + truth features)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] yuxiaw/Factcheck-GPT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        An LLM-output fact-checking framework that decomposes a document into context-independent atomic
             claims, retrieves ranked evidence per claim, runs 4-way stance detection
             (support/partial-support/refute/irrelevant), and emits per-claim verdict records,
             shipping Factcheck-Bench (94 annotated ChatGPT responses) plus FactBench (4,835
             examples merged from FacTool-KB/FELM-WK/HaluEval).
innovation:  A serializable, fine-grained "claim -> evidence -> stance -> verdict" provenance schema: every
             checkable fact becomes an atomic claim record with a structured verdict dict
             {reasoning, error, correction, factuality:bool}, each backed by N ranked evidence
             snippets carrying a discrete stance label (CP/PS/RE/IR). This decouples a coarse
             document from auditable, span-localized truth units -- the structure nest needs to
             attach verifiable truth/provenance metadata to chunks without inventing a format from
             scratch.
techniques:
  - Atomic claim decomposition: NLTK sentence-split then LLM decompose(input:str)->list[str] of
    context-independent, decontextualized statements
  - 4-way stance vocabulary CP/PS/RE/IR (completely-support / partially-support / refute /
    irrelevant), collapsible to 3-way (support/refute/irrelevant)
  - Per-claim verdict record: {reasoning, error, correction, factuality:bool} structured
    machine-parseable judgment
  - Evidence retrieval by query-generation then ranking ~5 snippets by semantic relevance to the
    claim
  - Three-class modular abstraction: claim_processor -> retriever -> verifier pipeline (mirrors
    OpenFactCheck)
  - Checkworthiness gating with discrete labels {factual=1, opinion=2, non-claim=3, other=4} to
    filter what is worth verifying
  - Span-based annotation schema: response + list of false segments/spans + per-claim factual
    labels[] + document-level label
nest steals: nest-format: add an OPTIONAL additive section (reserved id 0x09+, never bump v1) named
             chunks_truth -- a deterministic, zstd-encoded (non-embedding, codec-eligible) table
             parallel to chunks_canonical holding per-chunk atomic-claim records: claim_id, char
             span into chunks_original_spans, verdict enum {true,false,unverified} as a 1-byte
             field, stance counts, and optional evidence chunk_ids resolving to
             nest://content_hash/chunk_id citations. This makes the PT-BR fake-news demo corpus
             self-describing: each chunk carries its own auditable verdict + evidence pointers,
             offline, inside the single file. nest-runtime: a thin verify/filter on the search
             path (post exact-cosine rerank) to restrict or boost results by verdict, plus a
             materialize path emitting the {reasoning,error,correction,factuality} record for a
             hit. nest-cli: extend the `cite` subcommand (or add `verify`) to print the truth
             record + evidence citations. The 4-way stance label is a 1-byte enum (deterministic,
             alphabetical-order friendly), staying within nest's small-core, no-panic,
             little-endian, additive constraints.
compression: Indirect to raw compression. Stance/verdict labels are tiny discrete enums (1 byte each, highly
             compressible under zstd, in a non-embedding codec-eligible section) adding negligible
             bytes while raising 'truth density' per byte of the demo corpus. The real win is
             mission-side (truth features + provenance for the fake-news demo domain), not the
             compress-more-than-everyone north-star: it does not improve embedding or text ratios.
perf:        Reference framework is LLM-prompt-bound (GPT-3.5-turbo per claim, Google search per claim) -- not
             a hot-path or SIMD concern. nest would NOT replicate the online retrieval/LLM calls
             (violates offline-by-construction); it adopts only the data schema, built once at
             python build time and served as static bytes. Serving overhead is just decoding a
             small zstd section plus an enum compare -- effectively free relative to the SIMD
             dot-product hot path.
license:     Apache-2.0 (per repo; verify before vendoring any prompt text or dataset rows -- the PT-BR demo
             should adopt the schema design only, not their data)

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] wenhuchen/Table-Fact-Checking
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        TabFact is a fact-verification dataset (118k statements over 16.5k Wikipedia tables,
             ENTAILED/REFUTED) shipped with a Latent Program Algorithm (LPA) that compiles each
             natural-language statement into a small typed DSL of ~60 table operations and
             verifies it by symbolic execution via a memoized, trigger-word-gated beam search.
innovation:  A closed, strongly-typed operator DSL (~60 ops over types num/str/row/header_num/header_str/bool)
             where each operator carries a set of natural-language "trigger words" plus POS-tag
             constraints; statement words gate which operators are eligible (must_have /
             must_not_have sets), collapsing an exponential program space into a tractable typed
             beam search whose intermediate results are memoized by command-string hash. The
             verification answer is the deterministic boolean OUTPUT of executing the synthesized
             program against the table, not a learned similarity score.
techniques:
  - Typed program DSL: ~60 ops (count, filter_eq/greater/less[_eq], filter_str_eq fuzzy,
    argmax/argmin, max/min/avg/sum, hop/str_hop/num_hop, all_eq/all_greater universal quantifiers,
    within/not_within, before/after ordering, diff/add, and/or) each with a fixed argument-type
    signature
  - Trigger-word gating: every op maps to NL trigger words + POS tags (max/argmax keyed on JJS/RBS
    superlatives, 'than any'); presence/absence builds must_have/must_not_have sets that prune the
    candidate operator set before search
  - Memoized beam search (dynamic_programming): hist[step] holds states; an op fires only when
    typed argument slots are filled; results cached by command string via call() memoization;
    saved_hash per step dedupes identical states
  - Anytime termination: stop at >100 complete programs or 40s wall-clock; breadth-first so
    shorter programs surface first
  - Entity linking via #xxx;row,col# span notation: longest-text-span match of statement mentions
    to cells/headers, pandas-dtype-driven num/str typing, separate string vs number memory pools
    (mem_str/mem_num, head_str/head_num)
  - Horizontal vs vertical table linearization (preprocess_BERT --scan) turning a 2D table into a
    token sequence for the Table-BERT baseline
  - Deterministic symbolic execution: the program's boolean output IS the verdict, fully
    reproducible and explainable
nest steals: Adapt the typed-operator DSL + trigger-word gating into nest-runtime as a structured-query path
             beside cosine/hnsw/bm25, operating over the canonical text section and a future
             tabular/parquet section. (1) Define a small Rust enum of typed ops (filter_eq,
             greater, count, argmax, within, before/after) as a #[repr(C)] op-code table persisted
             as an OPTIONAL additive section (reserved id 0x09+) holding precomputed
             trigger-word->op-id maps and a per-chunk column type map (num/str), keeping the
             format additive and deterministic. (2) Implement the memoized beam search in
             nest-runtime as a no-panic, seeded, deterministic synthesizer (typed thiserror
             errors, files split ops/exec/search to stay <=300 loc) so a query like 'no team
             scored more than 3 goals' compiles to all_less(filter..) and returns a real boolean
             verdict citable via nest://content_hash/chunk_id, turning the PT-BR fake-news demo
             corpus into an explainable verifier rather than pure retrieval. (3) The
             trigger-word->op gating plus POS/type constraints is a compact lossless index of
             query semantics that complements bm25; the closed op set is a fixed dictionary that
             zstd-compresses well in a non-embedding section.
compression: Indirect. No novel byte-level codec, but two ideas help nest's compress-more goal: (a) a CLOSED
             operator vocabulary + trigger-word dictionary is a tiny fixed symbol table that
             zstd-compresses near-perfectly in a non-embedding section, so storing verification
             programs costs almost nothing; (b) command-string-hash memoization is structural
             dedup of repeated subexpressions, the same principle behind nest's content_hash
             dedup. The real value is feature breadth (deterministic structured truth-checking
             over tables), not raw ratio.
perf:        LPA is pure-Python beam search bounded to depth ~6, beam width 5-7, capped at 100 programs / 40s
             per statement; single-threaded, no SIMD. In Rust on nest's mmap path it would be far
             faster: the search is branch-heavy integer/string work, not float dot-products, so it
             does NOT compete for the SIMD hot path and runs lazily only when a query is
             structured. Memory: two small per-statement pools (strings, numbers) plus a per-step
             state hash set; fits the offline single-binary model trivially.
license:     Code released on GitHub by Wen-hu Chen et al. (UCSB); repo historically carries a
             permissive/research license (verify the exact LICENSE file before vendoring). Dataset
             built from Wikipedia tables, so CC BY-SA upstream considerations apply.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
[MEDIUM] safe-graph/GNN-FakeNews
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
what:        A collection of GNN fake-news detectors (GCNFN, BiGCN, GNN-CL, UPFD-GCN/GAT/SAGE) shipping the
             UPFD dataset: tree-structured Twitter retweet-propagation graphs (politifact 314
             graphs / gossipcop 5,464 graphs) classified fake/real, with node features stored as
             scipy CSR sparse .npz matrices and topology as a single global COO edge list.
innovation:  Decouple the corpus into two orthogonal on-disk planes that compress with different codecs: (1) a
             single global COO edge list (A.txt) plus a node-to-graph-id index that reconstructs
             thousands of small graphs from one flat integer array, and (2) high-dimensional
             node-feature matrices stored as scipy CSR sparse .npz (sp.load_npz) so mostly-zero
             rows cost near nothing on disk while staying densifiable on demand. The propagation
             graph itself IS the truth signal: who-retweeted-whom structure is a fact-checking
             feature, separable from textual content.
techniques:
  - UPFD propagation tree: root=news node, leaves=retweeting users, edge u->v iff v retweeted news
    from u; framed as fake/real graph classification
  - Global COO edge list (A.txt, comma-separated long pairs) coalesced once, with
    node_graph_id.npy partitioning one flat node array into N subgraphs
  - scipy CSR sparse .npz (sp.load_npz + .todense()) for node-feature matrices
    new_{feature}_feature.npz
  - Four interchangeable feature encoders keyed by name: profile=10d, spacy word2vec=300d,
    bert(bert-as-service)=768d, content=300+10=310d
  - Index-array train/val/test splits (train_idx/val_idx/test_idx.npy) referencing graph ids, not
    copies of data
  - Separation of integer topology plane (RLE/delta/varint friendly) from float feature plane
    (quantizable) as independent codec targets
nest steals: Two moves. (1) Demo/truth corpus: ingest UPFD via nest-python as a demo dataset beside the PT-BR
             fake-news sets -- each propagation graph becomes one provenance-tagged record; the
             310d content vectors map onto nest-format embeddings (preset tiny: int8+zstd+hnsw),
             and nest://content_hash/chunk_id reopens the exact news node. Real benchmark for
             'compress AND serve datasets' serving the factcheck-datasets + truth-features domain.
             (2) Reserve a future additive section (id 0x09+, RESERVED in v1, no version bump) for
             graph topology: store edges as a global delta+varint COO array plus a node_graph_id
             partition index, mirroring A.txt/node_graph_id.npy -- topology integers go through
             the non-embedding zstd path while embeddings stay raw-mmap on the hot path. That
             two-plane split (integer topology codec vs float feature codec) is the pattern to
             steal into nest-runtime's planned graph search.
compression: Medium-high. Transferable insight is the two-plane decomposition: integer graph topology (COO +
             partition index) and float feature matrices are statistically different and want
             different codecs -- topology compresses brutally with delta+varint+zstd (sorted,
             low-entropy ids), features quantize/sparsify well (CSR for sparse profile rows, int8
             for dense embeddings). The CSR-.npz nonzero-only trick is a free win for any sparse
             feature section. Their global-edge-list-plus-id-index is exactly how nest should pack
             many small graphs into one flat deterministic byte run.
perf:        Datasets are small (gossipcop ~314k nodes/309k edges total; politifact ~41k/41k) and graphs are
             shallow ego-trees averaging 58-131 nodes, so topology fits in mmap and traversal is
             bounded. Feature matrices drive size: 768d bert dense per node dominates, which is
             why CSR sparse storage + on-demand densify matters; for nest, int8 on the 310d
             content vectors is the right call. Recent analyses note features are often linearly
             separable by class, so aggressive quantization should preserve discriminative power.
license:     Academic use only (dataset); code Apache-2.0 (safe-graph org). Raw data via Google Drive (~1.2GB),
             redistribution restricted -- vendor the loader/format pattern, not the raw corpus.
